Mechanics

Unit and Dimensions

Unit

Measurement of any physical quantity is expressed in terms of an internationally accepted standard called a unit.

Fundamental units

Lengthmetrem
Masskilogramkg
Timeseconds

PrefixSymbolValue

nanon\(10^{-9}\)
picop\(10^{-12}\)
kilok\(10^{3}\)
megaM\(10^{6}\)

Dimensions and dimensional formulae

  • The dimensions of a physical quantity are the powers to which the fundamental quantities (mass \(M\), length \(L\), time \(T\), current \(A\), temperature \(K\) (or \(\Theta\)), amount of substance \(mol\), luminous intensity \(cd\)) must be raised to represent it.
  • Example: force \(=[MLT^{-2}]\) means \([F]=M^1L^1T^{-2}\).
  • Pure numbers, angles (radian), and ratios of like quantities are dimensionless. Arguments of \(\sin,\cos,\exp,\log\) must be dimensionless.
  • Only quantities of the same dimensions can be added, subtracted or equated; both sides of a physical equation have the same dimensions (principle of homogeneity).
  • Derivatives and integrals: \([\dfrac{dy}{dx}]=[y]/[x]\), \([\int y\,dx]=[y][x]\).

Dimensional formulae of common quantities

Quantities with equal dimensions; useful combinations

  • Work, energy, torque, heat: \([ML^2T^{-2}]\). Frequency, angular velocity, \(\dfrac RL\), \(\dfrac1{RC}\), \(\dfrac1{\sqrt{LC}}\): \([T^{-1}]\).
  • Time-like combinations (dimension \(T\)): \(\dfrac LR\), \(RC\), \(\sqrt{LC}\), \(\sqrt{\dfrac lg}\), \(\sqrt{\dfrac mk}\), \(\dfrac\eta{\text{stress}}\).
  • Speed-like (dimension \(LT^{-1}\)): \(\dfrac1{\sqrt{\mu_0\varepsilon_0}}\), \(\dfrac EB\), \(\sqrt{\dfrac Y\rho}\), \(\sqrt{\dfrac{\text{tension}}{\text{mass per length}}}\), \(\sqrt{\dfrac P\rho}\), \(\sqrt{gl}\).
  • \(\dfrac{\text{energy}}{\text{volume}}=\) pressure \(=\) \(\dfrac{B^2}{2\mu_0}=\tfrac12\varepsilon_0E^2\): \([ML^{-1}T^{-2}]\).
  • \(\dfrac hmv\), \(\dfrac h{p}\): length; \(\dfrac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\): \([ML^3T^{-2}]\); \(\dfrac{h}{2\pi}\) and \(L\): same.
  • \(\dfrac{Gm^2}{r}\), \(\dfrac{q^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0r}\), \(mgh\), \(\tfrac12CV^2\), \(\tfrac12LI^2\), \(Pv\), \(kT\): all energy.

Uses and limits of dimensional analysis

  • Check an equation: every term must have the same dimensions (a necessary, not sufficient, check).
  • Derive a relation when the quantity depends on a product of powers of known variables: \(T=k\,l^a g^b m^c\); equate powers of \(M,L,T\) to get \(T\propto\sqrt{l/g}\), \(c=0\).
  • Convert units: \(n_1u_1=n_2u_2\), so \(n_2=n_1\left(\dfrac{M_1}{M_2}\right)^a\left(\dfrac{L_1}{L_2}\right)^b\left(\dfrac{T_1}{T_2}\right)^c\).
  • Limits: cannot fix dimensionless constants (\(2\pi,\tfrac12\), etc.); cannot handle trigonometric/exponential functions of dimensional arguments; fails if a quantity depends on a sum of terms or on more than three variables with no unique solution; cannot tell vector from scalar; cannot distinguish quantities of the same dimension (work vs torque).
  • If \(Q=\dfrac{a^pb^q}{c^r}\), the fractional error is \(\dfrac{\Delta Q}Q=p\dfrac{\Delta a}a+q\dfrac{\Delta b}b+r\dfrac{\Delta c}c\) (maximum).

Definitions of base units (SI)

  • Second: \(9\,192\,631\,770\) periods of the radiation of the \(^{133}\)Cs hyperfine transition.
  • Metre: the distance travelled by light in vacuum in \(\dfrac1{299\,792\,458}\) s (fixing \(c\)).
  • Kilogram: defined by fixing the Planck constant \(h=6.62607015\times10^{-34}\ \mathrm{J\,s}\).
  • Ampere: defined by fixing the elementary charge \(e=1.602176634\times10^{-19}\ \mathrm C\). Kelvin: fixing \(k=1.380649\times10^{-23}\ \mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}\). Mole: exactly \(6.02214076\times10^{23}\) entities.
  • Candela: luminous intensity of a source of monochromatic \(540\times10^{12}\) Hz radiation with radiant intensity \(\dfrac1{683}\ \mathrm{W\,sr^{-1}}\).
  • Radian: angle subtended at the centre by an arc equal to the radius; steradian: solid angle subtending an area \(r^2\) on a sphere of radius \(r\) (\(4\pi\) sr for a full sphere).
2
Mechanics

Vectors & Mathematical Tools

Scalars, vectors and their addition

  • Scalars have magnitude only (mass, time, speed, distance, energy, work, power, temperature, charge, potential, pressure, density, current, angle); vectors have magnitude and direction and obey the vector addition laws (displacement, velocity, acceleration, force, momentum, impulse, torque, angular velocity, electric/magnetic field). Current, pressure and surface tension are not vectors despite having direction.
  • Parallelogram law: for vectors \(\vec A,\vec B\) at angle \(\theta\):
\[R=\sqrt{A^2+B^2+2AB\cos\theta},\qquad\tan\phi=\frac{B\sin\theta}{A+B\cos\theta}\ (\phi\text{ from }\vec A)\]\[\left|\vec A-\vec B\right|=\sqrt{A^2+B^2-2AB\cos\theta}\]
  • \(R_{\max}=A+B\) (\(\theta=0^\circ\)); \(R_{\min}=|A-B|\) (\(\theta=180^\circ\)); \(|A-B|\le R\le A+B\). For \(A=B\): \(R=2A\cos\frac\theta2\), directed along the bisector.
  • Three vectors can have a zero resultant only if each is less than or equal to the sum of the other two (they form a closed triangle). Lami’s theorem: for three forces in equilibrium, \(\dfrac{F_1}{\sin\alpha}=\dfrac{F_2}{\sin\beta}=\dfrac{F_3}{\sin\gamma}\), where \(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\) are the angles opposite to \(F_1,F_2,F_3\) (between the other two forces).

Components, unit vectors and direction cosines

\[\vec A=A_x\hat i+A_y\hat j+A_z\hat k,\quad|\vec A|=\sqrt{A_x^2+A_y^2+A_z^2},\quad\hat A=\frac{\vec A}{|\vec A|}\]\[A_x=A\cos\theta,\ A_y=A\sin\theta\ (\theta\text{ from the }x\text{-axis}),\qquad\cos^2\alpha+\cos^2\beta+\cos^2\gamma=1\]
  • Two vectors are equal iff their components are equal; \(\vec A\parallel\vec B\iff\vec A=\lambda\vec B\).
  • Unit vector in the direction of \((a,b,c)\): \(\dfrac{a\hat i+b\hat j+c\hat k}{\sqrt{a^2+b^2+c^2}}\). A vector of magnitude \(m\) along \(\vec A\): \(m\hat A\).
  • Component of \(\vec A\) along \(\vec B\): \(A_\parallel=\vec A\cdot\hat B\); perpendicular component \(\vec A-(\vec A\cdot\hat B)\hat B\). A unit vector perpendicular to the plane of \(\vec A,\vec B\): \(\pm\dfrac{\vec A\times\vec B}{|\vec A\times\vec B|}\).
  • Position vector of the point \((x,y,z)\) is \(\vec r=x\hat i+y\hat j+z\hat k\); \(\overrightarrow{PQ}=\vec r_Q-\vec r_P\).

Dot (scalar) product and cross (vector) product

\[\vec A\cdot\vec B=AB\cos\theta=A_xB_x+A_yB_y+A_zB_z,\qquad\vec A\times\vec B=AB\sin\theta\,\hat n=\begin{vmatrix}\hat i&\hat j&\hat k\\A_x&A_y&A_z\\B_x&B_y&B_z\end{vmatrix}\]
  • \(\hat i\cdot\hat i=1\), \(\hat i\cdot\hat j=0\); \(\hat i\times\hat j=\hat k\), \(\hat j\times\hat k=\hat i\), \(\hat k\times\hat i=\hat j\); \(\vec A\times\vec A=\vec 0\).
  • \(\vec A\cdot\vec B=\vec B\cdot\vec A\); \(\vec A\times\vec B=-\vec B\times\vec A\); both are distributive. \(\vec A\perp\vec B\iff\vec A\cdot\vec B=0\); \(\vec A\parallel\vec B\iff\vec A\times\vec B=\vec 0\).
  • \(\cos\theta=\dfrac{\vec A\cdot\vec B}{AB}\). \(|\vec A\times\vec B|\) is the area of the parallelogram on \(\vec A,\vec B\); the triangle has half of that.
  • Direction of \(\vec A\times\vec B\): right-hand rule (curl fingers from \(\vec A\) to \(\vec B\); the thumb gives the direction), perpendicular to both.
  • Scalar triple product \(\vec A\cdot(\vec B\times\vec C)\) = volume of the parallelepiped; \(\vec A\times(\vec B\times\vec C)=\vec B(\vec A\cdot\vec C)-\vec C(\vec A\cdot\vec B)\).
  • Physics uses: \(W=\vec F\cdot\vec s\); \(P=\vec F\cdot\vec v\); flux \(\vec E\cdot\vec A\); \(\vec\tau=\vec r\times\vec F\); \(\vec L=\vec r\times\vec p\); \(\vec F=q\vec v\times\vec B\); \(\vec v=\vec\omega\times\vec r\); \(\vec\mu=I\vec A\).

Calculus formulas used in physics

\(f(x)\)\(\dfrac{df}{dx}\)\(f(x)\)\(\dfrac{df}{dx}\)
\(x^n\)\(nx^{n-1}\)\(\sin x\)\(\cos x\)
\(e^{ax}\)\(ae^{ax}\)\(\cos x\)\(-\sin x\)
\(\ln x\)\(\dfrac1x\)\(\tan x\)\(\sec^2x\)
\(uv\)\(u'v+uv'\)\(\dfrac uv\)\(\dfrac{u'v-uv'}{v^2}\)
\(f(g(x))\)\(f'(g)\,g'(x)\)\(\dfrac1x\) (integral)\(\int\dfrac{dx}x=\ln x\)
\[\int x^n\,dx=\frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1}\ (n\ne-1),\quad\int e^{ax}dx=\frac{e^{ax}}a,\quad\int\sin x\,dx=-\cos x,\quad\int\cos x\,dx=\sin x\]
  • Velocity \(v=\dfrac{dx}{dt}\), acceleration \(a=\dfrac{dv}{dt}=v\dfrac{dv}{dx}\); displacement \(=\int v\,dt\); work \(=\int F\,dx\); \(F=-\dfrac{dU}{dx}\); power \(=\dfrac{dW}{dt}\); impulse \(=\int F\,dt\); charge \(=\int I\,dt\).
  • Maxima/minima of \(f\): \(f'(x)=0\); maximum if \(f''\lt0\), minimum if \(f''\gt0\). Slope of a graph \(=\) derivative; area under a graph \(=\) integral.
  • Rate problems: if \(y=y(x)\) and \(x=x(t)\) then \(\dfrac{dy}{dt}=\dfrac{dy}{dx}\dfrac{dx}{dt}\).
  • Average value of \(f\) over \([a,b]\): \(\dfrac1{b-a}\int_a^bf\,dx\); \(\langle\sin^2\rangle=\langle\cos^2\rangle=\dfrac12\) over a period.

Approximations, trigonometry and algebra for physics

  • Binomial: \((1+x)^n\approx1+nx\) for \(|x|\ll1\); so \(\dfrac1{1+x}\approx1-x\), \(\sqrt{1+x}\approx1+\dfrac x2\), \((1+x)^{-1/2}\approx1-\dfrac x2\).
  • Small angles (radian): \(\sin\theta\approx\tan\theta\approx\theta\), \(\cos\theta\approx1-\dfrac{\theta^2}2\); \(e^x\approx1+x\); \(\ln(1+x)\approx x\).
  • \(\sin2\theta=2\sin\theta\cos\theta\); \(\cos2\theta=1-2\sin^2\theta=2\cos^2\theta-1\); \(\sin(A\pm B)=\sin A\cos B\pm\cos A\sin B\); \(\sin C+\sin D=2\sin\frac{C+D}2\cos\frac{C-D}2\); \(\cos C+\cos D=2\cos\frac{C+D}2\cos\frac{C-D}2\); \(\cos C-\cos D=-2\sin\frac{C+D}2\sin\frac{C-D}2\).
  • Quadratic \(ax^2+bx+c=0\): \(x=\dfrac{-b\pm\sqrt{b^2-4ac}}{2a}\). Log: \(\log_{10}2=0.301\), \(\log_{10}3=0.477\), \(\ln10=2.303\), \(\ln2=0.693\); \(e=2.718\), \(\pi=3.1416\), \(\sqrt2=1.414\), \(\sqrt3=1.732\), \(\sqrt5=2.236\).
  • Geometry: circle \(2\pi r\), \(\pi r^2\); sphere \(4\pi r^2\), \(\dfrac43\pi r^3\); cylinder \(2\pi rh\) (curved), \(\pi r^2h\); cone \(\pi rl\) (curved), \(\dfrac13\pi r^2h\); ellipse area \(\pi ab\); arc \(s=r\theta\); sector \(\dfrac12r^2\theta\); solid angle \(\Omega=\dfrac{A}{r^2}\).
  • Sum of A.P.: \(\dfrac n2[2a+(n-1)d]\); G.P.: \(\dfrac{a(r^n-1)}{r-1}\); infinite G.P.: \(\dfrac a{1-r}\); \(1+2+\cdots+n=\dfrac{n(n+1)}2\); \(1^2+\cdots+n^2=\dfrac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}6\).

Graph reading and common shapes

  • Slope of \(x\)–\(t\) graph = velocity; of \(v\)–\(t\) = acceleration; of \(U\)–\(x\) = \(-F\); of \(V\)–\(I\) = resistance; of \(\sigma\)–\(\varepsilon\) = Young’s modulus; of \(\log y\) vs \(\log x\) = the power \(n\) in \(y\propto x^n\).
  • Area under \(v\)–\(t\) = displacement; \(a\)–\(t\) = change in velocity; \(F\)–\(x\) = work; \(F\)–\(t\) = impulse; \(P\)–\(V\) = work done by gas; \(I\)–\(t\) = charge; \(P\)–\(t\) = energy.
  • Straight line \(y=mx+c\) (slope \(m\), intercept \(c\)); parabola \(y=kx^2\) (e.g. \(s=\tfrac12at^2\), \(K=\tfrac{p^2}{2m}\)); hyperbola \(xy=\) const (e.g. Boyle’s law, \(v\propto1/r\)); exponential \(y=y_0e^{\pm kt}\) (decay/charging); sinusoid \(y=A\sin(\omega t+\phi)\).
  • Proportionality: \(y\propto x\) (straight line through origin); \(y\propto x^2\) (parabola); \(y\propto1/x\) (rectangular hyperbola); \(y\propto\sqrt x\) (half-parabola). Percentage change in \(y\propto x^n\): \(\%\Delta y\approx n\,\%\Delta x\).
3
Mechanics

Rectilinear Motion

Average velocity (in an interval)

\[v_{av}=\bar v=\langle v\rangle=\frac{\text{total displacement}}{\text{total time taken}}=\frac{\vec r_f-\vec r_i}{\Delta t}\]

Average speed (in an interval)

\[\text{average speed}=\frac{\text{total distance travelled}}{\text{total time taken}}\]

Instantaneous velocity (at an instant)

\[\vec v_{inst}=\lim_{\Delta t\to0}\left(\frac{\Delta\vec r}{\Delta t}\right)\]

Average acceleration (in an interval)

\[\vec a_{av}=\frac{\Delta\vec v}{\Delta t}=\frac{\vec v_f-\vec v_i}{\Delta t}\]

Instantaneous acceleration (at an instant)

\[\vec a=\frac{d\vec v}{dt}=\lim_{\Delta t\to0}\left(\frac{\Delta\vec v}{\Delta t}\right)\]

Graphs in uniformly accelerated motion along a straight line (\(a\ne0\))

  • \(x\)-\(t\) graph: \(x\) is a quadratic polynomial in \(t\), so the graph is a parabola. It opens upward for \(a\gt0\) and downward for \(a\lt0\).
  • \(v\)-\(t\) graph: \(v\) is a linear polynomial in \(t\), so the graph is a straight line of slope \(a\). It rises for positive \(a\) and falls for negative \(a\), starting from \(u\) at \(t=0\).
  • \(a\)-\(t\) graph: a horizontal line, because \(a\) is constant, above the axis for positive acceleration and below it for negative.

Maxima and minima

\[\frac{dy}{dx}=0\ \&\ \frac{d}{dx}\!\left(\frac{dy}{dx}\right)\lt0\ \text{ at a maximum}\]\[\frac{dy}{dx}=0\ \&\ \frac{d}{dx}\!\left(\frac{dy}{dx}\right)\gt0\ \text{ at a minimum}\]

Equations of motion (constant acceleration)

\[v=u+at\]\[s=ut+\tfrac12at^2,\quad s=vt-\tfrac12at^2,\quad x_f=x_i+ut+\tfrac12at^2\]\[v^2=u^2+2as\]\[s=\frac{(u+v)}2\,t\qquad s_n=u+\frac a2(2n-1)\]

Freely falling bodies (\(u=0\), upward positive)

\[v=-gt\]\[s=-\tfrac12gt^2,\quad s=vt+\tfrac12gt^2,\quad h_f=h_i-\tfrac12gt^2\]\[v^2=-2gs\]\[s_n=-\frac g2(2n-1)\]

Distance, displacement, speed and velocity

  • Distance is the total length of the path (scalar, never decreases); displacement is the shortest vector from the initial to the final position (can be zero or negative). \(|\text{displacement}|\le\text{distance}\).
  • Average speed \(=\dfrac{\text{total distance}}{\text{total time}}\); average velocity \(=\dfrac{\text{displacement}}{\text{time}}\); \(|\vec v_{avg}|\le v_{avg\,speed}\), with equality only for motion along a straight line without reversal.
  • Instantaneous speed \(=|\text{instantaneous velocity}|\), but average speed \(\ne|\)average velocity\(|\) in general.
  • Half the distance at \(v_1\) and half at \(v_2\): \(v_{avg}=\dfrac{2v_1v_2}{v_1+v_2}\) (harmonic mean). Half the time at \(v_1\), half at \(v_2\): \(v_{avg}=\dfrac{v_1+v_2}2\) (arithmetic mean). For three equal distances: \(\dfrac3{1/v_1+1/v_2+1/v_3}\).
  • For a round trip (up and down, or out and back) the average velocity is zero.

Uniformly accelerated motion: more results

\[s=\frac{(u+v)}2t,\qquad s_{n\text{th}}=u+\frac a2(2n-1)\ \text{(distance covered in the }n\text{th second)}\]\[v_{avg}=\frac{u+v}2\ (a\text{ constant}),\qquad v^2-u^2=2as,\qquad s=vt-\tfrac12at^2\]
  • Starting from rest: distances in successive equal time intervals are in the ratio \(1:3:5:7:\cdots\); \(s\propto t^2\); \(v\propto t\); \(v\propto\sqrt s\); times to cover successive equal distances are in the ratio \(1:(\sqrt2-1):(\sqrt3-\sqrt2):\cdots\).
  • Velocity at the midpoint of the path (distance): \(\sqrt{\dfrac{u^2+v^2}2}\); velocity at the mid-time \(=\dfrac{u+v}2\). The speed at mid-distance is greater than the speed at mid-time for accelerated motion.
  • Stopping a vehicle moving at \(u\) with deceleration \(a\): time \(\dfrac ua\), distance \(\dfrac{u^2}{2a}\); stopping distance \(\propto u^2\). Total stopping distance \(=\) reaction distance \(u\,t_r\) \(+\ \dfrac{u^2}{2a}\).
  • When velocity and acceleration are opposite the body reverses at \(t=\dfrac ua\). Total distance in time \(t\gt\dfrac ua\): \(\dfrac{u^2}{2a}+\dfrac12a\left(t-\dfrac ua\right)^2\); displacement: \(ut-\dfrac12at^2\).
  • Sign convention: choose a positive direction; \(u,a,s,v\) carry signs. For a quadratic in \(t\) the two roots may correspond to going and returning.

Variable acceleration (calculus method)

GivenMethod
\(a=f(t)\)\(v=u+\int f(t)dt\); \(x=x_0+\int v\,dt\)
\(a=f(x)\)\(v\,dv=f(x)\,dx\Rightarrow\tfrac12v^2=\tfrac12u^2+\int f(x)dx\)
\(a=f(v)\)\(\dfrac{dv}{f(v)}=dt\) (to find \(v(t)\)) or \(\dfrac{v\,dv}{f(v)}=dx\) (to find \(v(x)\))
\(a=-kv\)\(v=u\,e^{-kt}\); \(x=\dfrac uk(1-e^{-kt})\); total distance \(\dfrac uk\); \(v=u-kx\)
\(a=-kx\) (SHM-like)\(v^2=u^2-kx^2\) type; oscillates with \(\omega=\sqrt k\)
\(a=kt\)\(v=u+\tfrac12kt^2\); \(x=ut+\tfrac16kt^3\)
\(x=At^2+Bt+C\)\(v=2At+B\); \(a=2A\) (uniform acceleration)

If \(v\) is given as a function of \(x\) (e.g. \(v=k\sqrt x\)), then \(a=v\dfrac{dv}{dx}\); the time to travel from \(x_1\) to \(x_2\) is \(\displaystyle\int\dfrac{dx}{v}\).

Interpreting motion graphs

GraphSlopeAreaFeatures
\(x\)–\(t\)velocity—straight line: constant \(v\); upward curve: \(a\gt0\); turning point where slope \(=0\)
\(v\)–\(t\)accelerationdisplacement (signed); distance \(=\int|v|dt\)crossing the time axis: reversal of direction
\(a\)–\(t\)jerkchange in velocity—
\(v\)–\(x\)\(\dfrac{dv}{dx}=\dfrac av\)—a straight line with a negative slope: \(a=-kv\)-type; parabola \(v^2\propto x\): constant \(a\)
  • For a body moving with constant acceleration, \(v\)–\(t\) is a straight line, \(x\)–\(t\) is a parabola; \(v\)–\(x\) is a parabola \(v^2=u^2+2as\).
  • A \(x\)–\(t\) graph cannot have two values of \(x\) at the same \(t\) and cannot have infinite slope; \(v\)–\(t\) cannot have two velocities at the same \(t\) either; but it may have a sharp kink (impulsive change).
  • Average acceleration \(=\dfrac{\Delta v}{\Delta t}\); average velocity over \([t_1,t_2]\) equals the height of the rectangle having the same area under \(v\)–\(t\).

Motion under gravity: all results (\(g\) downward, \(u\) upward)

\[t_{\text{up}}=\frac ug,\quad H_{\max}=\frac{u^2}{2g},\quad T_{\text{flight}}=\frac{2u}g,\quad\text{speed at return}=u\]
  • Time-symmetric: the time from a height \(h\) up to the top equals the time from the top back to \(h\); speed at the same height going up and coming down is equal.
  • Throw upward from a tower of height \(H\) with speed \(u\): \(-H=ut-\tfrac12gt^2\); the speed on reaching the ground \(=\sqrt{u^2+2gH}\) (same for throwing downward with \(u\)).
  • A body falling from rest: distance in the \(n\)th second \(=\dfrac g2(2n-1)\); in successive seconds \(\tfrac g2:\tfrac{3g}2:\tfrac{5g}2\cdots\). In time \(t\): \(s=\tfrac12gt^2\), \(v=gt\).
  • Two bodies: one dropped from \(H\) and one thrown up from the ground with \(u\) at the same instant meet after \(t=\dfrac Hu\) (relative acceleration is zero), at height \(H-\tfrac12g\dfrac{H^2}{u^2}\) above the ground. A body thrown down and one thrown up from the same point with the same speed reach the ground with equal speeds.
  • If a body passes the height \(h\) at times \(t_1\) (going up) and \(t_2\) (coming down): \(t_1+t_2=\dfrac{2u}g\), \(t_1t_2=\dfrac{2h}g\). The time spent above a level that is a distance \(d\) below the highest point is \(2\sqrt{\dfrac{2d}g}\).
  • A ball falling freely covers the last \(n\)th of its height in \(\tau\): solve \(h=\tfrac12gT^2\) and \(h-h/n=\tfrac12g(T-\tau)^2\).

Meeting, overtaking and relative-motion problems in one dimension

  • Two bodies with positions \(x_1(t),x_2(t)\) meet when \(x_1=x_2\); the relative position is \(x_{12}=x_1-x_2\), \(v_{12}=v_1-v_2\), \(a_{12}=a_1-a_2\).
  • Approaching bodies at distance \(d\) with speeds \(v_1,v_2\): meet after \(\dfrac d{v_1+v_2}\). Overtaking with speeds \(v_1\gt v_2\): time \(\dfrac d{v_1-v_2}\).
  • A train of length \(L\) crossing a pole takes \(\dfrac Lv\); crossing a platform of length \(P\): \(\dfrac{L+P}v\); two trains passing each other (opposite directions): \(\dfrac{L_1+L_2}{v_1+v_2}\); (same direction): \(\dfrac{L_1+L_2}{v_1-v_2}\).
  • A car starting from rest with acceleration \(a_1\) and chasing a truck moving at constant speed \(v\) that is \(d\) ahead: they meet when \(\tfrac12a_1t^2=vt+d\). The separation between two bodies is maximum or minimum when their relative velocity is zero (e.g. the truck is farthest ahead of a car that starts from the same point when the car’s speed becomes \(v\), at \(t=v/a_1\)).
  • A vehicle accelerating at \(a\) for time \(t_1\), moving at constant speed for \(t_2\), and decelerating at \(b\) to rest: distance \(=\tfrac12a t_1^2+(at_1)t_2+\dfrac{(at_1)^2}{2b}\); the area of the trapezium in the \(v\)–\(t\) graph.
  • Minimum time to cover a distance \(s\) starting and ending at rest with acceleration \(a\) and deceleration \(b\) (no cruise): \(t=\sqrt{\dfrac{2s(a+b)}{ab}}\), peak speed \(\sqrt{\dfrac{2sab}{a+b}}\).
4
Mechanics

Projectile Motion & Vectors

Time of flight

\[T=\frac{2u\sin\theta}{g}\]

Horizontal range

\[R=\frac{u^2\sin2\theta}{g}\]

Maximum height

\[H=\frac{u^2\sin^2\theta}{2g}\]

Trajectory equation (equation of path)

\[y=x\tan\theta-\frac{gx^2}{2u^2\cos^2\theta}=x\tan\theta\left(1-\frac xR\right)\]

Projection on an inclined plane

Plane inclined at angle \(\beta\) to the horizontal; projection with speed \(u\) at angle \(\alpha\) measured from the incline plane.

Up the inclineDown the incline
Range\(\dfrac{2u^2\sin\alpha\cos(\alpha+\beta)}{g\cos^2\beta}\)\(\dfrac{2u^2\sin\alpha\cos(\alpha-\beta)}{g\cos^2\beta}\)
Time of flight\(\dfrac{2u\sin\alpha}{g\cos\beta}\)\(\dfrac{2u\sin\alpha}{g\cos\beta}\)
Angle of projection for maximum range\(\dfrac\pi4-\dfrac\beta2\)\(\dfrac\pi4+\dfrac\beta2\)
Maximum range\(\dfrac{u^2}{g(1+\sin\beta)}\)\(\dfrac{u^2}{g(1-\sin\beta)}\)

Velocity, position and speed at any instant

\[x=u\cos\theta\,t,\qquad y=u\sin\theta\,t-\tfrac12gt^2\]\[v_x=u\cos\theta,\qquad v_y=u\sin\theta-gt,\qquad v=\sqrt{u^2-2gy}=\sqrt{u^2-2gu\sin\theta\,t+g^2t^2}\]
  • Direction of the velocity: \(\tan\alpha=\dfrac{v_y}{v_x}=\tan\theta-\dfrac{gt}{u\cos\theta}\). The speed is minimum at the highest point, where \(v=u\cos\theta\).
  • The speed depends only on the height \(y\) (energy conservation): the speed on landing at the same level equals \(u\).
  • The velocity is perpendicular to the initial velocity at \(t=\dfrac{u}{g\sin\theta}\) (from \(\vec u\cdot\vec v=0\)).
  • To find when the velocity makes an angle \(\alpha\) with the horizontal, use \(v_y=v_x\tan\alpha\), i.e. \(u\sin\theta-gt=\pm u\cos\theta\tan\alpha\) (plus sign while rising, minus sign while falling).
  • If the body is at height \(h\) at times \(t_1,t_2\): \(t_1+t_2=\dfrac{2u\sin\theta}g\) and \(t_1t_2=\dfrac{2h}g\). The time spent above height \(h\) is \(t_2-t_1\).
  • At the highest point: velocity horizontal \(=u\cos\theta\), acceleration \(=g\) (downward), time \(=\dfrac{u\sin\theta}g\), horizontal distance \(=\dfrac R2\).

Relations among \(T\), \(H\), \(R\) and \(\theta\)

\[\tan\theta=\frac{4H}R,\qquad R=\frac{4H}{\tan\theta},\qquad H=\frac{gT^2}8,\qquad R=\frac{gT^2}{2\tan\theta}\]
  • \(R_{\max}=\dfrac{u^2}g\) at \(\theta=45^\circ\); then \(H=\dfrac{R}{4}\) and \(H_{\max}\) (vertical throw) \(=\dfrac{u^2}{2g}=\dfrac{R_{\max}}2\).
  • Complementary angles \(\theta\) and \(90^\circ-\theta\) (also \(45^\circ\pm\phi\)) give the same range \(R=\dfrac{u^2\sin2\theta}g\). For them \(T_1T_2=\dfrac{2R}g\), \(\dfrac{T_1}{T_2}=\tan\theta\), \(\dfrac{H_1}{H_2}=\tan^2\theta\), \(R=4\sqrt{H_1H_2}\), \(H_1+H_2=\dfrac{u^2}{2g}\).
  • For a given speed the range is maximum at \(45^\circ\); the maximum range equals four times the maximum height at \(45^\circ\), and \(R_{\max}=2H_{\max,\text{vertical}}\).
  • At a given speed \(u\), \(H\) is maximum at \(\theta=90^\circ\) and \(T\) is maximum at \(\theta=90^\circ\); \(R\) is maximum at \(45^\circ\).
  • If \(R=H\): \(\tan\theta=4\) (\(\theta\approx76^\circ\)). If \(R=4H\): \(\theta=45^\circ\). If \(R=2H\): \(\tan\theta=2\).
  • Equation of path in terms of \(R,H\): \(y=\dfrac{4H}{R}x\left(1-\dfrac xR\right)\). It is a parabola whose axis is vertical and vertex is at \(\left(\dfrac R2,H\right)\).

Projectile from a height \(h\) (launched at angle \(\theta\) above the horizontal)

\[T=\frac{u\sin\theta+\sqrt{u^2\sin^2\theta+2gh}}g,\qquad R=u\cos\theta\;T,\qquad v_{\text{ground}}=\sqrt{u^2+2gh}\]
  • For a launch horizontally (\(\theta=0\)): \(T=\sqrt{\dfrac{2h}g}\), \(R=u\sqrt{\dfrac{2h}g}\), \(\tan\alpha=\dfrac{\sqrt{2gh}}u\) at landing; path \(y=-\dfrac{gx^2}{2u^2}\) (measured below the launch point).
  • For a launch at angle \(\theta\) below the horizontal replace \(\theta\to-\theta\): \(T=\dfrac{-u\sin\theta+\sqrt{u^2\sin^2\theta+2gh}}g\).
  • Maximum horizontal range from a height \(h\) at speed \(u\): \(R_{\max}=\dfrac ug\sqrt{u^2+2gh}\), obtained for \(\tan\theta=\dfrac{u}{\sqrt{u^2+2gh}}\) (less than \(45^\circ\)).
  • Maximum height above the ground \(=h+\dfrac{u^2\sin^2\theta}{2g}\). Time to the highest point is unchanged \(=\dfrac{u\sin\theta}g\).
  • The speed with which the ball reaches the ground is the same whatever the angle of projection (for fixed \(u\) and \(h\)).

Further results for ground-to-ground projectiles

  • Parabola of safety (envelope): for a fixed speed \(u\), every point that can be reached lies on or below \(y=\dfrac{u^2}{2g}-\dfrac{gx^2}{2u^2}\). A point \((x,y)\) is reachable if \(u^4\ge g^2x^2+2gu^2y\).
  • To hit a target \((x,y)\) the two possible angles satisfy \(\tan\theta_1+\tan\theta_2=\dfrac{2u^2}{gx}\), \(\tan\theta_1\tan\theta_2=1+\dfrac{2u^2y}{gx^2}\).
  • Radius of curvature: at the highest point \(r=\dfrac{u^2\cos^2\theta}g\); at the point of projection (or landing) \(r=\dfrac{u^2}{g\cos\theta}\). In general \(r=\dfrac{v^2}{g\cos\alpha}\) where \(\alpha\) is the angle of the velocity with the horizontal.
  • Average velocity over the whole flight: \(u\cos\theta\) horizontal. Change in momentum over the flight: \(2mu\sin\theta\) downward. Impulse of gravity \(=mgT\).
  • Average velocity from launch to the highest point: \(\vec v=u\cos\theta\,\hat i+\dfrac{u\sin\theta}2\hat j\), magnitude \(\dfrac u2\sqrt{1+3\cos^2\theta}\).
  • Angular momentum about the point of projection at time \(t\): \(L=\tfrac12mgu\cos\theta\,t^2\) (directed into the plane for a projectile moving to the right); torque \(=mg\,x=mgu\cos\theta\,t\).
  • Power of gravity at time \(t\): \(P=\vec F\cdot\vec v=-mg\,(u\sin\theta-gt)\); \(K=\tfrac12mv^2\); \(U=mgy\); \(K+U=\) const.
  • Kinetic energy at the top \(=\tfrac12mu^2\cos^2\theta=K_0\cos^2\theta\); at the top the potential energy gained is \(K_0\sin^2\theta\).

Projectile in a uniform acceleration field (wind, accelerating frame)

  • Resolve the motion along and perpendicular to the effective acceleration \(\vec g_{\text{eff}}=\vec g-\vec a_0\) (where \(\vec a_0\) is the frame’s acceleration, or the additional acceleration). Treat \(g_{\text{eff}}\) as the new gravity: all projectile formulas hold, with “horizontal” and “vertical” now perpendicular and parallel to \(\vec g_{\text{eff}}\).
  • Horizontal acceleration \(a_x\) (e.g. a steady wind, or a projectile launched from an accelerating car): vertical motion unchanged (\(T=\dfrac{2u\sin\theta}g\), \(H=\dfrac{u^2\sin^2\theta}{2g}\)); horizontal motion \(x=u\cos\theta\,t+\tfrac12a_xt^2\), so \(R=u\cos\theta\,T+\tfrac12a_xT^2\).
  • If the horizontal acceleration is such that the particle returns to its launch point: \(u\cos\theta\,T+\tfrac12a_xT^2=0\Rightarrow a_x=-\dfrac{g\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}=-g\cot\theta\).
  • In the frame of an accelerating lift (acceleration \(a\) upward) \(g\) is replaced by \(g+a\).
  • Charged-particle analogue: in a uniform field, \(a=\dfrac{qE}m\) replaces \(g\); the path is again a parabola.

Projectile motion on an inclined plane: extra results

  • Resolve along and perpendicular to the incline: \(g_\parallel=g\sin\beta\) (down the incline), \(g_\perp=g\cos\beta\). With \(\alpha\) measured from the incline: \(u_\parallel=u\cos\alpha\), \(u_\perp=u\sin\alpha\).
  • Up the incline: \(a_\parallel=-g\sin\beta\), \(a_\perp=-g\cos\beta\); \(T=\dfrac{2u\sin\alpha}{g\cos\beta}\); maximum height above the incline \(=\dfrac{u^2\sin^2\alpha}{2g\cos\beta}\).
  • Down the incline: \(a_\parallel=+g\sin\beta\), \(a_\perp=-g\cos\beta\); same \(T\) and the same height above the incline; range \(=u\cos\alpha\,T+\tfrac12g\sin\beta\,T^2\).
  • The projectile strikes the incline perpendicularly (going up the incline) when \(\cot\alpha=2\tan\beta\), i.e. \(\tan\alpha\tan\beta=\tfrac12\) (\(\alpha\) from the incline). Likewise the projectile strikes the incline horizontally when its vertical velocity is zero at the instant of impact (it is then at its highest point).
  • Body thrown horizontally with speed \(u\) from the top of a smooth incline of angle \(\beta\): \(T=\dfrac{2u\tan\beta}g\); horizontal distance \(\dfrac{2u^2\tan\beta}g\); distance along the incline \(\ell=\dfrac{2u^2\tan\beta}{g\cos\beta}\); impact speed \(u\sqrt{1+4\tan^2\beta}\).
  • If the angle \(\theta\) is measured from the horizontal, the range up the incline is \(R=\dfrac{2u^2\cos\theta\sin(\theta-\beta)}{g\cos^2\beta}\); it is maximum for \(\theta=45^\circ+\dfrac\beta2\), with \(R_{\max}=\dfrac{u^2}{g(1+\sin\beta)}\).

Two projectiles, and a projectile fired at a moving or falling target

  • Two particles in flight have a relative acceleration of zero: the relative velocity \(\vec v_1-\vec v_2\) is constant, so the line joining them moves with a constant velocity and the relative path is a straight line. Two projectiles launched from the same point at the same time with different angles are always separated by \(|\vec u_1-\vec u_2|\,t\).
  • Two bodies launched from different points collide in the air if the relative velocity \(\vec u_1-\vec u_2\) is directed along the line joining their initial positions.
  • Monkey and hunter: a bullet aimed directly at a monkey that drops the instant the gun is fired always hits it (if the bullet reaches the monkey’s vertical line before reaching the ground); both fall through the same \(\tfrac12gt^2\).
  • Particle projected horizontally from a point at height \(h\) and a second particle dropped from rest: both reach the ground together; speeds differ by the horizontal component.
  • Projectile launched from the top of a tower of height \(h\) and the same-speed projectile thrown vertically downward: they reach the ground with the same speed \(\sqrt{u^2+2gh}\).
  • A projectile that would have a range \(R\) but meets a smooth vertical wall at distance \(d\lt R\): the vertical motion is unaffected and the horizontal velocity reverses (multiplied by \(e\)). It lands at distance \(d-e(R-d)\) from the launch point (\(2d-R\) for an elastic impact).
5
Mechanics

Relative Motion

Relative velocity and acceleration

\[\vec v_{AB}=\vec v_A-\vec v_B\quad(\text{velocity of }A\text{ with respect to }B)\]\[\vec a_{AB}=\vec a_A-\vec a_B\quad(\text{acceleration of }A\text{ with respect to }B)\]

Relative motion along a straight line:

\[x_{BA}=x_B-x_A\]

Crossing a river

A boat or man in a river always moves in the direction of the resultant of the velocity of the boat (or man) and the velocity of the river flow. Let \(v_{mR}\) be the velocity of the man with respect to the river, \(v_R\) the river velocity, and \(d\) the river width.

1. Shortest time

Head straight across the river.

\[v_x=v_R\qquad v_y=v_{mR}\]\[v_m=\sqrt{v_{mR}^2+v_R^2}\]\[t=\frac{d}{v_{mR}}\]

Drift downstream \(x=v_R\,t\).

2. Shortest path

Zero drift: \(v_x=0\).

\[v_y=\sqrt{v_{mR}^2-v_R^2}\]\[t=\frac{d}{v_y}=\frac{d}{\sqrt{v_{mR}^2-v_R^2}}\]\[v_R-v_{mR}\sin\theta=0\ \Rightarrow\ \theta=\sin^{-1}\!\left(\frac{v_R}{v_{mR}}\right)\]

\(\theta\) is measured from the perpendicular to the bank, upstream.

Rain problems

\[\vec v_{Rm}=\vec v_R-\vec v_m\quad\text{or}\quad v_{Rm}=\sqrt{v_R^2+v_m^2}\]

Relative velocity in two and three dimensions

\[\vec v_{AB}=\vec v_A-\vec v_B=-\vec v_{BA},\qquad|\vec v_{AB}|=\sqrt{v_A^2+v_B^2-2v_Av_B\cos\theta}\]
  • \(\theta\) is the angle between \(\vec v_A\) and \(\vec v_B\). For \(\theta=0\): \(|v_A-v_B|\); for \(\theta=180^\circ\): \(v_A+v_B\); for \(\theta=90^\circ\): \(\sqrt{v_A^2+v_B^2}\).
  • Direction of \(\vec v_{AB}\): subtract the vectors (components) \(\vec v_{AB}=(v_{Ax}-v_{Bx})\hat i+(v_{Ay}-v_{By})\hat j\); the observer on \(B\) sees \(A\) moving along \(\vec v_{AB}\), as if \(B\) were at rest.
  • Transformation of velocity between frames: \(\vec v_{A,\text{ground}}=\vec v_{A,\text{B}}+\vec v_{B,\text{ground}}\) (for example, boat–river–ground, aeroplane–air–ground, rain–man–ground).
  • Relative velocity of two bodies moving with constant velocities is constant; a body moving with constant velocity is seen by an accelerated observer to be accelerating with \(-\vec a_{\text{observer}}\).
  • The relative acceleration \(\vec a_{AB}=\vec a_A-\vec a_B\); both bodies falling freely have \(\vec a_{AB}=0\), so each sees the other move with constant velocity.
  • Constraint relation: for a rigid rod or inextensible string joining two points, the components of their velocities along the rod (string) are equal; for a pulley system the lengths of the string give a relation between accelerations (\(\sum\ell=\) const).

Closest approach and collision of two moving bodies

\[\vec r=\vec r_A-\vec r_B,\qquad\vec v=\vec v_{AB},\qquad t_{\text{closest}}=-\frac{\vec r\cdot\vec v}{v^2},\qquad d_{\min}=\frac{|\vec r\times\vec v|}{|\vec v|}\]
  • If the relative velocity \(\vec v_{AB}\) makes an angle \(\phi\) with the line from \(A\) to \(B\) (initial distance \(d\)), then \(d_{\min}=d\sin\phi\) and the time of closest approach is \(\dfrac{d\cos\phi}{v_{AB}}\). The bodies collide only if \(\phi=0\), i.e. \(\vec v_{AB}\) points along \(\overrightarrow{AB}\).
  • The relative path of \(A\) as seen from \(B\) is a straight line (for constant velocities); \(d_{\min}\) is the perpendicular distance from \(B\) to this line.
  • Two ships (or cars) at right angles: ship \(A\) at distance \(a\) from the crossing moving at \(v_1\), ship \(B\) at distance \(b\) moving at \(v_2\) towards the crossing: \(d(t)^2=(a-v_1t)^2+(b-v_2t)^2\); minimum at \(t=\dfrac{av_1+bv_2}{v_1^2+v_2^2}\), \(d_{\min}=\dfrac{|av_2-bv_1|}{\sqrt{v_1^2+v_2^2}}\).
  • Collision condition for two bodies on a line (position \(x_1,x_2\)): \(x_1(t)=x_2(t)\) with real positive \(t\); for a body chasing another at the same speed they never meet.

River-crossing problems: all cases

Boat speed in still water \(v_b\), river speed \(w\), width \(d\). Heading makes angle \(\theta\) with the upstream direction (along the bank).

\[v_{\text{across}}=v_b\sin\theta,\qquad v_{\text{along}}=w-v_b\cos\theta\ (\text{downstream}),\qquad t=\frac{d}{v_b\sin\theta},\qquad x_{\text{drift}}=\frac{d\,(w-v_b\cos\theta)}{v_b\sin\theta}\]
ConditionResult
Minimum time\(\theta=90^\circ\); \(t_{\min}=\dfrac d{v_b}\); drift \(=\dfrac{wd}{v_b}\)
Zero drift (\(v_b\gt w\))\(\cos\theta=\dfrac w{v_b}\); \(t=\dfrac d{\sqrt{v_b^2-w^2}}\)
Minimum drift when \(v_b\lt w\) (zero drift impossible)\(\cos\theta=\dfrac{v_b}w\); \(x_{\min}=\dfrac{d\sqrt{w^2-v_b^2}}{v_b}\); the heading is perpendicular to the resultant velocity
Reach a point directly opposite to the startneed \(v_b\gt w\); heading upstream at \(\sin\phi=\dfrac w{v_b}\) from the perpendicular
Reach a point at downstream distance \(x\)\(\dfrac{w-v_b\cos\theta}{v_b\sin\theta}=\dfrac xd\)
  • Round trip over a distance \(L\) along the river: \(t=\dfrac L{v_b+w}+\dfrac L{v_b-w}=\dfrac{2Lv_b}{v_b^2-w^2}\) (longer than the still-water time \(\dfrac{2L}{v_b}\)). Round trip across the river and back (heading adjusted to reach the opposite point): \(t=\dfrac{2L}{\sqrt{v_b^2-w^2}}\), so \(\dfrac{t_{\text{along}}}{t_{\text{across}}}=\dfrac{v_b}{\sqrt{v_b^2-w^2}}\).
  • The time to cross depends only on the component of the boat’s velocity perpendicular to the bank; the river current does not change the crossing time.
  • Swimmer or boat in a wind or moving walkway: same vector equation \(\vec v_{\text{ground}}=\vec v_{\text{boat}}+\vec v_{\text{river}}\).

Rain, wind and aeroplane problems

  • Rain and a moving man: rain falls vertically with \(v_r\); a man walks with \(v_m\). The rain appears to come at angle \(\theta\) with the vertical, \(\tan\theta=\dfrac{v_m}{v_r}\) (the rain comes from the front). The umbrella should be tilted forward by \(\theta\) from the vertical. Speed of rain relative to the man \(=\sqrt{v_r^2+v_m^2}\).
  • If the rain falls at angle \(\alpha\) with the vertical (wind), the man must hold the umbrella along \(\vec v_{rm}=\vec v_r-\vec v_m\); the man feels the rain vertical when \(v_m=v_r\sin\alpha\) (walking in the horizontal direction of the rain).
  • If a man walking at speed \(v_m\) sees the rain falling vertically, the rain has a horizontal velocity \(v_m\) (in his direction) and some vertical speed \(v_y\). If he doubles his speed, the rain now appears to come at an angle \(\theta\) with the vertical where \(\tan\theta=\dfrac{v_m}{v_y}\).
  • Rain on a moving vehicle’s windows: the drop streaks on the side or rear windows are along \(\vec v_{rv}=\vec v_r-\vec v_{\text{vehicle}}\); the angle with the vertical \(\tan\theta=\dfrac{v_{\text{vehicle}}}{v_r}\).
  • Aeroplane and wind: \(\vec v_{\text{ground}}=\vec v_{\text{air}}+\vec v_{\text{wind}}\). To travel due north with a westerly or easterly wind, point the plane into the wind at \(\sin\phi=\dfrac{w}{v_{\text{air}}}\), and the ground speed is \(\sqrt{v_{\text{air}}^2-w^2}\). Flying with and against the wind over a distance \(L\) takes \(\dfrac{2Lv}{v^2-w^2}\).
  • Smoke or flag direction: a flag on a moving vehicle points along the wind velocity relative to the vehicle, \(\vec v_w-\vec v_{\text{vehicle}}\).

Escalators, chases and pursuit curves

  • Man and moving escalator: a man takes \(t_1\) to climb a stationary escalator and the escalator alone takes \(t_2\) to carry a stationary man. Moving in the same direction: \(t=\dfrac{t_1t_2}{t_1+t_2}\); opposite directions: \(t=\dfrac{t_1t_2}{t_2-t_1}\) (for \(t_2\gt t_1\)). Same idea for work/rate problems (add rates).
  • Number of visible steps \(N\) on an escalator: a man walking at a constant rate takes \(n_1\) steps going with the moving escalator and \(n_2\) steps going against it; then \(N=\dfrac{2n_1n_2}{n_1+n_2}\) (from \(N=n_1+e\,t_1=n_2-e\,t_2\), where \(e\) is the escalator speed in steps per second).
  • Pursuit on a regular polygon: \(n\) bodies at the vertices of a regular \(n\)-gon of side \(a\), each moving with speed \(v\) towards the next (clockwise). They meet at the centre after \(t=\dfrac{a}{v\,(1-\cos\frac{2\pi}n)}\). For \(n=3\): \(\dfrac{2a}{3v}\); for \(n=4\) (square): \(\dfrac av\); the path of each is a logarithmic spiral. Length of each path \(=vt\).
  • Pursuit of a body moving on a straight line (a dog always heading towards a rabbit): the rate of change of the separation is \(-v_1+v_2\cos\psi\), where \(\psi\) is the angle between the rabbit’s velocity and the line of sight. The pursuer catches the target if \(v_1\gt v_2\); if the speeds are equal and the target starts moving perpendicular to the line of sight (initial separation \(d\)), the separation tends to \(\dfrac d2\).
  • Relative motion of a ball thrown from a moving train: in the train frame it is an ordinary projectile; in the ground frame the horizontal velocity \(v_{\text{train}}\) is added. A ball dropped from the roof of a uniformly moving train falls straight down in the train frame and follows a parabola in the ground frame.
6
Mechanics

Newton's Laws of Motion

Third law of motion

\[\vec F_{AB}=-\vec F_{BA}\]

\(\vec F_{AB}\) is the force on \(A\) due to \(B\); \(\vec F_{BA}\) is the force on \(B\) due to \(A\).

Second law of motion

\[F_x=\frac{dP_x}{dt}=ma_x\quad F_y=\frac{dP_y}{dt}=ma_y\quad F_z=\frac{dP_z}{dt}=ma_z\]

Weighing machine

A weighing machine does not measure the weight. It measures the force exerted by the object on its upper surface.

Spring force

\[\vec F=-k\vec x\]

\(x\) is the displacement of the free end from its natural length (the deformation of the spring), and \(k\) is the spring constant.

Spring property

\[k\times\ell=\text{constant}\]

where \(\ell\) is the natural length of the spring.

Spring cut in the ratio \(m:n\)

\[\ell_1=\frac{m\ell}{m+n},\qquad\ell_2=\frac{n\ell}{m+n}\]\[k\ell=k_1\ell_1=k_2\ell_2\]

Springs in combination

Series:

\[\frac1{k_{eq}}=\frac1{k_1}+\frac1{k_2}+\cdots\]

Parallel:

\[k_{eq}=k_1+k_2+k_3+\cdots\]

Spring balance

It does not measure the weight. It measures the force exerted by the object on the hook.

Movable pulley

For a movable pulley \(P\) with the two ends of the string moving at \(V_1\) and \(V_2\):

\[V_P=\frac{V_1+V_2}2\qquad a_P=\frac{a_1+a_2}2\]

Two masses over a fixed pulley

\[a=\frac{(m_2-m_1)\,g}{m_1+m_2}\]\[T=\frac{2m_1m_2\,g}{m_1+m_2}\]

Wedge constraint

The components of velocity along the direction perpendicular to the contact plane are always equal for two objects in contact, if there is no deformation and they remain in contact.

Newton's law for a system

\[\vec F_{ext}=m_1\vec a_1+m_2\vec a_2+m_3\vec a_3+\cdots\]

\(\vec F_{ext}\) is the net external force on the system. \(m_1,m_2,m_3\) are the masses and \(\vec a_1,\vec a_2,\vec a_3\) the accelerations of the objects.

Newton's law for a non-inertial frame

\[\vec F_{Real}+\vec F_{Pseudo}=m\vec a\qquad\vec F_{Pseudo}=-m\,\vec a_{Frame}\]

The net sum of real and pseudo forces is taken in the resultant force. \(\vec a\) is the acceleration of the particle in the non-inertial frame.

  • Inertial frame: a frame of reference moving with constant velocity.
  • Non-inertial frame: a frame of reference moving with non-zero acceleration.

Laws of motion: statements and usage

  • First law (inertia): a body stays at rest or in uniform motion in a straight line unless acted on by a net external force. Inertial frames are those in which this law holds; any frame moving with constant velocity relative to an inertial frame is inertial. Inertia is measured by mass.
  • Second law: \(\vec F_{\text{net}}=\dfrac{d\vec p}{dt}\); for constant mass \(\vec F_{\text{net}}=m\vec a\), applied separately to each component: \(\sum F_x=ma_x,\ \sum F_y=ma_y\). 1 N \(=1\ \text{kg m s}^{-2}\); 1 kgf \(=9.8\) N; 1 dyne \(=10^{-5}\) N.
  • Third law: forces occur in action–reaction pairs, equal in magnitude, opposite in direction, acting on different bodies, simultaneous, of the same kind (e.g. both gravitational), and along the line joining them for central forces.
  • Equilibrium: \(\sum\vec F=0\) (translational) and \(\sum\vec\tau=0\) (rotational). Three coplanar concurrent forces: \(\dfrac{F_1}{\sin\alpha}=\dfrac{F_2}{\sin\beta}=\dfrac{F_3}{\sin\gamma}\) (Lami’s theorem).
  • Procedure: (1) isolate the body, (2) draw all forces on it (weight, normal, tension, friction, spring, applied, pseudo), (3) choose axes (usually along and perpendicular to the acceleration or incline), (4) write \(\sum F=ma\) for each body, (5) add constraint relations between accelerations.
  • Contact forces: the normal force acts perpendicular to the surface and adjusts so as to prevent penetration (\(N\ge0\)); the tension in a massless string is the same everywhere; an ideal pulley changes only the direction of the tension.
  • Weight \(W=mg\) (a body in free fall has zero apparent weight but the same true weight); the weight of a body on the Moon is about \(\tfrac16\) of its weight on Earth, mass is unchanged.

Blocks in contact, strings and ropes (smooth horizontal surface)

\[a=\frac{F}{m_1+m_2},\qquad N_{12}=\frac{m_2F}{m_1+m_2}\ \ (\text{push on }m_1),\qquad T=\frac{m_2F}{m_1+m_2}\ \ (\text{string between }m_1,m_2;\ F\text{ on }m_1)\]
  • Three blocks \(m_1,m_2,m_3\) pushed by \(F\) on \(m_1\): \(a=\dfrac F{m_1+m_2+m_3}\); force between \(m_1\) and \(m_2\): \(\dfrac{(m_2+m_3)F}{\sum m}\); between \(m_2\) and \(m_3\): \(\dfrac{m_3F}{\sum m}\).
  • If a force \(F_1\) pushes on one side and \(F_2\) on the opposite side (\(F_1\gt F_2\)): \(a=\dfrac{F_1-F_2}{m_1+m_2}\), contact force \(N=\dfrac{m_2F_1+m_1F_2}{m_1+m_2}\).
  • Uniform rope of mass \(M\), length \(L\), pulled with force \(F\) at one end on a smooth surface: \(a=\dfrac FM\); tension at distance \(x\) from the free end \(=\dfrac{Fx}L\); tension at distance \(x\) from the pulled end \(=F\left(1-\dfrac xL\right)\).
  • Hanging rope (weight \(Mg\)) suspended from the top: tension at distance \(y\) below the top \(=\dfrac{Mg(L-y)}L\). With a block \(m\) attached at the bottom: \(T=\left[m+M\dfrac{L-y}L\right]g\).
  • A massive rope pulled upward with force \(F\): \(a=\dfrac FM-g\); tension at the distance \(x\) from the lower end \(=\dfrac{Mx}{L}(g+a)\).
  • Rope slipping off a smooth table with a length \(x\) hanging: \(a=\dfrac{xg}L\); if it starts from rest with a length \(x_0\) hanging, the speed with which its last end leaves the table is \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{g(L^2-x_0^2)}L}\).
  • Block on block: if the lower block \(M\) is pulled by \(F\) and the upper block \(m\) is held by friction: \(a=\dfrac F{M+m}\) as long as \(\dfrac{mF}{M+m}\le\mu_smg\), i.e. \(F\le\mu_s(M+m)g\); friction on the upper block \(=\dfrac{mF}{M+m}\). If instead the force acts on the upper block (smooth ground), there is no slipping while \(F\le\mu_smg\dfrac{M+m}M\).

Inclined plane, wedge and pulley systems

  • Smooth incline: \(a=g\sin\theta\), \(N=mg\cos\theta\); time to slide a length \(\ell\) from rest: \(\sqrt{\dfrac{2\ell}{g\sin\theta}}\). Speed at the bottom of an incline of height \(h\): \(\sqrt{2gh}\).
  • Time to slide down chords: along any chord of a vertical circle that begins or ends at the top (or bottom) point, the time of descent is the same, \(\sqrt{\dfrac{2D}{g}}\) with \(D\) the diameter.
  • Horizontal force \(F\) on a block on a smooth incline: block is in equilibrium if \(F=mg\tan\theta\) (then \(N=\dfrac{mg}{\cos\theta}\)); acceleration up the incline \(a=\dfrac{F\cos\theta}m-g\sin\theta\), \(N=mg\cos\theta+F\sin\theta\).
  • Block on an accelerated wedge: if a smooth wedge of angle \(\theta\) is pushed horizontally with acceleration \(a_0\) in the direction of the horizontal component of the normal force on the block, the block stays at rest relative to the wedge when \(a_0=g\tan\theta\); then \(N=m\sqrt{g^2+a_0^2}=\dfrac{mg}{\cos\theta}\).
  • Block sliding on a free (smooth) wedge of mass \(M\) on a smooth floor: \(a_{\text{wedge}}=\dfrac{mg\sin\theta\cos\theta}{M+m\sin^2\theta}\), \(N=\dfrac{Mmg\cos\theta}{M+m\sin^2\theta}\), \(a_{\text{block rel. wedge}}=\dfrac{(M+m)g\sin\theta}{M+m\sin^2\theta}\). The centre of mass of the system moves vertically (no horizontal external force).
  • Atwood machine: \(a=\dfrac{(m_1-m_2)g}{m_1+m_2}\), \(T=\dfrac{2m_1m_2g}{m_1+m_2}\), force on the pulley support \(=2T=\dfrac{4m_1m_2g}{m_1+m_2}\). Centre of mass acceleration \(=\left(\dfrac{m_1-m_2}{m_1+m_2}\right)^2g\). In a lift accelerating up with \(a_0\): replace \(g\) by \(g+a_0\) (the tension is \(\dfrac{2m_1m_2(g+a_0)}{m_1+m_2}\)).
  • Mass on a smooth table connected over a pulley to a hanging mass: \(a=\dfrac{m_2g}{m_1+m_2}\), \(T=\dfrac{m_1m_2g}{m_1+m_2}\). With friction \(\mu\) on the table block: \(a=\dfrac{(m_2-\mu m_1)g}{m_1+m_2}\), \(T=\dfrac{m_1m_2(1+\mu)g}{m_1+m_2}\).
  • Mass on an incline over a pulley: \(m_1\) on the smooth incline, \(m_2\) hanging: \(a=\dfrac{(m_2-m_1\sin\theta)g}{m_1+m_2}\), \(T=\dfrac{m_1m_2(1+\sin\theta)g}{m_1+m_2}\). Two blocks on two inclines (angles \(\alpha,\beta\)), smooth: \(a=\dfrac{(m_1\sin\alpha-m_2\sin\beta)g}{m_1+m_2}\).
  • String constraint: for an inextensible string the length is constant, so the sum of (number of segments \(\times\) displacement) is zero; e.g. one end of the string is fixed to the ceiling, it passes under a movable pulley carrying \(m_2\) and then over a fixed pulley to \(m_1\): \(a_1=2a_2\) (the free end moves twice as fast as the movable pulley).

Lifts, apparent weight and pseudo forces

Lift (or frame) acceleration \(a\)Normal force on a person
Up (accelerating up or decelerating while going down)\(N=m(g+a)\)
Down (accelerating down or decelerating while going up)\(N=m(g-a)\)
Constant velocity\(N=mg\)
Free fall \((a=g)\)\(N=0\) (weightlessness)
Downward acceleration greater than \(g\)the person stays on the ceiling; \(N=m(a-g)\) acting downward
  • Pseudo force in a frame accelerating with \(\vec a_0\): \(\vec F_{\text{pseudo}}=-m\vec a_0\), acting at the centre of mass. Effective gravity \(\vec g_{\text{eff}}=\vec g-\vec a_0\).
  • Pendulum in a car accelerating at \(a_0\): equilibrium angle \(\tan\theta=\dfrac{a_0}g\), \(T=m\sqrt{g^2+a_0^2}\), and the period \(T_0=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac\ell{\sqrt{g^2+a_0^2}}}\). In a lift: \(T_0=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac\ell{g\pm a_0}}\).
  • A free surface of liquid in a car accelerating at \(a_0\) tilts so that \(\tan\theta=\dfrac{a_0}g\) with the horizontal; for a liquid in a lift the pressure at depth \(h\) is \(\rho(g+a_0)h\).
  • In a vehicle accelerating forward, an air bubble in a liquid (or a helium balloon in air) moves forward, whereas a hanging heavy body leans backward (the lighter-than-fluid object moves opposite to the pseudo force).
  • Rotating frame: centrifugal force \(m\omega^2r\) outward; Coriolis force \(-2m\,\vec\omega\times\vec v_{\text{rel}}\) (deflects a moving body sideways).
  • Spring balance in a lift reads \(m(g\pm a)\); a spring balance in free fall reads zero; a balance (beam) is unaffected by the lift acceleration (it compares masses).

Impulsive forces, springs and strings; force from a continuous flow of mass

  • Instantaneous situations: the force in a spring cannot change instantaneously (the extension is continuous), whereas the tension in a string (or the normal force) can change abruptly. When a string supporting a spring–mass system is cut, the spring force is the same just after the cut.
  • Two equal masses \(m\) hung from a ceiling: (a) spring from ceiling to \(A\), string from \(A\) to \(B\): on cutting the string, \(a_A=g\) upward and \(a_B=g\) downward. (b) string from ceiling to \(A\), spring from \(A\) to \(B\): on cutting the ceiling string, \(a_A=2g\) downward and \(a_B=0\). Always compare the forces just before and just after the change.
  • Force from a stream: a jet of density \(\rho\), cross-section \(A\), velocity \(v\) striking a wall normally: \(F=\rho Av^2\) (water stops), \(F=2\rho Av^2\) (rebounds with the same speed); on a wall moving away at \(u\): \(F=\rho A(v-u)^2\). Reaction thrust from a hole at depth \(h\): \(F=2\rho ghA\).
  • Machine gun: \(n\) bullets per second each of mass \(m\) and velocity \(v\): average force \(=nmv\); if bullets rebound: \(2nmv\).
  • Falling chain (length \(L\), mass per unit length \(\lambda\)) dropped on a table: when a length \(x\) has fallen on the table the table’s force \(=\lambda xg+\lambda v^2=3\lambda gx\) (using \(v^2=2gx\)). A conveyor belt moving at \(v\) receiving sand at the rate \(\dfrac{dm}{dt}\) needs an extra force \(F=v\dfrac{dm}{dt}\) and power \(v^2\dfrac{dm}{dt}\) (half of which is lost in friction).
  • Impulse–momentum theorem: \(\displaystyle\int\vec F\,dt=\Delta\vec p\); average force \(=\dfrac{\Delta p}{\Delta t}\); the area under an \(F\)–\(t\) graph is the impulse. For a ball hitting a wall with angle \(\theta\) to the normal and speed unchanged: \(\Delta p=2mv\cos\theta\) (normal to the wall).
  • Recoil of a gun: \(M V=mv\); recoil speed \(V=\dfrac{mv}{M}\); the ratio of the kinetic energies is \(\dfrac{K_{\text{gun}}}{K_{\text{bullet}}}=\dfrac mM\).
  • Man pulling the rope of a pulley he stands on: a man of mass \(m\) in a basket of mass \(M\) pulling the rope with force \(F\): total upward force \(2F\) (one on the man’s hand and one on the pulley); \(a=\dfrac{2F-(M+m)g}{M+m}\).
7
Mechanics

Friction

Kinetic friction

\[f_k=\mu_kN\]

The constant \(\mu_k\) is the coefficient of kinetic friction. Its value depends on the nature of the two surfaces in contact.

Static friction

It acts between two surfaces when there is a tendency of relative motion but no relative motion along the contact surfaces. It is a variable, self-adjusting force, with a maximum value called limiting friction.

\[f_{max}=\mu_sN\qquad0\le f_s\le f_{s\,max}\]

Friction against applied force

Static friction rises in step with the applied force (a straight line) up to the maximum \(\mu_sN\). Once the block slips, friction drops to the constant kinetic value \(\mu_kN\), which is slightly lower.

Laws of friction, angle of friction and angle of repose

  • Friction is a contact force opposing relative motion (or the tendency of motion) between two surfaces; it is independent of the apparent area of contact (and, for kinetic friction, of the speed), depends on the nature and roughness of the surfaces, and is proportional to the normal reaction \(N\).
  • Static friction adjusts itself: \(0\le f_s\le\mu_sN\); at the point of sliding, \(f_{s,\max}=\mu_sN\). Kinetic friction: \(f_k=\mu_kN\) (constant, independent of speed); usually \(\mu_k\lt\mu_s\). Rolling friction is much smaller than sliding friction (\(\mu_r\ll\mu_k\)).
  • Angle of friction \(\lambda\): the angle between the resultant contact force \(R=\sqrt{N^2+f^2}\) and the normal at the point of limiting friction, \(\tan\lambda=\mu_s\). The resultant contact force has maximum magnitude \(N\sqrt{1+\mu_s^2}\).
  • Angle of repose \(\alpha\) (the angle of the incline at which a block just begins to slide): \(\tan\alpha=\mu_s\); thus \(\alpha=\lambda\). The angle of repose of a heap of sand is such that its slope has \(\tan\alpha=\mu\).
  • Cone of friction: the cone of semi-vertical angle \(\lambda\) about the normal. A body does not slip if the resultant of the other forces acting on it lies inside this cone (the contact force can then balance it).
  • Frictional force can be driving as well as retarding: walking, the driving wheels of a car (forward friction), a block carried by an accelerating truck, a belt carrying a body. The maximum acceleration that friction can give is \(a_{\max}=\mu_sg\).
  • Graph of friction versus applied force: \(f=F\) (static, linear) up to \(\mu_sN\), then drops to the constant value \(\mu_kN\).

Inclined plane with friction

\[\text{down the incline: }a=g(\sin\theta-\mu_k\cos\theta),\qquad\text{up the incline (decelerating): }a=g(\sin\theta+\mu_k\cos\theta)\]
  • The block is on the verge of sliding for \(\tan\theta=\mu_s\); for \(\theta\lt\lambda\) the block stays at rest with static friction \(f=mg\sin\theta\) (not \(\mu mg\cos\theta\)).
  • Block thrown up with speed \(u\): maximum distance up \(s=\dfrac{u^2}{2g(\sin\theta+\mu\cos\theta)}\); it comes back down only if \(\tan\theta\gt\mu_s\); the speed on returning \(v=u\sqrt{\dfrac{\sin\theta-\mu\cos\theta}{\sin\theta+\mu\cos\theta}}\).
  • A block takes \(n\) times longer to slide down a rough incline than a smooth one (same angle): \(\mu=\left(1-\dfrac1{n^2}\right)\tan\theta\).
  • Least force to move a block on a horizontal surface: a pull at angle \(\alpha\) above the horizontal: \(F=\dfrac{\mu mg}{\cos\alpha+\mu\sin\alpha}\), minimum at \(\tan\alpha=\mu\): \(F_{\min}=\dfrac{\mu mg}{\sqrt{1+\mu^2}}=mg\sin\lambda\). A push at angle \(\alpha\) below the horizontal: \(F=\dfrac{\mu mg}{\cos\alpha-\mu\sin\alpha}\) (jamming if \(\tan\alpha\ge1/\mu\)).
  • To push a block up the incline with a force along the incline: \(F=mg(\sin\theta+\mu\cos\theta)\); with a horizontal force: \(F=mg\tan(\theta+\lambda)=mg\dfrac{\sin\theta+\mu\cos\theta}{\cos\theta-\mu\sin\theta}\); with a force at angle \(\alpha\) above the incline: \(F=\dfrac{mg(\sin\theta+\mu\cos\theta)}{\cos\alpha+\mu\sin\alpha}\), minimum \(F=mg\sin(\theta+\lambda)\) when \(\tan\alpha=\mu\).
  • Least horizontal force to prevent sliding down a rough incline: \(F=mg\tan(\theta-\lambda)=mg\dfrac{\sin\theta-\mu\cos\theta}{\cos\theta+\mu\sin\theta}\) (for \(\theta\gt\lambda\)).
  • Rough wedge accelerating horizontally: a block on the incline stays at rest relative to the wedge for \(a_{\min}\le a_0\le a_{\max}\) with \(a_{\min}=g\dfrac{\sin\theta-\mu\cos\theta}{\cos\theta+\mu\sin\theta}=g\tan(\theta-\lambda)\), \(a_{\max}=g\dfrac{\sin\theta+\mu\cos\theta}{\cos\theta-\mu\sin\theta}=g\tan(\theta+\lambda)\).

Stacked blocks, trucks, belts and motion with friction

  • Block on the floor of an accelerating truck: it moves with the truck if \(a_0\le\mu_sg\). For \(a_0\gt\mu_kg\) the block slips backwards relative to the truck with relative acceleration \(a_0-\mu_kg\); it falls off a truck of length \(\ell\) (starting at the front) after \(t=\sqrt{\dfrac{2\ell}{a_0-\mu_kg}}\). Its acceleration relative to the ground is \(\mu_kg\) forward.
  • Two blocks (\(m\) on \(M\)), friction coefficient \(\mu_1\) between them and \(\mu_2\) between \(M\) and the ground, force \(F\) applied on \(M\): nothing moves if \(F\le\mu_2(M+m)g\); the two move together (\(a=\dfrac{F-\mu_2(M+m)g}{M+m}\)) as long as \(a\le\mu_1g\), i.e. \(F\le(\mu_1+\mu_2)(M+m)g\); beyond this \(m\) slips on \(M\).
  • Maximum acceleration of the block on top (friction only) \(=\mu_1g\). Once slipping begins: \(a_m=\mu_1g\), \(a_M=\dfrac{F-\mu_1mg-\mu_2(M+m)g}M\).
  • Block placed on a moving conveyor belt (belt speed \(v\)): it accelerates at \(\mu g\) until its speed equals \(v\): time \(\dfrac v{\mu g}\), distance moved by the block \(\dfrac{v^2}{2\mu g}\), relative sliding distance \(\dfrac{v^2}{2\mu g}\); heat generated \(Q=\tfrac12mv^2\) (equal to the kinetic energy gained). Work done by the belt’s motor \(=mv^2\).
  • Stopping distance of a vehicle (all wheels locked or maximum braking) with initial speed \(u\): \(s=\dfrac{u^2}{2\mu g}\); stopping time \(\dfrac u{\mu g}\). The minimum distance of a car on a level road from a speed \(u\) is the same, independent of its mass. On an incline \(\theta\): \(s=\dfrac{u^2}{2g(\mu\cos\theta\pm\sin\theta)}\) (\(+\) going uphill).
  • Maximum acceleration of a car on a rough level road \(=\mu g\). On a level curve of radius \(r\), friction supplies the centripetal force, so \(v_{\max}=\sqrt{\mu rg}\); a body on a rotating turntable stays put up to \(\omega_{\max}=\sqrt{\dfrac{\mu g}r}\).
  • Two blocks connected by a string: block on a rough table (\(\mu\)) and a hanging block: they move if \(m_2\gt\mu_sm_1\), with \(a=\dfrac{(m_2-\mu_km_1)g}{m_1+m_2}\).
  • Work done by friction: \(W=-f\,s\) for a displacement \(s\) of the body; the heat generated between two surfaces is \(Q=f_k\times\) (relative sliding distance). Static friction does no work on the surface it rolls on without slipping.

Walls, ladders and rolling

  • Block pressed against a vertical wall by a horizontal force \(F\): it does not slide down if \(\mu_sF\ge mg\), i.e. \(F\ge\dfrac{mg}{\mu_s}\). Friction \(=mg\) (static) in equilibrium. For a block on the inner wall of an accelerating cart (cart acceleration \(a_0\) towards the wall): \(N=ma_0\), no slipping if \(a_0\ge\dfrac g{\mu_s}\).
  • Ladder (length \(\ell\), weight \(W\)) against a smooth wall making angle \(\theta\) with the ground: \(N_{\text{ground}}=W\), \(N_{\text{wall}}=f=\tfrac12W\cot\theta\); the ladder is in equilibrium if \(\tan\theta\ge\dfrac1{2\mu_s}\). A man of weight \(w\) climbing a distance \(x\) along the ladder: \(N_{\text{wall}}=f=\left(\tfrac12W+\dfrac{wx}\ell\right)\cot\theta\); equilibrium holds up to \(x=\dfrac\ell w\left[\mu_s(W+w)\tan\theta-\tfrac12W\right]\) (or the top, \(x=\ell\)).
  • Ladder with friction at both ground (\(\mu_1\)) and wall (\(\mu_2\)): at the limiting condition \(\tan\theta=\dfrac{1-\mu_1\mu_2}{2\mu_1}\) (for a uniform ladder).
  • Rolling without slipping: the contact point is instantaneously at rest; static friction does no work; \(f\le\mu_sN\). A rolling body on a rough incline: \(a=\dfrac{g\sin\theta}{1+I/(mR^2)}\), friction \(f=\dfrac{mg\sin\theta}{1+mR^2/I}\); rolling without slipping requires \(\mu_s\ge\dfrac{\tan\theta}{1+mR^2/I}\) (solid cylinder: \(\mu_s\ge\tfrac13\tan\theta\); solid sphere: \(\mu_s\ge\tfrac27\tan\theta\); ring: \(\mu_s\ge\tfrac12\tan\theta\)).
  • Pulling a roller or car with the engine: for a driving wheel the friction at the ground is forward; for a freely rolling wheel it is small and backward (rolling resistance).
  • Belt and rope friction (capstan): a rope wound through an angle \(\phi\) on a rough fixed cylinder: \(T_2=T_1e^{\mu\phi}\) just before slipping (\(T_2\gt T_1\)).
8
Mechanics

Work, Power & Energy

Work done by a constant force

\[W=\vec F\cdot\vec S\]

Work done by multiple forces

\[\Sigma\vec F=\vec F_1+\vec F_2+\vec F_3+\cdots\]\[W=[\Sigma\vec F]\cdot\vec S\]\[W=\vec F_1\cdot\vec S+\vec F_2\cdot\vec S+\cdots\ \text{ or }\ W=W_1+W_2+W_3+\cdots\]

Work done by a variable force

\[dW=\vec F\cdot d\vec s\]

Momentum and kinetic energy

\[K=\frac{p^2}{2m}\qquad p=\sqrt{2mK}\]

\(p\) is the linear momentum.

Potential energy

\[\int_{U_1}^{U_2}dU=-\int_{r_1}^{r_2}\vec F\cdot d\vec r\quad\text{i.e.}\quad U_2-U_1=-\int_{r_1}^{r_2}\vec F\cdot d\vec r=-W\]\[U=-\int_\infty^r\vec F\cdot d\vec r=-W\]

Conservative forces

\[F=-\frac{\partial U}{\partial r}\]

Work-energy theorem

\[W_C+W_{NC}+W_{PS}=\Delta K\]

\(W_C\): conservative forces, \(W_{NC}\): non-conservative forces, \(W_{PS}\): pseudo forces.

Modified form of the work-energy theorem

\[W_C=-\Delta U\]\[W_{NC}+W_{PS}=\Delta K+\Delta U\]\[W_{NC}+W_{PS}=\Delta E\]

Power

The average power delivered by an agent is

\[P_{av}=\bar P=\frac Wt\]\[P=\frac{\vec F\cdot d\vec S}{dt}=\vec F\cdot\frac{d\vec S}{dt}=\vec F\cdot\vec v\]

Work done by common forces

ForceWork done
Constant force\(W=\vec F\cdot\vec s=Fs\cos\theta\); \(W\gt0\) for \(\theta\lt90^\circ\), \(W=0\) at \(90^\circ\), \(W\lt0\) for \(\theta\gt90^\circ\)
Variable force\(W=\displaystyle\int\vec F\cdot d\vec r=\int F_x\,dx\) (area under the \(F\)–\(x\) graph)
Gravity (near the surface)\(W_g=-mg(h_2-h_1)\) (path independent); for a closed path \(W=0\)
Spring force \(F=-kx\)\(W=-\tfrac12k(x_2^2-x_1^2)\); the work done by an external agent is \(+\tfrac12k(x_2^2-x_1^2)\)
Friction (kinetic)\(W=-f_k\,s\) (path-dependent; \(s\) is the path length); heat produced \(=f_k\times\) relative sliding distance
Normal reaction on a body sliding on a fixed surfacezero (always perpendicular to the displacement)
Tension in a pendulum string; centripetal force in uniform circular motion; magnetic forcezero (always perpendicular to velocity)
Static friction on a body carried by an accelerating surfacemay be positive (it accelerates the body); on a rolling body without slipping it does no net work
Internal forces in a rigid body, or tension in an inextensible stringnet work zero (sum over all pairs)
Electric force\(W=q(V_i-V_f)\)
Pseudo force in a non-inertial frame\(W=\vec F_{ps}\cdot\vec s\) (it must be included when using the work–energy theorem in that frame)
  • Work depends on the frame of reference (displacement is frame dependent); the work–energy theorem holds in every inertial frame. Work is a scalar; the net work is the algebraic sum.
  • Work done by a force while the body is moving with constant velocity (e.g. lifting slowly): the external agent’s work \(=\) change in potential energy; the net work is zero.
  • Units: 1 J \(=1\) N m \(=10^7\) erg; 1 eV \(=1.6\times10^{-19}\) J; 1 kWh \(=3.6\times10^6\) J; 1 cal \(=4.18\) J.

Work done in lifting, pulling and stretching: standard results

SituationMinimum work
Uniform rod (length \(\ell\), mass \(m\)) raised from horizontal to vertical about one end\(\dfrac{mg\ell}2\)
Uniform chain (length \(L\), mass \(M\)) hanging fully, pulled up onto a table\(\dfrac{MgL}2\)
Part of the chain of length \(\ell\) hanging, pulled up\(\dfrac{Mg\ell^2}{2L}\)
\(n\) identical blocks (mass \(m\), thickness \(h\)) lying flat, piled one above the other\(\dfrac{n(n-1)}2mgh\)
Uniform cube (edge \(a\), mass \(m\)) tilted about an edge to the unstable position (diagonal vertical)\(\dfrac{mga(\sqrt2-1)}2\)
Fill a tank or pump out a tank of liquid (volume \(V\), density \(\rho\)) from depth \(h_{cm}\) of its centre of mass\(\rho Vg\,h_{cm}\)
Stretching a spring from \(x_1\) to \(x_2\)\(\tfrac12k(x_2^2-x_1^2)\)
Stretching a wire by \(\Delta\ell\)\(\tfrac12F\Delta\ell=\dfrac{YA\Delta\ell^2}{2\ell}\)
Charging a capacitor to \(Q\)\(\dfrac{Q^2}{2C}\)
Lifting a body of mass \(m\) from the Earth’s surface to height \(h\)\(mgh\) if \(h\ll R\); in general \(\dfrac{GMmh}{R(R+h)}\)
  • Work done in raising a body slowly through height \(h\) along any path (frictionless): \(mgh\). With friction on an incline of angle \(\theta\): \(mg h+\mu mg\cos\theta\dfrac h{\sin\theta}=mgh(1+\mu\cot\theta)\).
  • Man climbing stairs or a ladder: \(W=mgh\); power \(=\dfrac{mgh}t\) (the work done against gravity only; the internal chemical energy used is more).

Potential-energy curves, equilibrium and conservative forces

\[F_x=-\frac{dU}{dx},\qquad\vec F=-\nabla U=-\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial x}\hat i+\frac{\partial U}{\partial y}\hat j+\frac{\partial U}{\partial z}\hat k\right),\qquad U(x)=-\int F\,dx\]
  • Conservative force: work is independent of the path, \(\oint\vec F\cdot d\vec r=0\) for every closed path, and \(W=U_i-U_f\) (e.g. gravity, spring, electrostatic, any central force). Non-conservative: friction, viscous or air drag, and any force that depends on velocity or on the path (e.g. a human muscle force).
  • Equilibrium: \(\dfrac{dU}{dx}=0\). Stable if \(\dfrac{d^2U}{dx^2}\gt0\) (minimum of \(U\)); unstable if \(\dfrac{d^2U}{dx^2}\lt0\) (maximum); neutral if \(U\) is constant (flat).
  • Small oscillations about a stable equilibrium \(x_0\): \(\omega=\sqrt{\dfrac{U''(x_0)}m}\); effective spring constant \(k=U''(x_0)\).
  • With \(E=K+U\) constant: the particle is confined to the region \(U(x)\le E\); points where \(U=E\) are turning points (\(K=0\)); \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{2(E-U)}m}\); \(K\) is maximum where \(U\) is minimum.
  • The slope of the \(U\)–\(x\) graph is \(-F\): force points toward lower potential energy; \(F=0\) at a maximum, minimum or inflection point of \(U\).
  • Molecular potential: \(U(r)=\dfrac{A}{r^{12}}-\dfrac B{r^6}\); equilibrium separation \(r_0=\left(\dfrac{2A}B\right)^{1/6}\); depth \(U_{\min}=-\dfrac{B^2}{4A}\) (binding energy); the force is repulsive for \(r\lt r_0\) and attractive for \(r\gt r_0\).
  • Potential energy is defined up to an additive constant; only differences matter. A system of particles: \(U\) belongs to the whole system (the mutual pair), not to a single body.
  • For the field \(\vec F=-\nabla U\): \(F_x=-\partial U/\partial x\). A force field \(\vec F\) is conservative if \(\dfrac{\partial F_x}{\partial y}=\dfrac{\partial F_y}{\partial x}\) (and similarly for other pairs).

Conservation of mechanical energy: standard results

\[K_1+U_1=K_2+U_2\quad(\text{only conservative forces do work}),\qquad K_1+U_1+W_{\text{nc}}=K_2+U_2\]
  • Body falling on a spring (mass \(m\) dropped from height \(h\) above the free end of a spring of stiffness \(k\)): \(mg(h+x)=\tfrac12kx^2\Rightarrow x_{\max}=\dfrac{mg+\sqrt{m^2g^2+2kmgh}}k\). If \(h=0\) (the mass is released on the unstretched spring): \(x_{\max}=\dfrac{2mg}k\), twice the equilibrium extension \(\dfrac{mg}k\). The maximum speed occurs at the equilibrium position \(x=\dfrac{mg}k\): \(v^2_{\max}=2gh+\dfrac{mg^2}k\).
  • Spring on a horizontal surface (block of mass \(m\) with speed \(v\) hitting a spring): \(\tfrac12mv^2=\tfrac12kx^2\Rightarrow x=v\sqrt{\dfrac mk}\). For a block sliding down an incline of angle \(\theta\) into the spring: \(\tfrac12mv^2+mgx\sin\theta=\tfrac12kx^2\).
  • Pendulum released from angle \(\theta_0\): speed at angle \(\theta\): \(v=\sqrt{2g\ell(\cos\theta-\cos\theta_0)}\); at the lowest point \(v=\sqrt{2g\ell(1-\cos\theta_0)}\) and \(T=mg(3-2\cos\theta_0)\).
  • Loop-the-loop: a block released on a smooth track from height \(h\) just completes a vertical loop of radius \(R\) if \(h=\dfrac52R\) (minimum speed at the bottom \(\sqrt{5gR}\)). For a bead on a smooth ring (or a body on a rod) \(h_{\min}=2R\) (the speed at the top can be zero).
  • Sphere on a smooth hemisphere (radius \(R\)), slipping off from the top: it leaves the surface when \(\cos\theta=\dfrac23\) (with the vertical), at a vertical depth \(\dfrac R3\) below the top, with speed \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{2gR}3}\).
  • Chain sliding off a smooth table: from rest with a length \(x_0\) hanging, the speed when the last link leaves: \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{g(L^2-x_0^2)}L}\).
  • Rod falling about a hinge (length \(\ell\), from horizontal): \(\tfrac12I\omega^2=mg\dfrac\ell2\Rightarrow\omega=\sqrt{\dfrac{3g}\ell}\); speed of the free end \(\sqrt{3g\ell}\).
  • Block on a rough incline: \(\tfrac12mv^2=mg\,s\,(\sin\theta-\mu\cos\theta)\) for a slide of length \(s\) from rest. A block released from height \(h\) on a smooth incline and then moving on a rough horizontal surface stops after \(s=\dfrac h\mu\) (independent of the angle).
  • Elastic string / bungee: a jumper falling a length \(\ell_0\) of unstretched rope then a further \(x\): \(mg(\ell_0+x)=\tfrac12kx^2\).
  • Two blocks and a pulley: the loss in \(U\) of the system equals the gain in total \(K\) (include the kinetic energy of both blocks and any rotating pulley).
  • Mechanical energy lost to friction/air resistance appears as heat; \(\Delta E_{\text{mech}}=W_{\text{nc}}\). Total energy (all forms) is always conserved.

Power: engines, pumps, wind and motion at constant power

\[P_{\text{avg}}=\frac Wt,\qquad P=\vec F\cdot\vec v=\frac{dW}{dt},\qquad\eta=\frac{P_{\text{out}}}{P_{\text{in}}}\]
  • 1 W \(=1\) J s\(^{-1}\); 1 horse power \(=746\) W; 1 kWh \(=3.6\times10^6\) J. Work from a \(P\)–\(t\) graph: area under it.
  • Vehicle with a constant power \(P\): tractive force \(F=\dfrac Pv\); acceleration \(a=\dfrac1m\left(\dfrac Pv-R\right)\) where \(R\) is the resistance. The maximum speed (\(a=0\)): \(v_{\max}=\dfrac PR\). If the resistance is \(R=kv\): \(v_{\max}=\sqrt{\dfrac Pk}\); if \(R=kv^2\): \(v_{\max}=\left(\dfrac Pk\right)^{1/3}\). On a slope: \(P=(R+mg\sin\theta)v\).
  • Constant power, no resistance, starting from rest: \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{2Pt}m}\), \(x=\dfrac23\sqrt{\dfrac{2P}m}\,t^{3/2}\), \(a=\sqrt{\dfrac{P}{2mt}}\). Time to reach speed \(v\): \(t=\dfrac{mv^2}{2P}\).
  • Constant force from rest: \(P=Fv=\dfrac{F^2t}m\); average power over \(t\): \(\dfrac{F^2t}{2m}\) (half the instantaneous value at \(t\)).
  • Pump or crane: \(P=\dfrac{dm}{dt}\left(gh+\tfrac12v^2\right)=\rho Q\left(gh+\tfrac12v^2\right)\) for a volume flow rate \(Q\); \(P_{\text{in}}=P_{\text{out}}/\eta\). Power of a waterfall \(=\rho Qgh\).
  • Wind (or water) turbine: kinetic power available \(=\tfrac12\rho Av^3\) (\(A\) is the swept area); the maximum fraction extractable is the Betz limit \(\dfrac{16}{27}\approx59\%\).
  • Rotational power: \(P=\tau\omega\); a motor of torque \(\tau\) at \(N\) rev min\(^{-1}\): \(P=\dfrac{2\pi N\tau}{60}\).
  • Electrical: \(P=VI=I^2R=\dfrac{V^2}R\) (see current electricity).
  • Man pulling a load: if the lifting speed is constant, power \(=mgv\); if the load accelerates, \(P=m(g+a)v\) increases with time.
  • Power of the force of gravity on a freely falling body at time \(t\): \(mg\cdot gt=mg^2t\) (increases linearly); average over the fall \(=\tfrac12mg^2t\).
9
Mechanics

Circular Motion

Average angular velocity

\[\omega_{av}=\frac{\theta_2-\theta_1}{t_2-t_1}=\frac{\Delta\theta}{\Delta t}\]

Instantaneous angular velocity

\[\omega=\frac{d\theta}{dt}\]

Average angular acceleration

\[\alpha_{av}=\frac{\omega_2-\omega_1}{t_2-t_1}=\frac{\Delta\omega}{\Delta t}\]

Instantaneous angular acceleration

\[\alpha=\frac{d\omega}{dt}=\omega\frac{d\omega}{d\theta}\]

Speed and angular velocity

\[v=r\omega\qquad\vec v=\vec\omega\times\vec r\]

Tangential acceleration

Rate of change of speed:

\[a_t=\frac{dV}{dt}=r\frac{d\omega}{dt}=\omega\frac{dr}{dt}\]

Radial (centripetal) acceleration

\[a_r=\frac{v^2}r=\omega^2r\]

Total acceleration

\[\vec a=\vec a_t+\vec a_r\ \Rightarrow\ a=(a_t^2+a_r^2)^{1/2}\]\[\vec a_t=\vec\alpha\times\vec r\qquad\vec a_r=\vec\omega\times\vec v\]

Angular acceleration (non-uniform circular motion)

\[\vec\alpha=\frac{d\vec\omega}{dt}\]

Radius of curvature

\[R=\frac{v^2}{a_\perp}=\frac{mv^2}{F_\perp}\]

If \(y=f(x)\):

\[R=\frac{\left[1+\left(\dfrac{dy}{dx}\right)^2\right]^{3/2}}{\dfrac{d^2y}{dx^2}}\]

Normal reaction on a concave bridge

\[N=mg\cos\theta+\frac{mv^2}r\]

Normal reaction on a convex bridge

\[N=mg\cos\theta-\frac{mv^2}r\]

Skidding

Vehicle on a level road: \(v_{safe}\le\sqrt{\mu gr}\)

Object on a rotating platform: \(\omega_{max}=\sqrt{\mu g/r}\)

Bending of a cyclist

\[\tan\theta=\frac{v^2}{rg}\]

Banking of road

Without friction:

\[\tan\theta=\frac{v^2}{rg}\]

With friction:

\[\frac{v^2}{rg}=\frac{\mu+\tan\theta}{1-\mu\tan\theta}\]

Maximum and minimum safe speed on a banked frictional road

\[V_{max}=\left[\frac{rg(\mu+\tan\theta)}{(1-\mu\tan\theta)}\right]^{1/2}\qquad V_{min}=\left[\frac{rg(\tan\theta-\mu)}{(1+\mu\tan\theta)}\right]^{1/2}\]

Centrifugal (pseudo) force

\[f=m\omega^2r\]

It acts outwards when the particle itself is taken as the frame.

Effect of earth's rotation on apparent weight

\[N=mg-mR\omega^2\cos^2\theta\]

\(\theta\) is the latitude of the place.

Vertical circle: critical condition

Minimum speed at the lowest point to complete the circle, for the three cases in the booklet (a rigid rod, a bead in a circular track, and a tube, where the support can push as well as pull):

\[V_{min}=\sqrt{4gL}\]

For a string, or an object on the inside of a loop, the minimum speed at the lowest point is \(\sqrt{5gL}\) instead.

Conical pendulum

\[T\cos\theta=mg\qquad T\sin\theta=m\omega^2r\]\[\text{Time period}=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{L\cos\theta}{g}}\]

Relations among angular variables

\(\omega_0\) is the initial angular velocity.

\[\omega=\omega_0+\alpha t\]\[\theta=\omega_0t+\tfrac12\alpha t^2\]\[\omega^2=\omega_0^2+2\alpha\theta\]

Uniform circular motion: core relations

\[v=\omega r=\frac{2\pi r}T=2\pi fr,\qquad a_c=\frac{v^2}r=\omega^2r=\frac{4\pi^2r}{T^2}=\omega v,\qquad F_c=\frac{mv^2}r=m\omega^2r\]
  • \(\omega=\dfrac{2\pi}T=2\pi f\); 1 rpm \(=\dfrac{2\pi}{60}\) rad s\(^{-1}\). Vector forms: \(\vec v=\vec\omega\times\vec r\), \(\vec a_c=\vec\omega\times\vec v=-\omega^2\vec r\). The direction of \(\vec\omega\) is given by the right-hand rule and is along the axis.
  • The centripetal force is not a new force: it is the net force toward the centre supplied by tension, gravity, friction, normal reaction, electric or magnetic force. It does no work (perpendicular to \(\vec v\)); speed and kinetic energy stay constant, but velocity and momentum change.
  • Change over an angle \(\Delta\theta\): \(|\Delta\vec v|=2v\sin\dfrac{\Delta\theta}2\). Over a half revolution \(|\Delta\vec v|=2v\), average acceleration \(=\dfrac{2v}{T/2}=\dfrac{2v^2}{\pi r}\), average velocity \(=\dfrac{2r}{T/2}=\dfrac{2v}\pi\) (average speed \(=v\)). Over a full revolution average velocity and average acceleration are zero.
  • Angle turned in time \(t\): \(\theta=\omega t\); arc length \(s=r\theta\); chord length \(2r\sin\dfrac\theta2\); number of revolutions \(n=\dfrac\theta{2\pi}=ft\).
  • Particles on the same radius or on a rigid rotating body have the same \(\omega\); their speeds are proportional to \(r\) and their centripetal accelerations \(a_c\propto r\). Particles connected by a belt have the same linear speed (\(\omega_1r_1=\omega_2r_2\)); coaxial wheels have the same \(\omega\).
  • Two particles on circles with \(\omega_1\gt\omega_2\) (same direction) meet again after \(t=\dfrac{2\pi}{\omega_1-\omega_2}\); in opposite directions \(t=\dfrac{2\pi}{\omega_1+\omega_2}\). Clock: \(\omega_{\text{min}}=\dfrac{2\pi}{3600}\) rad s\(^{-1}\), \(\omega_{\text{hour}}=\dfrac{2\pi}{43200}\) rad s\(^{-1}\); the hands coincide every \(\dfrac{12}{11}\) h \(=65\tfrac5{11}\) min and are at right angles 44 times a day.
  • Earth’s rotation: \(\omega=\dfrac{2\pi}{86400}=7.27\times10^{-5}\) rad s\(^{-1}\); speed of a point at latitude \(\lambda\): \(v=\omega R\cos\lambda\) (\(465\) m s\(^{-1}\) at the equator).

Vertical circle on a string: tension and speed at every position

\(u\) is the speed at the lowest point; \(\theta\) is the angle of the string from the downward vertical; \(\ell\) is the string length.

\[v^2=u^2-2g\ell(1-\cos\theta),\qquad T=\frac{mv^2}\ell+mg\cos\theta=\frac{m(u^2-2g\ell)}\ell+3mg\cos\theta\]
PositionSpeedTension (for \(u=\sqrt{5g\ell}\))
Lowest point\(u\)\(6mg\)
Horizontal (ends of the horizontal diameter)\(\sqrt{u^2-2g\ell}\) (\(=\sqrt{3g\ell}\))\(3mg\)
Highest point\(\sqrt{u^2-4g\ell}\) (\(=\sqrt{g\ell}\))\(0\)
  • String (or the inside of a loop): must satisfy \(T\ge0\) at the top, so \(v_{\text{top}}\ge\sqrt{g\ell}\) and \(u\ge\sqrt{5g\ell}\). Then \(T_{\text{bottom}}-T_{\text{top}}=6mg\) always.
  • Rod, tube or bead on a ring (support can push): the speed at the top only has to be \(\gt0\), so \(u\ge\sqrt{4g\ell}=2\sqrt{g\ell}\).
  • For \(\sqrt{2g\ell}\lt u\lt\sqrt{5g\ell}\) the string becomes slack in the upper half of the circle and the body leaves the circle as a projectile. The slack position is at \(\cos\theta=-\dfrac{u^2-2g\ell}{3g\ell}\) (\(\theta\) from the lowest point), where the speed is \(v^2=g\ell\sin\alpha\) with \(\alpha\) the height angle above the horizontal diameter. For \(u\le\sqrt{2g\ell}\) the body swings like a pendulum, never rising above the horizontal diameter.
  • After becoming slack at height angle \(\alpha\) above the horizontal, the particle passes through the centre of the circle only if \(\sin\alpha=\dfrac1{\sqrt3}\); this needs \(u^2=g\ell(2+\sqrt3)\).
  • A bucket of water swung in a vertical circle of radius \(r\) keeps the water in only if the speed at the top satisfies \(v\ge\sqrt{gr}\).
  • A body on the outside of a smooth vertical circle (top of a sphere, convex bridge) leaves the surface if \(v\ge\sqrt{gR}\) at the top; for a cyclist/vehicle on a convex bridge \(N=mg-\dfrac{mv^2}R\); on a concave dip \(N=mg+\dfrac{mv^2}R\).

Non-uniform circular motion

\[a_t=\frac{dv}{dt}=r\alpha,\quad a_c=\frac{v^2}r,\quad a=\sqrt{a_t^2+a_c^2},\quad\tan\varphi=\frac{a_c}{a_t}\ (\varphi\text{ from the velocity})\]
  • The tangential force \(F_t=ma_t\) changes the speed; the radial force \(F_r=\dfrac{mv^2}r\) changes the direction. Power of the net force \(=F_tv\); work \(=\int F_t\,ds\).
  • For constant \(a_t\): \(v=v_0+a_tt\), \(s=v_0t+\tfrac12a_tt^2\), \(v^2=v_0^2+2a_ts\); the number of revolutions in \(t\) is \(\dfrac s{2\pi r}\). The ratio \(\dfrac{a_t}{a_c}=\dfrac{r^2\alpha}{v^2}\) decreases with time.
  • When \(a_c=a_t\) the total acceleration makes an angle \(45^\circ\) with the velocity.
  • If a particle moves with speed \(v=kt\) around a circle of radius \(r\): \(a_t=k\), \(a_c=\dfrac{k^2t^2}r\); total \(a=k\sqrt{1+\dfrac{k^2t^4}{r^2}}\). If \(v\) increases with the distance as \(v=k s\), \(a_t=k^2s\).
  • Tension in a rotating rod (mass \(M\), length \(L\), angular speed \(\omega\) about one end): at distance \(x\) from the axis \(T=\dfrac{M\omega^2(L^2-x^2)}{2L}\). Tension in a rotating ring (radius \(R\), mass per unit length \(\lambda\), angular speed \(\omega\)): \(T=\lambda\omega^2R^2=\lambda v^2\).
  • A particle of mass \(m\) at the end of a string of length \(L\) on a smooth horizontal table: \(T=m\omega^2L\); if the string is shortened (radial pull) angular momentum \(mvr\) is conserved: \(v_1r_1=v_2r_2\); work done by the pull \(=\tfrac12m(v_2^2-v_1^2)\); \(T\propto r^{-3}\).

Horizontal circles: conical pendulum, bowl, walls of death and rotating liquids

  • Conical pendulum (length \(\ell\), cone half-angle \(\theta\)): \(T=\dfrac{mg}{\cos\theta}=m\omega^2\ell\), \(\omega^2=\dfrac g{\ell\cos\theta}\), \(v=\sqrt{rg\tan\theta}\) with \(r=\ell\sin\theta\); the height of the cone \(h=\ell\cos\theta=\dfrac g{\omega^2}\), so \(\omega\) alone fixes \(h\) (period \(2\pi\sqrt{h/g}\)).
  • Bead in a smooth hemispherical bowl (radius \(R\)) rotating with the bowl at \(\omega\): the bead at angle \(\theta\) from the vertical through the centre has \(\cos\theta=\dfrac g{\omega^2R}\) (exists only for \(\omega\ge\sqrt{g/R}\)); \(N=m\omega^2R\). If a rough bowl, \(\theta\) lies in a range given by friction.
  • Wall of death / rotor: a person on a vertical wall of radius \(r\) is held by friction against gravity: \(\mu N=mg\) with \(N=\dfrac{mv^2}r\), so \(v_{\min}=\sqrt{\dfrac{gr}\mu}\), \(\omega_{\min}=\sqrt{\dfrac g{\mu r}}\). A motorcyclist on a vertical wall needs the same condition.
  • Rotating liquid in a cylinder (angular speed \(\omega\)): the free surface is a paraboloid \(y=\dfrac{\omega^2r^2}{2g}\) (\(y\) measured from the lowest point on the axis). At a fixed horizontal level the pressure rises with the radius as \(p=p_{\text{axis}}+\tfrac12\rho\omega^2r^2\); along a vertical line it rises by \(\rho g\) per unit depth.
