In a tube of length \(L\) filled with liquid and rotated about one end, the gauge pressure at the far end is \(\tfrac12\rho\omega^2L^2\).
  • Cyclist on a circular track: leaning angle \(\tan\theta=\dfrac{v^2}{rg}\) from the vertical; the ground force makes angle \(\theta\) with the vertical; for no slipping \(\mu\ge\tan\theta\), i.e. \(v\le\sqrt{\mu rg}\).
  • Railway banking: outer rail raised by \(h\) above the inner rail for gauge \(\ell\): \(\tan\theta\approx\dfrac h\ell=\dfrac{v^2}{rg}\), so \(h=\dfrac{\ell v^2}{rg}\). Speed above the design value presses the outer rail’s flange against it.
  • Skidding vs overturning (toppling) of a car on a flat curve (CG height \(H\), track width \(d\)): skids if \(v\gt\sqrt{\mu rg}\); topples if \(v\gt\sqrt{\dfrac{rgd}{2H}}\). The car topples first if \(\dfrac d{2H}\lt\mu\). While turning, the outer wheels carry \(\dfrac{mg}2+\dfrac{mv^2H}{rd}\) and the inner wheels \(\dfrac{mg}2-\dfrac{mv^2H}{rd}\).
  • Particle in a rotating smooth tube or on a rod: in the rotating frame \(\ddot r=\omega^2r\), so \(r=Ae^{\omega t}+Be^{-\omega t}\); the radial speed at distance \(r\) from the axis starting from rest at \(r_0\): \(v_r=\omega\sqrt{r^2-r_0^2}\).
  • Earth’s rotation: effective gravity \(g'=g-R\omega^2\cos^2\lambda\) (latitude \(\lambda\)); at the equator \(g'=g-R\omega^2\) (reduced by about \(0.034\) m s\(^{-2}\)); if the Earth spun with \(\omega=\sqrt{g/R}\) (period \(\approx84.6\) min) bodies at the equator would be weightless. At the poles \(g'=g\).
10
Mechanics

Centre of Mass

Mass moment

\[\vec M=m\vec r\]

Centre of mass of a system of \(n\) discrete particles

\[\vec r_{cm}=\frac{m_1\vec r_1+m_2\vec r_2+\cdots+m_n\vec r_n}{m_1+m_2+\cdots+m_n}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^nm_i\vec r_i}{\sum_{i=1}^nm_i}=\frac1M\sum_{i=1}^nm_i\vec r_i\]

Centre of mass of a continuous mass distribution

\[x_{cm}=\frac{\int x\,dm}{\int dm},\qquad y_{cm}=\frac{\int y\,dm}{\int dm},\qquad z_{cm}=\frac{\int z\,dm}{\int dm}\]

\(\int dm=M\), the mass of the body.

Centre of mass of some common systems

SystemPosition of centre of mass
Two point masses \(m_1r_1=m_2r_2\)\(r_1=\dfrac{m_2L}{m_1+m_2}\) from \(m_1\), \(r_2=\dfrac{m_1L}{m_1+m_2}\) from \(m_2\). It lies closer to the heavier mass.
Rectangular plate (by symmetry)\(x_c=\dfrac b2,\ y_c=\dfrac L2\)
Triangular plateAt the centroid: \(y_c=\dfrac h3\)
Semi-circular ring\(y_c=\dfrac{2R}{\pi},\ x_c=0\)
Semi-circular disc\(y_c=\dfrac{4R}{3\pi},\ x_c=0\)
Hemispherical shell\(y_c=\dfrac R2,\ x_c=0\)
Solid hemisphere\(y_c=\dfrac{3R}8,\ x_c=0\)
Circular cone (solid)\(y_c=\dfrac h4\)
Circular cone (hollow)\(y_c=\dfrac h3\)

Distances are measured from the base (or the centre of the flat face) along the axis of symmetry.

Motion of the centre of mass and conservation of momentum

Velocity of the centre of mass

\[\vec v_{cm}=\frac{m_1\frac{d\vec r_1}{dt}+m_2\frac{d\vec r_2}{dt}+\cdots+m_n\frac{d\vec r_n}{dt}}{M}=\frac{m_1\vec v_1+m_2\vec v_2+\cdots+m_n\vec v_n}{M}\]\[\vec P_{system}=M\vec v_{cm}\]

Acceleration of the centre of mass

\[\vec a_{cm}=\frac{m_1\vec a_1+m_2\vec a_2+\cdots+m_n\vec a_n}{M}=\frac{\text{net force on system}}{M}=\frac{\text{net external force}+\text{net internal force}}{M}=\frac{\text{net external force}}{M}\]\[\vec F_{ext}=M\vec a_{cm}\]

Impulse

The impulse of a force \(F\) acting on a body is

\[\vec J=\int_{t_i}^{t_f}\vec F\,dt\qquad\vec J=\Delta\vec P\ \ \text{(impulse-momentum theorem)}\]
  • Gravitational force and spring force are always non-impulsive.
  • An impulsive force can only be balanced by another impulsive force.

Coefficient of restitution \((e)\)

\[e=\frac{\text{impulse of reformation}}{\text{impulse of deformation}}=\frac{\int F_r\,dt}{\int F_d\,dt}=\frac{\text{velocity of separation along line of impact}}{\text{velocity of approach along line of impact}}\]
\(e\)ImpulseVelocitiesKinetic energyCollision
\(e=1\)reformation \(=\) deformationseparation \(=\) approachmay be conservedelastic
\(e=0\)reformation \(=0\)separation \(=0\)not conservedperfectly inelastic
\(0\lt e\lt1\)reformation \(\lt\) deformationseparation \(\lt\) approachnot conservedinelastic

Variable mass system

If mass is added to or ejected from a system at rate \(\mu\) kg/s with relative velocity \(\vec v_{rel}\) (with respect to the system), the force exerted by this mass on the system has magnitude \(\mu|\vec v_{rel}|\).

Thrust force

\[\vec F_t=\vec v_{rel}\left(\frac{dm}{dt}\right)\]

Rocket propulsion (gravity ignored, initial velocity \(u=0\)):

\[v=v_r\ln\!\left(\frac{m_0}{m}\right)\]

More centres of mass (uniform bodies)

BodyDistance of the centre of mass
Circular arc of radius \(R\) subtending angle \(2\alpha\) at the centre\(\dfrac{R\sin\alpha}\alpha\) from the centre (semicircle: \(\dfrac{2R}\pi\))
Circular sector (disc) of angle \(2\alpha\)\(\dfrac{2R\sin\alpha}{3\alpha}\) from the centre (semicircle: \(\dfrac{4R}{3\pi}\))
Quarter-circular ring / quarter disc\(\dfrac{2R}\pi\) / \(\dfrac{4R}{3\pi}\) from each straight edge
Triangle with vertices \((x_i,y_i)\)\(\left(\dfrac{x_1+x_2+x_3}3,\dfrac{y_1+y_2+y_3}3\right)\) (intersection of the medians; \(\dfrac h3\) from the base)
Solid pyramid or cone; hollow cone (lateral surface only)\(\dfrac h4\) from the base; \(\dfrac h3\) from the base
Non-uniform rod, \(\lambda=kx\) (\(0\le x\le L\))\(\dfrac{2L}3\) from the light end
Rod with density \(\rho=\rho_0(1+x/L)\)\(\dfrac{5L}9\)
Region under the parabola \(y=\dfrac h{a^2}x^2\) for \(0\le x\le a\)\(\bar x=\dfrac{3a}4,\ \bar y=\dfrac{3h}{10}\)
Region between the parabola \(y^2=4ax\) and the line \(x=h\)\(\bar x=\dfrac{3h}5\) from the vertex
  • Symmetry: the centre of mass of a uniform body lies on every axis or plane of symmetry; for a uniform ring, disc, sphere, cylinder, rod, rectangle or cube it is at the geometric centre. It need not lie inside the body (ring, hollow shell, boomerang).
  • Rod made of two parts of lengths \(\ell_1,\ell_2\) and linear densities \(\lambda_1,\lambda_2\): \(x_{cm}=\dfrac{\lambda_1\ell_1\cdot\ell_1/2+\lambda_2\ell_2(\ell_1+\ell_2/2)}{\lambda_1\ell_1+\lambda_2\ell_2}\).

Composite bodies and bodies with cavities

\[\vec r_{cm}=\frac{\sum m_i\vec r_{i,cm}}{\sum m_i}\quad(\text{treat each part as a point mass at its own centre of mass})\]
  • Cavity (negative-mass method): remove the cut-out part by adding a body of negative mass at its centre.
  • Disc of radius \(R\) with a circular hole of radius \(r\) whose centre is at distance \(d\) from the centre of the disc: \(x_{cm}=-\dfrac{r^2d}{R^2-r^2}\) (on the side opposite to the hole).
  • Solid sphere of radius \(R\) with a spherical cavity of radius \(r\) at distance \(d\) from the centre: \(x_{cm}=-\dfrac{r^3d}{R^3-r^3}\). If the cavity has radius \(R/2\) and touches the surface (\(d=R/2\)): \(x_{cm}=-\dfrac R{14}\).
  • Square plate of side \(a\) with a square of side \(\dfrac a2\) removed from one corner: \(x_{cm}=y_{cm}=\dfrac{7a}{12}\) measured from the corner where the square was removed, along each side.
  • Cone on a hemisphere, or cylinder with a hemispherical cap: find each centre of mass from the common base and use the weighted average.
  • If a body at rest breaks into pieces under internal forces only, the centre of mass of the pieces stays where it was.

Centre-of-mass frame, reduced mass and kinetic energy

\[K_{\text{total}}=\tfrac12Mv_{cm}^2+K_{\text{about cm}},\qquad K_{\text{about cm}}=\tfrac12\mu v_{\text{rel}}^2,\qquad\mu=\frac{m_1m_2}{m_1+m_2}\]
  • In the centre-of-mass frame the total momentum is zero: \(m_1\vec v_1'=-m_2\vec v_2'\); speeds are inversely proportional to masses; \(K_1':K_2'=m_2:m_1\).
  • The two-body problem reduces to one body of mass \(\mu\) with relative acceleration \(\vec a_{\text{rel}}=\vec a_1-\vec a_2=\vec F_{12}\left(\dfrac1{m_1}+\dfrac1{m_2}\right)=\dfrac{\vec F}{\mu}\).
  • Two blocks \(m_1,m_2\) joined by a spring of constant \(k\) on a smooth surface oscillate with \(\omega=\sqrt{\dfrac k\mu}\), \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac\mu k}\) about the stationary (or uniformly moving) centre of mass; for a diatomic molecule \(\nu=\dfrac1{2\pi}\sqrt{\dfrac k\mu}\).
  • If the blocks are given a push so that \(m_1\) moves with \(v\) and \(m_2\) is at rest: \(v_{cm}=\dfrac{m_1v}{m_1+m_2}\); the maximum extension is when their velocities are equal: \(\tfrac12kx_{\max}^2=\tfrac12\mu v^2\).
  • The maximum kinetic energy that can be converted into other forms in any collision is \(\tfrac12\mu v_{\text{rel}}^2\) (the kinetic energy in the CM frame).
  • Angular momentum: \(\vec L=\vec r_{cm}\times M\vec v_{cm}+\vec L_{\text{about cm}}\); the angular momentum of a two-body system about its CM is \(\mu\,\vec r\times\vec v_{\text{rel}}\) with \(\vec r=\vec r_1-\vec r_2\).

Applications of the motion of the centre of mass

  • No external force on the system (or none along a direction): the centre of mass keeps its velocity (zero if initially at rest) in that direction, so \(\sum m_i\Delta x_i=0\).
  • Man on a boat (mass of man \(m\), boat \(M\), still water): when the man walks a distance \(L\) relative to the boat, the boat moves \(\dfrac{mL}{M+m}\) in the opposite direction; the man moves \(\dfrac{ML}{M+m}\) relative to the ground. The boat’s speed relative to the water at any instant: \(V_B=\dfrac{mv_{\text{rel}}}{M+m}\).
  • Block (\(m\)) sliding down a smooth wedge (\(M\)) on a smooth floor: the wedge moves back by \(\dfrac{mb}{M+m}\) where \(b\) is the horizontal width of the wedge’s incline.
  • Explosion of a projectile: internal forces cannot change the motion of the centre of mass. If a shell bursts at the top of its path into two parts, the centre of mass of the fragments continues on the original parabola; if both fragments land at the same moment (for example when their velocities just after the burst are horizontal), their centre of mass lands at the original range: \(m_1x_1+m_2x_2=(m_1+m_2)R\), with positions measured from the launch point.
  • Person on a plank on smooth ice: a person who runs from one end to the other end of the plank: the plank slides back so that \(m\Delta x_{\text{man}}+M\Delta x_{\text{plank}}=0\).
  • Pulley and blocks: the acceleration of the centre of mass of a system is \(\vec a_{cm}=\dfrac{\sum m_i\vec a_i}{\sum m_i}\); for two masses on a light string over a fixed pulley it is \(\left(\dfrac{m_1-m_2}{m_1+m_2}\right)^2g\) downward.
  • Chain or liquid falling: for a heap/chain falling to the table, use \(F_{\text{ext}}=\dfrac{dP_{cm}}{dt}\) with \(P_{cm}=M\,v_{cm}\); the table’s reaction at the instant a length \(x\) has fallen is \(3\lambda gx\) (for a chain in free fall).
  • Position of the centre of mass of a body in free fall or in a projectile motion follows the standard equations with \(g\), whatever the internal motion.

Rocket and variable-mass motion: more results

\[v=v_0+v_{\text{rel}}\ln\frac{m_0}{m}-gt\quad(\text{vertical, constant exhaust speed }v_{\text{rel}}\text{ relative to the rocket})\]
  • Thrust \(F=v_{\text{rel}}\left|\dfrac{dm}{dt}\right|\). Acceleration at any instant: \(a=\dfrac{v_{\text{rel}}|dm/dt|}m-g\). The rocket lifts off only if \(v_{\text{rel}}\left|\dfrac{dm}{dt}\right|\gt m_0g\).
  • Mass of the rocket at time \(t\) for a constant burn rate \(\mu\): \(m=m_0-\mu t\); \(v(t)=v_{\text{rel}}\ln\dfrac{m_0}{m_0-\mu t}-gt\). The velocity gained does not depend on the burn rate when gravity is ignored.
  • Multi-stage rocket: total speed gain is the sum of the speeds from each stage, \(\Delta v=\sum v_{\text{rel},i}\ln\dfrac{m_{0,i}}{m_{f,i}}\).
  • Raindrop (or any body) gaining mass \(m\) from a stationary medium (velocity of the added mass zero): \(\dfrac{d(mv)}{dt}=F\Rightarrow m\dfrac{dv}{dt}=F-v\dfrac{dm}{dt}\). If the drop’s mass grows in proportion to the distance fallen (\(m\propto x\)), its acceleration is \(\dfrac g3\).
  • Body losing mass with zero relative velocity (e.g. a leaking sand bag, drops falling off a moving cart): the velocity of the remaining body does not change (there is no thrust).
  • Conveyor belt receiving material: force required \(F=v\dfrac{dm}{dt}\); power \(v^2\dfrac{dm}{dt}\); half of it appears as kinetic energy of the material and half as heat.
  • Pulling a chain from a heap: to lift a chain from a heap at constant speed \(v\), the lifting force is \(F=\lambda gx+\lambda v^2\) (\(x\) is the length already lifted).
11
Mechanics

Collisions & Momentum

Linear momentum, conservation and types of collision

\[\vec p=m\vec v,\qquad K=\frac{p^2}{2m},\qquad\vec F_{\text{net}}=\frac{d\vec p}{dt}\]
  • Conservation of momentum: if the net external force on a system is zero (or, during a very short collision, external forces are negligible compared with the impulsive internal forces), \(\sum\vec p\) is constant: \(m_1\vec u_1+m_2\vec u_2=m_1\vec v_1+m_2\vec v_2\). It holds component by component: a component is conserved if the external force along it is zero.
  • During a collision the impulsive forces are large and brief; weight, spring forces and friction from a rough surface are not impulsive (unless the surface itself is the colliding body). Momentum of the colliding pair is conserved even if mechanical energy is not.
  • Collision types: elastic (\(e=1\), kinetic energy conserved), inelastic (\(0\lt e\lt1\)), perfectly inelastic (\(e=0\), the bodies move together after the collision; maximum loss of kinetic energy consistent with momentum conservation). \(e=\dfrac{v_2-v_1}{u_1-u_2}\) along the line of impact (Newton’s experimental law); \(0\le e\le1\).
  • Head-on (central) collision: velocities lie along the line joining the centres; oblique: they do not. Collisions of smooth bodies exert force only along the line of centres.
  • For equal momenta \(p\): \(K\propto\dfrac1m\); for equal kinetic energies \(p\propto\sqrt m\). The heavier body has more momentum at equal \(K\) and less \(K\) at equal \(p\).
  • Impulse of the collision force \(\vec J=\int\vec F\,dt=\Delta\vec p\); average force \(\bar F=\dfrac J{\Delta t}\), where \(\Delta t\) is the duration of contact.

Head-on collision of two bodies (restitution \(e\))

\[v_1=\frac{(m_1-em_2)u_1+(1+e)m_2u_2}{m_1+m_2},\qquad v_2=\frac{(m_2-em_1)u_2+(1+e)m_1u_1}{m_1+m_2}\]\[\Delta K=\tfrac12\mu(1-e^2)(u_1-u_2)^2,\qquad\mu=\frac{m_1m_2}{m_1+m_2}\]
  • Elastic (\(e=1\)): \(v_1=\dfrac{(m_1-m_2)u_1+2m_2u_2}{m_1+m_2}\), \(v_2=\dfrac{(m_2-m_1)u_2+2m_1u_1}{m_1+m_2}\); also \(v_1=2v_{cm}-u_1\), \(v_2=2v_{cm}-u_2\) (the velocities relative to the centre of mass are reversed), and \(u_1-u_2=-(v_1-v_2)\).
  • Perfectly inelastic (\(e=0\)): \(v=\dfrac{m_1u_1+m_2u_2}{m_1+m_2}\); \(\Delta K=\tfrac12\mu(u_1-u_2)^2\). If \(m_2\) is initially at rest the fraction of kinetic energy lost is \(\dfrac{m_2}{m_1+m_2}\).
  • If \(m_2\) is at rest: fractional loss of kinetic energy \(=\dfrac{m_2}{m_1+m_2}(1-e^2)\); speeds \(v_1=\dfrac{m_1-em_2}{m_1+m_2}u_1\), \(v_2=\dfrac{(1+e)m_1}{m_1+m_2}u_1\).
  • Elastic collision, target at rest: \(v_1=\dfrac{m_1-m_2}{m_1+m_2}u_1\), \(v_2=\dfrac{2m_1}{m_1+m_2}u_1\); fraction of kinetic energy transferred \(\dfrac{4m_1m_2}{(m_1+m_2)^2}\) (maximum, \(=1\), for \(m_1=m_2\)).
  • In an elastic chain \(m_1\to m_2\to m_3\) (with \(m_2\) initially at rest between the other two), the kinetic energy finally given to \(m_3\) is maximum when \(m_2=\sqrt{m_1m_3}\).
  • The kinetic energy lost in a perfectly inelastic collision equals the kinetic energy in the CM frame: \(\tfrac12\mu v_{\text{rel}}^2\).
  • Final speeds satisfy \(v_2-v_1=e(u_1-u_2)\) (speed of separation \(=e\times\) speed of approach).

Special cases of elastic head-on collisions

CaseResult
Equal massesthe bodies exchange velocities (\(v_1=u_2\), \(v_2=u_1\)); a body at rest takes the whole velocity (Newton’s cradle)
\(m_1\ll m_2\), \(m_2\) at rest\(v_1\approx-u_1\) (the light body bounces back with almost the same speed), \(v_2\approx0\)
\(m_1\gg m_2\), \(m_2\) at rest\(v_1\approx u_1\), \(v_2\approx2u_1\) (the light body flies off at twice the speed)
Ball hitting a very heavy stationary wallrebounds with the same speed (\(e=1\)) or \(ev\) (inelastic); impulse on the ball \(m(1+e)v\)
Ball (speed \(v\)) hit head-on by a very heavy bat moving at \(V\) towards itrebound speed \(ev+(1+e)V\); elastic \(v+2V\)
Heavy body at rest struck by a small mass at speed \(u\)momentum transferred \(m(1+e)u\)
Neutron (mass \(m\)) with a nucleus of mass number \(A\) at restfraction of energy retained after a head-on collision \(\left(\dfrac{A-1}{A+1}\right)^2\) (a good moderator is light, e.g. hydrogen)

Bouncing balls, oblique impact with a surface and moving walls

  • Ball dropped from height \(h\) onto a floor with restitution \(e\): speed just before impact \(v_0=\sqrt{2gh}\); after the first impact \(ev_0\); height reached \(h_1=e^2h\); after \(n\) impacts \(h_n=e^{2n}h\) and speed \(e^nv_0\).
  • Time between the \(n\)th and \((n+1)\)th impact \(=2e^n\sqrt{\dfrac{2h}g}\). Total time until it stops: \(T=\sqrt{\dfrac{2h}g}\,\dfrac{1+e}{1-e}\); total distance travelled \(=h\,\dfrac{1+e^2}{1-e^2}\); ratio of successive times \(=e\); \(e=\sqrt{h_{n+1}/h_n}\).
  • If the ball is projected so that it bounces repeatedly with the horizontal velocity unchanged: successive horizontal ranges form a G.P. with ratio \(e\); the vertical component after each impact is multiplied by \(e\).
  • Oblique impact on a smooth fixed surface (speed \(v\), angle of incidence \(\theta\) with the normal): the tangential component \(v\sin\theta\) is unchanged, the normal component becomes \(ev\cos\theta\) (reversed). The angle of rebound \(\phi\): \(\tan\phi=\dfrac{\tan\theta}e\); speed after impact \(v'=v\sqrt{\sin^2\theta+e^2\cos^2\theta}\); impulse on the surface \(J=m v\cos\theta(1+e)\) (normal); loss of kinetic energy \(\tfrac12mv^2\cos^2\theta(1-e^2)\). For \(e=1\): angle of reflection equals the angle of incidence.
  • A ball bouncing off an inclined plane or hitting the inside corner of two walls: resolve along the normal and the tangent of each surface in turn.
  • Ball falling vertically (speed \(v\)) onto a smooth incline of angle \(\beta\): the normal component \(v\cos\beta\) becomes \(ev\cos\beta\) (outwards) and the component \(v\sin\beta\) along the incline is unchanged. The time between the first and second impacts is \(t=\dfrac{2ev\cos\beta}{g\cos\beta}=\dfrac{2ev}g\), independent of \(\beta\). For \(e=1\) and \(\beta=45^\circ\) the ball leaves horizontally.

Oblique collision of two smooth bodies

  • Take the line of centres (line of impact) as the \(x\)-axis. The impulse acts only along it, so for each body the velocity component perpendicular to the line of centres is unchanged, while the components along it obey momentum conservation and \(v_{2x}-v_{1x}=e(u_{1x}-u_{2x})\).
  • Equal masses, elastic, one body initially at rest: after the collision the two bodies move at right angles to each other. If \(\phi\) is the angle between the incident velocity \(u\) and the line of centres at impact, the target moves along the line of centres with speed \(u\cos\phi\), and the incident body moves perpendicular to it with speed \(u\sin\phi\) (its unchanged tangential component).
  • Spheres of radius \(r_1,r_2\) colliding with impact parameter \(b\) (perpendicular distance between the lines of motion): \(\sin\phi=\dfrac b{r_1+r_2}\), where \(\phi\) is the angle between the relative velocity and the line of centres. \(b=0\) is a head-on collision.
  • In the general inelastic case with equal masses and the target at rest the total kinetic energy lost is \(\tfrac14m u^2\cos^2\phi\,(1-e^2)\).
  • Collisions in the CM frame: in an elastic collision the speed of each body relative to the centre of mass is unchanged and only its direction rotates (in one dimension it simply reverses).

Bullet–block, ballistic pendulum, springs and string jerks

  • Bullet embedded in a block (bullet \(m\), speed \(u\); block \(M\) at rest on a smooth surface): \(v=\dfrac{mu}{m+M}\); fraction of kinetic energy lost \(=\dfrac M{m+M}\); heat generated \(=\dfrac{mM}{2(m+M)}u^2\). If the resistive force on the bullet is constant \(F\): depth of penetration \(d=\dfrac{\mu u^2}{2F}\) with \(\mu=\dfrac{mM}{m+M}\) (for a fixed block \(d=\dfrac{mu^2}{2F}\)); time of penetration \(\dfrac{\mu u}{F}\).
  • Ballistic pendulum: the block hangs from strings; after the bullet embeds, \(v=\dfrac{mu}{m+M}\) and the block rises \(h=\dfrac{v^2}{2g}\), so \(u=\dfrac{m+M}m\sqrt{2gh}\) (or \(h=\ell(1-\cos\theta)\) for an angle \(\theta\)). For the combination to complete the vertical circle on a string \(u=\dfrac{m+M}m\sqrt{5g\ell}\); on a light rod \(u=\dfrac{m+M}m\,2\sqrt{g\ell}\).
  • Bullet passing through a block: \(mu=mv+MV\); the energy lost \(\Delta E=\tfrac12mu^2-\tfrac12mv^2-\tfrac12MV^2\); the number of identical planks a bullet of speed \(u\) can pierce, if it emerges from the first with speed \(v\): \(n=\dfrac{u^2}{u^2-v^2}\) (constant resistive force).
  • Block hits a spring attached to a second block (all on a smooth surface): maximum compression when both have the same velocity \(v_{cm}\): \(\tfrac12kx_{\max}^2=\tfrac12\mu u^2\), i.e. \(x_{\max}=u\sqrt{\dfrac\mu k}\); when the spring returns to its natural length the velocities are those of an elastic collision. The first block, if \(m_1\) hits \(m_2\) at rest, comes to rest when \(m_1=m_2\).
  • Sudden tightening of a string (a jerk between two bodies, or one body and a fixed point): an impulsive tension acts along the string; the component of velocity along the string of the two ends becomes equal (inextensible string), the perpendicular components are unchanged. If a string is jerked taut by a ball falling at angle \(\theta\) to the string, only the component along the string is lost.
  • Block on a block (a falling mass on a pan or a spring-supported plate): momentum is conserved in the instant of impact (no time for spring force to act): \(mv=(m+M)V\); the new equilibrium position is lower than the old one by \(\dfrac{mg}k\), so at the instant of impact the plate is \(x_0=\dfrac{mg}k\) above the new equilibrium. The subsequent SHM has \(\omega=\sqrt{\dfrac k{m+M}}\) and amplitude \(A=\sqrt{x_0^2+\dfrac{V^2}{\omega^2}}\).
  • Collision of a body with a rod or disc (hinged or free): angular momentum about the hinge (or about the centre of mass) is conserved instead of linear momentum (see rigid body dynamics).

Explosions, recoil and decay

  • A system at rest breaking into parts under internal forces: \(\sum\vec p=0\); for two fragments \(m_1\vec v_1=-m_2\vec v_2\), so \(\dfrac{v_1}{v_2}=\dfrac{m_2}{m_1}\) and \(\dfrac{K_1}{K_2}=\dfrac{m_2}{m_1}\); if the energy released is \(Q\): \(K_1=\dfrac{m_2}{m_1+m_2}Q\), \(K_2=\dfrac{m_1}{m_1+m_2}Q\). Kinetic energy is not conserved (chemical or nuclear energy is converted).
  • Gun and bullet: recoil velocity \(V=\dfrac{mv}M\) (opposite); kinetic-energy ratio gun:bullet \(=\dfrac mM\).
  • Two blocks pushed apart by a compressed spring (spring constant \(k\), compression \(x\)): \(\tfrac12kx^2=\tfrac12m_1v_1^2+\tfrac12m_2v_2^2\) with \(m_1v_1=m_2v_2\).
  • Radioactive decay at rest (\(\alpha\) emission from a nucleus of mass number \(A\)): \(K_\alpha=\dfrac{A-4}AQ\), \(K_{\text{daughter}}=\dfrac4AQ\). The heavy daughter nucleus recoils with momentum equal and opposite to the emitted particle’s.
  • Photon emission or absorption: a photon of energy \(E\) carries momentum \(p=\dfrac Ec\); the recoil kinetic energy of an atom of mass \(M\) is \(\dfrac{E^2}{2Mc^2}\) (negligible compared with \(E\)).
  • Shell exploding in flight: momentum is conserved just after the burst; each fragment then moves as a projectile; the centre of mass of the fragments continues on the original path.
  • Man throwing a ball from a boat or skater pushing a wall: momentum of the system (man + boat/ball) is conserved; the work done by the man \(=\) total kinetic energy produced.
12
Mechanics

Rigid Body Dynamics

1. Rigid body

For a rigid body, the distance between any two particles stays unchanged. For two points \(A\) and \(B\) at angles \(\theta_1\) and \(\theta_2\) to the line joining them:

\[V_A\cos\theta_1=V_B\cos\theta_2\]

\(V_{BA}\) is the relative velocity of point \(B\) with respect to point \(A\). A rigid body can have pure translational motion, pure rotational motion, or combined translational and rotational motion.

2. Moment of inertia \((I)\)

The capability of a system to oppose the change produced in the rotational motion of a body. It is a scalar, positive quantity, and its SI unit is kg m².

\[I=m_1r_1^2+m_2r_2^2+\cdots=I_1+I_2+I_3+\cdots\]
  • Single particle: \(I=mr^2\), where \(r\) is the perpendicular distance from the axis.
  • System of particles: \(\displaystyle I=\sum_{i=1}^nm_ir_i^2\)
  • Continuous object: \(\displaystyle I=\int r^2\,dm\), where \(dm\) is the mass of a small element
  • Larger object: \(\displaystyle I=\int dI_{element}\)

3. Two important theorems

Perpendicular axis theorem (only for plane lamina, i.e. 2-D objects). When the object lies in the \(x\)-\(y\) plane:

\[I_z=I_x+I_y\]

Parallel axis theorem (any type of object):

\[I_{AB}=I_{cm}+Md^2\]

Moments of inertia of common objects

ObjectAxis\(I\)
Solid spherethrough the centre\(\dfrac25MR^2\)
Hollow spherethrough the centre\(\dfrac23MR^2\)
Ringthrough the centre, perpendicular to the plane\(MR^2\) (uniform or non-uniform)
Disc (or a sector of a disc)through the centre, perpendicular to the plane\(\dfrac{MR^2}2\)
Hollow cylinderalong the axis\(MR^2\) (uniform or non-uniform)
Solid cylinderalong the axis\(\dfrac{MR^2}2\)
Thin rod, mass \(M\), length \(L\)perpendicular, through one end\(\dfrac{ML^2}3\)
Thin rod, mass \(M\), length \(L\)perpendicular, through the centre\(\dfrac{ML^2}{12}\)
Two rods, each mass \(m\) and length \(\ell\), joined at a pointthrough the joint, perpendicular to their plane\(\dfrac{2m\ell^2}3\)
Square plate, side \(a\)in the plane, through the centre (along a diagonal or a mid-line)\(\dfrac{Ma^2}{12}\)
Square plate, side \(a\)perpendicular to the plane, through the centre\(\dfrac{Ma^2}6\)
Rectangular plate, sides \(a\) and \(b\)perpendicular to the plane, through the centre\(\dfrac{M(a^2+b^2)}{12}\)
Cuboid, edges \(a,b,c\)through the centres of the \(a\times b\) faces\(\dfrac{M(a^2+b^2)}{12}\)

All values are for uniform objects unless noted.

4. Radius of gyration

\[I=MK^2\]

5. Torque

\[\vec\tau=\vec r\times\vec F\]

Relation between \(\tau\) and \(\alpha\) (hinged object or pure rotation):

\[\vec\tau_{ext}\big)_{Hinge}=I_{Hinge}\,\vec\alpha\]

where \(\vec\tau_{ext})_{Hinge}\) is the net external torque about the hinge point and \(I_{Hinge}\) is the moment of inertia about the hinge point. For particles of mass \(M_1,M_2,\ldots\) at distances \(r_1,r_2,\ldots\):

\[F_{1t}=M_1a_{1t}=M_1r_1\alpha\qquad F_{2t}=M_2a_{2t}=M_2r_2\alpha\]\[\tau_{resultant}=F_{1t}r_1+F_{2t}r_2+\cdots=M_1\alpha r_1^2+M_2\alpha r_2^2+\cdots=I\alpha\]

Rotational kinetic energy and forces on a rotating body

\[K=\tfrac12I\omega^2\]\[\vec P=M\vec v_{CM}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\vec F_{external}=M\vec a_{CM}\]

The net external force has a centripetal part and a tangential part:

\[F_C=ma_C=m\frac{v^2}{r_{CM}}=m\omega^2r_{CM}\qquad F_t=ma_t=m\alpha\,r_{CM}\]

6. Rotational equilibrium

Translational equilibrium:

\[\Sigma F_x=0\qquad\Sigma F_y=0\]

Rotational equilibrium:

\[\Sigma\Gamma_z=0\]

7. Angular momentum \((\vec L)\)

7.1 Of a particle about a point

\[\vec L=\vec r\times\vec P\ \Rightarrow\ L=rP\sin\theta\]\[|\vec L|=r_\perp\times P=P_\perp\times r\]

7.3 Of a rigid body rotating about a fixed axis \(H\)

\[\vec L_H=I_H\vec\omega\]

7.4 Conservation of angular momentum: the angular momentum of a particle or system stays constant if \(\vec\tau_{ext}=0\) about that point or axis.

7.5 Torque and angular momentum:

\[\vec\tau=\frac{d\vec L}{dt}\]

7.6 Impulse of torque:

\[\int\tau\,dt=\Delta J\quad(\Delta J=\text{change in angular momentum})\]

Kinematics of a rigid body

For particles \(P\) and \(Q\) of a rigid body, \(r_{P/Q}\) stays constant. \(\theta,\omega,\alpha\) are the same about every point of the body.

Velocity of \(P\) when \(Q\) moves with \(V_Q\):

\[V_P=\sqrt{V_Q^2+(\omega r)^2+2V_Q\,\omega r\cos\theta}\]

Relative to \(Q\), \(P\) has speed \(\omega r\) (tangential) and acceleration components \(\omega^2r\) (radial) and \(\alpha r\) (tangential).

Dynamics of a rigid body

\[\vec\tau_{cm}=I_{cm}\vec\alpha\qquad\vec F_{ext}=M\vec a_{cm}\qquad\vec P_{system}=M\vec v_{cm}\]\[\text{Total K.E.}=\tfrac12Mv_{cm}^2+\tfrac12I_{cm}\omega^2\]\[\vec L_{AB}=\vec L\text{ about C.M.}+\vec L\text{ of C.M. about }AB=I_{cm}\vec\omega+\vec r_{cm}\times M\vec v_{cm}\]

Rotational analogues of linear motion

LinearRotational (fixed axis)
displacement \(x\), velocity \(v\), acceleration \(a\)angle \(\theta\), angular velocity \(\omega\), angular acceleration \(\alpha\)
mass \(m\)moment of inertia \(I=\sum m_ir_i^2=\int r^2dm\)
force \(F\)torque \(\tau=rF\sin\theta=I\alpha\)
momentum \(p=mv\), \(F=\dfrac{dp}{dt}\)angular momentum \(L=I\omega\), \(\tau=\dfrac{dL}{dt}\)
kinetic energy \(\tfrac12mv^2\)rotational kinetic energy \(\tfrac12I\omega^2\)
work \(W=\int F\,dx\); power \(P=Fv\)work \(W=\int\tau\,d\theta\); power \(P=\tau\omega\)
\(v=u+at,\ s=ut+\tfrac12at^2,\ v^2=u^2+2as\)\(\omega=\omega_0+\alpha t,\ \theta=\omega_0t+\tfrac12\alpha t^2,\ \omega^2=\omega_0^2+2\alpha\theta\)
impulse \(\int F\,dt=\Delta p\)angular impulse \(\int\tau\,dt=\Delta L\)
  • Work–energy theorem for rotation: \(W_{\text{net}}=\tfrac12I\omega_2^2-\tfrac12I\omega_1^2\); a constant torque \(\tau\) acting through angle \(\theta\) does \(W=\tau\theta\).
  • Torque by a couple: \(\tau=F\,d\) (\(d\) is the perpendicular distance between the forces); it is the same about every point and produces pure rotation.
  • Relation of a point’s linear and angular quantities: \(v=\omega r\), \(a_t=\alpha r\), \(a_c=\omega^2r\).
  • Moment of inertia is additive for bodies about the same axis. It depends on the axis, the mass and how the mass is distributed (not on \(\omega\)).

More moments of inertia (uniform bodies, mass \(M\))

BodyAxis\(I\)
Ring (radius \(R\))a diameter\(\tfrac12MR^2\)
Ringtangent in its plane / tangent perpendicular to its plane\(\tfrac32MR^2\) / \(2MR^2\)
Disca diameter\(\tfrac14MR^2\)
Disctangent in its plane / tangent perpendicular to its plane\(\tfrac54MR^2\) / \(\tfrac32MR^2\)
Annular disc (radii \(R_1\lt R_2\))perpendicular axis through the centre\(\tfrac12M(R_1^2+R_2^2)\)
Solid cylinder (radius \(R\), length \(L\))transverse axis through the centre\(M\left(\dfrac{R^2}4+\dfrac{L^2}{12}\right)\)
Solid spheretangent\(\tfrac75MR^2\)
Hollow (thin-shell) spheretangent\(\tfrac53MR^2\)
Solid hemisphereany axis through the centre of the flat face (symmetry axis or diameter)\(\tfrac25MR^2\)
Hollow hemispheresame axes\(\tfrac23MR^2\)
Solid cone (base radius \(R\))its axis\(\tfrac3{10}MR^2\)
Rectangular plate (sides \(a,b\))an edge of length \(a\)\(\tfrac13Mb^2\)
Square frame of four rods (side \(a\), total mass \(M\))perpendicular to the plane through the centre / a mid-line in the plane\(\tfrac13Ma^2\) / \(\tfrac16Ma^2\)
Thin rod, length \(L\)through the centre, inclined at angle \(\theta\) to the rod\(\dfrac{ML^2}{12}\sin^2\theta\) (through an end: \(\dfrac{ML^2}3\sin^2\theta\))
Rod with \(\lambda=kx\) (\(0\le x\le L\))perpendicular axis through \(x=0\)\(\tfrac12ML^2\)
  • Radius of gyration: \(K=\sqrt{I/M}\); ring about its axis \(K=R\); disc \(\dfrac R{\sqrt2}\); solid sphere \(R\sqrt{\tfrac25}\); rod about its end \(\dfrac L{\sqrt3}\).
  • The perpendicular-axis theorem (\(I_z=I_x+I_y\)) applies only to plane laminas; for a ring or disc \(I_{\text{diameter}}=\tfrac12I_z\). The parallel-axis theorem applies to any body, with one axis through the centre of mass.

Pure rolling: kinematics and energy

\[v_{cm}=\omega R,\qquad a_{cm}=\alpha R,\qquad K=\tfrac12Mv_{cm}^2\left(1+\frac{k^2}{R^2}\right)=\tfrac12I_{P}\,\omega^2\ \ (P=\text{contact point})\]
  • The point of contact is instantaneously at rest; the centre moves at \(v\); the top point moves at \(2v\). A point on the rim at angle \(\phi\) (at the centre) from the contact point has speed \(2v\sin\dfrac\phi2\) (speed \(=\omega\times\) its distance from the contact point). Every point on the rim of a uniformly rolling wheel has an acceleration \(\omega^2R\) towards the centre.
  • For a shape with radius of gyration \(k\): \(\dfrac{K_{\text{rot}}}{K_{\text{trans}}}=\dfrac{k^2}{R^2}\); \(\dfrac{K_{\text{rot}}}{K_{\text{total}}}=\dfrac{k^2}{R^2+k^2}\): ring \(\tfrac12\), disc or solid cylinder \(\tfrac13\), hollow sphere \(\tfrac25\), solid sphere \(\tfrac27\).
  • Angular momentum about the contact point (or any fixed point on the ground line): \(L=I_{cm}\omega+Mv_{cm}R\).
  • Rolling without slipping on a fixed surface: static friction does no work; \(f\le\mu_sN\). If the surface itself moves (plank, belt) the condition is on the velocity relative to the surface.
  • Cycloid (path of a rim point): height \(2R\); one arch length \(8R\); horizontal span \(2\pi R\); area under one arch \(3\pi R^2\); radius of curvature \(4R\) at the highest point.
  • Two bodies rolling down the same incline from the same height: the one with the smaller \(k/R\) reaches the bottom first (solid sphere, then disc, hollow sphere, ring). On a smooth incline all bodies slide together (\(a=g\sin\theta\)) whatever their mass, shape or size.

Rolling on an incline and under applied forces

Body\(k^2/R^2\)\(a=\dfrac{g\sin\theta}{1+k^2/R^2}\)Friction \(f\)\(\mu_s\ge\)
Ring / hollow cylinder\(1\)\(\tfrac12g\sin\theta\)\(\tfrac12Mg\sin\theta\)\(\tfrac12\tan\theta\)
Disc / solid cylinder\(\tfrac12\)\(\tfrac23g\sin\theta\)\(\tfrac13Mg\sin\theta\)\(\tfrac13\tan\theta\)
Hollow sphere\(\tfrac23\)\(\tfrac35g\sin\theta\)\(\tfrac25Mg\sin\theta\)\(\tfrac25\tan\theta\)
Solid sphere\(\tfrac25\)\(\tfrac57g\sin\theta\)\(\tfrac27Mg\sin\theta\)\(\tfrac27\tan\theta\)
  • General: \(f=\dfrac{Mg\sin\theta}{1+R^2/k^2}\) (up the incline); speed at the bottom of a height \(h\): \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{2gh}{1+k^2/R^2}}\); time to roll a length \(\ell\): \(t=\sqrt{\dfrac{2\ell(1+k^2/R^2)}{g\sin\theta}}\). If \(\mu\lt\) the above limit the body rolls and slips; energy is then lost as heat.
  • Horizontal force \(F\) at the centre of a body rolling on a rough floor: \(a=\dfrac F{M(1+k^2/R^2)}\); friction (backward) \(f=\dfrac{Fk^2}{R^2+k^2}\) (disc: \(\dfrac F3\)).
  • Horizontal force \(F\) at a height \(h\) above the centre: from \(F-f=Ma\) and \(Fh+fR=I\alpha\) with \(a=\alpha R\): \(a=\dfrac{F(1+h/R)}{M(1+k^2/R^2)}\), \(f=\dfrac{F(k^2/R^2-h/R)}{1+k^2/R^2}\) (\(f\) is taken backward; a negative value means forward). For a force at the top (\(h=R\)) of a disc: \(a=\dfrac{4F}{3M}\) and friction \(\dfrac F3\) acts forward. Friction is zero if the force is applied at \(h=\dfrac{k^2}R\) (disc: \(\dfrac R2\) above the centre).
  • Spool, yo-yo or a cylinder with a string wound on it and the free end fixed to the ceiling: \(a=\dfrac g{1+k^2/r^2}\), \(T=\dfrac{Mg\,k^2/r^2}{1+k^2/r^2}\); for a disc \(a=\dfrac23g\), \(T=\dfrac{Mg}3\) (\(r\) is the radius at which the string unwinds).
  • Sliding that turns into rolling: a body projected with speed \(v_0\) and no spin on a rough floor decelerates at \(\mu g\) and spins up with \(\alpha=\dfrac{\mu gR}{k^2}\). Rolling begins after \(t=\dfrac{v_0}{\mu g(1+R^2/k^2)}\) when \(v=\dfrac{v_0}{1+k^2/R^2}\) (solid sphere: \(\tfrac57v_0\); disc: \(\tfrac23v_0\); ring: \(\tfrac12v_0\)). Angular momentum about any point on the floor line is conserved; kinetic energy lost \(=\tfrac12Mv_0^2\dfrac{k^2/R^2}{1+k^2/R^2}\).
  • A body released on an incline of angle \(\theta\) rolls if \(\tan\theta\le\mu_s(1+R^2/k^2)\); on a very smooth incline it slides without rolling and reaches the bottom first.

Hinged rods, angular impulse and conservation of angular momentum

  • Uniform rod (\(M,\ell\)) released from horizontal, hinged at one end: \(\alpha=\dfrac{3g}{2\ell}\) initially; the end falls with acceleration \(\dfrac{3g}2\); hinge reaction \(\dfrac{Mg}4\) (upward) at that instant. In the vertical position \(\omega=\sqrt{\dfrac{3g}\ell}\); speed of the free end \(\sqrt{3g\ell}\); hinge force \(\dfrac{5Mg}2\) (upward). At angle \(\theta\) below horizontal: \(\omega^2=\dfrac{3g\sin\theta}\ell\), \(\alpha=\dfrac{3g\cos\theta}{2\ell}\).
  • Rod standing vertically on a rough floor and toppling: the free end touches the ground with speed \(\sqrt{3g\ell}\) (if the foot does not slip); at angle \(\theta\) from the vertical \(\omega^2=\dfrac{3g(1-\cos\theta)}\ell\). The point at distance \(\dfrac{2\ell}3\) from the hinge has the acceleration \(g\) (free-fall) at the horizontal position; points beyond it fall faster than \(g\).
  • Angular collision: a particle \(m\) hits a hinged body and sticks: \(mu\,d=(I+md^2)\omega\) (\(d\) = perpendicular distance from the hinge to the line of the velocity). For a collision with restitution \(e\), add the condition that the relative speed of separation at the point of impact equals \(e\) times the relative speed of approach (\(e=1\): kinetic energy is conserved).
  • Free rod struck by an impulse \(J\) at distance \(x\) from its centre (perpendicular to the rod): \(v_{cm}=\dfrac Jm\), \(\omega=\dfrac{Jx}I=\dfrac{12Jx}{m\ell^2}\); the point at distance \(\dfrac{\ell^2}{12x}\) on the opposite side of the centre stays at rest (instantaneously). A rod hinged at one end and struck at \(x_0=\dfrac{2\ell}3\) from the hinge (the centre of percussion) experiences no impulse at the hinge. In general \(x_0=\dfrac{I_{\text{hinge}}}{m\,d_{cm}}\).
  • Conservation of angular momentum (\(\tau_{ext}=0\)): \(I_1\omega_1=I_2\omega_2\). A skater or a person on a turntable pulling in the arms: \(\omega\) increases and the kinetic energy increases (the work is done by the person). A particle moving in a plane about a fixed point: \(mvr\sin\theta\) conserved.
  • Two discs brought into contact (clutch): \(I_1\omega_1+I_2\omega_2=(I_1+I_2)\omega\); loss of kinetic energy \(\tfrac12\dfrac{I_1I_2}{I_1+I_2}(\omega_1-\omega_2)^2\). A person of mass \(m\) walking along the rim of a turntable (radius \(R\), moment of inertia \(I\), initially at rest) with speed \(v\) relative to the turntable: \(L=0\Rightarrow mR(v-\omega R)=I\omega\), so the turntable turns the other way with \(\omega=\dfrac{mvR}{I+mR^2}\).
  • Child jumping on or off a merry-go-round; falling objects stuck on a spinning disc: conserve angular momentum about the axis (no external torque about it).

Equilibrium, toppling, sliding and gyroscopic effects

  • Toppling vs sliding: a block (cube of side \(a\), mass \(m\), centre of mass at height \(a/2\)) pulled by a horizontal force \(F\) applied at height \(h\) above the floor: it slides if \(F\gt\mu mg\); it topples about the front edge if \(Fh\gt mg\dfrac a2\). It slides first if \(\mu\lt\dfrac a{2h}\); it topples first if \(\mu\gt\dfrac a{2h}\). When just on the verge of toppling the normal reaction acts at the edge.
  • A body is in stable equilibrium on a plane if the vertical through its centre of mass falls within the base. A block of base width \(b\) with its centre of mass at height \(H\) on an incline of angle \(\theta\) topples (if it does not slide first) when \(\tan\theta\gt\dfrac b{2H}\); it slides first if \(\mu\lt\dfrac b{2H}\). Wider and lower is more stable.
  • Wheel (radius \(R\), weight \(W\)) over a step of height \(h\): minimum horizontal force at the centre \(F=W\dfrac{\sqrt{h(2R-h)}}{R-h}\); applied at the top of the wheel \(F=W\dfrac{\sqrt{h(2R-h)}}{2R-h}\); the least force applied at the centre (axle), obtained when it is perpendicular to the line from the step edge to the centre, \(F_{\min}=W\dfrac{\sqrt{h(2R-h)}}R\). The reaction of the ground at the lower contact becomes zero at the verge.
  • Beams on supports: use \(\sum F=0\) and \(\sum\tau=0\) about a convenient point (one through which unknown forces pass). A uniform beam of weight \(W\) and length \(L\) resting on two supports at its ends carries \(\dfrac W2\) on each; an extra load \(w\) placed at distance \(x\) from end \(A\) adds \(w\dfrac{L-x}L\) to the support at \(A\) and \(w\dfrac xL\) to the support at the other end.
  • Gyroscope and precession: a spinning wheel (moment of inertia \(I\), spin \(\omega\)) whose axle is supported at one end and pivoted so that the weight \(mg\) acts at distance \(d\) from the pivot has a torque \(mgd\) perpendicular to \(\vec L\); \(\vec L\) turns (precesses) about the vertical with angular velocity \(\Omega=\dfrac{mgd}{I\omega}\) (valid for fast spin). A faster spin gives slower precession. A bicycle leans and turns for the same reason; the Earth’s axis precesses with a period of about 26 000 years.
  • Disc (mass \(m_1\), radius \(R\)) rolling without slipping on a plank (mass \(m_2\)) that is pulled by a force \(F\) on a smooth floor: with friction \(f\) on the disc, \(f=m_1a\), \(fR=\tfrac12m_1R^2\alpha\) and \(\alpha R=a_p-a\) (no slipping), giving \(a=\dfrac{a_p}3\) and \(a_p=\dfrac{3F}{3m_2+m_1}\).
13
Oscillations, waves & heat

Simple Harmonic Motion

S.H.M.

\[F=-kx\]

General equation of S.H.M.: \(x=A\sin(\omega t+\phi)\). Here \((\omega t+\phi)\) is the phase of the motion and \(\phi\) is the initial phase.

Angular frequency \((\omega)\)

\[\omega=\frac{2\pi}T=2\pi f\]

Time period \((T)\)

\[T=\frac{2\pi}\omega=2\pi\sqrt{\frac mk}\]

Speed and acceleration

\[v=\omega\sqrt{A^2-x^2}\]\[a=-\omega^2x\]

Kinetic energy (KE)

\[\tfrac12mv^2=\tfrac12m\omega^2(A^2-x^2)=\tfrac12k(A^2-x^2)\]

Potential energy (PE)

\[\tfrac12kx^2\]

Total mechanical energy (TME)

\[\text{K.E.}+\text{P.E.}=\tfrac12k(A^2-x^2)+\tfrac12kx^2=\tfrac12kA^2\]

This is constant.

Spring-mass system

(1) A mass \(m\) on a spring of constant \(k\), on a smooth surface or hanging vertically:

\[T=2\pi\sqrt{\frac mk}\]

(2) Two masses \(m_1\) and \(m_2\) joined by a spring:

\[T=2\pi\sqrt{\frac\mu k},\qquad\mu=\frac{m_1m_2}{m_1+m_2}\ \text{(reduced mass)}\]

Combination of springs

Series: \(\dfrac1{k_{eq}}=\dfrac1{k_1}+\dfrac1{k_2}\)

Parallel: \(k_{eq}=k_1+k_2\)

Simple pendulum

\[T=2\pi\sqrt{\frac\ell g}=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{g_{eff}}}\ \ (\text{in an accelerating frame})\]

\(g_{eff}\) is the net acceleration due to the pseudo force and gravitational force.

Compound (physical) pendulum

\[T=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{I}{mg\ell}}\]

where \(I=I_{CM}+m\ell^2\) and \(\ell\) is the distance between the point of suspension and the centre of mass.

Torsional pendulum

\[T=2\pi\sqrt{\frac IC}\]

\(C\) is the torsional constant.

Superposition of S.H.M.s along the same direction

For \(x_1=A_1\sin\omega t\) and \(x_2=A_2\sin(\omega t+\theta)\), with the resultant \(x=A\sin(\omega t+\phi)\):

\[A=\sqrt{A_1^2+A_2^2+2A_1A_2\cos\theta}\qquad\tan\phi=\frac{A_2\sin\theta}{A_1+A_2\cos\theta}\]

1. Damped oscillation

Damping force: \(\vec F=-b\vec v\). Equation of motion: \(m\dfrac{dv}{dt}=-kx-bv\)

  • \(b^2-4mk\gt0\): over damping
  • \(b^2-4mk=0\): critical damping
  • \(b^2-4mk\lt0\): under damping

For small damping the solution has the form

\[x=A_0e^{-bt/2m}\sin[\omega't+\delta],\qquad\omega'=\sqrt{\frac km-\left(\frac b{2m}\right)^2}\]

For small \(b\):

\[\omega'\approx\sqrt{k/m}=\omega_0\qquad A=A_0e^{-bt/2m}\qquad E(t)=\tfrac12kA^2e^{-bt/m}\]

Quality factor:

\[Q=2\pi\frac{E}{|\Delta E|}=\frac{\omega'}{2\omega_\gamma},\qquad\omega'=\sqrt{\frac km-\frac{b^2}{4m^2}},\quad\omega_\gamma=\frac b{2m}\]

2. Forced oscillations and resonance

External force \(F(t)=F_0\cos\omega_dt\); response \(x(t)=A\cos(\omega_dt+\phi)\).

\[A=\frac{F_0}{\sqrt{m^2(\omega^2-\omega_d^2)^2+\omega_d^2b^2}}\qquad\tan\phi=\frac{-v_0}{\omega_dx_0}\]
  • (a) Small damping: \(A=\dfrac{F_0}{m(\omega^2-\omega_d^2)}\)
  • (b) Driving frequency close to the natural frequency: \(A=\dfrac{F_0}{\omega_db}\)

SHM: equation, phase and the relations among \(x,v,a\)

\[\frac{d^2x}{dt^2}=-\omega^2x\ \ (\text{or }F=-kx),\qquad x=A\sin(\omega t+\phi),\quad v=A\omega\cos(\omega t+\phi),\quad a=-A\omega^2\sin(\omega t+\phi)\]\[v=\pm\omega\sqrt{A^2-x^2},\qquad v_{\max}=A\omega,\quad a_{\max}=A\omega^2=\omega v_{\max},\quad\omega=2\pi f=\frac{2\pi}T\]
  • Velocity leads displacement by \(\dfrac\pi2\); acceleration leads velocity by \(\dfrac\pi2\) and is in antiphase with displacement. \(v\) is maximum at the mean position and zero at the extremes; \(a\) is zero at the mean position and maximum at the extremes. \(v\)–\(x\) graph: ellipse \(\dfrac{x^2}{A^2}+\dfrac{v^2}{A^2\omega^2}=1\); \(a\)–\(x\) graph: straight line of slope \(-\omega^2\); \(v\)–\(a\) graph: ellipse.
  • Initial phase \(\phi\) from the initial condition: \(x(0)=A\sin\phi\), \(v(0)=A\omega\cos\phi\), so \(\tan\phi=\dfrac{\omega x_0}{v_0}\). Starting from the mean position moving positive: \(\phi=0\); from the positive extreme: \(\phi=\dfrac\pi2\) for the sine form (cosine form: \(x=A\cos\omega t\)).
  • Test for SHM: the net restoring force (or torque) is proportional to the displacement and directed toward equilibrium; then \(\omega^2=\) (coefficient of \(-x\) in \(a\)); \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{\text{inertia factor}}{\text{restoring factor}}}\). For a potential \(U(x)\) with a stable minimum at \(x_0\): \(\omega=\sqrt{\dfrac{U''(x_0)}m}\).
  • Two-point method: a particle has velocities \(v_1,v_2\) at displacements \(x_1,x_2\): \(\omega^2=\dfrac{v_1^2-v_2^2}{x_2^2-x_1^2}\), \(A^2=\dfrac{v_1^2x_2^2-v_2^2x_1^2}{v_1^2-v_2^2}\). With accelerations \(a_1,a_2\) at \(x_1,x_2\): \(\omega^2=-\dfrac{a_1}{x_1}\).
  • Average speed over a half or full period \(=\dfrac{4A}T\); average speed over a quarter period starting at an extreme position (or at the mean position) \(=\dfrac{4A}T\). Average velocity over a full period is zero; over a half period from one extreme to the other its magnitude is \(\dfrac{2A}{T/2}=\dfrac{4A}T\).
  • A uniform circular motion of radius \(A\) and angular speed \(\omega\), projected on a diameter, is SHM with the same \(A\) and \(\omega\): \(x=A\sin(\omega t+\phi)\) (the phasor/reference-circle method).
  • Two particles in SHM with the same \(A\) and \(\omega\) and phase difference \(\delta\) (\(x_1=A\sin\omega t\), \(x_2=A\sin(\omega t+\delta)\)) are at the same position when their phasors are mirror images about the vertical axis; they first meet at \(x=A\cos\dfrac\delta2\) when \(\omega t=\dfrac{\pi-\delta}2\).

Energy and time-based results in SHM

\[E=\tfrac12kA^2=\tfrac12m\omega^2A^2,\qquad K=\tfrac12k(A^2-x^2),\qquad U=\tfrac12kx^2,\qquad K=U\ \text{at}\ x=\pm\frac A{\sqrt2}\]
  • \(K\) and \(U\) oscillate at frequency \(2f\) (period \(\tfrac T2\)) between \(0\) and \(E\); their time-averages are both \(\tfrac E2\) (\(K=\tfrac E2(1+\cos2(\omega t+\phi))\)). The space average over the path: \(\langle U\rangle=\dfrac E3\), \(\langle K\rangle=\dfrac{2E}3\).
  • The fraction of energy as kinetic at displacement \(x\): \(1-\dfrac{x^2}{A^2}\); at \(x=\dfrac A2\): \(\dfrac34\) kinetic and \(\dfrac14\) potential. At \(x=A\sqrt{\dfrac n{n+1}}\), \(K:U=1:n\).
  • Times between positions (starting at \(x=0\) going out): \(0\to\dfrac A2\): \(\dfrac T{12}\); \(\dfrac A2\to A\): \(\dfrac T6\); \(0\to\dfrac A{\sqrt2}\): \(\dfrac T8\); \(\dfrac A{\sqrt2}\to A\): \(\dfrac T8\); \(0\to\dfrac{\sqrt3}2A\): \(\dfrac T6\); \(\dfrac{\sqrt3}2A\to A\): \(\dfrac T{12}\); \(0\to A\): \(\dfrac T4\). In general \(t=\dfrac1\omega\sin^{-1}\dfrac xA\) from the mean position.
  • Distance travelled in a given time: in one period \(4A\); in \(\tfrac T2\), \(2A\); in \(\tfrac T4\), \(A\) only if the quarter starts at the mean or an extreme position; in \(\tfrac T{12}\) starting at the mean position: \(\dfrac A2\).
  • A constant additional force \(F_0\) (for example gravity on a vertical spring): \(m\ddot x=-kx+F_0\) still gives SHM with the same \(\omega=\sqrt{k/m}\), but about the shifted centre \(x_0=\dfrac{F_0}k\); the total energy \(\tfrac12kA^2\) is measured from the new centre.

Spring–mass systems: more cases

  • Vertical spring: \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac mk}=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{x_0}g}\) where \(x_0=\dfrac{mg}k\) is the static extension; gravity only shifts the equilibrium. The same \(T\) holds on an incline, in a lift (\(T\) unchanged, equilibrium shifts) and on the Moon.
  • Spring with mass \(m_s\) of its own: effective mass \(m+\dfrac{m_s}3\), so \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{m+m_s/3}k}\).
  • Springs: in parallel \(k=k_1+k_2\) (e.g. block between two springs on opposite sides); in series \(\dfrac1k=\dfrac1{k_1}+\dfrac1{k_2}\). A spring cut in the ratio \(n:m\): \(k_1=\dfrac{(n+m)}nk\) etc.
  • Block hanging from a spring over a pulley with a massive pulley (moment of inertia \(I\), radius \(R\)): \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{m+I/R^2}k}\) (for a disc pulley of mass \(M\), \(m+\dfrac M2\)).
  • Disc or cylinder attached to a spring and rolling without slipping: energy \(E=\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac32mv^2+\tfrac12kx^2\), so \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{3m}{2k}}\). In general effective mass \(m\left(1+\dfrac{k_g^2}{R^2}\right)\).
  • Two blocks \(m_1,m_2\) joined by a spring (\(k\)) on a smooth surface: \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac\mu k}\), \(\mu=\dfrac{m_1m_2}{m_1+m_2}\).
  • Block on a vibrating platform or on another oscillating block: the upper block stays at rest relative to the lower (horizontal oscillation) if the amplitude \(A\le\dfrac{\mu_sg}{\omega^2}=\dfrac{\mu_sg(M+m)}k\). For vertical SHM of a platform the upper body leaves it if \(A\gt\dfrac g{\omega^2}\).
  • Spring and a constant external force or charge in an electric field: the equilibrium is shifted by \(\dfrac Fk\); the amplitude for a sudden application of force \(F\) is \(\dfrac Fk\) (the maximum extension is \(\dfrac{2F}k\)).
  • Block on a smooth surface attached to springs on both sides and displaced perpendicular to the springs: the restoring force is nonlinear for large displacements (not SHM unless the springs are pre-stretched).

Pendulums and other oscillators: more cases

  • Simple pendulum: \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac\ell g}\); \(\ell=0.994\) m for the seconds pendulum (\(T=2\) s). Large amplitude \(\theta_0\): \(T\approx2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac\ell g}\left(1+\dfrac{\theta_0^2}{16}\right)\); independent of mass and (for small angles) of amplitude.
  • Period of a pendulum of length \(\ell\) comparable with Earth’s radius: \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac1{g\left(\frac1\ell+\frac1R\right)}}\); for \(\ell\to\infty\), \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac Rg}\approx84.6\) min.
  • Effective gravity: \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac\ell{g_{\text{eff}}}}\). In a lift accelerating up, \(g_{\text{eff}}=g+a\) (down, \(g-a\); free fall: no oscillation). In a car accelerating horizontally with \(a\): \(g_{\text{eff}}=\sqrt{g^2+a^2}\). A bob of density \(\rho\) in a liquid of density \(\sigma\): \(g_{\text{eff}}=g\left(1-\dfrac\sigma\rho\right)\). A charge \(q\) in a vertical electric field \(E\): \(g_{\text{eff}}=g\pm\dfrac{qE}m\).
  • Change of period: \(\dfrac{\Delta T}T=\tfrac12\dfrac{\Delta\ell}\ell-\tfrac12\dfrac{\Delta g}g\). With temperature: \(\dfrac{\Delta T}T=\tfrac12\alpha\Delta\theta\) (a pendulum clock loses \(\tfrac12\alpha\Delta\theta\times86400\) s per day when heated). At height \(h\): \(\dfrac{\Delta T}T=\dfrac hR\); at depth \(d\): \(\dfrac{\Delta T}T=\dfrac d{2R}\).
  • Pendulum with a nail: when the string is obstructed by a nail at a distance \(\ell_2\) below the suspension point, the period is \(T=\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{\ell_1}g}+\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{\ell_2}g}\) (half swings of lengths \(\ell_1\) and \(\ell_2\)).
  • Physical pendulum (mass \(m\), pivot at distance \(d\) from the centre of mass, radius of gyration \(k\) about the centre of mass): \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{I_{\text{pivot}}}{mgd}}=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{k^2+d^2}{gd}}\); equivalent length \(\ell_{eq}=\dfrac{k^2+d^2}d\); the periods about the pivot and about the centre of oscillation (at distance \(\dfrac{k^2}d\) beyond the centre of mass) are equal; minimum period at \(d=k\). Rod about an end: \(\ell_{eq}=\dfrac{2L}3\); thin ring about a point on it: \(2R\); disc about its edge: \(\dfrac{3R}2\).
  • Oscillation of a liquid in a U-tube (total column length \(\ell\)): \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac\ell{2g}}\). Floating cylinder pushed down: \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac m{\rho Ag}}=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{h}g}\) (\(h\) = submerged depth). A body oscillating in a sphere or bowl: sliding ball \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{R-r}g}\); solid ball rolling \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{7(R-r)}{5g}}\).
  • Tunnel through the Earth (uniform density, no friction): along any chord \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac Rg}\approx84.6\) min; through a diameter, the time to reach the other side is \(\dfrac T2\approx42.3\) min with speed at the centre \(\sqrt{gR}\approx7.9\) km s\(^{-1}\); \(g_{\text{inside}}=\dfrac{gr}R\).
  • Torsional pendulum: \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac IC}\) with torsion constant \(C=\dfrac{\pi\eta r^4}{2\ell}\) for a wire of radius \(r\), length \(\ell\) and rigidity modulus \(\eta\). Angular SHM: \(\tau=-C\theta\Rightarrow\ddot\theta=-\dfrac CI\theta\).
  • Energy method: write \(E=\tfrac12M_{\text{eff}}\dot q^2+\tfrac12K_{\text{eff}}q^2\Rightarrow\omega=\sqrt{\dfrac{K_{\text{eff}}}{M_{\text{eff}}}}\). Electrical analogue: LC circuit \(\omega=\dfrac1{\sqrt{LC}}\) (\(L\leftrightarrow m\), \(1/C\leftrightarrow k\)).

Superposition of SHMs, Lissajous figures, damped and forced oscillations

  • Same frequency, same line: \(x_1=A_1\sin\omega t,\ x_2=A_2\sin(\omega t+\delta)\): resultant amplitude \(A=\sqrt{A_1^2+A_2^2+2A_1A_2\cos\delta}\), phase \(\tan\phi=\dfrac{A_2\sin\delta}{A_1+A_2\cos\delta}\). In phase: \(A_1+A_2\); antiphase: \(|A_1-A_2|\); quadrature: \(\sqrt{A_1^2+A_2^2}\). Equal amplitudes: \(A=2A_1\cos\dfrac\delta2\).
  • Close frequencies on the same line: beats of frequency \(|f_1-f_2|\) (see sound waves); amplitude \(2A\cos\dfrac{(\omega_1-\omega_2)t}2\).
  • Perpendicular SHMs (Lissajous): \(x=A\sin\omega t,\ y=B\sin(\omega t+\delta)\): \(\dfrac{x^2}{A^2}+\dfrac{y^2}{B^2}-\dfrac{2xy}{AB}\cos\delta=\sin^2\delta\). \(\delta=0\): straight line \(y=\dfrac BAx\); \(\delta=\pi\): line \(y=-\dfrac BAx\); \(\delta=\dfrac\pi2\): ellipse \(\dfrac{x^2}{A^2}+\dfrac{y^2}{B^2}=1\) (circle if \(A=B\)). With frequency ratio \(1:2\) the figure is a parabola or a figure “8”, depending on the phase.
  • Damped oscillator (\(m\ddot x+b\dot x+kx=0\)): \(x=A_0e^{-bt/2m}\cos(\omega't+\phi)\), \(\omega'=\sqrt{\dfrac k m-\dfrac{b^2}{4m^2}}\); energy \(E=E_0e^{-bt/m}\); amplitude falls to \(\dfrac1e\) in time \(\dfrac{2m}b\), energy in \(\dfrac mb\). Critical damping \(b_c=2\sqrt{km}\); \(b\gt b_c\) is overdamped (no oscillation). Quality factor \(Q=\dfrac{\omega_0m}b=\dfrac{\sqrt{km}}b\).
  • Forced oscillation with \(F=F_0\cos\omega_dt\): steady-state amplitude \(A=\dfrac{F_0}{\sqrt{m^2(\omega_0^2-\omega_d^2)^2+b^2\omega_d^2}}\); resonance when \(\omega_d\approx\omega_0\) (exactly \(\omega_d=\sqrt{\omega_0^2-\dfrac{b^2}{2m^2}}\) for the amplitude maximum), where \(A\approx\dfrac{F_0}{b\omega_0}\). Smaller damping gives a taller, sharper peak. At resonance the displacement lags the force by \(\dfrac\pi2\).
  • The natural (free) frequency depends only on the system; the forced oscillation always occurs at the driving frequency.
14
Oscillations, waves & heat

String Waves

General equation of wave motion

\[\frac{\partial^2y}{\partial t^2}=v^2\frac{\partial^2y}{\partial x^2}\]\[y(x,t)=f\!\left(t\pm\frac xv\right)\quad\text{where }y(x,t)\text{ should be finite everywhere}\]
  • \(f\!\left(t+\dfrac xv\right)\) represents a wave travelling in the \(-x\) direction.
  • \(f\!\left(t-\dfrac xv\right)\) represents a wave travelling in the \(+x\) direction.
\[y=A\sin(\omega t\pm kx+\phi)\]

Terms related to wave motion (1-D progressive sine wave)

Wave number (propagation constant):

\[k=\frac{2\pi}\lambda=\frac\omega v\ \ (\text{rad m}^{-1})\]

Phase of the wave: the argument of the harmonic function \((\omega t\pm kx+\phi)\). The phase difference \(\Delta\phi\) is the difference in phase of two particles at any time \(t\):

\[\Delta\phi=\frac{2\pi}\lambda\Delta x\qquad\text{also}\qquad\Delta\phi=\frac{2\pi}T\Delta t\]

Speed of a transverse wave on a string

\[v=\sqrt{\frac T\mu}\]

\(T\) is the tension and \(\mu\) the mass per unit length.

Power transmitted by a sine wave

\[\langle P\rangle=2\pi^2f^2A^2\mu v\]\[I=\frac{\langle P\rangle}S=2\pi^2f^2A^2\rho v\]

Reflection and refraction of waves

Incident wave \(y_i=A_i\sin(\omega t-k_1x)\).

Incident from a rarer to a denser medium (\(v_2\lt v_1\)):

\[y_t=A_t\sin(\omega t-k_2x)\qquad y_r=-A_r\sin(\omega t+k_1x)\]

Incident from a denser to a rarer medium (\(v_2\gt v_1\)):

\[y_t=A_t\sin(\omega t-k_2x)\qquad y_r=A_r\sin(\omega t+k_1x)\]

Amplitudes of the reflected and transmitted waves

\[A_r=\left|\frac{k_1-k_2}{k_1+k_2}\right|A_i\qquad A_t=\frac{2k_1}{k_1+k_2}A_i\]

Standing (stationary) waves

\[y_1=A\sin(\omega t-kx+\theta_1)\qquad y_2=A\sin(\omega t+kx+\theta_2)\]\[y_1+y_2=\left[2A\cos\!\left(kx+\frac{\theta_2-\theta_1}2\right)\right]\sin\!\left(\omega t+\frac{\theta_1+\theta_2}2\right)\]

The quantity \(2A\cos\!\left(kx+\dfrac{\theta_2-\theta_1}2\right)\) is the resultant amplitude at \(x\). Where it is zero are nodes; where it is \(2A\) are antinodes.

  • Distance between successive nodes (or antinodes) \(=\dfrac\lambda2\).
  • Distance between a successive node and antinode \(=\lambda/4\).
  • All particles in the same segment (between two successive nodes) vibrate in the same phase; particles in consecutive segments vibrate in opposite phase.
  • Nodes are permanently at rest, so energy cannot be transmitted across them.

Vibrations of strings (standing wave)

(a) Fixed at both ends. The fixed ends are nodes, so \(L=\dfrac\lambda2,\ \dfrac{2\lambda}2,\ \dfrac{3\lambda}2,\ldots\) are possible:

\[L=\frac{n\lambda}2\ \ \text{or}\ \ \lambda=\frac{2L}n,\quad n=1,2,3,\ldots\]\[v=\sqrt{\frac T\mu}\qquad f_n=\frac n{2L}\sqrt{\frac T\mu},\quad n=\text{number of loops}\]

(b) String free at one end. Fundamental mode: \(L=\dfrac\lambda4\), i.e. \(\lambda=4L\). First overtone: \(L=\dfrac{3\lambda}4\), so \(\lambda=\dfrac{4L}3\).

\[f_1=\frac3{4L}\sqrt{\frac T\mu}\ \text{(first overtone)}\qquad f_2=\frac5{4L}\sqrt{\frac T\mu}\ \text{(second overtone)}\]\[f_n=\frac{\left(n+\frac12\right)}{2L}\sqrt{\frac T\mu}=\frac{(2n+1)}{4L}\sqrt{\frac T\mu}\]

Progressive waves: forms, phase and particle motion

\[y=A\sin(kx-\omega t+\phi)\ \ (\text{towards }+x),\qquad y=A\sin(kx+\omega t+\phi)\ \ (\text{towards }-x),\qquad v=f\lambda=\frac\omega k=\frac\lambda T\]
  • \(k=\dfrac{2\pi}\lambda\), \(\omega=2\pi f=\dfrac{2\pi}T\). A pulse \(y=f(x-vt)\) moves to the right with speed \(v\) without changing shape (non-dispersive medium); \(f(x+vt)\) moves left. The wave equation is \(\dfrac{\partial^2y}{\partial t^2}=v^2\dfrac{\partial^2y}{\partial x^2}\) (every function of \(x\mp vt\) satisfies it).
  • Particle velocity: \(v_p=\dfrac{\partial y}{\partial t}=-\omega A\cos(kx-\omega t+\phi)\), maximum \(\omega A\) (not the wave speed); particle acceleration \(=-\omega^2y\). For a right-moving wave \(v_p=-v\dfrac{\partial y}{\partial x}\); \(v_{p,\max}=vk A\), and \(\dfrac{v_{p,\max}}v=kA\) (the maximum slope).
  • To find the direction in which a particle is moving, shift the waveform slightly along the direction of propagation: the particle goes to where its neighbour’s displacement was.
  • Phase difference between two points separated by \(\Delta x\): \(\Delta\phi=k\Delta x=\dfrac{2\pi}\lambda\Delta x\); between two instants at the same point \(\Delta\phi=\omega\Delta t\). Path difference \(\lambda\) \(\leftrightarrow\) phase \(2\pi\); \(\dfrac\lambda2\leftrightarrow\pi\).
  • The frequency is fixed by the source; the speed is fixed by the medium; \(\lambda=\dfrac vf\) changes when the wave enters another medium. If the speed doubles, the wavelength doubles.
  • In a plane wave the amplitude stays constant, intensity constant. For a spherical wave \(y=\dfrac Ar\sin(kr-\omega t)\), \(I\propto\dfrac1{r^2}\), amplitude \(\propto\dfrac1r\). For a cylindrical wave \(I\propto\dfrac1r\), amplitude \(\propto\dfrac1{\sqrt r}\).
  • Sine and cosine forms differ only in the initial phase \(\phi\): \(A\cos(kx-\omega t)=A\sin(kx-\omega t+\tfrac\pi2)\). A wave given as \(y=a\sin(bx+ct)\) has speed \(\dfrac cb\) in the \(-x\) direction, \(f=\dfrac c{2\pi}\), \(\lambda=\dfrac{2\pi}b\).
  • Superposition principle: when waves overlap the displacements add algebraically; waves pass through each other unchanged. Two equal pulses in opposite phase cancel at the instant they overlap (the string is momentarily straight but the particles are moving).

Wave speed in different media and on non-uniform strings

MediumSpeed
Transverse wave on a stretched string\(v=\sqrt{\dfrac T\mu}=\sqrt{\dfrac T{A\rho}}\)
Longitudinal wave in a solid rod\(v=\sqrt{\dfrac Y\rho}\)
Transverse (shear) wave in a solid\(v=\sqrt{\dfrac\eta\rho}\)
Longitudinal wave in a liquid\(v=\sqrt{\dfrac B\rho}\)
Sound in an ideal gas\(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{\gamma P}\rho}=\sqrt{\dfrac{\gamma RT}M}\)
  • String of uniform wire (radius \(r\), density \(\rho\)): \(\mu=\pi r^2\rho\); \(v=\dfrac1r\sqrt{\dfrac T{\pi\rho}}\).
  • Hanging rope (length \(L\), uniform): the tension at height \(y\) above the lower free end is \(\mu gy\), so \(v=\sqrt{gy}\) (zero at the free end). Time for a pulse to run from the bottom to the top: \(t=2\sqrt{\dfrac Lg}\); the pulse accelerates with \(a=\dfrac g2\) as it climbs.
  • A rope hanging with a heavy block of mass \(M\) at its end: \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{Mg+\mu gy}\mu}\) at height \(y\) above the block.
  • String of non-uniform density \(\mu(x)\): the time to travel a distance \(L\) is \(t=\displaystyle\int_0^L\dfrac{dx}{v(x)}=\int_0^L\sqrt{\dfrac{\mu(x)}T}\,dx\).
  • Change of speed with tension and density: \(\dfrac{\Delta v}v=\tfrac12\dfrac{\Delta T}T-\tfrac12\dfrac{\Delta\mu}\mu\). Heating a string whose ends are fixed lowers the tension and so the frequency.
  • Rotating rope or a wave on a spinning string: in the rotating frame the tension supplies the centripetal force; for a circular loop of rope rotating with speed \(u\): \(T=\mu u^2\) and the wave speed relative to the rope is \(\sqrt{T/\mu}=u\), so a pulse can stay fixed in space.

Energy, impedance, reflection and transmission of pulses

  • Energy per unit length (travelling sine wave): \(\dfrac{dE}{dx}=\tfrac12\mu\omega^2A^2\) (half kinetic, half potential); power \(P=\tfrac12\mu\omega^2A^2v=\tfrac12Z\omega^2A^2\) with the string impedance \(Z=\mu v=\sqrt{T\mu}\). Intensity \(\propto A^2\omega^2\): doubling the amplitude or the frequency quadruples the power.
  • At a boundary between strings of impedances \(Z_1,Z_2\) (speeds \(v_1,v_2\)): the reflected and transmitted amplitudes are \(A_r=\dfrac{Z_1-Z_2}{Z_1+Z_2}A_i=\dfrac{v_2-v_1}{v_2+v_1}A_i\) and \(A_t=\dfrac{2Z_1}{Z_1+Z_2}A_i=\dfrac{2v_2}{v_1+v_2}A_i\). The fractions of the power reflected and transmitted: \(R=\left(\dfrac{Z_1-Z_2}{Z_1+Z_2}\right)^2\), \(\mathcal T=\dfrac{4Z_1Z_2}{(Z_1+Z_2)^2}\); \(R+\mathcal T=1\). (The sign conventions: \(A_r\gt0\) means no phase change, \(A_r\lt0\) a phase change of \(\pi\).)
  • Fixed end (\(Z_2\to\infty\)): complete reflection with a phase change \(\pi\) (the pulse returns inverted). Free end (\(Z_2\to0\)): complete reflection without phase change (upright). Reflection from a denser (heavier) string inverts the pulse; from a lighter string no inversion; the transmitted pulse is never inverted.
  • Equal wave speeds on both sides (\(Z_1=Z_2\)): no reflection.
  • Frequency does not change on reflection or transmission; the wavelength changes in the second medium (\(\lambda_2=\dfrac{v_2}f\)).
  • Two identical pulses travelling towards each other overlap completely at one instant. If they have opposite signs the string is momentarily flat and all the energy is kinetic; if they have the same sign the particle velocities cancel and all the energy is potential.

Standing waves on strings: more results

  • Fixed at both ends: \(f_n=\dfrac n{2L}\sqrt{\dfrac T\mu}=nf_1\) (\(n=1,2,3,\dots\)) — all harmonics are present; \(n\) is the number of loops (\(n-1\) nodes between the ends, \(n+1\) in all including the ends); the \((n-1)\)th overtone is the \(n\)th harmonic.
  • Fixed at one end and free at the other (e.g. a string tied to a ring sliding on a rod): \(f_n=\dfrac{(2n-1)}{4L}\sqrt{\dfrac T\mu}\); only odd harmonics are present (\(f_1,3f_1,5f_1\dots\)), the free end is an antinode.
  • Amplitude at position \(x\) (node at \(x=0\)): \(A(x)=2A|\sin kx|\); particle velocity amplitude \(2A\omega|\sin kx|\); maximum kinetic energy of a loop (all of it when the string is straight) \(=\) maximum potential energy (when all particles are at their extreme position). The total energy of a loop does not flow between loops.
  • Amplitudes from the initial plucking: a string plucked at a point at a distance \(\dfrac Ln\) from an end cannot have the \(n\)th harmonic or its multiples (that point would be a node). Plucked at the centre: only odd harmonics.
  • Sonometer: \(f=\dfrac1{2L}\sqrt{\dfrac T\mu}\); so \(f\propto\dfrac1L\) (at fixed \(T\)), \(f\propto\sqrt T\) (at fixed \(L\)), \(f\propto\dfrac1{\sqrt\mu}\) and \(f\propto\dfrac1r\) for the same material. Two identical wires with a difference in length produce beats \(f_1-f_2\); tension increases the pitch. Resonance: when a tuning fork of frequency \(f\) makes the wire vibrate, \(L\) is \(\dfrac{n}{2f}\sqrt{\dfrac T\mu}\); if the weights are hung and then immersed in water the tension reduces by the buoyant force (\(T'=T-\rho_wVg\)) and so does \(f\).
  • Melde’s experiment: a string driven by a fork of frequency \(f_0\); in the transverse arrangement (string perpendicular to the prong vibration) the string vibrates at \(f_0\): \(f_0=\dfrac p{2L}\sqrt{\dfrac T\mu}\); in the longitudinal arrangement the string vibrates at \(\dfrac{f_0}2\), so \(f_0=\dfrac pL\sqrt{\dfrac T\mu}\) (\(p\) loops). \(p^2T=\) constant for a given string.
  • Standing wave from reflection at \(x=0\): incident \(y_i=A\sin(\omega t-kx)\) travelling towards \(x=0\) from the right is better written as \(A\sin(\omega t+kx)\). Fixed end: \(y_r=-A\sin(\omega t-kx)\), \(y=2A\sin kx\cos\omega t\) (node at \(x=0\)). Free end: \(y_r=+A\sin(\omega t-kx)\), \(y=2A\cos kx\sin\omega t\) (antinode at \(x=0\)).
15
Oscillations, waves & heat

Heat & Thermodynamics

Kinetic theory of gases

Total translational K.E. of a gas:

\[\tfrac12M\langle V^2\rangle=\tfrac32PV=\tfrac32nRT\]\[\langle V^2\rangle=\frac{3P}\rho\qquad V_{rms}=\sqrt{\frac{3P}\rho}=\sqrt{\frac{3RT}{M_{mol}}}=\sqrt{\frac{3KT}m}\]
  • \(V_{rms}\propto\sqrt T\)
  • Average speed \(\bar V=\sqrt{\dfrac{8KT}{\pi m}}=1.59\sqrt{\dfrac{KT}m}\)
  • \(V_{rms}=1.73\sqrt{\dfrac{KT}m}\)
  • Most probable speed \(V_p=\sqrt{\dfrac{2KT}m}=1.41\sqrt{\dfrac{KT}m}\)
  • Hence \(V_{rms}\gt\bar V\gt V_{mp}\)

Degrees of freedom

Gas\(f\)
Monatomic3
Diatomic5
Polyatomic6

Maxwell's law of equipartition of energy

Total K.E. of a molecule \(=\tfrac12fKT\). For an ideal gas the internal energy is

\[U=\frac f2nRT\]

Thermodynamic processes

ProcessWork doneChange in internal energy
Isothermal\(W=2.303\,nRT\log_{10}\dfrac{V_f}{V_i}\)\(\Delta U=0\)
Isochoric\(dW=0\)\(\Delta U=n\dfrac f2R\Delta T=\) heat given
Isobaric\(\Delta W=nR(T_f-T_i)\)\(\Delta U=nC_V\Delta T\), heat given \(\Delta Q=\Delta U+\Delta W\)
Adiabatic\(\Delta W=\dfrac{nR(T_i-T_f)}{\gamma-1}\)
Cyclic\(\Delta Q=\Delta W\)

Specific heat and molar heat capacity of an ideal gas

\[C_V=\frac f2R\qquad C_p=\left(\frac f2+1\right)R\]\[\gamma=\frac{C_p}{C_V}=\left[1+\frac2f\right]\qquad\text{Mayer's equation: }C_p-C_V=R\ \ (\text{ideal gas only})\]
Gas\(C_p/C_V\)
Monatomic1.67
Diatomic1.4
Triatomic1.33

Mixture of non-reacting gases

\[\text{Mol. wt.}=\frac{n_1M_1+n_2M_2}{n_1+n_2}\qquad C_v=\frac{n_1C_{v_1}+n_2C_{v_2}}{n_1+n_2}\]\[\gamma=\frac{C_{p(mix)}}{C_{v(mix)}}=\frac{n_1C_{p_1}+n_2C_{p_2}+\cdots}{n_1C_{v_1}+n_2C_{v_2}+\cdots}\]

Heat engines

An engine takes heat \(Q_H\) from a hot reservoir at \(T_H\), does work \(W\), and rejects \(Q_L\) to a cold reservoir at \(T_L\).

\[\eta=\frac{\text{work done by the engine}}{\text{heat supplied to it}}=\frac W{Q_H}=\frac{Q_H-Q_L}{Q_H}=1-\frac{Q_L}{Q_H}\]

Second law of thermodynamics

  • Kelvin-Planck statement: it is impossible to construct an engine, operating in a cycle, that produces no effect other than extracting heat from a reservoir and performing an equivalent amount of work.
  • Clausius statement: it is impossible to make heat flow from a body at a lower temperature to a body at a higher temperature without doing external work on the working substance.

Entropy

The change in entropy of the system is

\[\Delta S=\frac{\Delta Q}T\ \Rightarrow\ S_f-S_i=\int_i^f\frac{\Delta Q}T\]

In an adiabatic reversible process, the entropy of the system remains constant.

Carnot engine

Operations: I isothermal expansion, II adiabatic expansion, III isothermal compression, IV adiabatic compression. The cycle runs \(A(P_1,V_1)\to B(P_2,V_2)\to C(P_3,V_3)\to D(P_4,V_4)\).

Thermal efficiency

\[\frac{V_2}{V_1}=\frac{V_3}{V_4}\ \Rightarrow\ \frac{Q_2}{Q_1}=\frac{T_2}{T_1}\ \Rightarrow\ \eta=1-\frac{T_2}{T_1}\]

\(T_1\) is the source (hot) temperature and \(T_2\) the sink temperature.

Refrigerator (heat pump)

It takes heat \(Q_2\) from the cold body at \(T_2\), uses work \(W\), and delivers \(Q_1\) to the hot body at \(T_1\).

\[\beta=\frac{Q_2}W=\frac1{\dfrac{T_1}{T_2}-1}\quad\text{(coefficient of performance)}\]

Types of thermometers

\[\text{Liquid: }T=\left[\frac{\ell-\ell_0}{\ell_{100}-\ell_0}\right]\times100\]\[\text{Gas, constant volume: }T=\left[\frac{P-P_0}{P_{100}-P_0}\right]\times100,\quad P=P_0+\rho gh\]\[\text{Gas, constant pressure: }T=\left[\frac V{V-V'}\right]T_0\]\[\text{Electrical resistance: }T=\left[\frac{R_t-R_0}{R_{100}-R_0}\right]\times100\]

Thermal expansion

(a) Linear: \(\alpha=\dfrac{\Delta L}{L_0\Delta T}\) or \(L=L_0(1+\alpha\Delta T)\)

(b) Area (superficial): \(\beta=\dfrac{\Delta A}{A_0\Delta T}\) or \(A=A_0(1+\beta\Delta T)\)

(c) Volume (cubical): \(\gamma=\dfrac{\Delta V}{V_0\Delta T}\) or \(V=V_0(1+\gamma\Delta T)\)

\[\frac\alpha1=\frac\beta2=\frac\gamma3\]

Thermal stress of a material

\[\frac FA=Y\frac{\Delta\ell}\ell\]

Energy stored per unit volume

\[E=\tfrac12K(\Delta L)^2\quad\text{or}\quad E=\frac12\frac{AY}L(\Delta L)^2\]

Variation of the time period of pendulum clocks

\[\Delta T=\tfrac12\alpha\,\Delta\theta\,T\]

\(T'\lt T\): clock fast (time gain). \(T'\gt T\): clock slow (time loss).

Calorimetry

\[\text{Specific heat }S=\frac Q{m\Delta T}\qquad\text{Molar specific heat }C=\frac{\Delta Q}{n\Delta T}\qquad\text{Water equivalent}=m_wS_w\]

Heat transfer: conduction

\[\frac{dQ}{dt}=-KA\frac{dT}{dx}\qquad\text{Thermal resistance }R=\frac\ell{KA}\]

Rods in series (equal areas \(A_1=A_2=\cdots\)):

\[\frac{\ell_{eq}}{K_{eq}}=\frac{\ell_1}{K_1}+\frac{\ell_2}{K_2}+\cdots\]

Rods in parallel (equal lengths \(\ell_1=\ell_2=\cdots\)):

\[K_{eq}A_{eq}=K_1A_1+K_2A_2+\cdots\]

Radiation

For absorption, reflection and transmission: \(r+t+a=1\).

\[\text{Emissive power }E=\frac{\Delta U}{A\,\Delta t}\qquad\text{Spectral emissive power }E_\lambda=\frac{dE}{d\lambda}\]\[\text{Emissivity }e=\frac{E\text{ of a body at }T}{E\text{ of a black body at }T}\]
  • Kirchhoff's law: \(\dfrac{E(\text{body})}{a(\text{body})}=E(\text{black body})\)
  • Wien's displacement law: \(\lambda_m T=b\), with \(b=0.282\) cm K
  • Stefan-Boltzmann law: \(u=\sigma T^4\), \(\sigma=5.67\times10^{-8}\) W/m² K⁴. Net loss \(\Delta u=u-u_0=e\sigma A(T^4-T_0^4)\)
  • Newton's law of cooling: \(\dfrac{d\theta}{dt}=-k(\theta-\theta_0)\), so \(\theta=\theta_0+(\theta_i-\theta_0)e^{-kt}\)

Temperature scales, gas laws and the ideal-gas equation

\[\frac C5=\frac{F-32}9=\frac{K-273}5,\qquad PV=nRT=NkT=\frac mMRT,\qquad P=\frac{\rho RT}M\]
  • \(R=8.314\) J mol\(^{-1}\)K\(^{-1}=0.0821\) L atm mol\(^{-1}\)K\(^{-1}=1.987\) cal mol\(^{-1}\)K\(^{-1}\); \(k=\dfrac R{N_A}=1.38\times10^{-23}\) J K\(^{-1}\); \(N_A=6.022\times10^{23}\) mol\(^{-1}\). 1 atm \(=1.013\times10^5\) Pa \(=760\) mm Hg. At STP (273 K, 1 atm) one mole occupies \(22.4\) L.
  • Boyle: \(PV=\) const (constant \(T,n\)); Charles: \(\dfrac VT=\) const (constant \(P\)); Gay-Lussac: \(\dfrac PT=\) const (constant \(V\)); Avogadro: equal volumes of gases at the same \(T\) and \(P\) have equal numbers of molecules; combined: \(\dfrac{P_1V_1}{T_1}=\dfrac{P_2V_2}{T_2}\). Volume coefficient \(\dfrac1{273}\) K\(^{-1}\) per degree of the 0 °C volume.
  • Dalton’s law of partial pressures: \(P=\sum P_i\), with \(P_i=\dfrac{n_i}nP=\dfrac{n_iRT}V\); the molar mass of a mixture \(M=\dfrac{\sum n_iM_i}{\sum n_i}\).
  • Gas trapped by a mercury column or a liquid: the gas pressure is \(P=P_0+h\rho g\) if the liquid column lies above the gas (open end up) and \(P=P_0-h\rho g\) if the liquid hangs below the gas (open end down); apply \(P_1V_1=P_2V_2\) at constant \(T\) with \(V=\) (tube cross-section) \(\times\) length. A bubble rising from depth \(h\) in a lake: \(V_2=V_1\dfrac{P_0+\rho gh}{P_0}\) (if the temperature is constant).
  • Balloon lift: net upward force \(=V(\rho_{\text{air}}-\rho_{\text{gas}})g-\)(load and envelope weights).
  • Van der Waals gas (\(n\) moles): \(\left(P+\dfrac{an^2}{V^2}\right)(V-nb)=nRT\). \(b\) is four times the volume of the molecules in a mole, \(a\) measures the intermolecular attraction. Critical constants (per mole): \(V_c=3b\), \(P_c=\dfrac a{27b^2}\), \(T_c=\dfrac{8a}{27Rb}\); Boyle temperature \(T_B=\dfrac a{Rb}\); \(\dfrac{P_cV_c}{RT_c}=\dfrac38\). Real gases approach ideal behaviour at high temperature and low pressure; the compressibility factor \(Z=\dfrac{PV}{nRT}=1\) for an ideal gas.

Kinetic theory of gases: speeds, pressure and mean free path

\[P=\tfrac13\rho\langle v^2\rangle=\tfrac13nm\langle v^2\rangle=\tfrac23n\langle K\rangle,\qquad\langle K\rangle=\tfrac32kT,\qquad U_{\text{ideal}}=\frac f2nRT\]\[v_{rms}=\sqrt{\frac{3RT}M}=\sqrt{\frac{3kT}m},\quad\bar v=\sqrt{\frac{8RT}{\pi M}},\quad v_{mp}=\sqrt{\frac{2RT}M},\quad v_{mp}:\bar v:v_{rms}=1:1.128:1.225\]
  • \(n=\dfrac NV\) is the number density (\(n=\dfrac P{kT}\)). \(v\propto\sqrt{T/M}\): at the same temperature the lighter gas is faster. \(v_{rms}\) of hydrogen at 0 °C \(\approx1.84\) km s\(^{-1}\); of air \(\approx485\) m s\(^{-1}\).
  • Pressure is due to the rate of change of momentum at the walls; at fixed \(T\) it rises on compression because the collision rate increases. The average translational energy per molecule depends only on \(T\) (not on the gas). \(\langle K\rangle\) of any gas at 300 K \(\approx0.039\) eV.
  • Graham’s law: the rate of effusion (or diffusion) of a gas through a small hole is \(\propto\dfrac1{\sqrt M}\propto\dfrac1{\sqrt\rho}\); \(\dfrac{r_1}{r_2}=\sqrt{\dfrac{M_2}{M_1}}\).
  • Mean free path: \(\lambda=\dfrac1{\sqrt2\pi d^2n}=\dfrac{kT}{\sqrt2\pi d^2P}\) (\(d\) = molecular diameter); \(\lambda\propto\dfrac TP\); collision frequency \(=\dfrac{\bar v}\lambda\). At STP for air \(\lambda\approx10^{-7}\) m.
  • Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution of speeds \(f(v)\propto v^2e^{-mv^2/2kT}\): the area under the curve is the total number of molecules; as \(T\) rises the peak (\(v_{mp}\)) moves to higher speed and the curve flattens; at lower \(M\) the curve is broader. Always \(v_{rms}\gt\bar v\gt v_{mp}\).
  • The speed of sound in a gas \(v_s=\sqrt{\dfrac{\gamma P}\rho}=v_{rms}\sqrt{\dfrac\gamma3}\); \(v_s\propto\sqrt T\) (and independent of \(P\) at fixed \(T\)).
  • Degrees of freedom: monatomic 3 (\(\gamma=\tfrac53\)); diatomic 5 at ordinary temperatures (\(\gamma=\tfrac75\)), 7 when the vibrational mode is active (\(\gamma=\tfrac97\)); non-linear polyatomic 6 (\(\gamma=\tfrac43\)); a solid has \(f=6\) (3 kinetic + 3 potential), giving \(C=3R\approx25\) J mol\(^{-1}\)K\(^{-1}\) (Dulong–Petit). Each degree of freedom carries \(\tfrac12kT\) per molecule (\(\tfrac12RT\) per mole), vibration counts twice.
  • Mixture of two gases (moles \(n_1,n_2\), degrees of freedom \(f_1,f_2\)): \(f_{\text{mix}}=\dfrac{n_1f_1+n_2f_2}{n_1+n_2}\), \(\gamma_{\text{mix}}=1+\dfrac{2(n_1+n_2)}{n_1f_1+n_2f_2}\); the final temperature when two gases at \(T_1,T_2\) are mixed in an insulated rigid vessel: \(T=\dfrac{n_1C_{V1}T_1+n_2C_{V2}T_2}{n_1C_{V1}+n_2C_{V2}}\).

First law of thermodynamics and processes of an ideal gas

\[Q=\Delta U+W,\qquad W=\int P\,dV,\qquad\Delta U=nC_V\Delta T\ (\text{any process})\]
ProcessCondition\(W\) (by the gas)\(Q\)
Isochoric\(V\) const\(0\)\(nC_V\Delta T=\dfrac{V\Delta P}{\gamma-1}\)
Isobaric\(P\) const\(P\Delta V=nR\Delta T\)\(nC_p\Delta T\)
Isothermal\(T\) const\(nRT\ln\dfrac{V_2}{V_1}=nRT\ln\dfrac{P_1}{P_2}\)\(=W\)
Adiabatic\(Q=0\): \(PV^\gamma\), \(TV^{\gamma-1}\), \(P^{1-\gamma}T^\gamma\) const\(\dfrac{P_1V_1-P_2V_2}{\gamma-1}=\dfrac{nR(T_1-T_2)}{\gamma-1}\)\(0\)
Polytropic\(PV^x\) const\(\dfrac{nR(T_1-T_2)}{x-1}\)\(nC\Delta T\), \(C=C_V+\dfrac R{1-x}\)
Free expansioninto a vacuum\(0\)\(0\); \(\Delta T=0\) for an ideal gas
  • Sign convention: \(Q\gt0\) heat given to the gas; \(W\gt0\) work done by the gas. (If \(W\) is work done on the gas, \(Q=\Delta U-W_{\text{on}}\).) The work is the area under the \(P\)–\(V\) curve; it is positive for expansion and negative for compression.
  • Exponents: isobaric \(x=0\), isothermal \(x=1\), adiabatic \(x=\gamma\), isochoric \(x=\infty\). For a process \(P=kV\) (a straight line through the origin) \(x=-1\), \(C=C_V+\dfrac R2\); for \(P\propto V^{-1}\) isothermal.
  • On a \(P\)–\(V\) diagram the slope of an adiabatic is \(-\dfrac{\gamma P}V\), of an isotherm \(-\dfrac PV\): the adiabatic is steeper by the factor \(\gamma\). Bulk moduli: \(B_{\text{iso}}=P\), \(B_{\text{adi}}=\gamma P\).
  • Isobaric heating: \(\Delta U:W:Q=1:(\gamma-1):\gamma\); for a monatomic gas \(Q=\tfrac52nR\Delta T\), \(W=\tfrac25Q\), \(\Delta U=\tfrac35Q\); for a diatomic gas \(W=\tfrac27Q\).
  • Cyclic process: \(\Delta U=0\), so \(W_{\text{net}}=Q_{\text{net}}=\) area enclosed by the loop (positive for clockwise on \(P\)–\(V\)). Heat absorbed is the sum of the positive \(Q\)’s; \(\eta=\dfrac{W_{\text{net}}}{Q_{\text{absorbed}}}\).
  • Mayer’s relation \(C_p-C_V=R\); \(C_V=\dfrac R{\gamma-1}\), \(C_p=\dfrac{\gamma R}{\gamma-1}\). In terms of specific heats per unit mass: \(c_p-c_v=\dfrac RM\).
  • A gas expanding against a spring-loaded piston (area \(A\), spring constant \(k\), atmosphere \(P_0\)): \(W=P_0A\Delta x+\tfrac12k(x_2^2-x_1^2)\).
  • A heat-conducting piston/cylinder: slow processes in a thin-walled conducting vessel are isothermal; fast ones in an insulated vessel are adiabatic. For the same \(\Delta V\) more work is done in an isothermal expansion than an adiabatic one, and the final pressure is lower in the adiabatic case.

Heat engines, cycles, refrigerators and entropy

  • Carnot: \(\eta=1-\dfrac{T_L}{T_H}\) (kelvin); no engine between two reservoirs is more efficient (Carnot’s theorem); all reversible engines between the same temperatures have the same efficiency. Lowering the sink temperature by \(\Delta T\) raises \(\eta\) more than raising the source temperature by the same \(\Delta T\), since \(\left|\dfrac{\partial\eta}{\partial T_L}\right|=\dfrac1{T_H}\gt\dfrac{\partial\eta}{\partial T_H}=\dfrac{T_L}{T_H^2}\).
  • Two Carnot engines in series (the second uses the heat rejected by the first), intermediate temperature \(T_m\): equal efficiencies \(\Rightarrow T_m=\sqrt{T_HT_L}\); equal work \(\Rightarrow T_m=\dfrac{T_H+T_L}2\).
  • Refrigerator: \(\beta=\dfrac{Q_L}{W}=\dfrac{T_L}{T_H-T_L}\); heat pump: \(\dfrac{Q_H}W=\dfrac{T_H}{T_H-T_L}=1+\beta\). The relation \(\eta=\dfrac1{1+\beta}\) for an engine run backwards.
  • Otto cycle (petrol engine; adiabatic compression ratio \(r=\dfrac{V_{\max}}{V_{\min}}\)): \(\eta=1-\dfrac1{r^{\gamma-1}}\). Diesel cycle (cut-off ratio \(\rho=\dfrac{V_3}{V_2}\)): \(\eta=1-\dfrac1{r^{\gamma-1}}\cdot\dfrac{\rho^\gamma-1}{\gamma(\rho-1)}\).
