  • Entropy (state function): \(\Delta S=\displaystyle\int\dfrac{dQ_{\text{rev}}}T\). Heating a mass \(m\) of specific heat \(c\): \(\Delta S=mc\ln\dfrac{T_2}{T_1}\); phase change \(\Delta S=\dfrac{mL}T\); ideal gas: \(\Delta S=nC_V\ln\dfrac{T_2}{T_1}+nR\ln\dfrac{V_2}{V_1}=nC_p\ln\dfrac{T_2}{T_1}-nR\ln\dfrac{P_2}{P_1}\); free expansion \(\Delta S=nR\ln\dfrac{V_2}{V_1}\gt0\) although \(Q=0\).
  • Heat \(Q\) flowing irreversibly from \(T_1\) to \(T_2\lt T_1\): \(\Delta S=Q\left(\dfrac1{T_2}-\dfrac1{T_1}\right)\gt0\). Mixing equal masses of water at \(T_1,T_2\): \(T_f=\dfrac{T_1+T_2}2\), \(\Delta S=mc\ln\dfrac{(T_1+T_2)^2}{4T_1T_2}\ge0\).
  • \(\Delta S_{\text{universe}}\ge0\) (equality for reversible processes); reversible adiabatic processes are isentropic.

Calorimetry, change of state and mechanical equivalent

QuantityValue
Specific heat of water / ice / steam\(4186\) J kg\(^{-1}\)K\(^{-1}=1\) cal g\(^{-1}\)°C\(^{-1}\); \(2100\) (\(0.5\) cal); \(2010\) (\(0.48\) cal)
Latent heat of fusion of ice\(3.34\times10^5\) J kg\(^{-1}\) (\(80\) cal g\(^{-1}\))
Latent heat of vaporisation of water\(2.26\times10^6\) J kg\(^{-1}\) (\(540\) cal g\(^{-1}\))
Mechanical equivalent of heat \(J\)\(4.186\) J cal\(^{-1}\)
  • \(Q=mc\Delta T\), \(Q=mL\); heat capacity \(C=mc\); water equivalent \(=mc/c_w\) (mass of water with the same heat capacity). Principle of calorimetry: heat lost by hot bodies \(=\) heat gained by cold bodies (isolated system), including the calorimeter.
  • Mixing ice at 0 °C with hot water: compare the heat available to cool the water to 0 °C with the heat needed to melt all the ice, \(mL\). If the water gives less than \(m_iL\), only part melts and the final temperature is 0 °C; the mass melted is \(\dfrac{m_wc_w(T-0)}L\).
  • Steam at 100 °C condensing then cooling: \(Q=m_sL_v+m_sc_w(100-T_f)\). Cooling curves are flat during a change of state.
  • Heating a body electrically or by a flame at constant power \(P\): \(Pt=mc\Delta T\) (plus \(mL\) at a change of state). On a heating curve the time of each segment is proportional to the heat it needs; for example \(\dfrac{t_{\text{melting ice}}}{t_{\text{heating water }0\to100^\circ\text{C}}}=\dfrac{80}{100}\).
  • Mechanical to heat: \(mgh=J\,mc\Delta T\) when all the energy is converted; water falling through height \(h\) warms by \(\Delta T=\dfrac{gh}c\approx0.234\) °C per 100 m. A bullet stopped in a fixed block: \(\tfrac12mv^2=\) heat produced (if all of it warms the bullet, \(\Delta T=\dfrac{v^2}{2Jc}\) in the appropriate units). Joule’s paddle-wheel experiment gave the value of \(J\).
  • Evaporation occurs at all temperatures from the surface and cools the liquid; boiling occurs when the saturated vapour pressure equals the external pressure (so the boiling point rises with pressure). Relative humidity \(=\dfrac{\text{actual vapour pressure}}{\text{saturated vapour pressure at that temperature}}\times100\%\); the dew point is the temperature at which the air becomes saturated.
  • Regelation: the melting point of ice falls with increasing pressure (ice contracts on melting); the triple point of water is 273.16 K at 611 Pa. Above the critical temperature a gas cannot be liquefied by pressure alone.

Thermal expansion: more results

  • For an isotropic solid: \(\beta\approx2\alpha\), \(\gamma\approx3\alpha\) (\(\alpha:\beta:\gamma=1:2:3\)). A hole in a plate or the gap in a ring expands as if it were made of the surrounding material; a ring heated expands outward, its inner diameter increases.
  • Density and temperature: \(\rho_T=\dfrac{\rho_0}{1+\gamma\Delta T}\approx\rho_0(1-\gamma\Delta T)\). The fraction of a body floating in a liquid changes because both the body and the liquid expand.
  • Liquid in a vessel: \(\gamma_{\text{real}}=\gamma_{\text{apparent}}+\gamma_{\text{vessel}}\) (\(\gamma_{\text{vessel}}=3\alpha_{\text{vessel}}\)); the volume overflowing from a full vessel when heated by \(\Delta T\) is \(V\gamma_{\text{app}}\Delta T\).
  • Anomalous expansion of water: from 0 °C to 4 °C water contracts; maximum density \(1000\) kg m\(^{-3}\) at 4 °C. A lake freezes from the top downward.
  • Bimetallic strip (two strips of thickness \(t\), \(\alpha_2\gt\alpha_1\)): heated by \(\Delta T\), it bends into an arc of radius \(R=\dfrac t{(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)\Delta T}\) with the metal of larger \(\alpha\) on the outer (convex) side.
  • Thermal stress: a rod fully constrained between rigid walls and heated by \(\Delta T\): stress \(\sigma=Y\alpha\Delta T\), force \(F=YA\alpha\Delta T\), and the energy stored per unit volume \(\tfrac12Y(\alpha\Delta T)^2\). If a gap \(\delta\) is left, the compressive stress is \(Y\left(\alpha\Delta T-\dfrac\delta L\right)\).
  • Rods in series (lengths \(L_1,L_2\)): effective \(\alpha=\dfrac{\alpha_1L_1+\alpha_2L_2}{L_1+L_2}\). To keep the length difference between two rods of different materials constant on heating: \(\alpha_1L_1=\alpha_2L_2\).
  • A scale calibrated at \(T_0\) and used at a higher temperature reads smaller than the true length: measured \(\approx\) true \(\times(1-\alpha\Delta T)\). A pendulum clock runs slow when heated (\(\Delta T/T=\tfrac12\alpha\Delta\theta\)).
  • A rotating disc heated by \(\Delta\theta\): \(I\propto R^2\) rises, \(L=I\omega\) is conserved, so \(\omega'\approx\omega(1-2\alpha\Delta\theta)\).
  • For an ideal gas \(\gamma_P=\dfrac1T\) (volume coefficient at constant pressure) and the pressure coefficient at constant volume is also \(\dfrac1T\).

Conduction and Newton’s law of cooling: more results

  • Steady state: heat current \(H=\dfrac{\Delta T}{R_{th}}\), with \(R_{th}=\dfrac\ell{KA}\); thermal resistances add in series, conductances in parallel (analogy with \(I=\dfrac{\Delta V}R\)).
  • Two rods in series (same area) with ends held at \(T_1\) and \(T_2\): the junction temperature \(T=\dfrac{\frac{K_1}{\ell_1}T_1+\frac{K_2}{\ell_2}T_2}{\frac{K_1}{\ell_1}+\frac{K_2}{\ell_2}}\); the temperature varies linearly along each rod.
  • Cylindrical shell (length \(\ell\), radii \(r_1\lt r_2\)): \(H=\dfrac{2\pi K\ell(T_1-T_2)}{\ln(r_2/r_1)}\) (radial flow). Spherical shell: \(H=\dfrac{4\pi Kr_1r_2(T_1-T_2)}{r_2-r_1}\).
  • Parallel conductors (same length, areas \(A_1,A_2\)): \(K_{eq}=\dfrac{K_1A_1+K_2A_2}{A_1+A_2}\); series (same area): \(K_{eq}=\dfrac{\ell_1+\ell_2}{\ell_1/K_1+\ell_2/K_2}\).
  • Ice on a pond: the thickness \(x\) of ice grows as \(\rho L\,x\,dx=K\Delta T\,dt\), so the time to grow from \(x_1\) to \(x_2\) is \(t=\dfrac{\rho L}{2K\Delta T}(x_2^2-x_1^2)\); \(t\propto x^2\).
  • Newton’s law of cooling: \(\dfrac{d\theta}{dt}=-k(\theta-\theta_0)\), \(k=\dfrac{hA}{mc}\); \(\theta(t)=\theta_0+(\theta_i-\theta_0)e^{-kt}\). For a small fall from \(\theta_1\) to \(\theta_2\) in time \(t\) (average method): \(\dfrac{\theta_1-\theta_2}t=k\left(\dfrac{\theta_1+\theta_2}2-\theta_0\right)\). The time to cool from \(\theta_i\) to \(\theta\): \(t=\dfrac1k\ln\dfrac{\theta_i-\theta_0}{\theta-\theta_0}\).
  • A body heated at power \(P\) reaches the steady temperature where \(P=hA(\theta-\theta_0)\); the approach is exponential with time constant \(\dfrac{mc}{hA}\).
  • Convection: heat carried by moving fluid (natural by density differences; forced by pumps). A good conductor feels colder than a poor one at the same temperature because it conducts heat from the hand faster.

Thermal radiation: more results

  • Stefan–Boltzmann law: power radiated by a body of surface area \(A\), emissivity \(e\), at absolute temperature \(T\): \(P=e\sigma AT^4\); \(\sigma=5.67\times10^{-8}\) W m\(^{-2}\)K\(^{-4}\). Net loss to surroundings at \(T_0\): \(P_{\text{net}}=e\sigma A(T^4-T_0^4)\); for small differences \(P_{\text{net}}\approx4e\sigma AT_0^3\,\Delta T\) (which leads to Newton’s law).
  • Wien’s displacement law: \(\lambda_mT=b=2.9\times10^{-3}\) m K; as \(T\) rises the peak of the black-body spectrum moves to shorter wavelengths; the area under the spectral curve \(\propto T^4\). The Sun (\(\lambda_m\approx500\) nm) has \(T\approx5800\) K.
  • Rate of cooling by radiation: \(\dfrac{dT}{dt}=-\dfrac{e\sigma A}{mc}(T^4-T_0^4)\); for similar spheres \(\dfrac{A}{mc}\propto\dfrac1{r\rho c}\): smaller bodies cool faster.
  • For a perfectly black body \(a=e=1\); \(a_\lambda=e_\lambda\) for each wavelength (Kirchhoff). Good absorbers are good emitters; polished metal is a poor absorber and poor emitter. A body in thermal equilibrium emits as much as it absorbs (Prévost).
  • Sun and Earth: solar constant \(S=\sigma T_s^4\left(\dfrac{R_s}d\right)^2\approx1.37\) kW m\(^{-2}\); intensity \(\propto\dfrac1{d^2}\). Equilibrium temperature of a black planet: \(T_p=T_s\sqrt{\dfrac{R_s}{2d}}\) (\(\approx278\) K for Earth); with albedo \(a\): \(T_p=T_s\left[\dfrac{(1-a)}{4}\right]^{1/4}\sqrt{\dfrac{R_s}{d}}\).
  • A cavity with a small hole behaves as a black body; the radiation inside is isotropic and a function of \(T\) only. Radiation power of a sphere: \(4\pi r^2\sigma T^4\); when \(T\) is doubled the power rises \(16\) times; when the radius is doubled at the same \(T\), the power rises \(4\) times.
  • Thermos flask: silvered double walls (low emissivity), vacuum (no conduction or convection) and a cork stopper reduce the three modes of heat transfer.
16
Electricity & magnetism

Electrostatics

Coulomb force between two point charges

\[\vec F=\frac1{4\pi\epsilon_0\epsilon_r}\frac{q_1q_2}{|\vec r|^3}\vec r=\frac1{4\pi\epsilon_0\epsilon_r}\frac{q_1q_2}{|\vec r|^2}\hat r\]

Electric field intensity

The force experienced by a unit positive charge:

\[\vec E=\frac{\vec F}{q_0}\]

The force on a charge \(q\) at a point with field \(\vec E\) is \(\vec F=q\vec E\).

Electric potential

If \((W_{\infty P})_{ext}\) is the work required to move a point charge \(q\) from infinity to a point \(P\), the potential at \(P\) is

\[V_P=\left.\frac{(W_{\infty P})_{ext}}q\right]_{acc=0}\]

The potential difference between points \(A\) and \(B\) is \(V_A-V_B\).

Field and potential formulae

SourceField \(\vec E\)Potential \(V\)
Point charge\(\dfrac{Kq}{|\vec r|^2}\hat r=\dfrac{Kq}{r^3}\vec r\)\(\dfrac{Kq}r\)
Infinitely long line charge\(\dfrac\lambda{2\pi\epsilon_0r}\hat r=\dfrac{2K\lambda\hat r}r\)not defined; \(v_B-v_A=-2K\lambda\ln(r_B/r_A)\)
Infinite non-conducting thin sheet\(\dfrac\sigma{2\epsilon_0}\hat n\)not defined; \(v_B-v_A=-\dfrac\sigma{2\epsilon_0}(r_B-r_A)\)
Infinitely large charged conducting sheet\(\dfrac\sigma{\epsilon_0}\hat n\)not defined; \(v_B-v_A=-\dfrac\sigma{\epsilon_0}(r_B-r_A)\)
Uniformly charged ring, on the axis at distance \(x\)\(E_{axis}=\dfrac{KQx}{(R^2+x^2)^{3/2}}\), \(E_{centre}=0\)\(V_{axis}=\dfrac{KQ}{\sqrt{R^2+x^2}}\), \(V_{centre}=\dfrac{KQ}R\)
Hollow conducting / non-conducting / solid conducting sphere, \(r\ge R\)\(\dfrac{kQ}{|\vec r|^2}\hat r\)\(\dfrac{KQ}r\)
The same sphere, \(r\lt R\)\(0\)\(\dfrac{KQ}R\)
Uniformly charged solid non-conducting sphere, \(r\ge R\)\(\dfrac{kQ}{|\vec r|^2}\hat r\)\(\dfrac{KQ}r\)
The same sphere, \(r\le R\)\(\dfrac{KQ\vec r}{R^3}=\dfrac{\rho\vec r}{3\epsilon_0}\)\(\dfrac\rho{6\epsilon_0}(3R^2-r^2)\)
Thin uniformly charged disc (surface charge density \(\sigma\)), on the axis\(\dfrac\sigma{2\epsilon_0}\left[1-\dfrac x{\sqrt{R^2+x^2}}\right]\)\(\dfrac\sigma{2\epsilon_0}\left[\sqrt{R^2+x^2}-x\right]\)

Work and potential energy

Work done by an external agent in taking a charge \(q\) from \(A\) to \(B\):

\[(W_{ext})_{AB}=q(V_B-V_A)\quad\text{or}\quad(W_{el})_{AB}=q(V_A-V_B)\]

Electrostatic potential energy of a point charge: \(U=qV\).

P.E. of a system of charges

\[U=\frac{U_1+U_2+\cdots}2=(U_{12}+U_{13}+\cdots+U_{1n})+(U_{23}+U_{24}+\cdots+U_{2n})+(U_{34}+\cdots)+\cdots\]

Energy density

\[\tfrac12\epsilon E^2\]

Self energy

Uniformly charged shell: \(U_{self}=\dfrac{KQ^2}{2R}\)

Uniformly charged solid non-conducting sphere: \(U_{self}=\dfrac{3KQ^2}{5R}\)

Electric field intensity due to a dipole

\[\text{On the axis: }\vec E=\frac{2K\vec P}{r^3}\qquad\text{On the equatorial position: }\vec E=-\frac{K\vec P}{r^3}\]\[\text{At a general point }O(r,\theta):\ E_{res}=\frac{KP}{r^3}\sqrt{1+3\cos^2\theta}\]

Dipole in an external field

Potential energy: \(U=-\vec p\cdot\vec E\)

Uniform field: torque \(\vec\tau=\vec p\times\vec E\); net force \(\vec F=0\)

Non-uniform field: torque \(\vec\tau=\vec p\times\vec E\); \(U=-\vec p\cdot\vec E\); net force \(|F|=\left|p\dfrac{\partial E}{\partial r}\right|\)

Potential due to a dipole at a general point \((r,\theta)\):

\[V=\frac{P\cos\theta}{4\pi\epsilon_0r^2}=\frac{\vec p\cdot\vec r}{4\pi\epsilon_0r^3}\]

Electric flux and Gauss's law

\[\phi_E=\int_S\vec E\cdot d\vec S=\int_SE_n\,dS\]\[\phi_E=\oint\vec E\cdot d\vec S=\frac{q_{in}}{\epsilon_0}\quad\text{(flux through a closed surface)}\]

Field near a conducting surface

\[\frac\sigma{\epsilon_0}\hat n\]

Electric pressure

At the surface of a conductor:

\[P=\frac{\sigma^2}{2\epsilon_0}\]

where \(\sigma\) is the local surface charge density.

Potential difference and field

\[V_B-V_A=-\int_A^B\vec E\cdot d\vec r\]\[\vec E=-\left[\hat i\frac\partial{\partial x}V+\hat j\frac\partial{\partial y}V+\hat k\frac\partial{\partial z}V\right]=-\nabla V=-\operatorname{grad}V\]

Charge, Coulomb’s law and equilibrium of charges

  • Charge is quantised (\(q=ne\), \(e=1.602\times10^{-19}\) C), conserved, additive, and invariant (does not depend on speed). \(K=\dfrac1{4\pi\epsilon_0}=9\times10^9\) N m\(^2\)C\(^{-2}\), \(\epsilon_0=8.85\times10^{-12}\) C\(^2\)N\(^{-1}\)m\(^{-2}\). In a medium of dielectric constant \(\epsilon_r\) the force is reduced to \(\dfrac1{\epsilon_r}\) of the vacuum value.
  • Superposition: the force on a charge is the vector sum of the forces due to each other charge, which are not affected by the presence of the others. For a charge distribution: \(\vec E=K\displaystyle\int\dfrac{dq}{r^2}\hat r\).
  • The electrostatic force between a proton and an electron is about \(2.3\times10^{39}\) times the gravitational force between them: \(\dfrac{e^2}{4\pi\epsilon_0Gm_em_p}\).
  • Two small identical charged spheres on strings (each of mass \(m\), charge \(q\), string length \(\ell\), angle \(\theta\) with the vertical): \(\tan\theta=\dfrac{F}{mg}\), with \(F=\dfrac{Kq^2}{(2\ell\sin\theta)^2}\); \(T=\dfrac{mg}{\cos\theta}\). If the spheres are immersed in a liquid of density \(\sigma\) with the same angle, \(\dfrac{\rho}{\rho-\sigma}=\epsilon_r\) (for the spheres of density \(\rho\)).
  • Three charges in equilibrium on a line: a free charge \(q_3\) placed between like charges \(q_1\) and \(q_2\) (separation \(d\)) is in equilibrium at \(x=\dfrac{\sqrt{q_1}\,d}{\sqrt{q_1}+\sqrt{q_2}}\) from \(q_1\). For all three to be in equilibrium \(q_3=-\dfrac{q_1q_2}{(\sqrt{q_1}+\sqrt{q_2})^2}\) (opposite in sign to the end charges). The equilibrium of such a system is unstable.
  • Electric field lines: start on positive charges and end on negative charges (or at infinity), never cross, are perpendicular to conductor surfaces, and their density is proportional to \(E\); tangent gives the direction of \(\vec E\). They are not the paths of charged particles except in special cases.
  • Equal charges at the corners of a regular polygon: the net field at the centre is zero. If one charge is removed, the field at the centre is \(\dfrac{Kq}{R^2}\), directed towards the vacant corner (for positive charges).

Fields and potentials of finite charge distributions

DistributionFieldPotential
Finite line charge \(\lambda\), at perpendicular distance \(r\)\(E_\perp=\dfrac{K\lambda}r(\sin\theta_1+\sin\theta_2)\), \(E_\parallel=\dfrac{K\lambda}r(\cos\theta_1-\cos\theta_2)\) (\(\theta_{1,2}\) measured from the perpendicular through the point); infinite line \(\dfrac{2K\lambda}r\); semi-infinite line at its end-perpendicular: \(E_\perp=E_\parallel=\dfrac{K\lambda}r\)on the axis (nearest end at \(a\), length \(L\)): \(K\lambda\ln\dfrac{a+L}a\); on the perpendicular bisector: \(2K\lambda\ln\dfrac{L/2+\sqrt{L^2/4+r^2}}r\)
Rod on its axis (beyond the end)\(\dfrac{K\lambda L}{a(a+L)}\)\(K\lambda\ln\dfrac{a+L}a\)
Circular arc (radius \(R\), angle \(2\alpha\), \(\lambda\)) at the centre\(\dfrac{2K\lambda\sin\alpha}R\) (semicircle: \(\dfrac{2K\lambda}R\); quarter: \(\dfrac{\sqrt2K\lambda}R\))\(\dfrac{KQ}R\) for any arc
Disc (\(\sigma\)) at the centre—\(\dfrac{\sigma R}{2\epsilon_0}\)
Hollow hemispherical shell (\(\sigma\)) at the centre of the flat face\(\dfrac\sigma{4\epsilon_0}=\dfrac{KQ}{2R^2}\)\(\dfrac{KQ}R\)
Solid hemisphere (\(\rho\)) at the centre of the flat face\(\dfrac{\rho R}{4\epsilon_0}\)—
Uniformly charged solid sphere at the centre\(0\)\(\dfrac{3KQ}{2R}\) (surface \(\dfrac{KQ}R\))
Infinite slab (thickness \(t\), \(\rho\)), at distance \(x\) from its mid-planeinside \(\dfrac{\rho x}{\epsilon_0}\); outside \(\dfrac{\rho t}{2\epsilon_0}\)—
Uniformly charged infinite solid cylinder (radius \(R\))inside \(\dfrac{\rho r}{2\epsilon_0}\); outside \(\dfrac{\lambda}{2\pi\epsilon_0r}\) (\(\lambda=\rho\pi R^2\))—
Spherical cavity (centre displaced by \(\vec d\)) in a uniformly charged sphereuniform inside the cavity: \(\vec E=\dfrac{\rho\vec d}{3\epsilon_0}\) (\(\vec d\) from the centre of the sphere to the centre of the cavity)—
Non-uniform sphere \(\rho=\rho_0\dfrac rR\)inside \(E=\dfrac{\rho_0r^2}{4\epsilon_0R}\)—
  • Potential is a scalar: for \(N\) charges at equal distance \(R\) from a point the potential there is \(\dfrac K R\sum q_i\) whatever their arrangement. At the centre of a square of side \(a\) carrying charges \(q\) at its corners: \(V=\dfrac{4\sqrt2Kq}a\), \(E=0\).
  • The field on the axis of a ring has its maximum at \(x=\dfrac R{\sqrt2}\), \(E_{\max}=\dfrac{KQ}{R^2}\cdot\dfrac2{3\sqrt3}\). A charge near the centre of the ring on the axis performs SHM with \(\omega^2=\dfrac{KQq}{mR^3}\).

Gauss’s law: flux results

  • \(\oint\vec E\cdot d\vec S=\dfrac{q_{\text{in}}}{\epsilon_0}\): \(\vec E\) is due to all charges, but the flux depends only on the charge enclosed. Flux of a point charge through a surface that subtends a solid angle \(\Omega\) at the charge: \(\phi=\dfrac{q\Omega}{4\pi\epsilon_0}\).
  • Charge \(q\) on the axis of a disc (or circle) of radius \(R\) at distance \(h\): \(\phi=\dfrac q{2\epsilon_0}\left(1-\dfrac h{\sqrt{h^2+R^2}}\right)=\dfrac q{2\epsilon_0}(1-\cos\theta)\), with \(\theta\) the half-angle of the cone. A charge lying in the plane of the disc gives zero flux through it.
  • Cube: charge \(q\) at the centre: \(\dfrac q{6\epsilon_0}\) through each face. Charge at a corner: \(\dfrac q{8\epsilon_0}\) is the flux through the whole cube; \(\dfrac q{24\epsilon_0}\) through each of the three faces not meeting at that corner and zero through the three faces that meet at the corner. (Imagine eight identical cubes meeting at the charge: by symmetry each carries one-eighth of \(\dfrac q{\epsilon_0}\).)
  • Symmetric fields: sphere \(E=\dfrac{Kq_{\text{in}}}{r^2}\); long cylinder \(E=\dfrac{\lambda_{\text{in}}}{2\pi\epsilon_0r}\); infinite plane \(E=\dfrac\sigma{2\epsilon_0}\). A Gaussian surface through a charged region must be chosen so that \(E\) is constant (or zero) over each part.
  • Parallel sheets (charge densities \(\sigma_1,\sigma_2\) on non-conducting sheets): between them \(E=\dfrac{\sigma_1-\sigma_2}{2\epsilon_0}\) (equal and opposite: \(\dfrac\sigma{\epsilon_0}\) between, \(0\) outside; equal and like: \(0\) between, \(\dfrac\sigma{\epsilon_0}\) outside). The field of each sheet is unaffected by the other (superposition).
  • Two parallel conducting plates with total charges \(Q_1,Q_2\) and area \(A\): the outer faces carry \(\dfrac{Q_1+Q_2}2\) each; the inner faces \(\pm\dfrac{Q_1-Q_2}2\) (+ on plate 1); the field between the plates \(=\dfrac{Q_1-Q_2}{2A\epsilon_0}\), and outside \(\dfrac{Q_1+Q_2}{2A\epsilon_0}\).
  • The flux through a closed surface is zero if the enclosed net charge is zero, even if \(\vec E\ne0\) on it; for a uniform field, the flux through any closed surface is zero.

Conductors in electrostatics

  • Inside a conductor in equilibrium \(\vec E=0\) and the potential is constant (the surface is an equipotential); excess charge lies on the surface; the field just outside is \(\dfrac\sigma{\epsilon_0}\hat n\); the surface charge density is larger where the radius of curvature is smaller (\(\sigma\propto\dfrac1R\) for connected spheres), so sharp points discharge (corona, lightning rods).
  • Electrostatic shielding: the field inside a hollow conductor cavity (without charges) is zero, whatever the outside field. A charge \(q\) placed inside the cavity induces \(-q\) on the cavity wall and \(+q\) on the outer surface of a neutral conductor; the field outside is the same as if the charge were at the centre (for a sphere), independent of the position of \(q\) in the cavity.
  • Charge sharing: two conducting spheres of radii \(r_1,r_2\) joined by a wire: \(V_1=V_2=\dfrac{q_1+q_2}{4\pi\epsilon_0(r_1+r_2)}\); charges in the ratio \(\dfrac{q_1}{q_2}=\dfrac{r_1}{r_2}\); surface densities \(\dfrac{\sigma_1}{\sigma_2}=\dfrac{r_2}{r_1}\); the energy lost (as heat) \(=\dfrac12\dfrac{C_1C_2}{C_1+C_2}(V_1-V_2)^2\).
  • Concentric spherical shells (inner radius \(a\), charge \(q\); outer radius \(b\), charge \(Q\)): \(V_a=K\left(\dfrac qa+\dfrac Qb\right)\), \(V_b=\dfrac{K(q+Q)}b\). Connecting the shells moves all the charge to the outer shell. Earthing the outer shell leaves the charge \(-q\) on its inner surface and none outside; earthing the inner shell gives it the charge \(-\dfrac{Qa}b\).
  • Induced charge on an earthed sphere: a point charge \(q\) at distance \(d\gt R\) from the centre of an earthed conducting sphere of radius \(R\) induces \(-\dfrac{qR}d\) on the sphere.
  • Potential of a charged conductor: \(V=\dfrac{KQ}R\); capacitance \(C=4\pi\epsilon_0R\). Earth is at zero potential and acts as an infinite reservoir of charge.
  • Combination of charged drops: \(n\) identical drops (radius \(r\), charge \(q\), potential \(V\)) combine into one: \(R=n^{1/3}r\), \(Q=nq\), \(V'=n^{2/3}V\), \(\sigma'=n^{1/3}\sigma\), and the electrostatic energy becomes \(n^{5/3}\) times that of a single small drop (\(n^{2/3}\) times the total initial energy).
  • Charged soap bubble (radius \(R\), surface tension \(S\), surface charge density \(\sigma\)): the outward electric pressure \(\dfrac{\sigma^2}{2\epsilon_0}\) reduces the excess pressure: \(P_{\text{in}}-P_{\text{out}}=\dfrac{4S}R-\dfrac{\sigma^2}{2\epsilon_0}\); in equilibrium at atmospheric pressure inside and out: \(\sigma^2=\dfrac{8\epsilon_0S}R\).
  • Force on a charged conductor surface element: \(dF=\dfrac{\sigma^2}{2\epsilon_0}dS\) (outward). Force between the two hemispheres of a charged spherical conductor: \(F=\dfrac{\sigma^2}{2\epsilon_0}\pi R^2=\dfrac{Q^2}{32\pi\epsilon_0R^2}\).

Charged particles in electric fields; dipoles

  • Acceleration through a potential difference \(V\): \(\tfrac12mv^2=qV\); for an electron \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{2eV}m}=5.93\times10^5\sqrt V\) m s\(^{-1}\) (\(V\) in volts); 1 eV \(=1.602\times10^{-19}\) J. A proton or \(\alpha\)-particle in the same field gains \(eV\) or \(2eV\); the final speed \(\propto\sqrt{q/m}\).
  • Deflection in a uniform field \(E\) perpendicular to the initial velocity \(v_0\) (plates of length \(\ell\)): time in the field \(\dfrac\ell{v_0}\); \(y=\dfrac{qE\ell^2}{2mv_0^2}\); \(\tan\theta=\dfrac{qE\ell}{mv_0^2}\); the path inside is a parabola; on a screen at distance \(D\) from the plates’ centre, \(Y=D\tan\theta\). The particle just misses the plate if \(y=\dfrac d2\).
  • Closest approach (head-on) of a particle of charge \(q_1\), mass \(m\) and speed \(v\) to a fixed charge \(q_2\): \(\tfrac12mv^2=\dfrac{Kq_1q_2}{r_{\min}}\), so \(r_{\min}=\dfrac{2Kq_1q_2}{mv^2}\). With an impact parameter \(b\), use conservation of angular momentum \(mv_0b=mv\,r_{\min}\) together with energy conservation.
  • Two charges released from rest at separation \(r\) (masses \(m_1,m_2\)): \(m_1v_1=m_2v_2\) and \(\tfrac12m_1v_1^2+\tfrac12m_2v_2^2=\dfrac{Kq_1q_2}r\) at infinity, so \(v_1=\sqrt{\dfrac{2Kq_1q_2m_2}{m_1r(m_1+m_2)}}\).
  • Charge oscillating along the axis of a ring (\(Q\), radius \(R\)) for \(x\ll R\): \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{4\pi\epsilon_0mR^3}{qQ}}\). Inside a uniformly charged non-conducting sphere \(E=\dfrac{KQr}{R^3}\), so a charge of opposite sign oscillates with the same period.
  • Electric dipole (\(p=q\cdot2a\), SI unit C m; 1 debye \(=3.34\times10^{-30}\) C m): \(U=-pE\cos\theta\); torque \(\tau=pE\sin\theta\); stable equilibrium \(\theta=0\) (\(U=-pE\)), unstable \(\theta=\pi\) (\(U=+pE\)). Work to turn the dipole from \(\theta_1\) to \(\theta_2\): \(W=pE(\cos\theta_1-\cos\theta_2)\). Small oscillations: \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac I{pE}}\).
  • Field components of a dipole at \((r,\theta)\): \(E_r=\dfrac{2Kp\cos\theta}{r^3}\), \(E_\theta=\dfrac{Kp\sin\theta}{r^3}\). The force between a point charge \(q\) and a dipole on its axis: \(F=\dfrac{2Kpq}{r^3}\) (attractive if the nearer pole is opposite); on the equatorial line \(\dfrac{Kpq}{r^3}\). The dipole moment of a system of charges \(\vec p=\sum q_i\vec r_i\) is independent of the origin only if the net charge is zero.
  • A charge \(q\) in a uniform field \(E\) and gravity: the trajectory is a parabola about the direction of \(m\vec g+q\vec E\); a pendulum bob has \(g_{\text{eff}}=g\pm\dfrac{qE}m\).

Energy of charge configurations and potential relations

  • \(U=\dfrac12\sum_iq_iV_i\) (sum over all charges, \(V_i\) the potential due to the others). Three charges at the corners of an equilateral triangle of side \(a\): \(U=\dfrac{3Kq^2}a\) (equal \(q\)). Four equal charges at a square’s corners: \(U=\dfrac{(4+\sqrt2)Kq^2}a\). Work to bring a charge from infinity to a point of potential \(V\): \(qV\).
  • Energy stored in a field: \(u=\tfrac12\epsilon_0E^2\) per unit volume (\(\tfrac12\epsilon E^2\) in a dielectric); \(U=\displaystyle\int\tfrac12\epsilon_0E^2dV\). Charged conducting sphere of radius \(R\): \(U=\dfrac{KQ^2}{2R}\); the energy stored outside the radius \(r\gt R\) is \(\dfrac{KQ^2}{2r}\).
  • \(\vec E=-\nabla V\): \(E_x=-\dfrac{\partial V}{\partial x}\); \(E\) is perpendicular to equipotential surfaces and points toward decreasing \(V\); \(|E|=\dfrac{\Delta V}{\Delta r}\) between nearby surfaces. A uniform field \(E\) gives \(V=-Ex+\) const, so surfaces are equally spaced planes. For \(V=ax^2\): \(E=-2ax\).
  • No work is done in moving a charge along an equipotential. The work in moving a charge between two points depends only on \(\Delta V\) (not on the path); \(\oint\vec E\cdot d\vec l=0\) for electrostatic fields.
  • Potential at a point due to a uniformly charged spherical shell, for \(r\lt R\), equals the surface value \(\dfrac{KQ}R\); thus the potential at the common centre of concentric shells is \(\sum\dfrac{KQ_i}{R_i}\).
  • Zero-field and zero-potential points on the line of two charges (separation \(d\)): for like charges \(q_1,q_2\), \(E=0\) between them at \(x=\dfrac{d\sqrt{q_1}}{\sqrt{q_1}+\sqrt{q_2}}\) from \(q_1\) (nearer the smaller charge) and \(V\) is nowhere zero. For unlike charges \(q_1\gt0\) and \(-q_2\) with \(q_1\gt q_2\): \(E=0\) only outside the segment, at a distance \(\dfrac{d\sqrt{q_2}}{\sqrt{q_1}-\sqrt{q_2}}\) beyond the smaller charge; \(V=0\) at \(x=\dfrac{q_1d}{q_1+q_2}\) from \(q_1\) (between them) and at \(x=\dfrac{q_1d}{q_1-q_2}\) (beyond the smaller charge).
17
Electricity & magnetism

Current Electricity

1. Electric current

\[I_{av}=\frac{\Delta q}{\Delta t}\]

Instantaneous current:

\[i=\lim_{\Delta t\to0}\frac{\Delta q}{\Delta t}=\frac{dq}{dt}\]

2. Electric current in a conductor

\[I=nAeV_d\]\[v_d=\frac\lambda\tau\qquad v_d=\frac{\tfrac12\left(\frac{eE}m\right)\tau^2}{\tau}=\frac12\frac{eE}m\tau\]\[I=neAv_d\]

3. Current density

\[\vec J=\frac{dI}{dS}\hat n\]

4. Electrical resistance and Ohm's law

\[I=neAv_d=neA\left(\frac{eE}{2m}\right)\tau=\left(\frac{ne^2\tau}{2m}\right)AE\]\[E=\frac V\ell\quad\text{so}\quad I=\left(\frac{ne^2\tau}{2m}\right)\left(\frac A\ell\right)V=\left(\frac A{\rho\ell}\right)V=\frac VR\ \Rightarrow\ V=IR\]\[\rho=\frac{2m}{ne^2\tau}=\frac1\sigma\]

\(\rho\) is the resistivity (specific resistance) and \(\sigma\) the conductivity. Current in a conductor is proportional to the potential difference across its ends: this is Ohm's law.

Units: \(R\): ohm (\(\Omega\)); \(\rho\): ohm-metre (\(\Omega\) m); \(\sigma\): siemens per metre (\(\Omega^{-1}\)m\(^{-1}\)).

Dependence of resistance on temperature

\[R=R_0(1+\alpha\theta)\]

Electric current in a resistance

\[I=\frac{V_2-V_1}R\]

Current flows from the point at higher potential to the point at lower potential.

5. Electrical power and heat

\[P=VI\qquad\text{Energy}=\int P\,dt\]\[P=I^2R=VI=\frac{V^2}R\]\[H=VIt=I^2Rt=\frac{V^2}Rt\ \ (\text{joule})\qquad H=\frac{I^2RT}{4.2}\ \ (\text{calorie})\]

9. Kirchhoff's laws

Current law (junction law): \(\Sigma I_{in}=\Sigma I_{out}\)

Voltage law (loop law): \(\Sigma IR+\Sigma\text{EMF}=0\)

10. Combination of resistances

In series: \(R_{eq}\) is greater than any single resistor.

\[R=R_1+R_2+R_3+\cdots+R_n\qquad V=V_1+V_2+V_3+\cdots+V_n\]\[V_1=\frac{R_1}{R_1+R_2+\cdots+R_n}V,\quad V_2=\frac{R_2}{R_1+R_2+\cdots+R_n}V,\ \ldots\]

In parallel:

\[\frac1{R_{eq}}=\frac1{R_1}+\frac1{R_2}+\frac1{R_3}+\cdots\]

11. Wheatstone network (4-terminal network)

When the current through the galvanometer is zero (null or balance point):

\[\frac PQ=\frac RS\quad\text{i.e.}\quad PS=QR\]

13. Grouping of cells

13.1 Cells in series (write each emf with its polarity):

\[E_{eq}=E_1+E_2+\cdots+E_n\qquad r_{eq}=r_1+r_2+r_3+\cdots+r_n\]

13.2 Cells in parallel (use emf with polarity):

\[E_{eq}=\frac{\frac{\varepsilon_1}{r_1}+\frac{\varepsilon_2}{r_2}+\cdots+\frac{\varepsilon_n}{r_n}}{\frac1{r_1}+\frac1{r_2}+\cdots+\frac1{r_n}}\qquad\frac1{r_{eq}}=\frac1{r_1}+\frac1{r_2}+\cdots+\frac1{r_n}\]

15. Ammeter

A small shunt resistance \(S\) is connected in parallel with a galvanometer to convert it into an ammeter. An ideal ammeter has zero resistance. If the maximum current to be measured is \(I\), and \(I_G\) and \(R_G\) are the galvanometer's full-scale current and resistance:

\[I_G\,R_G=(I-I_G)\,S\qquad S=\frac{I_G\,R_G}{I-I_G}\qquad S\approx\frac{I_G\times R_G}{I}\ \ (I\gg I_G)\]

16. Voltmeter

A high resistance \(R_s\) is put in series with a galvanometer, to measure the potential difference across a resistor in a circuit. For the maximum potential difference:

\[V=I_G R_s+I_G R_G\qquad R_s=\frac V{I_G}-R_G\]

If \(R_G\ll R_s\), then \(R_s\approx\dfrac V{I_G}\).

17. Potentiometer

A cell of emf \(\varepsilon\) and internal resistance \(r\) drives a potentiometer wire \(AB\) of resistance \(R\) and length \(L\).

\[I=\frac\varepsilon{r+R}\qquad V_A-V_B=\frac\varepsilon{R+r}\cdot R\]

Potential gradient \(x\) is the potential difference per unit length of wire:

\[x=\frac{V_A-V_B}L=\frac\varepsilon{R+r}\cdot\frac RL\]

Applications of the potentiometer

(a) Compare the emfs of two cells. The balance lengths are \(\ell_1\) and \(\ell_2\):

\[\varepsilon_1=x\ell_1\quad\varepsilon_2=x\ell_2\quad\Rightarrow\quad\frac{\varepsilon_1}{\varepsilon_2}=\frac{\ell_1}{\ell_2}\]

If one emf is known, the other can be found. If \(x\) is known, both can be found.

(b) Find the current if the resistance is known:

\[V_A-V_C=x\ell_1\quad IR_1=x\ell_1\quad I=\frac{x\ell_1}{R_1}\]

\(R_2\) can be found in the same way. A potentiometer behaves as an ideal voltmeter because it draws no current from the circuit at the balance point.

(c) Find the internal resistance of a cell. First arrangement: \(\varepsilon'=x\ell_1\). Second arrangement, with a known resistance \(R\) across the cell: \(IR=x\ell_2\).

\[I=\frac{x\ell_2}R=\frac{\varepsilon'}{r'+R}\ \Rightarrow\ \frac{x\ell_1}{r'+R}=\frac{x\ell_2}R\ \Rightarrow\ r'=\left[\frac{\ell_1-\ell_2}{\ell_2}\right]R\]

(d) An ammeter and a voltmeter can be graduated using a potentiometer. (e) An ammeter and a voltmeter can be calibrated using a potentiometer.

18. Metre bridge (measures an unknown resistance)

If \(AB=\ell\) cm, then \(BC=(100-\ell)\) cm. Resistance of the wire is proportional to its length, since \(\rho\) and \(A\) are the same throughout: \(R=\sigma\ell\), where \(\sigma\) is the resistance per cm of wire.

\[P=\sigma\ell\quad(1)\qquad Q=\sigma(100-\ell)\quad(2)\qquad\frac PQ=\frac\ell{100-\ell}\]

Applying the balanced Wheatstone bridge condition \(RQ=PX\):

\[X=R\frac QP=\frac{100-\ell}\ell R\]

Since \(R\) and \(\ell\) are known, \(X\) can be calculated.

Drift velocity, resistivity and shapes of resistors

\[I=nAev_d,\qquad\vec j=\frac IA\hat n=\sigma\vec E,\qquad v_d=\mu E=\frac{eE\tau}m,\qquad\rho=\frac m{ne^2\tau},\qquad R=\frac{\rho\ell}A=\frac V I\]
  • \(v_d\) is of the order \(10^{-4}\) m s\(^{-1}\) in a metal; the signal travels at nearly the speed of light because the field is set up through the wire almost instantly. \(\mu\) (mobility, m\(^2\)V\(^{-1}\)s\(^{-1}\)) \(=\dfrac{v_d}E\); \(\tau\) is the relaxation (mean collision) time. \(\sigma=\dfrac1\rho=ne\mu\).
  • Ohm’s law holds for a constant temperature: \(V\propto I\). Non-ohmic: diode, thermistor, filament lamp (the \(V\)–\(I\) curve bends as the filament heats up).
  • Stretching a wire (volume constant): if the length becomes \(n\) times, \(R'=n^2R\); if the radius is multiplied by \(k\), \(R'=\dfrac R{k^4}\); a wire cut into \(n\) equal pieces has each piece \(R/n\) and \(n\) pieces in parallel give \(\dfrac R{n^2}\). Percentage change for a small stretch: \(\dfrac{\Delta R}R\approx2\dfrac{\Delta\ell}\ell\).
  • Non-uniform conductors: \(R=\displaystyle\int\dfrac{\rho\,dx}{A(x)}\). A frustum of a cone (end radii \(r_1,r_2\), length \(\ell\)): \(R=\dfrac{\rho\ell}{\pi r_1r_2}\). Radial flow between coaxial cylinders (radii \(a\lt b\), length \(\ell\)): \(R=\dfrac{\rho}{2\pi\ell}\ln\dfrac ba\). Between concentric spheres: \(R=\dfrac\rho{4\pi}\left(\dfrac1a-\dfrac1b\right)\). A rod of linearly varying resistivity: integrate \(\rho(x)\).
  • Temperature dependence: \(R=R_0(1+\alpha\Delta T)\), \(\rho=\rho_0(1+\alpha\Delta T)\) (metals: \(\alpha\gt0\), small; semiconductors and insulators: \(\alpha\lt0\); alloys such as manganin and constantan: \(\alpha\approx0\)). The combined \(\alpha\) of two resistors in series is \(\alpha_{eq}=\dfrac{R_1\alpha_1+R_2\alpha_2}{R_1+R_2}\); \(\alpha_{eq}=0\) if \(R_1\alpha_1+R_2\alpha_2=0\).
  • Colour code of carbon resistors (first two bands digits, third the multiplier, fourth the tolerance): black 0, brown 1, red 2, orange 3, yellow 4, green 5, blue 6, violet 7, grey 8, white 9; gold \(\pm5\%\), silver \(\pm10\%\), none \(\pm20\%\).
  • Electron motion: the number of electrons crossing a section per second \(=\dfrac Ie\); \(\dfrac{dq}{dt}=I\); for \(I=I_0\sin\omega t\) the charge passed in time \(t\) is \(\int I\,dt\). Average current for time-varying \(I=kt\) over \(T\): \(\dfrac{kT}2\).
  • Conductor of uniform cross-section carrying a current: \(E=\dfrac V\ell\) is uniform; if the area varies, \(j\) and \(E\propto\dfrac1A\) but \(I\) is constant.

Cells: EMF, internal resistance and power transfer

\[I=\frac E{R+r},\qquad V_{\text{terminal}}=E-Ir\ (\text{discharging}),\quad V=E+Ir\ (\text{charging}),\qquad I_{\text{short}}=\frac Er\]
  • EMF is the work done per unit charge by the source in taking charge round the circuit (open-circuit terminal voltage); \(V\lt E\) when current is drawn from the cell; \(V=E\) when no current flows. The internal resistance \(r=\dfrac{E-V}I=R\left(\dfrac EV-1\right)\).
  • Power in the load: \(P=I^2R=\dfrac{E^2R}{(R+r)^2}\); it is maximum when \(R=r\) (maximum power transfer theorem): \(P_{\max}=\dfrac{E^2}{4r}\), efficiency \(\eta=\dfrac R{R+r}=50\%\). The efficiency of a source approaches 100 % only when \(R\gg r\).
  • Power supplied by the cell \(EI\); lost inside \(I^2r\); \(P_{\text{load}}=VI\). Two different loads \(R_1,R_2\) dissipate the same power if \(R_1R_2=r^2\).
  • Series: \(n\) cells (\(E,r\)): \(E_{eq}=nE\), \(r_{eq}=nr\). Parallel: \(E_{eq}=E\), \(r_{eq}=\dfrac rn\). Mixed (\(m\) parallel rows of \(n\) cells): \(E_{eq}=nE\), \(r_{eq}=\dfrac{nr}m\); \(I=\dfrac{nE}{R+nr/m}\), maximum for \(R=\dfrac{nr}m\). If a cell is connected in reverse in a series chain, its EMF subtracts, but its internal resistance still adds.
  • Two cells in parallel with EMFs \(E_1,E_2\) and internal resistances \(r_1,r_2\): \(E_{eq}=\dfrac{E_1/r_1+E_2/r_2}{1/r_1+1/r_2}\), \(r_{eq}=\dfrac{r_1r_2}{r_1+r_2}\); if opposing, the circulating current \(\dfrac{E_1-E_2}{r_1+r_2}\) flows with no external load.
  • Charging a battery of EMF \(E\) from a source \(V\) through resistance \(R\): \(I=\dfrac{V-E}{R+r}\); power wasted as heat \(I^2(R+r)\), stored (chemical) \(EI\).
  • A voltmeter of resistance \(R_V\) connected across a cell reads \(V=\dfrac{ER_V}{R_V+r}\); an ideal voltmeter reads \(E\).

Network analysis: rules, symmetry and standard equivalent resistances

  • Kirchhoff’s rules: junction rule \(\sum I_{in}=\sum I_{out}\) (charge conservation); loop rule \(\sum\Delta V=0\) round a closed loop (energy conservation). Sign conventions: crossing a resistor along the current, \(\Delta V=-IR\); against it, \(+IR\); crossing a cell from − to +, \(+E\).
  • Divider rules: two resistors in series, \(V_1=V\dfrac{R_1}{R_1+R_2}\); in parallel, \(I_1=I\dfrac{R_2}{R_1+R_2}\). Parallel equivalent of two: \(\dfrac{R_1R_2}{R_1+R_2}\); of \(n\) equal resistors \(\dfrac Rn\).
  • Symmetry: points that are at the same potential by symmetry can be joined (or the wire between them removed) without changing currents; a wire connecting equal potentials carries no current.
  • Wheatstone bridge: balanced when \(\dfrac PQ=\dfrac RS\) (no galvanometer current); the bridge is most sensitive when all four arms are of the same order. Unbalanced: use Kirchhoff’s rules or the star–delta transformation.
  • Star–delta: \(R_Y=\dfrac{R_aR_b}{R_a+R_b+R_c}\) (the star resistor at node 1 between two delta resistors \(R_a,R_b\) that meet there); \(R_\Delta=\dfrac{R_1R_2+R_2R_3+R_3R_1}{R_{\text{opposite}}}\). For equal resistors \(R_Y=\dfrac{R_\Delta}3\).
  • Standard results: cube of edge resistance \(R\) between adjacent corners \(\dfrac{7R}{12}\), face diagonal \(\dfrac{3R}4\), body diagonal \(\dfrac{5R}6\). Square of four equal resistors \(R\): between adjacent corners \(\dfrac{3R}4\), between opposite corners \(R\). Ring of total resistance \(R\): between two points subtending angle \(\theta\) at the centre \(\dfrac{R\theta(2\pi-\theta)}{4\pi^2}\) (diametrically opposite points \(\dfrac R4\)).
  • Infinite ladders: series arm \(a\) and shunt arm \(b\) repeating without end: \(R_{eq}=\dfrac{a+\sqrt{a^2+4ab}}2\) (for \(a=b=R\): \(R_{eq}=\dfrac{1+\sqrt5}2R\)). Infinite triangle or grid networks: apply symmetry and superposition.
  • Equivalent resistance by current injection: inject \(I\) at one terminal and remove it at the other, find \(V\) between them, \(R_{eq}=\dfrac VI\); for infinite lattices use superposition of the two injection patterns.
  • Steady-state rules with capacitors and inductors: a capacitor carries no current in steady state (open circuit) but initially behaves as a short circuit if uncharged; an inductor behaves as a short circuit in steady state and as an open circuit initially (see LR and RC circuits).
  • Combination of identical resistors: \(n\) resistors \(R\) give a minimum \(\dfrac Rn\) (all in parallel) and a maximum \(nR\) (all in series).

Heating effect, fuse, thermoelectricity and electrolysis

  • \(P=VI=I^2R=\dfrac{V^2}R\); energy \(=Pt\); 1 kWh \(=3.6\times10^6\) J. Heat \(H=I^2Rt\) (joule) \(=\dfrac{I^2Rt}{4.18}\) cal. A bulb rated \(V_0,P_0\) has \(R=\dfrac{V_0^2}{P_0}\) and rated current \(\dfrac{P_0}{V_0}\); at another voltage \(V\) its power is \(P_0\left(\dfrac V{V_0}\right)^2\) (if \(R\) is constant).
  • Bulbs in series carry the same current, so the one with larger \(R\) (lower wattage) glows brighter; in parallel they have the same voltage, so the one with larger wattage (smaller \(R\)) is brighter. Two bulbs \((V_0,P_1)\) and \((V_0,P_2)\) in series on a supply \(V_0\) share \(P=\dfrac{P_1P_2}{P_1+P_2}\).
  • Heater and water: \(Pt\,\eta=mc\Delta\theta\). Two coils in series: \(t=t_1+t_2\); in parallel: \(\dfrac1t=\dfrac1{t_1}+\dfrac1{t_2}\) (same supply, same heating).
  • Fuse wire: melts when the Joule heating exceeds the heat lost from its surface; \(\dfrac{I^2\rho\ell}{\pi r^2}=h\cdot2\pi r\ell\Rightarrow I_{\max}\propto r^{3/2}\); \(I_{\max}\) is independent of the length. The fuse wire should have a low melting point and high resistivity.
  • Thermoelectric effect: Seebeck effect — an EMF in a loop of two metals with their junctions at different temperatures, \(e=\alpha\theta+\tfrac12\beta\theta^2\) (\(\theta\) from the cold junction, in °C); neutral temperature \(\theta_n=-\dfrac\alpha\beta\); inversion temperature \(\theta_i=2\theta_n-\theta_{\text{cold}}\). Peltier effect: heat absorbed or evolved at a junction when a current flows (\(\Pi=\dfrac{dE}{dT}\,T\)); Thomson effect along a conductor with a temperature gradient. Thermocouple power \(\dfrac{de}{d\theta}=\alpha+\beta\theta\).
  • Electrolysis: Faraday’s first law \(m=ZIt=Zq\); second law \(\dfrac{m_1}{m_2}=\dfrac{E_1}{E_2}\) (equivalent weights) for the same charge; \(Z=\dfrac{M}{nF}\) with \(F=96500\) C mol\(^{-1}\) (the Faraday). The charge to deposit one mole of an ion of charge \(n\): \(nF\). Volume of gas liberated: from \(\dfrac{m}{M}\) moles at the given \(T\) and \(P\). Electrolytes obey Ohm’s law approximately; their resistance falls with temperature.
  • Electrical energy cost: units (kWh) \(=\dfrac{P(\text{watt})\times t(\text{hour})}{1000}\).

Galvanometer, ammeter, voltmeter and bridge measurements: more results

  • Moving-coil galvanometer: \(NIAB=C\theta\), so \(I=\dfrac C{NAB}\theta=k\theta\); current sensitivity \(\dfrac\theta I=\dfrac{NAB}C\); voltage sensitivity \(\dfrac\theta V=\dfrac{NAB}{CR_G}\). The deflection per unit current is increased by more turns, greater area, a stronger field, or a weaker torsional constant; increasing \(N\) increases \(R_G\), so the voltage sensitivity does not necessarily increase.
  • Ammeter (shunt \(S\)) from a galvanometer of resistance \(G\) and full-scale current \(I_g\) to read up to \(I\): \(S=\dfrac{I_gG}{I-I_g}\); resistance of the ammeter \(\dfrac{GS}{G+S}\). Voltmeter (series resistance \(R\)) for range \(V\): \(R=\dfrac V{I_g}-G\); resistance \(G+R\). Ideal ammeter has zero resistance; ideal voltmeter infinite resistance.
  • Errors in the ammeter–voltmeter method for \(R\): with the voltmeter directly across \(R\) the ammeter reads the sum of the currents, so \(R_{\text{meas}}=\dfrac{RR_V}{R+R_V}\lt R\) (good for low \(R\)); with the voltmeter across the ammeter and \(R\) in series, \(R_{\text{meas}}=R+R_A\gt R\) (good for high \(R\)).
  • Metre bridge: \(\dfrac RS=\dfrac\ell{100-\ell}\); the bridge is most accurate when the balance point is near the middle (\(\ell\approx50\) cm); end corrections \(\alpha,\beta\) are added to \(\ell\) and \(100-\ell\); interchange \(R\) and \(S\) to eliminate errors. Post-office box: same principle with ratio arms \(P,Q\).
  • Potentiometer: potential gradient \(k=\dfrac VL=\dfrac{I\rho}A\) (V m\(^{-1}\)); EMF comparison \(\dfrac{E_1}{E_2}=\dfrac{\ell_1}{\ell_2}\); internal resistance \(r=R\left(\dfrac{\ell_1}{\ell_2}-1\right)\) (\(\ell_1\) with the cell open, \(\ell_2\) with the cell shunted by \(R\)); sensitivity is increased by lowering the potential gradient (a long wire or a large series resistance). No current is drawn from the cell at balance, so it measures the true EMF; a voltmeter does not.
  • Series resistance in the potentiometer circuit: \(k=\dfrac{E}{(R_{wire}+R_h+r)}\cdot\dfrac{R_{wire}}{L}\) (\(R_h\) is the rheostat resistance in the driver circuit).
  • Ohmmeter: a galvanometer, cell and series resistance give full-scale deflection for zero measured resistance; deflection falls as \(R_x\) rises: \(I=\dfrac E{R_{int}+R_x}\).
18
Electricity & magnetism

Capacitance

1. Basics

\[q\propto V\ \Rightarrow\ q=CV\]

\(q\): charge on the positive plate. \(C\): capacitance. \(V\): potential difference between the positive and negative plates.

Energy stored:

\[U=\tfrac12CV^2=\frac{Q^2}{2C}=\frac{QV}2\]

Energy density:

\[\tfrac12\epsilon_0\epsilon_rE^2=\tfrac12\epsilon_0KE^2\qquad\text{(vacuum: }\tfrac12\epsilon_0E^2)\]

\(\epsilon_r\) is the relative permittivity of the medium, and \(K=\epsilon_r\) is the dielectric constant.

Parallel plate capacitor

\[C=\frac{\epsilon_0\epsilon_rA}d=\frac{K\epsilon_0A}d\]

\(A\): area of the plates. \(d\): distance between the plates (much smaller than the plate size).

Spherical capacitor

Isolated spherical conductor (hollow or solid):

\[C=4\pi\epsilon_0\epsilon_rR\]

Two concentric spheres of radii \(a\) and \(b\):

\[C=4\pi\epsilon_0\frac{ab}{b-a}\]

With a dielectric \(K_2\) between them:

\[C=\frac{4\pi\epsilon_0K_2\,ab}{b-a}\]

Cylindrical capacitor

For \(\ell\gg a,b\):

\[\text{Capacitance per unit length}=\frac{2\pi\epsilon_0}{\ln(b/a)}\ \text{F/m}\]

What capacitance depends on

  • Area of the plates
  • Distance between the plates
  • Dielectric medium between the plates

Field and force on a plate

Field between the plates:

\[E=\frac\sigma{\epsilon_0}=\frac Vd\]

\(\sigma\) is the surface charge density.

Force experienced by either plate:

\[F=\frac{q^2}{2A\epsilon_0}\]

2. Distribution of charge on connecting two charged capacitors

Two capacitors \(C_1\) and \(C_2\) with initial charges \(Q_1\), \(Q_2\) and potentials \(V_1\), \(V_2\) are connected (positive plates together).

(a) Common potential

\[V=\frac{C_1V_1+C_2V_2}{C_1+C_2}=\frac{\text{total charge}}{\text{total capacitance}}\]

(b) Final charges

\[Q_1'=C_1V=\frac{C_1}{C_1+C_2}(Q_1+Q_2)\qquad Q_2'=C_2V=\frac{C_2}{C_1+C_2}(Q_1+Q_2)\]

(c) Heat loss during redistribution

\[\Delta H=U_i-U_f=\frac12\frac{C_1C_2}{C_1+C_2}(V_1-V_2)^2\]

The energy is lost as Joule heating in the connecting wire.

3(i). Series combination

\[\frac1{C_{eq}}=\frac1{C_1}+\frac1{C_2}+\frac1{C_3}\]\[V_1:V_2:V_3=\frac1{C_1}:\frac1{C_2}:\frac1{C_3}\]

3(ii). Parallel combination

\[C_{eq}=C_1+C_2+C_3\]\[Q_1:Q_2:Q_3=C_1:C_2:C_3\]

4. Charging and discharging of a capacitor

(i) Charging (initially uncharged), through a resistor \(R\) from a battery \(V\):

\[q=q_0\left(1-e^{-t/\tau}\right)\qquad I=\frac{q_0}\tau e^{-t/\tau}=\frac VRe^{-t/\tau}\]

\(q_0=CV\) is the charge at steady state, and \(\tau=CR_{eq}\) is the time constant. At \(t=\tau\) the charge reaches \(0.63\,q_0\).

(ii) Discharging

\[q=q_0e^{-t/\tau}\qquad I=\frac{q_0}\tau e^{-t/\tau}\]

\(q_0\) is the initial charge. At \(t=\tau\) the charge falls to \(0.37\,q_0\).

5. Capacitor with a dielectric

(i) Capacitance in the presence of a dielectric:

\[C=\frac{K\epsilon_0A}d=KC_0\]

\(C_0\) is the capacitance without the dielectric.

(ii) Field inside the dielectric:

\[E_{in}=E-E_{ind}=\frac\sigma{\epsilon_0}-\frac{\sigma_b}{\epsilon_0}=\frac\sigma{K\epsilon_0}=\frac Vd\]

\(E=\sigma/\epsilon_0\) is the field without the dielectric; \(E_{ind}\) is due to the induced (bound) charge density \(\sigma_b\).

(iii) Bound charge density:

\[\sigma_b=\sigma\left(1-\frac1K\right)\]

6. Force on a dielectric

(i) Battery connected (plate width \(b\), separation \(d\)):

\[F=\frac{\epsilon_0b(K-1)V^2}{2d}\]

(ii) Battery not connected, with the dielectric inserted a distance \(x\):

\[F=\frac{Q^2}{2C^2}\frac{dC}{dx}\]

The force on the dielectric is zero when it is fully inside.

Energy in capacitors, redistribution of charge and heat loss

\[C=\frac QV,\qquad U=\tfrac12CV^2=\frac{Q^2}{2C}=\tfrac12QV,\qquad u=\tfrac12\epsilon_0E^2\ (\text{energy density})\]
  • A capacitor stores energy in the electric field between its plates (the energy \(\tfrac12\epsilon_0E^2\) per unit volume times the volume \(Ad\)). The work done by a battery in charging is \(QV=CV^2\): half is stored, half is dissipated as heat in the resistance (independent of \(R\)); the efficiency of charging from a constant source is \(50\%\).
  • Two capacitors \(C_1,C_2\) charged to \(V_1,V_2\) and connected in parallel (like plates joined): common potential \(V=\dfrac{C_1V_1+C_2V_2}{C_1+C_2}\); the energy lost \(=\dfrac12\dfrac{C_1C_2}{C_1+C_2}(V_1-V_2)^2\) (heat or radiation). Connected with opposite polarity (positive to negative): \(V=\dfrac{C_1V_1-C_2V_2}{C_1+C_2}\), energy lost \(\dfrac12\dfrac{C_1C_2}{C_1+C_2}(V_1+V_2)^2\).
  • A charged capacitor (\(C_1\), charged to \(V\)) connected to an uncharged \(C_2\): \(V'=\dfrac{C_1V}{C_1+C_2}\); the energy falls to \(\dfrac{C_1}{C_1+C_2}\) of the original; the energy lost \(=\dfrac12\dfrac{C_1C_2}{C_1+C_2}V^2\).
  • Series: \(Q\) the same on each capacitor, \(V\) divides inversely as \(C\): \(V_1=V\dfrac{C_2}{C_1+C_2}\); energy divides in the inverse ratio of \(C\) (\(\dfrac{U_1}{U_2}=\dfrac{C_2}{C_1}\)); in parallel \(V\) the same, \(Q\propto C\), \(U\propto C\). The maximum voltage of a series bank is limited by the capacitor with the smallest \(C\) (it takes the most voltage).
  • Work done by an external agent in charging from \(Q_1\) to \(Q_2\): \(\dfrac{Q_2^2-Q_1^2}{2C}\); charge on an isolated spherical conductor of radius \(R\): \(C=4\pi\epsilon_0R\) (the Earth: \(\approx711\ \mu\)F). Capacitance of two long parallel wires (radius \(a\), separation \(d\gg a\)) per unit length: \(\dfrac{\pi\epsilon_0}{\ln(d/a)}\).
  • The energy stored in the field of a charged conducting sphere outside radius \(r\gt R\): \(\dfrac{KQ^2}{2r}\) (the total \(\dfrac{KQ^2}{2R}=\tfrac12CV^2\) with \(C=4\pi\epsilon_0R\)).
  • The force of attraction between plates of a parallel-plate capacitor: \(F=\dfrac{Q^2}{2\epsilon_0A}=\dfrac{\epsilon_0AV^2}{2d^2}=\dfrac{QE}2\) (\(E\) is the field between the plates; the field due to the other plate alone is \(\dfrac E2\)).

Dielectrics, conducting slabs and partially filled capacitors

\[C=\frac{K\epsilon_0A}d,\qquad E=\frac{E_0}K=\frac\sigma{K\epsilon_0},\qquad\sigma_b=\sigma\left(1-\frac1K\right)\ (\text{induced surface charge density})\]
QuantityBattery disconnected (\(Q\) constant)Battery connected (\(V\) constant)
Capacitance \(C\)\(\to KC\)\(\to KC\)
Charge \(Q\)unchanged\(\to KQ\)
Potential \(V\)\(\to V/K\)unchanged
Field \(E\)\(\to E/K\)unchanged
Energy \(U\)\(\to U/K\) (the field pulls the slab in)\(\to KU\); battery does \(CV^2(K-1)\), half is stored
  • Slab of dielectric constant \(K\) and thickness \(t\) in a gap \(d\) (area \(A\)): \(C=\dfrac{\epsilon_0A}{d-t+t/K}\); the effective separation reduces by \(t\left(1-\dfrac1K\right)\). A conducting slab of thickness \(t\) (\(K\to\infty\)): \(C=\dfrac{\epsilon_0A}{d-t}\), independent of its position.
  • Layers in series (thicknesses \(t_i\), constants \(K_i\), same area): \(C=\dfrac{\epsilon_0A}{\sum t_i/K_i}\); the electric displacement \(D=\epsilon_0KE\) is the same in each layer, so \(E_i=\dfrac{\sigma}{\epsilon_0K_i}\) and the voltage across layer \(i\) is \(V_i=\dfrac{\sigma t_i}{\epsilon_0K_i}\).
  • Side-by-side dielectrics (areas \(A_1,A_2\), same gap): parallel combination \(C=\dfrac{\epsilon_0(K_1A_1+K_2A_2)}d\); the field is the same (\(E=V/d\)) in each, the charge densities differ.
  • The dielectric strength is the maximum field before breakdown; the maximum voltage across a capacitor \(V_{\max}=E_{\max}d\) (or across layers, the layer with the smallest \(E_{\max}t\) limits).
  • If a dielectric slab is partially inserted (length \(x\) of total length \(\ell\)) the capacitor behaves as two capacitors in parallel: \(C=\dfrac{\epsilon_0w}d\left[\ell+(K-1)x\right]\); the force pulling the slab in (battery connected) \(F=\dfrac{V^2}2\dfrac{dC}{dx}=\dfrac{\epsilon_0w(K-1)V^2}{2d}\), independent of \(x\).
  • An uncharged dielectric is attracted toward the region of strong field (into the capacitor); the energy of the system falls if \(Q\) is fixed.
  • Polarisation: polar molecules align (permanent dipoles); non-polar molecules acquire induced dipoles; the dielectric constant of a conductor is \(\infty\) (the field inside is zero). \(\chi_e=K-1\).

Moving plates and work done

  • Isolated capacitor (\(Q\) fixed) with the separation increased from \(d\) to \(d'\): \(C'=\dfrac{\epsilon_0A}{d'}\); \(V\) rises; the field \(E=\dfrac Q{\epsilon_0A}\) is unchanged; the work done by the external agent \(=\Delta U=\dfrac{Q^2(d'-d)}{2\epsilon_0A}=F\Delta d\).
  • Battery connected (\(V\) fixed) with the separation increased from \(d\) to \(d'\): \(Q\) falls (charge returns to the battery), \(E=\dfrac Vd\) falls; the energy stored falls by \(\tfrac12V^2(C-C')\). The battery does the work \(W_b=V\Delta Q=V^2(C'-C)\) (negative: it takes charge back), so the external agent does \(W_{\text{ext}}=\Delta U-W_b=\tfrac12V^2(C-C')\), which is positive.
  • If a metallic sheet of negligible thickness is inserted midway between the plates, \(C\) is unchanged (it is two capacitors in series, each \(2C\)); a thick sheet of thickness \(t\) raises \(C\) to \(\dfrac{\epsilon_0A}{d-t}\).
  • Capacitors connected so that plates are alternately joined (\(n\) plates of area \(A\) with gap \(d\), alternate plates joined): \(C=\dfrac{(n-1)\epsilon_0A}d\).
  • Spacing change at an angle: the capacitance of two plates making a small angle \(\theta\) (separation \(d\) at one edge, width \(w\), length \(\ell\)): \(C\approx\dfrac{\epsilon_0w}\theta\ln\left(1+\dfrac{\ell\theta}d\right)\).
  • Plates of different potentials: two parallel conducting plates with potentials \(V_1,V_2\): the field between \(=\dfrac{V_1-V_2}d\); charge on the inner faces \(\pm\epsilon_0A\dfrac{V_1-V_2}d\).

Capacitor networks and RC circuits

  • Ladder of capacitors (series arm \(C\), shunt arm \(C\), infinite): \(C_{eq}=\dfrac{\sqrt5-1}2C\). For a balanced bridge (\(\dfrac{C_1}{C_2}=\dfrac{C_3}{C_4}\)) the middle capacitor carries no charge and may be removed. Use symmetry: points at equal potential can be joined.
  • Charge conservation rule: for an isolated set of plates (nodes not connected to a battery) the algebraic sum of the charges on the plates connected to the node is conserved; combine with \(\sum\Delta V=0\) around each loop (\(V=\dfrac QC\) for each capacitor) to solve networks.
  • RC charging through \(R\) from a source \(E\): \(q=CE\left(1-e^{-t/RC}\right)\), \(V_C=E(1-e^{-t/\tau})\), \(I=\dfrac ER\,e^{-t/\tau}\), \(\tau=RC\). At \(t=\tau\) the charge reaches \(63\%\) of the final value; after \(0.693\,\tau\) half. The time for \(V_C\) to reach a fraction \(f\) of \(E\): \(t=-\tau\ln(1-f)\).
  • RC discharge: \(q=Q_0e^{-t/RC}\), \(I=\dfrac{Q_0}{RC}e^{-t/RC}\) (magnitude); after \(\tau\) the charge is \(37\%\) of \(Q_0\); the energy falls as \(U=U_0e^{-2t/RC}\) (half-life in energy \(0.347\,\tau\)); all of \(\tfrac12CV^2\) is dissipated in \(R\).
  • General transient: \(x(t)=x_\infty+(x_0-x_\infty)e^{-t/\tau}\) for any quantity (voltage across the capacitor, charge) in a first-order circuit. If the capacitor is charged by a source \(E\) with an initial charge \(q_0\): \(q=CE+(q_0-CE)e^{-t/\tau}\). With resistors \(R_1\) (series) and \(R_2\) (parallel with capacitor) the effective \(\tau=\dfrac{R_1R_2}{R_1+R_2}C\) (Thévenin resistance seen by the capacitor).
  • At \(t=0\) an uncharged capacitor acts as a short circuit; at \(t\to\infty\) it acts as an open circuit (no current), and the voltage across it equals the potential difference across the branch it is connected to.
  • Energy supplied by the source during charging: \(CE^2\); stored \(\tfrac12CE^2\); heat in the resistor \(\int I^2R\,dt=\tfrac12CE^2\).
  • The time constant of a capacitor discharging through a conductor of resistivity \(\rho\) and a dielectric of permittivity \(\epsilon\): \(\tau=\rho\epsilon\) (independent of geometry), e.g. leakage through the dielectric: \(\tau=\epsilon_0K\rho\).
19
Electricity & magnetism

Alternating Current

1. AC and DC current

A current that changes its direction periodically is called alternating current (AC). If a current keeps its direction constant it is called direct current (DC). A constant DC, a periodic DC and a variable (decaying) DC all keep one direction. An AC, whether sinusoidal or a square wave, reverses direction.

3. Root mean square value

The root mean square value of a function \(f\), from \(t_1\) to \(t_2\):

\[f_{rms}=\sqrt{\frac{\int_{t_1}^{t_2}f^2\,dt}{t_2-t_1}}\]

4. Power consumed or supplied in an AC circuit

Average power consumed in a cycle:

\[\langle P\rangle=\frac{\int_0^{2\pi/\omega}P\,dt}{2\pi/\omega}=\tfrac12V_mI_m\cos\phi=\frac{V_m}{\sqrt2}\cdot\frac{I_m}{\sqrt2}\cos\phi=V_{rms}I_{rms}\cos\phi\]

Here \(\cos\phi\) is called the power factor.

5. Some definitions

  • \(\cos\phi\) is the power factor; \(I_m\sin\phi\) is the wattless current.
  • Impedance: \(Z=\dfrac{V_m}{I_m}=\dfrac{V_{rms}}{I_{rms}}\)
  • \(\omega L\) is the inductive reactance, denoted \(X_L\).
  • \(\dfrac1{\omega C}\) is the capacitive reactance, denoted \(X_C\).

6. Purely resistive circuit

Source \(V_s=V_m\sin\omega t\):

\[I=\frac{V_s}R=\frac{V_m\sin\omega t}R=I_m\sin\omega t\]\[I_m=\frac{V_m}R\qquad I_{rms}=\frac{V_{rms}}R\qquad\langle P\rangle=V_{rms}I_{rms}\cos\phi=\frac{V_{rms}^2}R\]

7. Purely capacitive circuit

Source \(V_s=V_m\sin\omega t\):

\[I=\frac{V_m}{1/\omega C}\cos\omega t=\frac{V_m}{X_C}\cos\omega t=I_m\cos\omega t\qquad X_C=\frac1{\omega C}\]

The current leads the capacitor voltage by \(\pi/2\). In a phasor diagram, \(I_m\) is drawn \(90^\circ\) ahead of \(V_m\). Since \(\phi=90^\circ\):

\[\langle P\rangle=V_{rms}I_{rms}\cos\phi=0\]

Sinusoidal AC: peak, rms, average and other waveforms

\[V=V_m\sin\omega t,\quad I=I_m\sin(\omega t\pm\phi),\quad\omega=2\pi f=\frac{2\pi}T,\quad I_{rms}=\frac{I_m}{\sqrt2}=0.707I_m,\quad V_{rms}=\frac{V_m}{\sqrt2}\]
  • Mean value over a half cycle \(I_{av}=\dfrac{2I_m}\pi=0.637I_m=0.9I_{rms}\); over a full cycle it is zero. Form factor \(\dfrac{I_{rms}}{I_{av}}=\dfrac\pi{2\sqrt2}=1.11\). Peak-to-peak \(=2I_m\).
  • The 220 V (rms) mains has \(V_m=311\) V and, at 50 Hz, \(\omega=314\) rad s\(^{-1}\) and period 20 ms; the current is zero 100 times per second. AC meters (hot-wire, moving-iron) read rms; a moving-coil meter responds to the average (zero for pure AC). The heating effect is \(H=I_{rms}^2Rt\); the average power in a resistor \(\dfrac{V_{rms}^2}R\).
  • Charge passing in half a cycle: \(\displaystyle\int_0^{T/2}I_m\sin\omega t\,dt=\dfrac{2I_m}\omega=\dfrac{I_mT}\pi\). Time for \(I\) to go from \(0\) to \(\dfrac{I_m}2\): \(\dfrac T{12}\); from \(0\) to \(I_{rms}\): \(\dfrac T8\); from \(0\) to \(I_m\): \(\dfrac T4\).
  • Other waveforms (peak \(I_m\)): square wave (\(\pm I_m\)): \(I_{rms}=I_m\); triangular or sawtooth: \(I_{rms}=\dfrac{I_m}{\sqrt3}\), mean over half \(=\dfrac{I_m}2\); half-wave rectified sine: \(I_{rms}=\dfrac{I_m}2\), \(I_{av}=\dfrac{I_m}\pi\); full-wave rectified: \(I_{rms}=\dfrac{I_m}{\sqrt2}\), \(I_{av}=\dfrac{2I_m}\pi\).
  • DC with a superposed AC: \(I=I_1+I_2\sin\omega t\): \(I_{rms}=\sqrt{I_1^2+\tfrac12I_2^2}\). For \(I=I_1\sin\omega t+I_2\cos\omega t\): amplitude \(\sqrt{I_1^2+I_2^2}\), \(I_{rms}=\sqrt{\tfrac12(I_1^2+I_2^2)}\). For two AC sources of different frequencies in series the rms is \(\sqrt{V_{1,rms}^2+V_{2,rms}^2}\).
  • AC generator: a coil of \(N\) turns, area \(A\), rotating at \(\omega\) in a field \(B\): \(\varepsilon=NBA\omega\sin\omega t\), \(\varepsilon_m=NBA\omega\); the emf is maximum when the plane of the coil is parallel to \(\vec B\) (flux zero) and zero when the plane is perpendicular to \(\vec B\).

Pure inductor, phasors and the phase of voltage across each element

\[V=V_m\sin\omega t:\quad\text{inductor}\ I=\frac{V_m}{\omega L}\sin\left(\omega t-\frac\pi2\right),\quad X_L=\omega L=2\pi fL;\qquad\text{capacitor}\ I=\frac{V_m}{X_C}\sin\left(\omega t+\frac\pi2\right),\quad X_C=\frac1{\omega C}\]
  • In a pure inductor the current lags the voltage by \(\dfrac\pi2\); in a pure capacitor it leads by \(\dfrac\pi2\); in a resistor they are in phase. (“CIVIL”: in a capacitor \(I\) leads \(V\); in an inductor \(V\) leads \(I\).) The average power in a pure \(L\) or pure \(C\) is zero; energy is exchanged between the source and the field (instantaneous power \(=\tfrac12V_mI_m\sin2\omega t\) in magnitude).
  • \(X_L\propto f\): zero for DC (an inductor passes DC) and large for high frequencies (a choke blocks AC). \(X_C\propto\dfrac1f\): infinite for DC (a capacitor blocks DC) and small at high frequencies (a bypass).
  • Phasor rules: draw \(\vec V_R\) along the current, \(\vec V_L\) \(90^\circ\) ahead, \(\vec V_C\) \(90^\circ\) behind; add as vectors. The instantaneous voltages add algebraically (Kirchhoff’s loop rule), the rms/peak voltages add as vectors.
  • Series elements share the same current (phasors rotate together); parallel elements share the same voltage (add the branch currents as phasors).
  • RC and LR filters: an RC circuit with output across \(C\) is a low-pass filter, across \(R\) high-pass; the cut-off frequency (gain \(\dfrac1{\sqrt2}\)) is \(\omega_c=\dfrac1{RC}\); for an LR circuit \(\omega_c=\dfrac RL\) (output across \(R\) is low-pass).

Series \(LR\), \(RC\) and \(LCR\) circuits

\[Z=\sqrt{R^2+(X_L-X_C)^2},\quad I_m=\frac{V_m}Z,\quad\tan\phi=\frac{X_L-X_C}R,\quad I=I_m\sin(\omega t-\phi),\quad\cos\phi=\frac RZ\]
  • \(V_m^2=V_{R}^2+(V_L-V_C)^2\) with \(V_R=I_mR\), \(V_L=I_mX_L\), \(V_C=I_mX_C\) (peak or rms values consistently). \(\phi\gt0\) (current lags, inductive circuit) if \(X_L\gt X_C\); \(\phi\lt0\) (current leads, capacitive circuit) if \(X_C\gt X_L\); \(\phi=0\) at resonance.
  • LR: \(Z=\sqrt{R^2+\omega^2L^2}\), \(\tan\phi=\dfrac{\omega L}R\), current lags. RC: \(Z=\sqrt{R^2+\dfrac1{\omega^2C^2}}\), \(\tan\phi=-\dfrac1{\omega CR}\), current leads. In a DC circuit \(\omega=0\): \(L\) is a short circuit and \(C\) an open circuit.
  • Complex form: \(\tilde Z=R+j(X_L-X_C)\); \(\tilde I=\dfrac{\tilde V}{\tilde Z}\); impedances of elements: \(R\), \(j\omega L\), \(\dfrac1{j\omega C}\) combine like resistances in series and parallel.
  • The voltage across the inductor or capacitor can exceed the supply voltage (e.g. at resonance \(V_L=V_C=QV\)).
  • Parallel \(LCR\): \(I_R=\dfrac V R\) (in phase), \(I_L=\dfrac V{X_L}\) (lags by \(90^\circ\)), \(I_C=\dfrac V{X_C}\) (leads by \(90^\circ\)): \(I=\sqrt{I_R^2+(I_C-I_L)^2}\), \(\tan\phi=\dfrac{I_C-I_L}{I_R}\) (\(\phi\gt0\): current leads). At \(\omega_0=\dfrac1{\sqrt{LC}}\) the currents in \(L\) and \(C\) cancel, so the line current is minimum and equal to \(\dfrac VR\) (anti-resonance: the impedance of the parallel circuit is maximum, \(=R\)). The impedance of a practical tank circuit (a coil of resistance \(r\) in parallel with \(C\)) at resonance is \(\dfrac L{rC}\) (maximum).
  • Capacitor and inductor in series with equal \(X_L=X_C\) behave as a short; with a source of fixed voltage the current is limited only by \(R\).
  • The reading of an AC voltmeter across \(L\) and across \(C\) in series is \(|V_L-V_C|\), not the sum.

Resonance and quality factor of the series \(LCR\) circuit

\[\omega_0=\frac1{\sqrt{LC}},\quad f_0=\frac1{2\pi\sqrt{LC}},\quad Z_{\min}=R,\quad I_{\max}=\frac VR,\quad Q=\frac{\omega_0L}R=\frac1{\omega_0CR}=\frac1R\sqrt{\frac LC}=\frac{\omega_0}{\Delta\omega}\]
  • At resonance \(X_L=X_C\), \(V_L=V_C=QV_R\) (in antiphase and cancelling), \(\phi=0\), power factor \(1\), average power \(P_{\max}=\dfrac{V_{rms}^2}R\).
  • Bandwidth: the half-power (\(\dfrac1{\sqrt2}I_{\max}\)) frequencies are where \(|X_L-X_C|=R\): \(\omega_{1,2}\) with \(\omega_2-\omega_1=\Delta\omega=\dfrac RL\); \(\omega_{1,2}\approx\omega_0\mp\dfrac R{2L}\). A higher \(Q\) means a sharper resonance (narrower bandwidth) and less damping (smaller \(R\)). Two frequencies \(\omega_1,\omega_2\) give the same current if \(\omega_1\omega_2=\omega_0^2\).
  • For \(f\lt f_0\) the circuit is capacitive (current leads); for \(f\gt f_0\) it is inductive (current lags).
  • Power at frequency \(\omega\): \(P=\dfrac{V_{rms}^2R}{R^2+(X_L-X_C)^2}\); at the half-power points it is half the maximum.
  • Applications: tuning of a radio or TV set (change \(C\) so that \(f_0\) matches the carrier frequency); \(Q\) of a tuning circuit \(\sim10^2\) gives selectivity. The energy stored oscillates between \(L\) and \(C\) at resonance, while the source only supplies the energy dissipated in \(R\).
  • Damping: the series \(LCR\) circuit is the electrical analogue of the damped, driven oscillator: \(L\leftrightarrow m\), \(R\leftrightarrow b\), \(\dfrac1C\leftrightarrow k\); the free (undriven) circuit oscillates at \(\omega'=\sqrt{\dfrac1{LC}-\dfrac{R^2}{4L^2}}\) with the charge amplitude decaying as \(e^{-Rt/2L}\) (critical damping \(R=2\sqrt{L/C}\)).

Power in AC circuits, power-factor correction, chokes and transmission

  • \(P_{av}=V_{rms}I_{rms}\cos\phi=I_{rms}^2R\) (real power, watts). Apparent power \(S=V_{rms}I_{rms}\) (VA); reactive power \(Q_r=V_{rms}I_{rms}\sin\phi\) (var); \(S^2=P^2+Q_r^2\). The wattless current is \(I_{rms}\sin\phi\). The power factor \(\cos\phi=\dfrac RZ=\dfrac P S\).
  • The instantaneous power \(p=VI=V_mI_m\sin\omega t\sin(\omega t-\phi)\); its average over a cycle is \(\tfrac12V_mI_m\cos\phi\).
  • Power-factor correction: to raise the power factor from \(\cos\phi_1\) to \(\cos\phi_2\) at constant real power \(P\), connect a capacitor in parallel with \(C=\dfrac{P(\tan\phi_1-\tan\phi_2)}{\omega V_{rms}^2}\). It reduces the line current and the line loss.
  • Choke coil: an inductor of small resistance limits the AC current in a tube light without dissipating much power (\(\cos\phi\approx0\)); a resistor (rheostat) would waste power as heat. A choke does not work for DC.
  • Transmission losses: delivering power \(P\) at voltage \(V\) with power factor \(\cos\phi\) needs \(I=\dfrac P{V\cos\phi}\); line loss \(I^2R_{line}\propto\dfrac1{V^2}\). Stepping the voltage up by a factor \(n\) reduces the loss by \(n^2\); hence the use of transformers (high-voltage transmission).
  • Ideal transformer: \(\dfrac{V_s}{V_p}=\dfrac{N_s}{N_p}=\dfrac{I_p}{I_s}\), \(V_pI_p=V_sI_s\); a step-up transformer has \(N_s\gt N_p\), a step-down has \(N_s\lt N_p\). Efficiency \(=\dfrac{P_{out}}{P_{in}}\); losses: copper (\(I^2R\)), iron (eddy currents, hysteresis), flux leakage and humming.
  • An AC supply of frequency \(f\) heats a resistor identically to a DC of the same value as \(I_{rms}\).
  • The average power consumed in a series \(LCR\) circuit is the same as the power in \(R\) alone; the inductor and capacitor store energy and return it.
20
Electricity & magnetism

Magnetic Effect of Current & Magnetic Force on Charge

1. Field due to a moving point charge

\[\vec B=\frac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\cdot\frac{q(\vec v\times\vec r)}{r^3}\]

2. Biot-Savart law

\[d\vec B=\frac{\mu_0I}{4\pi}\left(\frac{d\vec\ell\times\vec r}{r^3}\right)\]

3. Field due to a straight wire

At a point \(P\) at distance \(r\), with the wire subtending angles \(\theta_1\) and \(\theta_2\):

\[B=\frac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\frac Ir(\sin\theta_1+\sin\theta_2)\]

4. Field due to an infinite straight wire

\[B=\frac{\mu_0}{2\pi}\frac Ir\]

5. Field due to a circular loop

(i) At the centre (\(N\) turns, radius \(r\)):

\[B=\frac{\mu_0NI}{2r}\]

(ii) On the axis at distance \(x\) (radius \(R\)):

\[B=\frac{\mu_0}2\cdot\frac{NIR^2}{(R^2+x^2)^{3/2}}\]

6. Field on the axis of a solenoid

\[B=\frac{\mu_0nI}2(\cos\theta_1-\cos\theta_2)\]

7. Ampere's law

\[\oint\vec B\cdot d\vec\ell=\mu_0I\]

8. Field due to a long cylindrical shell

\[B=0,\quad r\lt R\]\[B=\frac{\mu_0}{2\pi}\frac Ir,\quad r\ge R\]

9. Magnetic force on a moving point charge

\[\vec F=q(\vec v\times\vec B)\]

(i) \(\vec v\perp\vec B\) (circular path):

\[r=\frac{mv}{qB}\qquad T=\frac{2\pi m}{qB}\]

(ii) \(\vec v\) at angle \(\theta\) to \(\vec B\) (helical path):

\[r=\frac{mv\sin\theta}{qB}\qquad T=\frac{2\pi m}{qB}\qquad\text{Pitch}=\frac{2\pi mv\cos\theta}{qB}\]

In both fields:

\[\vec F=q\big[(\vec v\times\vec B)+\vec E\big]\]

10. Force on a current-carrying wire

\[\vec F=I(\vec\ell\times\vec B)\]

11. Magnetic moment of a current loop

\[M=N\cdot I\cdot A\]

12. Torque on a loop

\[\vec\tau=\vec M\times\vec B\]

13. Field due to a single pole

\[B=\frac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\cdot\frac m{r^2}\]

14. Field on the axis of a magnet

\[B=\frac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\cdot\frac{2M}{r^3}\]

15. Field on the equatorial line of a magnet

\[B=\frac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\cdot\frac M{r^3}\]

16. Field at a point \(P\) due to a magnet

\(P\) is at distance \(r\), at angle \(\theta\) from the axis:

\[B=\frac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\frac M{r^3}\sqrt{1+3\cos^2\theta}\]

Magnetic fields of common current distributions

SourceField \(B\)
Long straight wire, distance \(r\)\(\dfrac{\mu_0I}{2\pi r}\) (circles round the wire; right-hand rule)
Semi-infinite wire, at a point on the perpendicular through its end\(\dfrac{\mu_0I}{4\pi r}\)
Finite wire (angles \(\theta_1,\theta_2\) from the perpendicular)\(\dfrac{\mu_0I}{4\pi r}(\sin\theta_1+\sin\theta_2)\)
Point on the line of a straight wire (on its axis)\(0\)
Arc of a circle subtending angle \(\theta\) (rad) at the centre\(\dfrac{\mu_0I\theta}{4\pi R}\) (semicircle \(\dfrac{\mu_0I}{4R}\); full loop \(\dfrac{\mu_0I}{2R}\))
Square loop (side \(a\)) at the centre\(\dfrac{2\sqrt2\mu_0I}{\pi a}\)
Regular \(n\)-sided polygon inscribed in a circle of radius \(R\), at the centre\(\dfrac{\mu_0nI}{2\pi R}\tan\dfrac\pi n\) (\(\to\dfrac{\mu_0I}{2R}\) as \(n\to\infty\))
Circular loop, on the axis at distance \(x\) (\(N\) turns)\(\dfrac{\mu_0NIR^2}{2(R^2+x^2)^{3/2}}\); at large \(x\): \(\dfrac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\dfrac{2M}{x^3}\)
Helmholtz coils (two coaxial coils of radius \(R\), separation \(R\)), at the midpoint\(\left(\dfrac45\right)^{3/2}\dfrac{\mu_0NI}R=0.716\dfrac{\mu_0NI}R\) (nearly uniform field)
Long solenoid (\(n\) turns per unit length): inside / at the end\(\mu_0nI\) / \(\tfrac12\mu_0nI\); outside \(\approx0\)
Toroid (\(N\) turns, mean radius \(r\))\(\dfrac{\mu_0NI}{2\pi r}\) inside the core, \(0\) outside
Infinite current sheet (current per unit width \(K\))\(\dfrac{\mu_0K}2\) on either side, opposite directions
Long solid cylinder (radius \(R\), uniform current)\(\dfrac{\mu_0Ir}{2\pi R^2}\) inside; \(\dfrac{\mu_0I}{2\pi r}\) outside
Coaxial cable (inner current \(I\), outer \(-I\))between the conductors \(\dfrac{\mu_0I}{2\pi r}\); outside \(0\)
Cylinder with an off-axis cylindrical cavity (current density \(J\), axes separated by \(\vec d\))uniform in the cavity: \(B=\dfrac{\mu_0Jd}2\)
Rotating charged ring (\(q\), \(R\), \(\omega\)) at the centre\(\dfrac{\mu_0q\omega}{4\pi R}\) (\(I=\dfrac{q\omega}{2\pi}\))
Rotating charged disc (\(\sigma\)) at the centre\(\dfrac{\mu_0\sigma\omega R}2\)
  • Right-hand grip rule: thumb along the current, fingers curl in the direction of \(\vec B\) around a wire; for a loop, the curled fingers give the current and the thumb the field on the axis. \(\mu_0=4\pi\times10^{-7}\) T m A\(^{-1}\); 1 T \(=10^4\) gauss.
  • Ampère’s circuital law: \(\oint\vec B\cdot d\vec\ell=\mu_0I_{\text{enclosed}}\) (sign from the right-hand rule); use it when the field has symmetry (infinite wire, solenoid, toroid, sheet). \(\oint\vec B\cdot d\vec S=0\) always (no magnetic monopoles).
  • Biot–Savart superposition: \(\vec B=\dfrac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\displaystyle\int\dfrac{I\,d\vec\ell\times\hat r}{r^2}\); for a wire in the shape of a loop with straight and curved parts, add the contributions (a straight part pointing at the point gives zero).
  • Moving charge: \(B=\dfrac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\dfrac{qv\sin\theta}{r^2}\). At a point perpendicular to the velocity, \(\dfrac BE=\mu_0\epsilon_0v=\dfrac v{c^2}\) (since \(\mu_0\epsilon_0=\dfrac1{c^2}\)).
  • Two coaxial loops carrying currents in the same direction attract; for opposite directions repel. Field at the centre of two concentric coils in the same plane: \(\dfrac{\mu_0}2\left(\dfrac{N_1I_1}{R_1}\pm\dfrac{N_2I_2}{R_2}\right)\); for perpendicular coils the field is the vector sum \(\sqrt{B_1^2+B_2^2}\).

Charged particles in magnetic and electric fields

\[\vec F=q(\vec E+\vec v\times\vec B),\qquad r=\frac{mv}{qB}=\frac{p}{qB}=\frac{\sqrt{2mK}}{qB}=\frac1B\sqrt{\frac{2mV}q},\qquad T=\frac{2\pi m}{qB},\qquad f=\frac{qB}{2\pi m},\qquad\omega=\frac{qB}m\]
  • The magnetic force is perpendicular to \(\vec v\): it does no work and does not change the speed or kinetic energy; it changes only direction. \(T\) (and \(\omega\)) are independent of speed and radius. The sense of rotation: a positive charge circles clockwise when \(\vec B\) points toward the viewer (out of the page) and anticlockwise when it points away.
  • Helical motion (velocity at angle \(\theta\) to \(\vec B\)): radius \(r=\dfrac{mv\sin\theta}{qB}\), pitch \(p=v\cos\theta\,T=\dfrac{2\pi mv\cos\theta}{qB}\). A charge moving along \(\vec B\) feels no force.
  • Same accelerating voltage: \(r\propto\sqrt{\dfrac mq}\) (proton : deuteron : \(\alpha\) \(=1:\sqrt2:\sqrt2\)). Same speed: \(r\propto\dfrac mq\); same momentum: \(r\propto\dfrac1q\); same kinetic energy: \(r\propto\dfrac{\sqrt m}q\). Time period depends only on \(\dfrac mq\).
  • Entering a region of width \(d\) perpendicular to its boundary: it emerges after deflection \(\theta\) with \(\sin\theta=\dfrac dr=\dfrac{qBd}{mv}\) if \(d\lt r\); it is turned back (semicircle) if \(d\ge r\). Time spent \(=\dfrac{\theta m}{qB}\). Entering a half-space at angle \(\alpha\) with the boundary: exit at distance \(2r\sin\alpha\) along the boundary; the particle turns through \(2\alpha\) (or \(2\pi-2\alpha\) for the other sense).
  • Velocity selector (crossed \(E\) and \(B\)): \(qE=qvB\Rightarrow v=\dfrac EB\) (independent of \(q\) and \(m\)). Mass spectrometer: after acceleration through \(V\) the ions move on a semicircle in \(B\): \(m=\dfrac{qB^2r^2}{2V}\); the separation of two isotopes \(2(r_1-r_2)\).
  • Cyclotron: the oscillator frequency \(f=\dfrac{qB}{2\pi m}\); the maximum kinetic energy \(K_{\max}=\dfrac{q^2B^2R^2}{2m}\); the particle gains \(2qV\) per revolution; the number of turns \(=\dfrac{K_{\max}}{2qV}\). The cyclotron cannot accelerate electrons (their mass rises relativistically) or neutrons.
  • Crossed fields with a particle released from rest: the path is a cycloid; the drift speed \(\dfrac EB\) is perpendicular to both fields; the maximum displacement along \(\vec E\) is \(\dfrac{2mE}{qB^2}\); the maximum speed \(\dfrac{2E}B\). Parallel \(E\) and \(B\): helical motion with a pitch that grows with time.
  • Hall effect: a current \(I\) in a strip of thickness \(t\) (along \(\vec B\)) and carrier density \(n\): \(V_H=\dfrac{BI}{net}=Bv_dw\) (\(w\) the width); \(R_H=\dfrac1{ne}\); the sign of \(V_H\) shows whether the carriers are positive or negative (p-type or n-type semiconductor). Used to measure \(n\) and \(B\).
  • Thomson’s method for \(e/m\): balance the electric and magnetic forces (undeflected beam) to get \(v=\dfrac EB\); then, with \(B\) alone, the beam moves on a circle of radius \(r\) and \(\dfrac em=\dfrac v{Br}=\dfrac E{B^2r}\).
  • A charged particle spiralling toward a region of stronger field can be reflected (a magnetic mirror); this traps particles in the Van Allen belts and in magnetic bottles.

Forces on conductors, loops and magnets in a field

  • \(\vec F=I\vec\ell\times\vec B\) for a straight wire; for a curved wire in a uniform field \(\vec F=I\,\vec L_{AB}\times\vec B\), where \(\vec L_{AB}\) is the vector from the start to the end of the wire. Hence a closed loop in a uniform field has zero net force. A semicircular wire of radius \(R\) carrying \(I\) with its ends along the diameter: \(F=2BIR\) (perpendicular to the diameter).
  • Tension in a current-carrying ring (radius \(R\)) in a perpendicular field \(B\): \(T=BIR\).
  • Parallel wires: force per unit length \(\dfrac Fl=\dfrac{\mu_0I_1I_2}{2\pi d}\) — attraction for parallel currents, repulsion for antiparallel. Definition of the ampere: the current that gives \(2\times10^{-7}\) N m\(^{-1}\) between two long parallel wires 1 m apart.
  • Force on a rectangular loop (side \(a\) perpendicular and \(b\) parallel to a long wire carrying \(I_1\), nearest side at distance \(d\), loop current \(I_2\)): the net force \(=\dfrac{\mu_0I_1I_2b}{2\pi}\left(\dfrac1d-\dfrac1{d+a}\right)\) toward the wire for parallel currents in the near side.
  • Torque on a loop (\(N\) turns, area \(A\), current \(I\), angle \(\theta\) between \(\vec B\) and the normal): \(\vec\tau=\vec M\times\vec B\), \(\tau=NIAB\sin\theta\), magnetic moment \(M=NIA\) (direction by the right-hand rule). Potential energy \(U=-\vec M\cdot\vec B\); stable at \(\theta=0\), unstable at \(\theta=\pi\); work to turn from \(0\) to \(\pi\): \(2MB\). Small oscillations: \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{I_{\text{inertia}}}{MB}}\). In a non-uniform field the loop also feels a net force \(\vec F=\nabla(\vec M\cdot\vec B)\).
  • Moving-coil galvanometer (radial field): \(\tau=NIAB=C\theta\) so \(\theta\propto I\) and the scale is linear.
  • Rod on rails carrying current \(I\) in a field \(B\): force \(BI\ell\); with friction \(\mu\): it moves when \(BI\ell\gt\mu mg\). A wire of mass \(m\) suspended horizontally in a field is balanced when \(BI\ell=mg\). A current-carrying rod on an incline in a vertical field: \(BI\ell\cos\theta=mg\sin\theta\) for equilibrium.
  • Magnetic moment of charges in orbit: an electron in a circular orbit: \(M=IA=\dfrac{e\omega r^2}2=\dfrac{e}{2m}L\) (gyromagnetic ratio \(\dfrac e{2m}\)); the Bohr magneton \(\mu_B=\dfrac{eh}{4\pi m}=9.27\times10^{-24}\) A m\(^2\) (for \(L=\dfrac h{2\pi}\)). Rotating bodies of charge \(q\), mass \(m\): \(M=\dfrac q{2m}L\): ring \(\tfrac12q\omega R^2\), disc \(\tfrac14q\omega R^2\), thin spherical shell \(\tfrac13q\omega R^2\), solid sphere \(\tfrac15q\omega R^2\).
  • Two parallel beams of like charges moving in the same direction attract magnetically; the magnetic force is smaller than the electric repulsion by the factor \(\dfrac{v^2}{c^2}\).
21
Electricity & magnetism

Magnetism & Matter

Bar magnet as a magnetic dipole

  • A bar magnet of pole strength \(m\) (unit A m) and magnetic length \(2\ell\) (about \(\tfrac56\) of its geometric length) has magnetic dipole moment \(M=m\cdot2\ell\) (A m\(^2\) or J T\(^{-1}\)), directed from the south to the north pole. It is equivalent to a current loop or solenoid with \(M=NIA\). Poles always occur in pairs (isolated monopoles do not exist): \(\oint\vec B\cdot d\vec S=0\).
  • Coulomb’s law for poles: \(F=\dfrac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\dfrac{m_1m_2}{r^2}\); field of a pole \(B=\dfrac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\dfrac m{r^2}\).
  • Field of a short magnet (\(r\gg\ell\)): on the axis \(B=\dfrac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\dfrac{2M}{r^3}\); on the equatorial line \(B=\dfrac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\dfrac M{r^3}\) (opposite to \(\vec M\)); at \((r,\theta)\): \(B=\dfrac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\dfrac M{r^3}\sqrt{1+3\cos^2\theta}\); with \(\tan\phi=\dfrac12\tan\theta\) for the angle of the field with the radius. Exact fields for a magnet of half-length \(\ell\): axial \(\dfrac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\dfrac{2Mr}{(r^2-\ell^2)^2}\), equatorial \(\dfrac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\dfrac M{(r^2+\ell^2)^{3/2}}\).
  • In a uniform field \(B\): torque \(\tau=MB\sin\theta\); potential energy \(U=-MB\cos\theta\); work in turning from \(\theta_1\) to \(\theta_2\): \(MB(\cos\theta_1-\cos\theta_2)\); stable at \(\theta=0\); small oscillations \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac I{MB}}\), where \(I\) is the moment of inertia about the axis through the centre (bar magnet: \(I=\dfrac{W(L^2+b^2)}{12}\) for mass \(W\), length \(L\), breadth \(b\)). No net force in a uniform field.
  • Cutting and combining magnets: cut into \(n\) equal pieces across its length: pole strength unchanged, length \(\dfrac{1}{n}\), \(M'=\dfrac Mn\). Cut into \(n\) equal strips along the length: pole strength \(\dfrac mn\), length unchanged, \(M'=\dfrac Mn\).
