Bent into an arc of angle \(\phi\) (radius \(R=\dfrac{2\ell}\phi\)): new moment \(M'=m\cdot2R\sin\dfrac\phi2=\dfrac{2M\sin(\phi/2)}{\phi}\) (semicircle: \(\dfrac{2M}\pi\)). Two magnets with moments \(M_1,M_2\) fixed at angle \(\alpha\): resultant \(\sqrt{M_1^2+M_2^2+2M_1M_2\cos\alpha}\) (at right angles \(\sqrt{M_1^2+M_2^2}\); two identical magnets in the cross form: \(\sqrt2M\)).
  • Vibrating together (same centre, parallel or antiparallel): \(\dfrac{M_1}{M_2}=\dfrac{T_{d}^2+T_{s}^2}{T_{d}^2-T_{s}^2}\) where \(T_s\) and \(T_d\) are the periods with the magnets in the same and opposite senses (\(M_1\gt M_2\)).
  • Neutral points (where the magnet’s field cancels \(B_H\)): for a magnet with its north pole pointing north — on the equatorial line, \(\dfrac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\dfrac M{d^3}=B_H\) (approx.); for the north pole pointing south — on the axial line, \(\dfrac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\dfrac{2M}{d^3}=B_H\).
  • Field lines: closed loops (outside from N to S, inside from S to N), never intersect, denser where the field is stronger; the tangent gives the direction of \(\vec B\).

Earth’s magnetism and magnetometers

  • Elements of the Earth’s field: declination \(\alpha\) (angle between geographic and magnetic meridians), dip \(\delta\) (angle of the total field \(B_E\) with the horizontal), and the horizontal component \(B_H\). \(B_H=B_E\cos\delta\), \(B_V=B_E\sin\delta\), \(\tan\delta=\dfrac{B_V}{B_H}\), \(B_E=\sqrt{B_H^2+B_V^2}\). At the magnetic equator \(\delta=0\) (the needle is horizontal; \(B_V=0\)); at the magnetic poles \(\delta=90^\circ\) (\(B_H=0\)).
  • Apparent dip: in a vertical plane making angle \(\beta\) with the magnetic meridian: \(\tan\delta'=\dfrac{\tan\delta}{\cos\beta}\); in a plane perpendicular to the meridian \(\delta'=90^\circ\) (and a dip needle there stands vertical).
  • Magnitude of the Earth’s field about \(3\times10^{-5}\) T (equator) to \(6\times10^{-5}\) T (poles); \(B_H\approx3\times10^{-5}\) T in India; the Earth’s magnetic moment \(\approx8\times10^{22}\) A m\(^2\); the axis is inclined about \(11^\circ\) to the rotation axis; the magnetic south pole of the Earth’s dipole is near the geographic north pole.
  • Vibration magnetometer: a small magnet oscillating in the horizontal field: \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac I{MB_H}}\), so \(MB_H=\dfrac{4\pi^2I}{T^2}\). Comparing horizontal fields at two places with the same magnet: \(\dfrac{B_{H1}}{B_{H2}}=\dfrac{T_2^2}{T_1^2}\); with the same field, comparing magnets: \(\dfrac{M_1}{M_2}=\dfrac{I_1T_2^2}{I_2T_1^2}\).
  • Deflection magnetometer (tangent law): a magnet placed with its axis east–west at distance \(d\) deflects a compass needle through \(\theta\): Tan A (end-on position) \(B_H\tan\theta=\dfrac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\dfrac{2M}{d^3}\); Tan B (broadside position) \(B_H\tan\theta=\dfrac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\dfrac M{d^3}\). Then \(\dfrac M{B_H}=\dfrac{2\pi d^3\tan\theta}{\mu_0}\) (Tan A) and, with the vibration result, \(M\) and \(B_H\) are obtained separately.
  • Tangent galvanometer: \(B_{\text{coil}}=\dfrac{\mu_0NI}{2r}=B_H\tan\theta\), so \(I=K\tan\theta\) with the reduction factor \(K=\dfrac{2rB_H}{\mu_0N}\); the sensitivity is greatest near \(\theta=45^\circ\). The coil plane must lie in the magnetic meridian.
  • A freely suspended magnet aligns with the horizontal component of the Earth’s field (a compass needle). Magnetic compass is unreliable near the magnetic poles and near iron objects or currents.

Magnetisation, susceptibility and the three classes of magnetic materials

\[\vec B=\mu_0(\vec H+\vec M_z),\qquad\vec M_z=\chi\vec H,\qquad\mu_r=1+\chi,\qquad\vec B=\mu\vec H=\mu_0\mu_r\vec H\]
  • \(\vec H\) is the magnetising field (A m\(^{-1}\)), set by the free currents (\(H=nI\) in a solenoid or \(\dfrac{NI}{2\pi r}\) in a toroid); \(\vec M_z\) is the magnetisation = magnetic moment per unit volume (A m\(^{-1}\)); \(\chi\) susceptibility (dimensionless); \(\mu\) permeability (T m A\(^{-1}\) or H m\(^{-1}\)). A solenoid or toroid with a core: \(B=\mu_0\mu_rnI\). Magnetic energy density \(\tfrac12BH\).
DiamagneticParamagneticFerromagnetic
\(\chi\)small, negative (\(\sim-10^{-5}\))small, positive (\(10^{-5}\)–\(10^{-3}\))large, positive (\(10^2\)–\(10^5\))
\(\mu_r\)slightly less than 1slightly more than 1\(\gg1\)
In a non-uniform fieldmoves to the weaker field (repelled)moves to the stronger field (weakly attracted)strongly attracted
Atomic originno permanent moment; induced opposing momentspermanent moments, randomly oriented, align weaklypermanent moments aligned in domains
TemperatureindependentCurie law \(\chi=\dfrac CT\)becomes paramagnetic above the Curie temperature: \(\chi=\dfrac C{T-T_c}\)
ExamplesBi, Cu, Au, Si, water, NaCl, N\(_2\)Al, Na, Pt, Mn, O\(_2\), CuCl\(_2\)Fe, Co, Ni, Gd, alnico
  • A superconductor is a perfect diamagnet (\(\chi=-1\), \(\mu_r=0\)): the field is expelled from its interior (Meissner effect). Curie temperatures: Fe \(1043\) K, Co \(1388\) K, Ni \(627\) K.
  • A diamagnetic rod suspended in a field sets itself perpendicular to the field lines; a paramagnetic or ferromagnetic rod sets itself along them. A diamagnetic liquid in a U-tube between the poles of a magnet shows a depression in the limb in the stronger field (a paramagnetic liquid rises).
  • Domains: in a ferromagnet small regions (\(\sim10^{-6}\) m) are magnetised to saturation; an applied field enlarges the domains aligned with it. Heating above \(T_c\) destroys the domain structure.

Hysteresis, permanent magnets and electromagnets

  • Hysteresis loop (\(B\) versus \(H\) in a cycle): retentivity (remanence) is \(B\) when \(H=0\); coercivity is the reverse \(H\) needed to bring \(B\) to zero; the area of the loop is the energy dissipated per unit volume per cycle (heat). The magnetisation lags behind the field.
  • Soft iron: high permeability and saturation, low retentivity, low coercivity, narrow loop (small energy loss) — used in transformer and generator cores, electromagnets, relays and chokes. Steel and alnico: high retentivity and high coercivity, broad loop — used for permanent magnets (also cobalt steel, ticonal, ferrites). A good permanent magnet has high retentivity and high coercivity, so that it is hard to demagnetise.
  • Electromagnet: a solenoid with a soft-iron core: \(B=\mu_0\mu_rnI\) (the core multiplies the field about \(\mu_r\) times); the field can be switched off and controlled.
  • Magnetisation can be removed by heating above the Curie temperature, by hammering, or by an alternating field of decreasing amplitude (demagnetisation). Magnetic shielding: a soft-iron ring or shell diverts field lines around the enclosed region (high \(\mu_r\)).
  • Eddy-current and hysteresis losses in a transformer core are reduced by laminating the core with insulating varnish and using soft iron or silicon steel.
  • The field inside a bar magnet runs from south to north; \(\vec B\) lines are continuous; a bar magnet is equivalent to a solenoid of the same moment: \(M=\) (surface current per unit length) \(\times\) volume.
  • Atomic magnetism: an electron in orbit has \(\mu=\dfrac{e}{2m}L\) (orbital), Bohr magneton \(\mu_B=9.27\times10^{-24}\) A m\(^2\); the spin magnetic moment of the electron is about one Bohr magneton. In diamagnetic atoms the moments cancel in pairs.
22
Electricity & magnetism

Electromagnetic Induction

1. Magnetic flux

\[\phi=\int\vec B\cdot d\vec s\]

2. Faraday's laws of electromagnetic induction

\[E=-\frac{d\phi}{dt}\]

3. Lenz's law (conservation of energy)

The emf is induced in such a way that it opposes the cause that produced it.

4. Induced emf due to rotation

A conducting rod of length \(\ell\) rotating with angular speed \(\omega\) about one end, in a uniform perpendicular magnetic field \(B\):

\[E=\tfrac12B\omega\ell^2\]

EMF induced in a rotating disc

Between the centre and the edge of a disc of radius \(r\) rotating in a magnetic field \(B\):

\[E=\frac{B\omega r^2}2\]

5. Fixed loop in a varying magnetic field

If the field changes at rate \(\dfrac{dB}{dt}\), an electric field is generated. Its average tangential value along a circle of radius \(r\) is

\[E=\frac r2\frac{dB}{dt}\]

This electric field is non-conservative; its lines of force are closed curves.

6. Self induction

\[\varepsilon=-\frac{\Delta(N\phi)}{\Delta t}=-\frac{\Delta(LI)}{\Delta t}=-\frac{L\Delta I}{\Delta t}\]

The instantaneous emf:

\[\varepsilon=-\frac{d(N\phi)}{dt}=-\frac{d(LI)}{dt}=-L\frac{dI}{dt}\]

Self inductance of a solenoid \(=\mu_0n^2\pi r^2\ell\)

6.1 Inductor: \(V_A-L\dfrac{dI}{dt}=V_B\). Energy stored in an inductor:

\[U=\tfrac12LI^2\]

7. Growth of current in a series R-L circuit

A cell \(\varepsilon\), an inductor \(L\), a resistor \(R\) and a switch in series. The switch is closed at \(t=0\):

\[I=\frac\varepsilon R\left(1-e^{-Rt/L}\right)\]
  • \(L/R\) is the time constant, \(\tau\).
  • The final current is \(\varepsilon/R\), independent of \(L\).
  • After one time constant, the current is 63% of the final current.
  • A larger time constant means a slower rate of change of current.

8. Decay of current in an R-L circuit

Let the initial current be \(I_0\):

\[I=I_0e^{-Rt/L}\]

After one time constant, \(I=I_0e^{-1}=0.37\,I_0\).

9. Mutual inductance

Induction of emf in a (secondary) coil due to a change in current in another (primary) coil. If the primary current is \(I\), the total flux in the secondary is proportional to \(I\):

\[N\phi\,(\text{in secondary})=MI\]

The emf generated around the secondary is directly proportional to the rate at which the primary current changes.

10. Equivalent self inductance

\[L=\frac{V_A-V_B}{dI/dt}\]

Series combination:

\[L=L_1+L_2\ \ (\text{neglecting mutual inductance})\]\[L=L_1+L_2+2M\ \ (\text{coils coupled, windings in the same direction})\]\[L=L_1+L_2-2M\ \ (\text{coils coupled, windings in opposite directions})\]

Parallel combination (neglecting mutual inductance):

\[\frac1L=\frac1{L_1}+\frac1{L_2}\]

For two mutually coupled coils, \(M\le\sqrt{L_1L_2}\), i.e. \(M=k\sqrt{L_1L_2}\), where the coupling constant \(k\le1\).

11. Transformer

\[\frac{E_s}{E_p}=\frac{N_s}{N_p}=\frac{I_p}{I_s}\]

For a step-up transformer, \(N_s\gt N_p\Rightarrow E_s\gt E_p\).

12. LC oscillations

\[\omega^2=\frac1{LC}\]

Faraday–Lenz law, induced charge and motional emf

\[\varepsilon=-N\frac{d\Phi}{dt},\quad\Phi=BA\cos\theta,\quad I=\frac\varepsilon R,\quad q=\int I\,dt=\frac{N\Delta\Phi}R,\quad\varepsilon_{\text{motional}}=B\ell v\ (\text{for }\vec v,\vec B,\vec\ell\text{ mutually perpendicular})\]
  • The induced emf is produced by any change of flux: a change in \(B\), in the area \(A\), or in the orientation \(\theta\). The charge that flows depends only on the change of flux and the resistance, not on the time taken (\(q=\dfrac{N\Delta\Phi}R\)). Unit: 1 Wb \(=1\) T m\(^2=1\) V s.
  • Lenz’s law: the induced current opposes the change in flux (or the motion that causes it); it conserves energy. A loop in a field decreasing into the page gives a clockwise current (adds field into the page); a magnet approaching a loop is repelled, receding is attracted; the induced pole facing an approaching pole is like it.
  • Motional emf: general \(\varepsilon=\displaystyle\int(\vec v\times\vec B)\cdot d\vec\ell\); for a straight conductor \(\varepsilon=B\ell v\sin\theta\) where \(\theta\) is the angle between \(\vec v\) and \(\vec B\) (and \(\vec\ell\perp\) both). For a curved conductor the effective length is the straight chord joining its ends (projected perpendicular to \(\vec v\)). An aeroplane wing: \(\varepsilon=B_V\ell v\); the positive end is the one toward which \(\vec v\times\vec B\) points.
  • Rod rotating about one end with angular speed \(\omega\): \(\varepsilon=\tfrac12B\omega\ell^2\); about its centre each half develops \(\tfrac18B\omega\ell^2\) (centre to end), both ends are at the same potential and the net emf between the two ends is zero. If the rotation plane makes an angle \(\alpha\) with \(\vec B\), replace \(B\) by \(B\sin\alpha\) (the component perpendicular to the plane).
  • Loop moving out of a field region (side \(\ell\), speed \(v\), resistance \(R\)): while one side is in the field, \(\varepsilon=B\ell v\), \(I=\dfrac{B\ell v}R\), retarding force \(F=\dfrac{B^2\ell^2v}R\), power \(P=\dfrac{B^2\ell^2v^2}R\) equal to the rate of mechanical work; zero emf while the loop is entirely inside or outside the uniform field.
  • Rotating coil (\(N\) turns, area \(A\), \(\omega\), angle \(\theta=\omega t\) between \(\vec B\) and the normal): \(\Phi=NBA\cos\omega t\), \(\varepsilon=NBA\omega\sin\omega t\); \(\varepsilon_{\max}=NBA\omega\) when the plane is parallel to \(\vec B\).
  • Loop in a non-uniform field or moving near a wire: the two parallel sides at distances \(x\) and \(x+a\) from a straight wire carrying \(I\) (a rectangular loop of length \(b\) along the wire moving away at \(v\)): \(\varepsilon=\dfrac{\mu_0Ibv}{2\pi}\left(\dfrac1x-\dfrac1{x+a}\right)\). Mutual inductance between the wire and loop: \(M=\dfrac{\mu_0b}{2\pi}\ln\dfrac{x+a}x\).
  • If the area of a loop changes at the rate \(\dfrac{dA}{dt}\) in a uniform perpendicular field, \(\varepsilon=B\dfrac{dA}{dt}\); a circular loop with expanding radius \(r\): \(\varepsilon=2\pi rB\dfrac{dr}{dt}\).
  • Induced emf is a non-conservative effect; the induced electric field has closed field lines: \(\oint\vec E\cdot d\vec\ell=-\dfrac{d\Phi}{dt}\).

A conducting rod on rails: all standard cases

Rod of mass \(m\), length \(\ell\), resistance \(R\) (rails smooth, resistanceless) in a uniform field \(B\) perpendicular to the plane; \(\tau=\dfrac{mR}{B^2\ell^2}\).

SituationResult
Initial velocity \(v_0\), no applied force\(v=v_0e^{-t/\tau}\); distance \(x=v_0\tau(1-e^{-t/\tau})\); total distance \(\dfrac{mRv_0}{B^2\ell^2}\); total charge \(\dfrac{mv_0}{B\ell}\); heat produced \(\tfrac12mv_0^2\)
Constant force \(F\) from rest\(v=v_T(1-e^{-t/\tau})\), \(v_T=\dfrac{FR}{B^2\ell^2}\); power of \(F\) at \(v_T\) equals the heating rate \(\dfrac{B^2\ell^2v_T^2}R\)
Falling under gravity (vertical rails)\(v_T=\dfrac{mgR}{B^2\ell^2}\); \(v=v_T(1-e^{-t/\tau})\); \(a=g-\dfrac v\tau\)
Sliding down an incline (angle \(\theta\)), \(B\) perpendicular to the incline / \(B\) vertical\(v_T=\dfrac{mgR\sin\theta}{B^2\ell^2}\) / \(v_T=\dfrac{mgR\sin\theta}{B^2\ell^2\cos^2\theta}\)
Resistance replaced by a capacitor \(C\) (force \(F\))constant acceleration \(a=\dfrac F{m+B^2\ell^2C}\); constant current \(I=CB\ell a\); with gravity \(a=\dfrac{mg}{m+B^2\ell^2C}\)
Rod connected to an inductor \(L\) (force \(F\) or released from motion)simple harmonic motion with \(\omega=\dfrac{B\ell}{\sqrt{mL}}\) about \(x=\dfrac{FL}{B^2\ell^2}\); \(I=\dfrac{B\ell x}L\)
Rod connected to a battery \(E\) (motor)\(I=\dfrac{E-B\ell v}R\); starts with \(I_0=\dfrac ER\); final speed \(v=\dfrac E{B\ell}\) (no current); \(v=\dfrac E{B\ell}\left(1-e^{-t/\tau}\right)\); with a load force \(F\): \(v=\dfrac{E}{B\ell}-\dfrac{FR}{B^2\ell^2}\)
  • Energy balance: work by the external agent \(=\) heat in \(R\) \(+\) change in kinetic energy (\(+\) energy stored in \(C\) or \(L\)). The back emf of a motor is \(B\ell v\); the electrical power in \(=\) mechanical power out \(+\) \(I^2R\).
  • A rod sliding on a pair of rails connected by \(R\): the force from the field \(F=B I\ell\) always opposes the relative motion (Lenz).
  • Loop entering a field: a square loop (side \(a\), mass \(m\), resistance \(R\)) falling into a horizontal field region: while entering at speed \(v\), the magnetic force is \(\dfrac{B^2a^2v}R\); if \(\dfrac{B^2a^2v}R=mg\) it enters at constant (terminal) speed; once completely inside, the force vanishes and it accelerates at \(g\); the heat generated while entering a distance \(a\) at terminal speed \(=mga\).
  • Two rods on rails (masses \(m_1,m_2\), both moving): the emf is \(B\ell(v_1-v_2)\); the total momentum \(m_1v_1+m_2v_2\) is conserved (equal and opposite forces) if no external force acts; the relative velocity decays with time constant \(\dfrac{R_{tot}}{B^2\ell^2}\dfrac{m_1m_2}{m_1+m_2}\); the final common speed \(\dfrac{m_1v_{1i}+m_2v_{2i}}{m_1+m_2}\).

Induced electric fields, eddy currents and displacement current

  • Time-varying uniform \(B\) confined to a circle of radius \(R\) (axial): the induced electric field is tangential, \(E=\dfrac r2\dfrac{dB}{dt}\) for \(r\lt R\) and \(E=\dfrac{R^2}{2r}\dfrac{dB}{dt}\) for \(r\gt R\) (it exists even outside the region of the field); \(\oint\vec E\cdot d\vec\ell=-\dfrac{d\Phi}{dt}\). The induced field is non-conservative (closed lines); the potential difference between two points depends on the path.
  • A concentric ring of radius \(r\le R\) in such a field: \(\varepsilon=\pi r^2\dfrac{dB}{dt}\); if the ring is a conductor of resistance \(R_r\), the current \(I=\dfrac{\pi r^2}{R_r}\dfrac{dB}{dt}\). A charged particle (charge \(q\), mass \(m\)) at rest on the ring receives a tangential force \(qE\) (the principle of the betatron).
  • Eddy currents: currents induced in bulk conductors by a changing flux; they oppose the change (heating \(\propto\) \(f^2\)). Uses: induction furnace, magnetic braking of trains, the dead-beat galvanometer, electromagnetic damping, the induction cooker, the speedometer, the energy meter. Disadvantage: heat losses in transformer cores — reduced by lamination (thin insulated sheets). A magnet falling through a thick copper or aluminium tube reaches a terminal speed; a conducting ring with a slit shows no braking.
  • Skin effect: high-frequency AC flows near the surface of a conductor.
  • Displacement current (Maxwell): \(I_d=\epsilon_0\dfrac{d\Phi_E}{dt}\); the Ampère–Maxwell law: \(\oint\vec B\cdot d\vec\ell=\mu_0(I_c+I_d)\). In a charging parallel-plate capacitor \(I_d=I_c=\dfrac{dq}{dt}\) and the magnetic field between the plates at radius \(r\lt R\): \(B=\dfrac{\mu_0Ir}{2\pi R^2}\) (and \(\dfrac{\mu_0I}{2\pi r}\) for \(r\gt R\)). The displacement current is zero for a steady field and lets the continuity of current be maintained; it leads to electromagnetic waves.
  • Faraday disc and homopolar generator: a disc of radius \(r\) rotating at \(\omega\) in an axial field: \(\varepsilon=\tfrac12B\omega r^2\) between the axis and the rim; a metal wheel with spokes rotating in a plane normal to a field component \(B_\perp\): \(\varepsilon=\tfrac12B_\perp\omega r^2\) between the hub and the rim (all spokes are in parallel, so the emf does not depend on their number).

Self and mutual inductance: more results

  • \(L=\dfrac{N\Phi}I\); unit henry: 1 H \(=1\) Wb A\(^{-1}=1\) V s A\(^{-1}\); \(\varepsilon=-L\dfrac{dI}{dt}\); \(L\) depends on geometry and the core (\(\mu_r\)), \(L\propto N^2\). Dimension \([ML^2T^{-2}A^{-2}]\).
  • Long solenoid: \(L=\dfrac{\mu_0\mu_rN^2A}\ell=\mu_0\mu_rn^2A\ell\). Toroid (mean radius \(r\), cross-section \(A\)): \(L=\dfrac{\mu_0\mu_rN^2A}{2\pi r}\). Coaxial cable (radii \(a\lt b\)): \(\dfrac L\ell=\dfrac{\mu_0}{2\pi}\ln\dfrac ba\). Doubling the number of turns while keeping the length multiplies \(L\) by 4.
  • Energy stored in an inductor: \(U=\tfrac12LI^2\); the energy density of a magnetic field \(u=\dfrac{B^2}{2\mu_0}\) (\(\dfrac{B^2}{2\mu}\) in a material); \(U=\displaystyle\int\dfrac{B^2}{2\mu_0}dV\). Rate of supply of energy \(LI\dfrac{dI}{dt}\).
  • Mutual inductance: \(\Phi_2=MI_1\), \(\varepsilon_2=-M\dfrac{dI_1}{dt}\); \(M_{12}=M_{21}\); \(M=k\sqrt{L_1L_2}\) with \(0\le k\le1\) (\(k=1\) for perfect flux linkage). Two coaxial solenoids (the inner entirely within the outer, outer length \(\ell\), inner area \(A\)): \(M=\dfrac{\mu_0N_1N_2A}\ell\). A small single-turn coil of radius \(a\) at the centre of a large coil of radius \(b\) with \(N\) turns (\(a\ll b\)): \(M=\dfrac{\mu_0\pi a^2N}{2b}\). Coaxial loops far apart (\(x\gg a,b\)): \(M\approx\dfrac{\mu_0\pi a^2b^2}{2x^3}\).
  • Combinations: series without coupling \(L_1+L_2\), with coupling \(L_1+L_2\pm2M\) (\(+\) for aiding fluxes); parallel without coupling \(\dfrac1{L}=\dfrac1{L_1}+\dfrac1{L_2}\); with coupling (aiding) \(L=\dfrac{L_1L_2-M^2}{L_1+L_2-2M}\) (for opposing, \(+2M\)).
  • A coil with no resistance and a decaying current: induced emf \(=L\left|\dfrac{dI}{dt}\right|\) keeps the current in the same direction (opposes the decay); a spark appears when a circuit with a large \(L\) is broken because \(\dfrac{dI}{dt}\) is large.
  • Mechanical analogue: \(L\leftrightarrow m\), \(I\leftrightarrow v\), \(U=\tfrac12LI^2\leftrightarrow\tfrac12mv^2\); \(R\leftrightarrow\) friction constant.

\(RL\) transients and \(LC\) oscillations: more results

  • Growth: \(I=\dfrac ER\left(1-e^{-t/\tau}\right)\), \(\tau=\dfrac LR\); \(V_L=Ee^{-t/\tau}\), \(V_R=E\left(1-e^{-t/\tau}\right)\); at \(t=0\) the inductor acts as an open circuit (\(I=0\), \(\dfrac{dI}{dt}=\dfrac EL\)); at \(t\to\infty\) as a short circuit. The current reaches \(63\%\) of \(\dfrac ER\) at \(t=\tau\), half of it at \(0.693\,\tau\). Energy stored at the end \(\dfrac{LE^2}{2R^2}\); the battery supplies \(EI\), of which \(I^2R\) is heat and \(LI\dfrac{dI}{dt}\) goes to the field.
  • Decay (battery removed through a path of resistance \(R\)): \(I=I_0e^{-t/\tau}\); the energy \(\tfrac12LI_0^2\) is all dissipated in the resistor. In a circuit with an inductor in parallel with a resistor \(R_2\), opening the switch drives the current \(I_0\) through \(R_2\) with an initial voltage \(I_0R_2\) across it (possibly much larger than the battery emf).
  • General first-order law: \(I(t)=I_\infty+(I_0-I_\infty)e^{-t/\tau}\) with \(\tau=\dfrac L{R_{eq}}\), where \(R_{eq}\) is the Thévenin resistance seen by the inductor. The current through an inductor cannot change abruptly (just after switching it equals its value just before); the voltage across a capacitor cannot change abruptly.
  • LC circuit: charge \(q=Q_0\cos(\omega_0t+\phi)\), current \(I=-Q_0\omega_0\sin(\omega_0t+\phi)\), \(\omega_0=\dfrac1{\sqrt{LC}}\), \(T=2\pi\sqrt{LC}\); total energy \(\dfrac{Q_0^2}{2C}=\tfrac12LI_0^2\) with \(I_0=Q_0\omega_0\); \(U_E=\dfrac{q^2}{2C}\), \(U_B=\tfrac12LI^2\). The energy is equally shared when \(q=\dfrac{Q_0}{\sqrt2}\) (at \(t=\dfrac T8\)); the capacitor discharges completely in \(\dfrac T4=\dfrac\pi2\sqrt{LC}\). A capacitor charged to \(V\) and connected to \(L\): \(I_{\max}=V\sqrt{\dfrac CL}\).
  • Damped LCR: \(q=Q_0e^{-Rt/2L}\cos\omega't\), \(\omega'=\sqrt{\dfrac1{LC}-\dfrac{R^2}{4L^2}}\); the energy decays as \(e^{-Rt/L}\); critical damping at \(R=2\sqrt{\dfrac LC}\) (no oscillation for larger \(R\)).
  • Analogy: \(q\leftrightarrow x\), \(I\leftrightarrow v\), \(L\leftrightarrow m\), \(\dfrac1C\leftrightarrow k\), \(R\leftrightarrow b\); an LC oscillator radiates (energy loss) at high frequency, as in radio transmitters.
23
Optics & modern physics

Geometrical Optics

1. Reflection of light

\[\angle i=\angle r\]

Characteristics of an image in a plane mirror

  • Distance of object from the mirror \(=\) distance of image from the mirror.
  • The line joining a point object and its image is normal to the reflecting surface.
  • The image is the same size as the object.
  • For a real object the image is virtual; for a virtual object the image is real.

2. Velocity of object and image

From the mirror property (coordinates measured with respect to the mirror, which lies in the \(y\)-\(z\) plane):

\[x_{im}=-x_{om},\quad y_{im}=y_{om},\quad z_{im}=z_{om}\]

Differentiating with respect to time:

\[v_{(im)x}=-v_{(om)x},\quad v_{(im)y}=v_{(om)y},\quad v_{(im)z}=v_{(om)z}\]

3. Spherical mirror

\[\frac1v+\frac1u=\frac2R=\frac1f\qquad\text{(mirror formula)}\]

The \(x\)-coordinates of the centre of curvature and focus are negative for a concave mirror and positive for a convex mirror. Light reflects back in the \(-x\) direction, so a negative \(v\) indicates a real image and a positive \(v\) a virtual image.

(b) Lateral (transverse) magnification:

\[m=\frac{h_2}{h_1}\qquad m=-\frac vu\]

(d) Differentiating the mirror formula: \(\dfrac{dv}{du}=-\dfrac{v^2}{u^2}\)

(e) With respect to time:

\[\frac{dv}{dt}=-\frac{v^2}{u^2}\frac{du}{dt}\]

\(dv/dt\) is the velocity of the image along the principal axis and \(du/dt\) is the velocity of the object along the principal axis. The negative sign shows that the image, in a mirror, always moves in the direction opposite to the object (for velocity with respect to the mirror, along the \(x\)-axis).

(f) Newton's formula:

\[XY=f^2\]

\(X\) and \(Y\) are the distances (along the principal axis) of the object and image from the principal focus.

(g) Optical power of a mirror (in dioptres):

\[P=\frac1f\]

\(f\) is the focal length, with sign, in metres.

(h) Longitudinal magnification for an object along the principal axis that is not very small (the image is always inverted):

\[\frac{v_2-v_1}{u_2-u_1}\]

4. Refraction of light

\[\mu=\frac{\text{speed of light in vacuum}}{\text{speed of light in medium}}=\frac cv\]

4.1 Laws of refraction (Snell's law). \(\dfrac{\sin i}{\sin r}\) is constant for any pair of media and light of a given wavelength. More precisely:

\[\frac{\sin i}{\sin r}=\frac{n_2}{n_1}=\frac{v_1}{v_2}=\frac{\lambda_1}{\lambda_2}\]

4.2 Deviation of a ray incident at angle \(i\) and refracted at angle \(r\):

\[\delta=|i-r|\]

5. Principle of reversibility of light rays

A ray travelling along the path of the reflected ray is reflected along the path of the incident ray. A refracted ray reversed to travel back along its path is refracted along the path of the incident ray. So the incident and refracted rays are mutually reversible.

7. Apparent depth and shift of a submerged object

At near-normal incidence (small angle of incidence \(i\)):

\[d'=\frac d{n_{relative}}\qquad n_{relative}=\frac{n_i\ (\text{R.I. of medium of incidence})}{n_r\ (\text{R.I. of medium of refraction})}\]\[\text{Apparent shift}=d\left(1-\frac1{n_{rel}}\right)\]

Refraction through a composite slab (parallel media, seen from a medium of R.I. \(n_0\)):

\[\text{Apparent depth}=\frac{t_1}{n_{1\,rel}}+\frac{t_2}{n_{2\,rel}}+\frac{t_3}{n_{3\,rel}}+\cdots+\frac{t_n}{n_{n\,rel}}\]\[\text{Apparent shift}=t_1\left[1-\frac1{n_{1\,rel}}\right]+t_2\left[1-\frac1{n_{2\,rel}}\right]+\cdots+t_n\left[1-\frac1{n_{n\,rel}}\right]\]

The distance of the final image is measured from the final surface.

8. Critical angle and total internal reflection (T.I.R.)

\[C=\sin^{-1}\frac{n_r}{n_d}\]

\(n_r\) is the refractive index of the rarer medium and \(n_d\) of the denser medium.

Conditions for T.I.R.: (a) light is incident on the interface from the denser medium; (b) the angle of incidence is greater than the critical angle (\(i\gt C\)).

9. Refraction through a prism

9.1 Characteristics: for a prism of angle \(A\), with angle of incidence \(i\), emergence angle \(e\) and refraction angles \(r_1,r_2\):

\[\delta=(i+e)-(r_1+r_2)\quad\text{and}\quad r_1+r_2=A\ \ \therefore\ \ \delta=i+e-A\]

9.2 Variation of \(\delta\) with \(i\):

  • There is one and only one angle of incidence for which the angle of deviation is minimum.
  • At \(\delta=\delta_{min}\): \(i=e\) and \(r_1=r_2\), so the ray passes symmetrically through the prism. Then \(\delta_{min}=2i_{min}-A\), where \(r=A/2\): \[n_{rel}=\frac{\sin\left[\frac{A+\delta_m}2\right]}{\sin\left[\frac A2\right]},\quad n_{rel}=\frac{n_{prism}}{n_{surroundings}}\] Also \(\delta_{min}=(n-1)A\) for small \(A\).
  • For a thin prism (\(A\le10^\circ\)) and small \(i\), all deviations are \(\delta=(n_{rel}-1)A\).

10. Dispersion of light

The angular splitting of a ray of white light into its components, spreading in different directions. It occurs because waves of different wavelengths move at the same speed in vacuum but at different speeds in a medium. The variation of refractive index with wavelength is given by Cauchy's formula:

\[n(\lambda)=a+\frac b{\lambda^2}\]

where \(a\) and \(b\) are positive constants of the medium. The angle between the rays of the extreme colours in the dispersed light is the angle of dispersion.

For a prism of small angle \(A\) and small \(i\):

\[\theta=(n_v-n_r)A\quad(\text{angular dispersion})\qquad\delta_y=(n_y-1)A\quad(\text{mean deviation})\]

Dispersive power \((\omega)\) of the medium of the prism:

\[\omega=\frac{n_v-n_r}{n_y-1}=\frac{\delta_v-\delta_r}{\delta_y}=\frac\theta{\delta_y}=\frac{\text{angular dispersion}}{\text{deviation of mean ray (yellow)}}\]

If \(n_y\) is not given, take \(n_y=\dfrac{n_v+n_r}2\). Here \(n_v,n_r,n_y\) are the refractive indices for violet, red and yellow light.

11. Combination of two prisms

(a) Direct vision combination (dispersion without deviation):

\[\left[\frac{n_v+n_r}2-1\right]A=\left[\frac{n_v'+n_r'}2-1\right]A'\iff(n_y-1)A=(n_y'-1)A'\]

(b) Achromatic combination (deviation without dispersion):

\[(n_v-n_r)A=(n_v'-n_r')A'\]

12. Refraction at spherical surfaces

For paraxial rays on a spherical surface separating two media, with light moving from index \(n_1\) to index \(n_2\):

\[\frac{n_2}v-\frac{n_1}u=\frac{n_2-n_1}R\]

Transverse magnification (perpendicular to the principal axis):

\[m=\frac{v-R}{u-R}=\frac{v/n_2}{u/n_1}\]

13. Refraction at a spherical thin lens

A thin lens is convex if it is thicker at the middle and concave if it is thicker at the ends. For a thin lens with the same medium on both sides:

\[\frac1v-\frac1u=(n_{rel}-1)\left(\frac1{R_1}-\frac1{R_2}\right),\qquad n_{rel}=\frac{n_{lens}}{n_{medium}}\]\[\frac1f=(n_{rel}-1)\left(\frac1{R_1}-\frac1{R_2}\right)\ \ (\text{lens maker's formula})\]\[\frac1v-\frac1u=\frac1f\qquad m=\frac vu\]

Combination of lenses:

\[\frac1F=\frac1{f_1}+\frac1{f_2}+\frac1{f_3}+\cdots\]

Optical instruments: simple microscope

Magnifying power: \(\dfrac D{u_0}\)

Image at infinity: \(M_\infty=\dfrac Df\)

Image at the near point \(D\): \(M_D=1+\dfrac Df\)

Optical instruments: magnifying power and tube length

InstrumentSettingMagnifying powerLength
Compound microscopeGeneral\(M=\dfrac{v_0D}{u_0u_e}\)\(L=v_0+u_e\)
Image at infinity\(M_\infty=\dfrac{v_0D}{u_0f_e}\)\(L=v_0+f_e\)
Image at near point\(M_D=\dfrac{v_0}{u_0}\left(1+\dfrac D{f_e}\right)\)\(L_D=v_0+\dfrac{Df_e}{D+f_e}\)
Astronomical telescopeGeneral\(M=\dfrac{f_0}{u_e}\)\(L=f_0+u_e\)
Image at infinity\(M_\infty=\dfrac{f_0}{f_e}\)\(L=f_0+f_e\)
Image at near point\(M_D=\dfrac{f_0}{f_e}\left(1+\dfrac{f_e}D\right)\)\(L_D=f_0+\dfrac{Df_e}{D+f_e}\)
Terrestrial telescopeGeneral\(M=\dfrac{f_0}{u_e}\)\(L=f_0+4f+u_e\)
Image at infinity\(M_\infty=\dfrac{f_0}{f_e}\)\(L=f_0+4f+f_e\)
Image at near point\(M_D=\dfrac{f_0}{f_e}\left(1+\dfrac{f_e}D\right)\)\(L_D=f_0+4f+\dfrac{Df_e}{D+f_e}\)
Galilean telescopeGeneral\(M=\dfrac{f_0}{u_e}\)\(L=f_0-u_e\)
Image at infinity\(M_\infty=\dfrac{f_0}{f_e}\)\(L=f_0-f_e\)
Image at near point\(M_D=\dfrac{f_0}{f_e}\left(1-\dfrac{f_e}D\right)\)\(L_D=f_0-\dfrac{f_eD}{D-f_e}\)

Resolving power

\[\text{Microscope: }R=\frac1{\Delta d}=\frac{2\mu\sin\theta}\lambda\qquad\text{Telescope: }R=\frac1{\Delta\theta}=\frac a{1.22\lambda}\]

Plane mirrors: images, rotation and two-mirror systems

  • A plane mirror forms a virtual, erect, laterally inverted image of the same size, as far behind the mirror as the object is in front (\(m=+1\)). In vector form the reflected direction is \(\hat r=\hat i-2(\hat i\cdot\hat n)\hat n\). Fermat’s principle: a ray travelling between two points follows the path for which the optical path \(\int\mu\,ds\) is stationary (usually least); the laws of reflection and refraction follow from it.
  • Rotation of a mirror through \(\theta\) (axis in the plane of the mirror, perpendicular to the plane of incidence) with a fixed incident ray turns the reflected ray through \(2\theta\); the image of a fixed object moves on a circle about the axis through an angle \(2\theta\). A light spot on a scale at distance \(D\) moves \(D\tan2\theta\approx2D\theta\) (the mirror galvanometer, lever arm).
  • Velocities: the normal component of the velocity of the image relative to the mirror is the reverse of that of the object (\(v_{I\perp}=-v_{O\perp}\) in the mirror frame), while the component parallel to the mirror is the same. If an object moves toward a fixed mirror at \(v\), the image moves toward the mirror at \(v\) (the separation closes at \(2v\)); if the mirror moves toward a fixed object at \(v\), the image moves at \(2v\) in the same direction.
  • Minimum mirror height to see one’s whole image is half of the person’s height, whatever the distance; its lower edge must be half of the eye height above the floor (\(\tfrac12\) of the distance from the eyes to the feet).
  • Two plane mirrors inclined at \(\theta\): number of images \(n=\dfrac{360^\circ}\theta-1\) if \(\dfrac{360^\circ}\theta\) is an even integer (any position of the object); if it is an odd integer, \(n=\dfrac{360^\circ}\theta\) for an object off the bisector and \(\dfrac{360^\circ}\theta-1\) on the bisector; if it is not an integer, \(n\) equals its integer part. For \(\theta=90^\circ\), \(n=3\); for \(\theta=60^\circ\), \(n=5\); for \(\theta=0\) (parallel mirrors) \(n\to\infty\).
  • A ray reflected once from each of two mirrors inclined at \(\theta\lt90^\circ\) is deviated by \(\delta=360^\circ-2\theta=2(180^\circ-\theta)\) in total, independent of the angle of incidence; for two perpendicular mirrors \(\delta=180^\circ\) and the emergent ray is antiparallel to the incident ray (corner reflector, retroreflector).
  • Parallel mirrors a distance \(d\) apart, with the object at distance \(x\) from one of them: the images behind that first mirror are at distances \(x,\ 2d-x,\ 2d+x,\ 4d-x,\ 4d+x,\dots\) from it, with decreasing brightness.
  • A mirror inclined at \(\theta\) to a horizontal ray turns it through \(2\theta\); a horizontal beam reflected into a vertical beam needs a mirror at \(45^\circ\) to the horizontal.
  • Field of view: an eye at distance \(d\) in front of a mirror of width \(w\) sees objects within the lines joining the image of the eye to the edges of the mirror; the nearer the eye, the larger the field of view.

Spherical mirrors: image table, speeds and thick mirrors

Mirror formula \(\dfrac1v+\dfrac1u=\dfrac1f=\dfrac2R\) (Cartesian convention, pole at the origin, incident light travelling in the \(+x\) direction; for a concave mirror \(f\lt0\)); \(m=\dfrac{h_I}{h_O}=-\dfrac vu\). The focal length of a mirror does not depend on the surrounding medium (unlike a lens).

MirrorObject positionImage positionNature and size
ConcaveAt \(\infty\)At \(F\)Real, inverted, point-sized
Beyond \(C\)Between \(F\) and \(C\)Real, inverted, diminished
At \(C\)At \(C\)Real, inverted, same size
Between \(C\) and \(F\)Beyond \(C\)Real, inverted, magnified
At \(F\)At \(\infty\)Real, inverted, very large
Between \(F\) and \(P\)Behind the mirrorVirtual, erect, magnified
ConvexAt \(\infty\)At \(F\) (behind)Virtual, erect, point-sized
Anywhere in frontBetween \(P\) and \(F\) (behind)Virtual, erect, diminished
  • \(m=\dfrac{f}{f-u}=\dfrac{f-v}{f}\); Newton’s formula \(x_1x_2=f^2\) (distances of the object and image from the focus); \(m\gt0\) means an erect (virtual) image, \(m\lt0\) an inverted (real) image.
  • Axial motion: for a small displacement along the axis \(\dfrac{dv}{du}=-\dfrac{v^2}{u^2}=-m^2\), so \(v_{I}=-m^2v_{O}\) (velocities measured in the same Cartesian axis, relative to the mirror). For motion perpendicular to the axis (object distance fixed) \(v_{I\perp}=m\,v_{O\perp}\).
  • Intensity and aperture: the brightness of the image is proportional to the area (aperture) of the mirror used. If part of a mirror is covered or cut away, the image position and size are unchanged and only the brightness falls. A mirror cut by a plane containing the axis into two halves that are then separated gives two images.
  • Silvered thick glass plate (thickness \(t\), refractive index \(\mu\), silvered at the back): a point at distance \(a\) in front of the front surface has its image at distance \(a+\dfrac{2t}\mu\) behind the front surface (the plate acts as a plane mirror at the effective depth \(\dfrac t\mu\) behind the front surface).
  • Use: concave mirrors (shaving, dentist, headlights, telescopes, solar cookers — parabolic to avoid spherical aberration); convex mirrors (rear-view, street lamps) give a wide field of view. Spherical aberration occurs because marginal rays focus nearer to the pole than paraxial rays; use small apertures or parabolic mirrors.

Refraction: slabs, apparent depth, total internal reflection and fibres

  • Snell’s law: \(\mu_1\sin\theta_1=\mu_2\sin\theta_2\), \(\mu=\dfrac cv\), \(\lambda'=\dfrac\lambda\mu\); the frequency is unchanged. Through several parallel layers \(\mu_1\sin\theta_1=\mu_2\sin\theta_2=\dots\), so only the first and last media matter for the emergent angle.
  • Glass slab (thickness \(t\), angle of incidence \(i\), refraction \(r\)): the emergent ray is parallel to the incident ray with a lateral shift \(d=\dfrac{t\sin(i-r)}{\cos r}\). For a nearly normal view, an object seen through the slab appears shifted toward the observer by \(t\left(1-\dfrac1\mu\right)\). For several layers viewed normally from air the apparent depth is \(\sum\dfrac{t_i}{\mu_i}\).
  • Apparent depth: an object at real depth \(d\) in a medium of index \(\mu\) viewed from air normally appears at \(\dfrac d\mu\); an object in air viewed from the medium appears at \(\mu d\); in general \(d'=d\,\dfrac{\mu_{\text{observer}}}{\mu_{\text{object}}}\). Under an oblique view the apparent depth for angles \(i,r\) is \(d\dfrac{\tan r}{\tan i}\).
  • Total internal reflection needs the light to go from a denser to a rarer medium at an angle greater than the critical angle, \(\sin C=\dfrac{\mu_r}{\mu_d}\). A source at depth \(h\) under a liquid surface illuminates a circle of radius \(r=h\tan C=\dfrac{h}{\sqrt{\mu^2-1}}\) on the surface; area \(\dfrac{\pi h^2}{\mu^2-1}\). Diamond (\(\mu=2.42\), \(C\approx24.4^\circ\)) sparkles because most rays are totally reflected several times.
  • Examples of TIR: mirage (hot road, light bends away from the normal in the hot, rarer, lower air), looming over cold water, the glitter of air bubbles in water, a glass tube in water looking silvery, totally reflecting prisms (an isosceles right-angled prism turns a ray by \(90^\circ\) or \(180^\circ\) — for \(\mu\gt\sqrt2\) — and inverts the image; used in periscopes and binoculars).
  • Optical fibre (core \(\mu_1\), cladding \(\mu_2\lt\mu_1\)): rays are guided if they strike the core–cladding interface beyond the critical angle. The acceptance angle \(\theta_a\) at the end face satisfies \(\sin\theta_a=\sqrt{\mu_1^2-\mu_2^2}\) (in air); numerical aperture \(\text{NA}=\sqrt{\mu_1^2-\mu_2^2}\). A bent fibre needs a bend radius not too small. Used in endoscopy and communication (low loss, high bandwidth, no interference).
  • Atmospheric refraction: the Sun is visible about 2 minutes before it actually rises and after it sets (it appears flattened); stars twinkle because of the varying density of the air; the apparent position of a star is higher than the true one.
  • Refractive index of a medium for light of wavelength \(\lambda\) (Cauchy’s relation) \(\mu=A+\dfrac{B}{\lambda^2}+\dfrac{C}{\lambda^4}\); the relative index of medium 2 w.r.t. medium 1 is \({}_1\mu_2=\dfrac{\mu_2}{\mu_1}=\dfrac{v_1}{v_2}=\dfrac{\lambda_1}{\lambda_2}\).

Prism: deviation, minimum deviation, emergence and grazing conditions

\[r_1+r_2=A,\qquad \delta=i+e-A,\qquad \mu=\frac{\sin\frac{A+\delta_{\min}}2}{\sin\frac A2},\qquad \delta_{\text{thin}}=(\mu-1)A\]
  • Minimum deviation: \(i=e\), \(r_1=r_2=\dfrac A2\); the ray inside the prism is parallel to the base (for an isosceles prism). The \(\delta\)–\(i\) curve has a minimum; any other deviation occurs for two values of the angle of incidence (\(i\) and \(e\), by reversibility). For \(\delta_{\min}=A\): \(\mu=2\cos\dfrac A2\). An equilateral prism with \(\mu=\sqrt3\) has \(\delta_{\min}=60^\circ\).
  • Emergence condition: a ray emerges from the second face only if \(r_2\lt C\) (critical angle of the glass). For \(A\gt2C\) no ray emerges whatever the angle of incidence; for \(A\lt2C\) a ray emerges only if the angle of incidence exceeds \(i_{\min}\), where \(\sin i_{\min}=\mu\sin(A-C)\) (grazing emergence \(e=90^\circ\)).
  • Grazing incidence (\(i=90^\circ\)): \(r_1=C\), \(r_2=A-C\); the emergent angle follows from \(\sin e=\mu\sin(A-C)\) and the deviation is the maximum for the prism: \(\delta_{\max}=90^\circ+e-A\).
  • Normal incidence on the first face: \(r_1=0\), \(r_2=A\); the ray emerges if \(\mu\sin A\lt1\), with \(\sin e=\mu\sin A\) and \(\delta=e-A\). When \(\sin A\gt\dfrac1\mu\) the ray is totally reflected at the second face.
  • Prism in a liquid of index \(\mu'\): replace \(\mu\) by \(\dfrac{\mu}{\mu'}\); \(\delta=\left(\dfrac\mu{\mu'}-1\right)A\); if \(\mu'\gt\mu\) the ray bends toward the apex instead of toward the base. A prism with one face silvered returns the ray along its path when the ray strikes that face normally, i.e. \(\sin i=\mu\sin A\).
  • Thin prism, small angles: \(\delta=(\mu-1)A\) independent of the angle of incidence (for small \(i\)); two thin prisms in contact (same base side or opposite): net \(\delta=(\mu_1-1)A_1\pm(\mu_2-1)A_2\).
  • In a prism spectrometer the angle \(A\) of the prism and the angle of minimum deviation \(\delta_{\min}\) are measured and \(\mu\) found from the formula above for each wavelength.

Dispersion, achromatic and direct-vision combinations, rainbow, scattering

  • Dispersion: splitting of white light as \(\mu\) depends on wavelength (violet deviates most, red least; red travels fastest in glass). For a thin prism, angular dispersion \(\theta=\delta_v-\delta_r=(\mu_v-\mu_r)A\). Dispersive power \(\omega=\dfrac{\delta_v-\delta_r}{\delta_{\text{mean}}}=\dfrac{\mu_v-\mu_r}{\mu_y-1}\) (\(\mu_y\) for the mean yellow line); it depends only on the material (an Abbe number \(V=\dfrac1\omega\)). Flint glass has larger \(\omega\) than crown glass.
  • Achromatic combination of thin prisms (no net dispersion, prisms placed with opposite bases): \(\omega_1\delta_1=\omega_2\delta_2\), i.e. \((\mu_v-\mu_r)A=(\mu_v'-\mu_r')A'\). The net deviation is \(\delta=(\mu-1)A-(\mu'-1)A'\) and is not zero.
  • Direct-vision combination (zero deviation for the mean ray, but dispersion remains): \((\mu-1)A=(\mu'-1)A'\) with the prisms inverted; the net dispersion is \((\mu_v-\mu_r)A-(\mu_v'-\mu_r')A'\).
  • Chromatic aberration of a lens: \(f_v\lt f_r\) because \(\mu_v\gt\mu_r\); the longitudinal chromatic aberration \(f_r-f_v=\omega f\). An achromatic doublet (a convex crown lens in contact with a concave flint lens) satisfies \(\dfrac{\omega_1}{f_1}+\dfrac{\omega_2}{f_2}=0\), with \(f_1\) and \(f_2\) of opposite signs, and a net convergent focal length with \(\dfrac1f=\dfrac1{f_1}+\dfrac1{f_2}\).
  • Rainbow: sunlight is refracted, dispersed and internally reflected in raindrops. Primary bow: one internal reflection, angular radius about \(42^\circ\) (red, outer) to \(40^\circ\) (violet, inner); secondary bow: two internal reflections, about \(51^\circ\) (red, inner) to \(54^\circ\) (violet, outer), fainter with the colours reversed. The Sun is behind the observer. Bows exist only when the Sun is lower than \(42^\circ\).
  • Scattering: by particles much smaller than \(\lambda\) (Rayleigh scattering) the scattered intensity is \(\propto\dfrac1{\lambda^4}\): the sky is blue (blue is scattered more than red), the Sun looks red at sunrise or sunset (the path through the atmosphere is long and the blue is scattered away), danger signals are red (least scattered). Clouds look white (scattering by large droplets is independent of \(\lambda\)). The sky looks black to an astronaut where there is no atmosphere. Violet is scattered even more but is absorbed in the upper atmosphere and our eye is less sensitive to it.
  • Colours of objects: a body appears coloured by the light it reflects or transmits; a red filter transmits only red light, and a green leaf appears black in red light; primary colours of light: red, green, blue; complementary pairs (red–cyan, green–magenta, blue–yellow) give white.

Refraction at spherical surfaces and thin lenses: more results

  • Single spherical surface (light from medium \(\mu_1\) into \(\mu_2\)): \(\dfrac{\mu_2}v-\dfrac{\mu_1}u=\dfrac{\mu_2-\mu_1}R\); lateral magnification \(m=\dfrac{\mu_1v}{\mu_2u}\); power \(P=\dfrac{\mu_2-\mu_1}R\); focal lengths \(f_1=-\dfrac{\mu_1R}{\mu_2-\mu_1}\) (object side), \(f_2=\dfrac{\mu_2R}{\mu_2-\mu_1}\) (image side); a plane surface (\(R\to\infty\)) gives \(v=\dfrac{\mu_2}{\mu_1}u\).
  • Solid glass sphere of radius \(R\): the focal length measured from the centre is \(\dfrac{\mu R}{2(\mu-1)}\); for \(\mu=2\) parallel rays focus at the back surface; for \(\mu=1.5\) at \(1.5R\) from the centre. A hemisphere with the plane face toward parallel light focuses at \(\dfrac R{\mu-1}\) from the curved surface (a plano-convex lens: \(f=\dfrac R{\mu-1}\)).
  • Lens-maker’s formula: \(\dfrac1f=(\mu-1)\left(\dfrac1{R_1}-\dfrac1{R_2}\right)\); in a surrounding medium \(\mu_m\): \(\dfrac1f=\left(\dfrac{\mu_l}{\mu_m}-1\right)\left(\dfrac1{R_1}-\dfrac1{R_2}\right)\). A convex glass lens in a medium of higher index (e.g. carbon disulphide) behaves as a diverging lens; if \(\mu_l=\mu_m\) the lens vanishes optically. An air bubble in water or glass shaped like a biconvex lens is diverging.
  • Lens formula: \(\dfrac1v-\dfrac1u=\dfrac1f\), \(m=\dfrac vu=\dfrac f{f+u}=\dfrac{f-v}{f}\); power \(P=\dfrac1f\) (in metres) in dioptre (D); Newton’s relation \(x_1x_2=f^2\). Axial motion: \(\dfrac{dv}{du}=\dfrac{v^2}{u^2}=m^2\), so the axial velocity of the image is \(m^2\) times that of the object, in the same direction; transverse: \(v_{I\perp}=m\,v_{O\perp}\).
  • Combination of two thin lenses separated by \(d\): \(\dfrac1F=\dfrac1{f_1}+\dfrac1{f_2}-\dfrac d{f_1f_2}\), \(P=P_1+P_2-dP_1P_2\); in contact \(P=P_1+P_2\). If \(d=f_1+f_2\) the system is afocal (a parallel beam leaves as a parallel beam; the beam width changes in the ratio \(\left|\dfrac{f_2}{f_1}\right|\)).
  • Displacement (Bessel) method: for an object–screen distance \(D\gt4f\) there are two lens positions separated by \(d\) that give a sharp image: \(f=\dfrac{D^2-d^2}{4D}\), \(m_1m_2=1\), and the object size is \(h_O=\sqrt{h_1h_2}\). The minimum distance between a real object and its real image is \(4f\) (at \(u=v=2f\), \(m=-1\)).
  • Lenses cut or combined: cutting a lens along a plane containing the axis leaves \(f\) unchanged (each half gives the same image, half as bright); cutting a symmetric biconvex lens through its thickness (perpendicular to the axis) gives two plano-convex lenses each with focal length \(2f\); cutting an equiconvex lens into two halves and joining them with different refractive indices gives two images. A lens with a part blocked forms the full image, dimmer.
  • Silvered lens (second surface silvered; light passes twice through the first surface): \(\dfrac1F=\dfrac2{f_L}+\dfrac1{f_m}\), where \(f_L\) is the focal length of the lens and \(f_m=\dfrac{R_2}2\) of the silvered surface treated as a concave mirror. For a plano-convex lens with the curved face toward the light and the plane face silvered: \(F=\dfrac R{2(\mu-1)}\); with the curved face silvered \(F=\dfrac R{2\mu}\).
  • Image shift by a slab placed between a lens and the object: the effective object distance changes by \(t\left(1-\dfrac1\mu\right)\). A plane mirror behind a thin lens makes the system a mirror of power \(2P\) (when it touches the lens).
  • Aberrations: spherical (marginal rays focus nearer — reduced by stops or aspheric surfaces, by using a plano-convex lens with its curved face toward a distant object), coma, astigmatism, distortion, and chromatic aberration (\(f_r-f_v=\omega f\)).

The human eye and its defects

  • The eye forms a real, inverted, diminished image on the retina (the brain re-inverts it). Cornea and aqueous humour provide about two-thirds of the power (\(\approx40\) D); the eye-lens (variable focal length) adjusts it by the ciliary muscles (accommodation). The total power is about \(60\) D with the lens–retina distance about \(2.5\) cm. Rods respond to dim light (no colour), cones to bright light and colour.
  • Far point of the normal eye: \(\infty\); near point (least distance of distinct vision): \(D=25\) cm. Power of accommodation \(=\dfrac1{x_n}-\dfrac1{x_f}\) (with distances in metres; about \(4\) D for a normal eye). The least angle of resolution of the eye is about \(1'\approx3\times10^{-4}\) rad; persistence of vision \(\approx\dfrac1{16}\) s.
  • Myopia (short-sightedness): the far point \(x_f\) is finite; the image of a distant object falls in front of the retina. Corrected by a concave lens of focal length \(f=-x_f\) (lens at the eye), i.e. power \(P=-\dfrac1{x_f}\). If the spectacles are at a distance \(d\) from the eye, \(f=-(x_f-d)\).
  • Hypermetropia (long-sightedness): the near point \(x_n\gt25\) cm; the image of a near object falls behind the retina. Corrected by a convex lens forming a virtual image at \(x_n\) of an object at \(25\) cm: \(\dfrac1f=\dfrac1{25}-\dfrac1{x_n}\) (cm\(^{-1}\)), i.e. \(P=\dfrac1{0.25}-\dfrac1{x_n}\) dioptre.
  • Presbyopia: the loss of accommodation with age (near point recedes and far point may come closer) corrected by bifocal lenses (concave on top, convex below). Astigmatism: unequal curvatures of the cornea in different planes, corrected by a cylindrical lens. Cataract: opacity of the eye lens, treated by implanting an intraocular lens.
  • The angle subtended at the eye (visual angle) determines the apparent size: \(\theta=\dfrac hD\); an optical instrument increases this angle.

Microscopes, telescopes and camera: more results

  • Simple microscope: image at the near point \(m=1+\dfrac Df\) (the eye is close to the lens); image at infinity (relaxed eye) \(m=\dfrac Df\). With \(D=25\) cm, \(m\) is larger for shorter \(f\).
  • Compound microscope (objective \(f_o\), eyepiece \(f_e\); both focal lengths short, \(f_o\lt f_e\)): final image at infinity, \(m=-\dfrac{v_o}{u_o}\dfrac D{f_e}\approx-\dfrac L{f_o}\dfrac D{f_e}\); final image at the near point \(m=-\dfrac{v_o}{u_o}\left(1+\dfrac D{f_e}\right)\), where \(L\) is the distance between the second focus of the objective and the first focus of the eyepiece; the tube length is \(v_o+u_e\) (\(u_e=f_e\) for the final image at infinity).
  • Astronomical telescope: normal adjustment (final image at infinity) \(m=-\dfrac{f_o}{f_e}\), length \(L=f_o+f_e\); final image at the near point \(m=-\dfrac{f_o}{f_e}\left(1+\dfrac{f_e}D\right)\), length \(L=f_o+\dfrac{Df_e}{D+f_e}\). The angular magnification is the ratio of the angles subtended by the image and by the object at the unaided eye: \(m=\dfrac\beta\alpha\); the aperture of the objective (large) gathers light \(\propto D_o^2\).
  • Terrestrial telescope (erecting lens of focal length \(f\) between the objective and eyepiece): \(m=\dfrac{f_o}{f_e}\) (erect image), \(L=f_o+f_e+4f\). Galilean telescope (concave eyepiece): \(m=\dfrac{f_o}{f_e}\) (erect image, image at infinity), \(L=f_o-f_e\); for the image at the near point \(m=\dfrac{f_o}{f_e}\left(1-\dfrac{f_e}D\right)\) (the magnitude of \(f_e\) used).
  • Reflecting telescopes (Newtonian, Cassegrain): a large parabolic concave mirror as the objective — no chromatic aberration, free of spherical aberration, a larger aperture is possible (mirror supported from behind, brighter and sharper images, greater resolving power).
  • Resolving limits (Rayleigh criterion): telescope — the minimum angular separation \(\Delta\theta=\dfrac{1.22\lambda}{D}\), resolving power \(\propto\dfrac D\lambda\); microscope — the least resolvable distance \(d_{\min}=\dfrac{1.22\lambda}{2\mu\sin\theta}=\dfrac{0.61\lambda}{\text{NA}}\) (resolving power \(\dfrac{2\mu\sin\theta}{1.22\lambda}\); an oil-immersion objective with larger \(\mu\) improves it; ultraviolet light and electron microscopes use smaller \(\lambda\)).
  • Camera: the f-number \(N=\dfrac fd\) (\(d\) = aperture diameter); the intensity at the film \(\propto\dfrac1{N^2}\) and the exposure \(\propto\dfrac{t}{N^2}\) (\(t\) = shutter time); successive stops (\(1.4,\,2,\,2.8,\,4\dots\)) change the light by a factor \(2\). A larger aperture gives less depth of field and a smaller one more. Brightness of an extended image \(\propto\left(\dfrac d f\right)^2\).
  • Photometry (basic): the illuminance produced by a point source of luminous intensity \(I\) at distance \(r\): \(E=\dfrac{I}{r^2}\cos\theta\) (inverse-square and Lambert’s cosine law); two sources balance in a photometer when \(\dfrac{I_1}{r_1^2}=\dfrac{I_2}{r_2^2}\).
24
Optics & modern physics

Modern Physics

Photoelectric effect

  • Work function is minimum for caesium (1.9 eV).
  • Work function \(W=h\nu_0=\dfrac{hc}{\lambda_0}\)
  • Photoelectric current is directly proportional to the intensity of incident radiation (\(\nu\) constant).
  • Photoelectrons ejected from the metal have kinetic energies from 0 to \(KE_{max}\), where \(KE_{max}=eV_s\) and \(V_s\) is the stopping potential.
  • Stopping potential is independent of the intensity of light used (\(\nu\) constant).

Einstein's equation for the photoelectric effect:

\[h\nu=W_0+K_{max}\ \Rightarrow\ \frac{hc}\lambda=\frac{hc}{\lambda_0}+eV_s\]

Intensity in terms of the electric field

\[I=\tfrac12\epsilon_0E^2c\]

Photon momentum and energy

\[p=\frac h\lambda\qquad\Delta E=\frac{12400}{\lambda(\text{Å})}\ \text{eV}\]

Force due to radiation (photon), no transmission

Absorption \(a\), reflection \(r\), with \(a+r=1\).

Light incident perpendicularly

CaseForcePressure
\(a=1,\ r=0\)\(\dfrac{IA}c\)\(\dfrac Ic\)
\(r=1,\ a=0\)\(\dfrac{2IA}c\)\(\dfrac{2I}c\)
\(0\lt r\lt1,\ a+r=1\)\(\dfrac{IA}c(1+r)\)\(\dfrac Ic(1+r)\)

Light incident at an angle \(\theta\) with the normal

CaseForcePressure
\(a=1,\ r=0\)\(\dfrac{IA\cos\theta}c\)\(\dfrac{F\cos\theta}A=\dfrac Ic\cos^2\theta\)
\(r=1,\ a=0\)\(\dfrac{2IA\cos^2\theta}c\)\(\dfrac{2I\cos^2\theta}c\)
\(0\lt r\lt1,\ a+r=1\)\(\dfrac{I\cos^2\theta}c(1+r)\)

De Broglie wavelength

\[\lambda=\frac h{mv}=\frac hP=\frac h{\sqrt{2mKE}}\]

Hydrogen-like atoms

Radius and speed of the electron in the \(n^{th}\) orbit:

\[r_n=\frac{n^2}Za_0,\quad a_0=0.529\ \text{Å}\qquad v_n=\frac Znv_0,\quad v_0=2.19\times10^6\ \text{m/s}\]

Energy in the \(n^{th}\) orbit:

\[E_n=E_1\frac{Z^2}{n^2},\qquad E_1=-13.6\ \text{eV}\]

Wavelength of spectral lines:

\[\frac1\lambda=R\left[\frac1{n_1^2}-\frac1{n_2^2}\right]\]
Series\(n_1\)\(n_2\)
Lyman12, 3, 4, …
Balmer23, 4, 5, …
Paschen34, 5, 6, …

The Lyman series is in the ultraviolet; the Paschen, Brackett and Pfund series are in the infrared region.

Total number of possible transitions from the \(n^{th}\) state: \(\dfrac{n(n-1)}2\)

If the motion of the nucleus is considered:

\[r_n=(0.529\,\text{Å})\frac{n^2}Z\cdot\frac m\mu\qquad E_n=(-13.6\ \text{eV})\frac{Z^2}{n^2}\cdot\frac\mu m\]

where \(\mu\) is the reduced mass, \(\mu=\dfrac{Mm}{M+m}\), and \(M\) is the mass of the nucleus.

X-rays

Minimum wavelength:

\[\lambda_{min}=\frac{hc}{eV_0}=\frac{12400}{V_0\,(\text{volt})}\ \text{Å}\]

Moseley's law: \(\sqrt\nu=a(z-b)\), where \(a\) and \(b\) are positive constants for one type of X-ray (independent of \(Z\)).

Nucleus

Average radius: \(R=R_0A^{1/3}\), with \(R_0=1.1\times10^{-15}\) m and \(A\) the mass number.

Binding energy of a nucleus of mass \(M\):

\[B=(ZM_p+NM_N-M)c^2\]

Radioactive decay

Alpha decay:

\[{}^A_ZX\to{}^{A-4}_{Z-2}Y+{}^4_2\text{He}\qquad Q=\left[m\left({}^A_ZX\right)-m\left({}^{A-4}_{Z-2}Y\right)-m\left({}^4_2\text{He}\right)\right]c^2\]

Beta-minus decay:

\[{}^A_ZX\to{}^A_{Z+1}Y+\beta^-+\bar\nu\qquad Q=\left[m\left({}^A_ZX\right)-m\left({}^A_{Z+1}Y\right)\right]c^2\]

Beta-plus decay:

\[{}^A_ZX\to{}^A_{Z-1}Y+\beta^++\nu\qquad Q=\left[m\left({}^A_ZX\right)-m\left({}^A_{Z-1}Y\right)-2m_e\right]c^2\]

Electron capture (X-rays are emitted when an atomic electron is captured):

\[{}^A_ZX+e\to{}^A_{Z-1}Y+\nu\qquad Q=\left[m\left({}^A_ZX\right)-m\left({}^A_{Z-1}Y\right)\right]c^2\]

Law of radioactive decay

The number of nuclei at time \(t\), where \(\lambda\) is the decay constant:

\[N=N_0e^{-\lambda t}\qquad\text{Activity }A=A_0e^{-\lambda t}\]\[\text{Half life }T_{1/2}=\frac{0.693}\lambda\qquad\text{Average life }T_{av}=\frac{T_{1/2}}{0.693}\]

Activity per unit mass is called specific activity. If a nucleus can decay by two processes with half-lives \(t_1\) and \(t_2\), the effective half-life is

\[\frac1t=\frac1{t_1}+\frac1{t_2}\]

Photoelectric effect: complete set of results

\[E=h\nu=\frac{hc}\lambda=\frac{12400}{\lambda(\text{Å})}\ \text{eV}=\frac{1240}{\lambda(\text{nm})}\ \text{eV},\qquad h\nu=\phi+K_{\max},\qquad K_{\max}=eV_s\]
  • Constants: \(h=6.63\times10^{-34}\) J s \(=4.14\times10^{-15}\) eV s, \(hc=1240\) eV nm, \(1\ \text{eV}=1.6\times10^{-19}\) J. Work function \(\phi=h\nu_0=\dfrac{hc}{\lambda_0}\): the minimum energy to remove an electron from the metal surface (about \(2\) eV for the alkali metals Cs, K, Na; \(4\)–\(5\) eV for Zn, Al, Cu, Ag; \(5.6\) eV for Pt).
  • Laws: (i) for a given metal there is a threshold frequency \(\nu_0\) below which no emission occurs, however intense the light; (ii) the saturation photocurrent is proportional to the intensity (\(\nu\gt\nu_0\)); (iii) \(K_{\max}\) and \(V_s\) depend on the frequency, not on the intensity, increasing linearly with \(\nu\); (iv) emission is instantaneous (\(\sim10^{-9}\) s). The wave theory fails on all four points; Einstein’s photon (quantum) picture explains them. Each photon gives all its energy to one electron.
  • Photon flux: a source of power \(P\) and wavelength \(\lambda\) emits \(n=\dfrac P{h\nu}=\dfrac{P\lambda}{hc}\) photons per second; an intensity \(I\) delivers \(\dfrac{I}{h\nu}\) photons per unit area per second. If a fraction \(\eta\) of the photons eject electrons, \(i_{\text{sat}}=\eta\,n\,e\). For a point source the photocurrent \(\propto\dfrac1{r^2}\), but \(V_s\) is unchanged with \(r\).
  • Applied potential: with an accelerating potential \(V\) the kinetic energy of the electrons reaching the anode is \(K=K_{\max}+eV\) (maximum); with a retarding potential \(V\lt V_s\) the current falls but is not zero; at \(V=V_s\) the current is zero. The photocurrent for \(V\gt0\) saturates when all the emitted electrons reach the anode.
  • Isolated metal body (sphere of radius \(r\)) illuminated: it loses electrons and gets positively charged until its potential stops further emission: \(V_{\max}=\dfrac{h\nu-\phi}e\), \(Q_{\max}=4\pi\epsilon_0rV_{\max}\). Photoelectrons entering a uniform magnetic field \(B\) perpendicular to their velocity move in circles of radius \(r=\dfrac{mv}{eB}=\dfrac{\sqrt{2mK}}{eB}\).
  • Mass of a photon (effective): \(m=\dfrac{h\nu}{c^2}=\dfrac h{\lambda c}\); rest mass zero; \(E=pc\); a photon is deflected by gravity (bending of starlight) but not by electric or magnetic fields.
  • Applications of the photocell: burglar alarm, automatic door opener, smoke detection, exposure meters, sound film, photomultiplier, solar cells (photovoltaic). Other photo-effects: photoconductive effect (resistance falls), photovoltaic effect.

Photoelectric effect: graphs and common problem types

  • \(V_s\) versus \(\nu\): a straight line, slope \(=\dfrac he\) (the same for all metals), intercept on the \(\nu\)-axis \(=\nu_0\), intercept on the \(V_s\)-axis \(=-\dfrac\phi e\). \(K_{\max}\) versus \(\nu\): slope \(h\) (Planck’s constant, determined by Millikan), intercept on the \(K\)-axis \(=-\phi\). Different metals give parallel lines (same slope \(h\)).
  • \(i\) versus \(V\): \(i\) is zero at \(V=-V_s\) and rises to a plateau at the saturation value. For a higher intensity at the same frequency the plateau is higher but the stopping potential is the same; for a higher frequency with the same number of photons per second the plateau is the same but \(V_s\) is larger in magnitude; for the same power a higher frequency means fewer photons, hence a lower plateau.
  • Two wavelengths on the same metal: \(\dfrac{hc}{\lambda_1}-\phi=eV_1\), \(\dfrac{hc}{\lambda_2}-\phi=eV_2\); subtracting gives \(h=\dfrac{e(V_1-V_2)\lambda_1\lambda_2}{c(\lambda_2-\lambda_1)}\) and \(\phi\) follows. If the light of frequency \(2\nu\) gives \(K_2\) when frequency \(\nu\) gives \(K_1\): \(K_2=K_1+h\nu\).
  • If \(\lambda\) is reduced from \(\lambda_1\) to \(\lambda_2\), the stopping potential increases by \(\Delta V_s=\dfrac{hc}e\left(\dfrac1{\lambda_2}-\dfrac1{\lambda_1}\right)\); the maximum speed is \(v_{\max}=\sqrt{\dfrac{2(h\nu-\phi)}m}\). The slowest photoelectrons have zero energy (those that lose energy to collisions on the way out); the range of energies is \(0\) to \(K_{\max}\).
  • Light of power \(P\) incident on a surface with a photon-to-electron efficiency \(\eta\): current \(i=\dfrac{\eta Pe\lambda}{hc}\). The momentum transferred to a perfectly absorbing plate by the light is \(\dfrac Pc\) per unit time (force), which is why the pressure of radiation is \(\dfrac Ic\).
  • Photon number in an electromagnetic wave: the number density of photons \(n=\dfrac{I}{ch\nu}\) (photons per unit volume) in a beam of intensity \(I\); the energy density of the beam is \(u=nh\nu=\dfrac Ic\).

Photon–particle interactions: Compton effect, pair production, recoil

  • Compton effect (scattering of an X-ray or \(\gamma\) photon by a free electron at rest): \(\lambda'-\lambda=\dfrac h{m_ec}(1-\cos\theta)=\lambda_C(1-\cos\theta)\), with the Compton wavelength \(\lambda_C=\dfrac h{m_ec}=2.43\times10^{-12}\) m. The shift is zero at \(\theta=0\) and maximum, \(2\lambda_C\), at \(\theta=180^\circ\); it is independent of \(\lambda\) and of the scatterer. Conservation of energy and momentum for the photon–electron collision: \(h\nu+m_ec^2=h\nu'+(K+m_ec^2)\), \(\vec p=\vec p'+\vec p_e\). The kinetic energy of the recoil electron: \(K=hc\left(\dfrac1\lambda-\dfrac1{\lambda'}\right)\). Compton scattering is the evidence for photon momentum (and the particle nature of light); the shift is observable only for \(\lambda\sim\lambda_C\) (X-rays, \(\gamma\)-rays).
  • Pair production: a photon of energy \(E\ge2m_ec^2=1.02\) MeV (near a nucleus, which absorbs the momentum) gives an electron–positron pair; the excess energy appears as kinetic energy: \(h\nu=2m_ec^2+K_{e^-}+K_{e^+}\). Annihilation: \(e^-+e^+\to2\gamma\) (each \(0.511\) MeV, emitted in opposite directions to conserve momentum); at least two photons are needed.
  • Photon emission by a free atom at rest: the atom recoils with momentum \(p=\dfrac{h\nu}c\); recoil kinetic energy \(\dfrac{(h\nu)^2}{2Mc^2}\), so the photon energy is slightly less than the energy gap: \(h\nu=\Delta E-\dfrac{(h\nu)^2}{2Mc^2}\). Recoil speed \(v=\dfrac{h\nu}{Mc}\). For absorption the photon needs slightly more than the energy gap.
  • Mass–energy relation: \(E=mc^2\); \(1\) u \(=931.5\) MeV\(/c^2\) \(=1.66\times10^{-27}\) kg; \(m_ec^2=0.511\) MeV, \(m_pc^2=938.3\) MeV, \(m_nc^2=939.6\) MeV.
  • Energy and momentum of a relativistic particle (for high-energy collisions): \(E^2=p^2c^2+m_0^2c^4\), \(K=E-m_0c^2\). For a photon \(E=pc\).

Matter waves, Davisson–Germer experiment and uncertainty principle

\[\lambda=\frac hp=\frac h{mv}=\frac h{\sqrt{2mK}},\qquad \lambda_e=\frac{12.27}{\sqrt V}\ \text{Å}\ \left(=\sqrt{\tfrac{150}V}\ \text{Å}\right),\qquad \Delta x\,\Delta p\ge\frac h{4\pi}\]
  • For a charged particle \(q\) accelerated through a potential difference \(V\): \(\lambda=\dfrac h{\sqrt{2mqV}}\). Electron: \(\dfrac{12.27}{\sqrt V}\) Å; proton: \(\dfrac{0.286}{\sqrt V}\) Å; deuteron: \(\dfrac{0.202}{\sqrt V}\) Å; \(\alpha\)-particle: \(\dfrac{0.101}{\sqrt V}\) Å. A neutron of energy \(E\) (eV): \(\lambda=\dfrac{0.286}{\sqrt E}\) Å; a thermal neutron or gas molecule at temperature \(T\): \(\lambda=\dfrac h{\sqrt{3mkT}}\) (\(\approx1.5\) Å for a neutron at \(300\) K; of the order of the lattice spacing, so used in diffraction).
  • Comparisons: for equal \(\lambda\) a photon and an electron have \(\dfrac{E_{\text{photon}}}{K_e}=\dfrac{2c}v\); for equal kinetic energy the lighter particle has the larger \(\lambda\) (\(\lambda\propto\dfrac1{\sqrt m}\)); for equal momentum the \(\lambda\) is the same. Velocity of a matter wave: the group velocity \(v_g=v\) (particle velocity), the phase velocity \(v_p=\dfrac{c^2}v\gt c\).
  • Bohr’s quantisation from de Broglie: a stable orbit contains a whole number of waves, \(2\pi r=n\lambda\), giving \(mvr=\dfrac{nh}{2\pi}\).
  • Davisson–Germer (1927): electrons of \(54\) eV (\(\lambda=1.67\) Å) scattered from a nickel crystal show a maximum at a scattering angle of \(50^\circ\): \(d\sin\phi=\lambda\) with \(d=2.15\) Å agrees with the de Broglie wavelength. G. P. Thomson observed electron diffraction by thin metal foils. Electron diffraction and neutron diffraction are used to study crystal structure; the electron microscope has a much better resolving power than the optical microscope because the electrons’ \(\lambda\) is far smaller.
  • Heisenberg’s uncertainty principle: \(\Delta x\,\Delta p_x\ge\dfrac h{4\pi}=\dfrac\hbar2\); \(\Delta E\,\Delta t\ge\dfrac\hbar2\); \(\Delta L\,\Delta\theta\ge\dfrac\hbar2\). It follows from the wave nature of matter. Consequences: an electron cannot exist in the nucleus (confining an electron within \(10^{-14}\) m would need a kinetic energy of the order of \(10\) MeV or more, far above the energies of the electrons emitted in \(\beta\)-decay); an atom has a zero-point energy; a particle in a box has \(E_{\min}\ne0\).
  • Particle in a one-dimensional box of length \(L\) (standing de Broglie waves): \(L=\dfrac{n\lambda}2\), \(p_n=\dfrac{nh}{2L}\), \(E_n=\dfrac{n^2h^2}{8mL^2}\).
  • The wave function \(\psi\) and its probability interpretation: \(|\psi|^2dV\) is the probability of finding the particle in the volume \(dV\) (the Born interpretation); a free particle of definite momentum has a plane wave \(\psi\propto e^{i(kx-\omega t)}\) with \(k=\dfrac{2\pi}\lambda\).

Bohr model: all the standard results

Postulates: (i) electrons revolve in certain stationary circular orbits without radiating; (ii) the angular momentum is quantised, \(L=mvr=\dfrac{nh}{2\pi}=n\hbar\) (\(n=1,2,3,\dots\)); (iii) radiation is emitted or absorbed only in transitions: \(h\nu=E_{n_2}-E_{n_1}\).

\[r_n=\frac{n^2}Z\,a_0\ (a_0=0.529\ \text{Å}),\qquad v_n=\frac ZnC_0,\ \ C_0=\frac{e^2}{2\epsilon_0h}=2.19\times10^6\ \text{m s}^{-1},\qquad E_n=-13.6\,\frac{Z^2}{n^2}\ \text{eV}\]
  • \(\dfrac{v_n}c=\dfrac{Z}{137\,n}\) (the fine-structure constant \(\alpha=\dfrac1{137}\)). Kinetic energy \(K=-E=+13.6\dfrac{Z^2}{n^2}\) eV; potential energy \(U=2E=-27.2\dfrac{Z^2}{n^2}\) eV; \(|E|=K=\dfrac{|U|}2\). The total energy increases (becomes less negative) with \(n\); the ground state has \(E=-13.6\) eV for \(\text{H}\), \(-54.4\) eV for \(\text{He}^+\), \(-122.4\) eV for \(\text{Li}^{2+}\).
  • Quantities with \(n\) and \(Z\): \(r\propto\dfrac{n^2}Z\); \(v\propto\dfrac Zn\); \(E\propto\dfrac{Z^2}{n^2}\); time period \(T_n=\dfrac{2\pi r_n}{v_n}=\dfrac{n^3}{Z^2}T_1\) (\(T_1=1.52\times10^{-16}\) s); frequency \(f_n=\dfrac{Z^2}{n^3}\,(6.58\times10^{15}\ \text{Hz})\); equivalent current \(i=ef\propto\dfrac{Z^2}{n^3}\) (\(1.05\) mA for \(n=1\) in H); magnetic field at the nucleus \(B\propto\dfrac{Z^3}{n^5}\) (\(12.5\) T for \(n=1\) in H); orbital magnetic moment \(\mu=\dfrac{evr}2=\dfrac{e}{2m}L=n\mu_B\), with the Bohr magneton \(\mu_B=\dfrac{eh}{4\pi m}=9.27\times10^{-24}\) A m\(^2\); \(\dfrac{\mu}{L}=\dfrac e{2m}\) (gyromagnetic ratio).
  • Excitation and ionisation: ionisation energy of H \(=13.6\) eV; first excitation (\(1\to2\)) \(=10.2\) eV; second (\(1\to3\)) \(=12.09\) eV; \(1\to4\): \(12.75\) eV. A photon excites the atom only if its energy equals exactly a level difference (or exceeds the ionisation energy, the excess going to the free electron); an electron of kinetic energy \(K\) can excite it to any level with a gap \(\le K\) (partial transfer allowed). If two identical hydrogen atoms collide head-on with one initially at rest, an excitation by a collision requires at least \(2\Delta E\) of kinetic energy (\(20.4\) eV) because half of the kinetic energy is retained as the motion of the centre of mass.
  • Photon energies: \(2\to1\): \(10.2\) eV (\(1216\) Å, Lyman-\(\alpha\)); \(3\to2\): \(1.89\) eV (\(6563\) Å, Balmer H\(_\alpha\), red); \(4\to2\): \(2.55\) eV (\(4861\) Å, H\(_\beta\), blue-green); \(\infty\to2\): \(3.4\) eV (\(3646\) Å).
  • Reduced mass: replace \(m\) by \(\mu=\dfrac{mM}{m+M}\) (the Rydberg constant becomes \(R_M=\dfrac{R_\infty}{1+m/M}\)). Positronium (e\(^-\), e\(^+\)): \(\mu=\dfrac m2\), so \(E_n=-\dfrac{6.8}{n^2}\) eV and the radii are doubled; muonic hydrogen (\(\mu^-\) with \(m_\mu\approx207m_e\)): much smaller orbits (about \(\tfrac1{186}\)) and larger binding energy; deuterium has slightly shorter wavelengths than hydrogen (isotope shift).
  • Franck–Hertz experiment (mercury vapour): electrons lose energy in discrete steps of \(4.9\) eV, proving the discrete energy levels. Limitations of the Bohr model: it holds only for one-electron atoms; it does not explain fine structure, intensities or the Zeeman effect, and cannot be applied to molecules; the electron has no definite orbit in quantum mechanics.
  • Laser (Light Amplification by Stimulated Emission of Radiation): stimulated emission by a photon of energy equal to the gap, giving a coherent photon; requires population inversion (more atoms in a metastable excited state than in the lower state, achieved by pumping) and an optical resonant cavity; the beam is monochromatic, coherent, highly directional and intense (He–Ne laser \(6328\) Å; ruby \(6943\) Å).

Hydrogen spectrum: series, limits and counting lines

\[\frac1\lambda=RZ^2\left(\frac1{n_1^2}-\frac1{n_2^2}\right),\qquad R=1.097\times10^7\ \text{m}^{-1},\qquad hcR=13.6\ \text{eV},\qquad \frac1R=911\ \text{Å}\]
Series\(n_1\)\(n_2\)RegionLongest \(\lambda\) (Å)Series limit (Å)
Lyman1\(2,3,\dots\)Ultraviolet1216912
Balmer2\(3,4,\dots\)Visible (and near UV)65633646
Paschen3\(4,5,\dots\)Infrared187518204
Brackett4\(5,6,\dots\)Infrared4050014585
Pfund5\(6,7,\dots\)Far infrared7460022790
  • For a series, the longest wavelength (first line) corresponds to \(n_2=n_1+1\) and the shortest (series limit) to \(n_2=\infty\): \(\lambda_{\min}=\dfrac{n_1^2}{RZ^2}\). The ratio of the longest to the shortest wavelength in a series is \(\dfrac{\lambda_{\max}}{\lambda_{\min}}=\dfrac{(n_1+1)^2}{2n_1+1}\): \(\dfrac43\) for Lyman, \(\dfrac95\) for Balmer, \(\dfrac{16}7\) for Paschen. The lines of a series crowd together toward the series limit.
  • Number of lines: a sample of many atoms excited to the level \(n\) can emit \(\dfrac{n(n-1)}2\) different lines in all; the number of lines in the series that end at level \(m\) from the level \(n\) is \(n-m\); a single atom can emit at most \(n-1\) photons while cascading down (one transition per photon).
  • Hydrogen-like ions: for the same \(n_1,n_2\) the photon energies of \(\text{He}^+\) are \(Z^2=4\) times those of H (wavelengths one quarter). A transition between levels \(2n_1\) and \(2n_2\) of \(\text{He}^+\) has the same wavelength as the \(n_1\to n_2\) transition of H (e.g. \(\text{He}^+\) \(4\to2\) equals H \(2\to1\), and \(\text{He}^+\) \(6\to4\) equals H \(3\to2\)); similarly for \(\text{Li}^{2+}\) with multiples of \(3\).
  • A hydrogen atom in the ground state absorbing a photon of energy \(E\ge10.2\) eV (matching a gap) can emit several lines when it falls back; if a beam of electrons of energy \(12.5\) eV excites ground-state H, the levels up to \(n=3\) are excited (\(12.09\) eV) and three lines are emitted (\(3\to1\), \(3\to2\), \(2\to1\)).
  • Absorption spectrum of H at ordinary temperature shows only the Lyman lines (all atoms are in the ground state); the Balmer lines appear in absorption only from excited atoms (stars).

X-rays: production, spectrum, Moseley’s law and Bragg’s law

  • Production (Coolidge tube): electrons from a heated filament are accelerated through a high potential \(V\) (tens of kV) onto a metal target of high atomic number and melting point (tungsten, molybdenum); about \(99\%\) of the energy appears as heat (the efficiency is about \(10^{-9}\,ZV\)), so the target is cooled. X-ray wavelengths: about \(0.1\)–\(100\) Å (\(10^{-11}\)–\(10^{-8}\) m); they travel at \(c\) and are not deflected by electric or magnetic fields.
  • Continuous spectrum (bremsstrahlung: electrons decelerated by the nuclei) with a sharp short-wavelength cut-off \(\lambda_{\min}=\dfrac{hc}{eV}=\dfrac{12400}{V}\) Å (Duane–Hunt law), independent of the target; \(\lambda_{\min}\) falls as \(V\) rises. Raising the filament current increases the number of electrons and hence the intensity of all the X-rays but leaves \(\lambda_{\min}\) unchanged.
  • Characteristic spectrum: sharp lines (K, L, M series) that depend only on the target material. A vacancy in the K shell filled from the L shell gives \(K_\alpha\), from the M shell gives \(K_\beta\) (\(K_\beta\) is of shorter wavelength than \(K_\alpha\)); L to M gives \(L_\alpha\). The characteristic lines appear only when \(eV\) exceeds the ionisation energy of the corresponding shell; their wavelengths do not change with \(V\).
  • Moseley’s law: \(\sqrt\nu=a(Z-b)\), where \(a\) and \(b\) are constants for a given line series. For \(K_\alpha\): \(\nu=\dfrac34Rc\,(Z-1)^2\), i.e. \(\nu=2.47\times10^{15}(Z-1)^2\) Hz (\(b=1\)); for \(L_\alpha\): \(\nu=\dfrac5{36}Rc\,(Z-7.4)^2\) (\(b\approx7.4\)). The plot of \(\sqrt\nu\) against \(Z\) is a straight line; Moseley’s law established the atomic number as the basis of the periodic table (\(\nu\) of \(K_\alpha\) \(\propto(Z-1)^2\)). The energy of the \(K_\alpha\) photon of an element is \(10.2\,(Z-1)^2\) eV.
  • Bragg’s law (diffraction of X-rays by crystal planes): \(2d\sin\theta=n\lambda\) (\(n=1,2,3,\dots\)), where \(d\) is the interplanar spacing and \(\theta\) the glancing angle between the incident ray and the planes (not from the normal); the deviation of the beam is \(2\theta\). Reflection occurs only for \(\lambda\le2d\); the maximum order is \(n_{\max}=\left\lfloor\dfrac{2d}\lambda\right\rfloor\). For a cubic crystal of side \(a\), \(d_{hkl}=\dfrac a{\sqrt{h^2+k^2+l^2}}\). Used to find \(d\) (crystal structure, atomic spacing) from a known \(\lambda\), or to determine \(\lambda\) of X-rays from a known \(d\) (X-ray spectrometer). Methods: Laue (white X-rays, fixed crystal), rotating crystal, Debye–Scherrer (powder).
  • Intensity and absorption: \(I=I_0e^{-\mu x}\) where \(\mu\) is the linear absorption coefficient; the half-value thickness \(x_{1/2}=\dfrac{0.693}\mu\); \(\mu\) increases with \(Z\) (as \(\approx Z^4\) by the photoelectric absorption) and decreases with energy. Heavy elements (lead, bone) absorb strongly; soft tissue lets X-rays through, so bones are seen in a radiograph.
  • Hard X-rays (short \(\lambda\), high \(V\)) penetrate more; soft X-rays (long \(\lambda\)) are absorbed more. Uses: medical diagnosis (radiography, CT scan) and therapy (cancer), industrial flaw detection, crystallography, security scanning. They are harmful (ionising radiation), damaging tissue.
  • Properties: ionise gases, affect photographic plates, cause fluorescence, show the photoelectric effect and the Compton effect, and show diffraction and interference (proof of the wave nature), and polarisation (transverse waves).

Rutherford’s \(\alpha\)-scattering and atomic models

  • Thomson’s (plum pudding) model: positive charge spread uniformly through the atom of radius \(\sim10^{-10}\) m with electrons embedded; fails to explain large-angle scattering. Rutherford–Geiger–Marsden experiment: \(\alpha\)-particles from a source on a thin gold foil: most pass through undeviated, a few are deflected through large angles, about \(1\) in \(8000\) is deflected by more than \(90^\circ\) (some back-scattered). Conclusion: the positive charge and almost all the mass are concentrated in a tiny nucleus (\(\sim10^{-15}\) m, about \(10^{-5}\) of the atom’s size); the atom is mostly empty. Rutherford’s model cannot explain atomic stability (an accelerating electron should radiate and spiral in) or line spectra; Bohr’s postulates address this.
  • Distance of closest approach (head-on, \(\alpha\)-particle of kinetic energy \(K\), nucleus charge \(Ze\)): \(K=\dfrac{1}{4\pi\epsilon_0}\dfrac{(2e)(Ze)}{r_0}\Rightarrow r_0=\dfrac{2Ze^2}{4\pi\epsilon_0K}=\dfrac{2.88\,Z}{K\ (\text{MeV})}\) fm. For \(5.5\) MeV on gold (\(Z=79\)), \(r_0\approx41\) fm. The estimate of the nuclear size (upper bound). For a proton \(r_0=\dfrac{1.44Z}{K\,(\text{MeV})}\) fm.
  • Impact parameter \(b\) for a scattering angle \(\theta\): \(b=\dfrac{Ze^2}{4\pi\epsilon_0K}\cot\dfrac\theta2=\dfrac{r_0}2\cot\dfrac\theta2\) (so \(b=0\) gives \(180^\circ\), a head-on collision with the least approach). A smaller \(b\) gives a larger deflection.
  • Scattering law: the number of \(\alpha\)-particles scattered per unit solid angle \(N(\theta)\propto\dfrac{1}{\sin^4(\theta/2)}\); also \(\propto Z^2\), the foil thickness, and \(\propto\dfrac1{K^2}\).
  • Atomic and nuclear scale: atom \(\sim10^{-10}\) m (\(1\) Å), nucleus \(\sim10^{-15}\) m (\(1\) fm); angular momentum of the electron in the \(n\)-th orbit \(n\hbar\); the neutron was discovered by Chadwick (1932), the electron by J. J. Thomson (\(e/m=1.76\times10^{11}\) C kg\(^{-1}\)), the electron charge measured by Millikan’s oil-drop experiment.
25
Optics & modern physics

Nuclei & Radioactivity

Nuclear composition, size, density and nuclear forces

\[R=R_0A^{1/3}\ (R_0\approx1.2\ \text{fm}),\qquad \rho_{\text{nuc}}=\frac{3m_u}{4\pi R_0^3}\approx2.3\times10^{17}\ \text{kg m}^{-3},\qquad 1\ \text{u}=931.5\ \text{MeV}/c^2\]
  • A nucleus \({}^A_ZX\) has \(Z\) protons and \(N=A-Z\) neutrons. Isotopes: same \(Z\), different \(A\) (\({}^1\text{H},{}^2\text{H},{}^3\text{H}\)); isobars: same \(A\) (\({}^{40}\text{Ar},{}^{40}\text{Ca}\)); isotones: same \(N\); isomers: same \(A\) and \(Z\) but different energy states.
  • Nuclear volume \(\propto A\) (so every nucleon takes the same volume); the density is the same for all nuclei (independent of \(A\)), about \(10^{14}\) times that of water. Size of the nucleus \(\sim10^{-15}\) m (fermi), size of the atom \(\sim10^{-10}\) m. Radius ratio of two nuclei: \(\dfrac{R_1}{R_2}=\left(\dfrac{A_1}{A_2}\right)^{1/3}\); e.g. for \({}^{27}\text{Al}\) \(R=3.6\) fm, for \({}^{125}\text{Te}\) \(R=6\) fm.
  • Masses: \(m_p=1.00728\) u \(=938.27\) MeV\(/c^2\); \(m_n=1.00866\) u \(=939.57\) MeV\(/c^2\); \(m_e=0.000549\) u \(=0.511\) MeV\(/c^2\). \(1\) u \(=\dfrac1{12}\) the mass of a \({}^{12}\text{C}\) atom \(=1.66\times10^{-27}\) kg.
  • Nuclear force: strong (about \(100\) times the electromagnetic force and \(10^{38}\) times gravity at nuclear distances), attractive, short range (\(\sim1\)–\(3\) fm, negligible beyond about \(10\) fm), charge independent (p–p, n–n and p–n forces are nearly equal), saturated (each nucleon interacts only with its neighbours, so \(BE\propto A\)), spin dependent, non-central and non-conservative; mediated by the exchange of \(\pi\)-mesons (Yukawa). At about \(0.8\) fm the force becomes repulsive.
  • Stability: light stable nuclei have \(N\approx Z\); heavy ones have \(N/Z\) up to about \(1.5\) (neutrons reduce the Coulomb repulsion). Nuclei with \(Z\gt83\) are all unstable. Even–even nuclei are the most stable; the “magic numbers” of \(2,8,20,28,50,82,126\) (shell model) give specially stable nuclei (e.g. \({}^4\text{He}\), \({}^{16}\text{O}\), \({}^{208}\text{Pb}\)).
  • Nuclear spin and magnetic moment: the nuclear magneton \(\mu_N=\dfrac{e\hbar}{2m_p}=5.05\times10^{-27}\) J T\(^{-1}\) (\(\approx\dfrac1{1836}\mu_B\)).

Mass defect, binding energy and the binding-energy curve
