\[\Delta m=Zm_p+(A-Z)m_n-M_{\text{nucleus}},\qquad B=\Delta m\,c^2=931.5\,\Delta m(\text{u})\ \text{MeV},\qquad \text{B.E. per nucleon}=\frac BA\]
  • With atomic masses (include the electrons): \(\Delta m=Zm_H+(A-Z)m_n-M_{\text{atom}}\), because the electron masses cancel (neglecting their binding). It is the mass converted to energy when the nucleons combine; the same energy is needed to separate the nucleus completely. Note that \(m_H\) is the mass of the hydrogen atom then.
  • Binding energy per nucleon curve: rises steeply for light nuclei (\({}^2\text{H}\): \(1.1\) MeV; \({}^3\text{H}\): \(2.8\); \({}^4\text{He}\): \(7.07\), an unusually high peak; \({}^{12}\text{C}\): \(7.7\); \({}^{16}\text{O}\): \(8.0\)), reaches a flat maximum of about \(8.7\) MeV near \(A\approx56\) (\({}^{56}\text{Fe}\), \({}^{62}\text{Ni}\)), and then falls slowly (\({}^{238}\text{U}\): \(7.6\) MeV). For most nuclei between \(A=30\) and \(170\) it is nearly constant at \(\approx8\) MeV (saturation of nuclear forces).
  • Energy release: if nuclei of lower \(\dfrac BA\) change into nuclei of higher \(\dfrac BA\), energy is released: fusion of light nuclei (\(A\lt56\)) and fission of heavy nuclei (\(A\gt56\)). Energy released \(=\) (total B.E. of the products) \(-\) (total B.E. of the reactants).
  • Separation energy of a nucleon: the energy needed to remove it, \(S=\left[M(A-1,Z)+m-M(A,Z)\right]c^2\). The binding energy of a nucleus depends on the volume (\(\propto A\)), surface (\(\propto-A^{2/3}\)), Coulomb repulsion (\(\propto-\dfrac{Z^2}{A^{1/3}}\)), asymmetry and pairing terms (semi-empirical mass formula).
  • Binding energy of the deuteron is \(2.22\) MeV (the energy of the \(\gamma\)-ray needed to split it); of the \(\alpha\)-particle \(28.3\) MeV.
  • The mass equivalent of energy: \(1\) kg \(\to9\times10^{16}\) J; \(1\) u \(\to931.5\) MeV; complete conversion of \(1\) g of matter gives \(9\times10^{13}\) J.

Radioactive decays: \(\alpha\), \(\beta\), \(\gamma\), displacement laws and Q-values

  • \(\alpha\)-decay: \({}^A_ZX\to{}^{A-4}_{Z-2}Y+{}^4_2\text{He}\); \(\alpha\)-particles are emitted with discrete energies (\(4\)–\(9\) MeV). With \(Q\) the energy released and the parent at rest, momentum conservation gives \(K_\alpha=Q\dfrac{M_d}{M_d+m_\alpha}\approx Q\left(\dfrac{A-4}{A}\right)\) and \(\dfrac{K_\alpha}{K_d}=\dfrac{M_d}{m_\alpha}\), \(\dfrac{v_d}{v_\alpha}=\dfrac{m_\alpha}{M_d}\). The \(\alpha\) is the nucleus of helium (charge \(+2e\), mass \(4\) u).
  • \(\beta^-\)-decay: \(n\to p+e^-+\bar\nu\); \(\beta^+\)-decay: \(p\to n+e^++\nu\) (needs \(Q\gt0\) with \(2m_e\) subtracted when atomic masses are used); electron capture competes with \(\beta^+\). The \(\beta\)-spectrum is continuous from \(0\) to \(K_{\max}\), because the energy is shared with the (anti)neutrino (Pauli’s neutrino hypothesis). \(\beta\) particles have energy up to a few MeV and speeds up to \(0.99c\).
  • \(\gamma\)-decay: the daughter nucleus in an excited state de-excites by emitting photons (\(0.1\)–\(\sim10\) MeV, discrete); \(A\) and \(Z\) do not change. \(\gamma\) rays are electromagnetic waves, uncharged and not deflected by fields.
  • Displacement (Soddy–Fajans) laws: an \(\alpha\) emission lowers \(Z\) by \(2\) and \(A\) by \(4\); a \(\beta^-\) emission raises \(Z\) by \(1\) (\(A\) unchanged); a \(\beta^+\) emission lowers \(Z\) by \(1\). From \({}^A_ZX\) to \({}^{A'}_{Z'}Y\): \(n_\alpha=\dfrac{A-A'}4\), \(n_{\beta^-}=2n_\alpha-(Z-Z')\). Example: \({}^{238}\text{U}\to{}^{206}\text{Pb}\) takes \(8\,\alpha\) and \(6\,\beta^-\).
  • Comparison: ionising power \(\alpha\gt\beta\gt\gamma\); penetrating power \(\gamma\gt\beta\gt\alpha\) (\(\alpha\) stopped by paper or a few cm of air, \(\beta\) by a few mm of aluminium, \(\gamma\) needs cm of lead). In a magnetic field \(\alpha\) and \(\beta^-\) bend in opposite senses with \(\beta\) deflected much more; \(\gamma\) is undeflected.
  • Radioactive series: \(4n\) (thorium; end \({}^{208}\text{Pb}\)), \(4n+1\) (neptunium; end \({}^{209}\text{Bi}\)), \(4n+2\) (uranium; end \({}^{206}\text{Pb}\)), \(4n+3\) (actinium; end \({}^{207}\text{Pb}\)). In a series the mass number changes only by \(\alpha\) emission (by \(4\)), so the family is determined by \(A\bmod4\).
  • Radioactivity is spontaneous, random and statistical (the decay of a given nucleus is unpredictable), and is unaffected by temperature, pressure or chemical combination (a nuclear, not an atomic, process). Detectors: Geiger–Müller counter, scintillation counter, cloud chamber, bubble chamber, solid-state detector.
  • Units: becquerel (1 Bq \(=1\) decay s\(^{-1}\)); curie (\(1\ \text{Ci}=3.7\times10^{10}\) Bq); rutherford (\(10^6\) Bq); absorbed dose in gray (\(1\) Gy \(=1\) J kg\(^{-1}\)); the dose equivalent in sievert (Sv).

Law of radioactive decay: all the standard results

\[N=N_0e^{-\lambda t},\quad A=\left|\frac{dN}{dt}\right|=\lambda N=A_0e^{-\lambda t},\quad T_{1/2}=\frac{\ln2}\lambda=0.693\,\tau,\quad \tau=\frac1\lambda=1.44\,T_{1/2}\]
  • After \(n\) half-lives (\(t=nT_{1/2}\)): \(N=\dfrac{N_0}{2^n}\); the fraction left \(=\left(\dfrac12\right)^{t/T_{1/2}}\); the fraction decayed \(=1-\left(\dfrac12\right)^{t/T_{1/2}}\). The time for a fraction \(f\) to remain: \(t=\tau\ln\dfrac1f=\dfrac{T_{1/2}}{0.693}\ln\dfrac1f\). After one mean life \(\tau\), \(\dfrac1e\approx37\%\) remains (\(63\%\) has decayed); after \(10\) half-lives about \(0.1\%\) remains. Note that the fraction does not decrease to zero in two half-lives.
  • Probability that a nucleus decays in a time \(t\): \(1-e^{-\lambda t}\); survival probability \(e^{-\lambda t}\); the probability of decay in a short time \(dt\) is \(\lambda\,dt\). Number of nuclei decaying between \(t_1\) and \(t_2\): \(N_0\left(e^{-\lambda t_1}-e^{-\lambda t_2}\right)\). The mean life is the average of the lifetimes of all the nuclei: \(\tau=\dfrac1\lambda\) (the median life is the half-life).
  • Number of nuclei in a sample of mass \(m\) with molar mass \(M\): \(N=\dfrac mMN_A\). Activity \(A=\lambda N\); specific activity \(=\dfrac{A}{m}\). Activity in curie or becquerel; activity falls to half in a half-life. Do not confuse the activity (decays per second) with the number of nuclei.
  • Two parallel decay channels (decay constants \(\lambda_1,\lambda_2\)): \(\lambda=\lambda_1+\lambda_2\), \(\dfrac1{T}=\dfrac1{T_1}+\dfrac1{T_2}\); the branching fractions are \(\dfrac{\lambda_1}\lambda\) and \(\dfrac{\lambda_2}\lambda\).
  • Successive decays \(A\xrightarrow{\lambda_A}B\xrightarrow{\lambda_B}C\): \(\dfrac{dN_B}{dt}=\lambda_AN_A-\lambda_BN_B\), and \(N_B=\dfrac{\lambda_AN_{A0}}{\lambda_B-\lambda_A}\left(e^{-\lambda_At}-e^{-\lambda_Bt}\right)\) (with \(N_B(0)=0\)); \(N_B\) is maximum at \(t=\dfrac{\ln(\lambda_B/\lambda_A)}{\lambda_B-\lambda_A}\) when \(\lambda_AN_A=\lambda_BN_B\). Transient equilibrium (\(T_A\gt T_B\)): \(\dfrac{N_B}{N_A}=\dfrac{\lambda_A}{\lambda_B-\lambda_A}\); secular equilibrium (\(T_A\gg T_B\)): \(\lambda_AN_A=\lambda_BN_B\), i.e. the activities are equal.
  • Constant production (a reactor or an accelerator forms nuclei at a constant rate \(R\)): \(N=\dfrac R\lambda\left(1-e^{-\lambda t}\right)\); the activity \(=R\left(1-e^{-\lambda t}\right)\) saturates at \(R\) after a few half-lives.
  • Radioactive dating: carbon-14 (\(T_{1/2}=5730\) y) is formed by cosmic-ray neutrons on \({}^{14}\text{N}\) and the \({}^{14}\text{C}/{}^{12}\text{C}\) ratio in living matter is \(\approx1.2\times10^{-12}\) (the activity of about \(15\) decays per minute per gram of carbon). After death \(A=A_0e^{-\lambda t}\), so the age \(t=\dfrac{T_{1/2}}{0.693}\ln\dfrac{A_0}A\). For uranium dating of rocks (\(T_{1/2}({}^{238}\text{U})=4.5\times10^9\) y) \(t=\dfrac1\lambda\ln\left(1+\dfrac{N_{\text{Pb}}}{N_{\text{U}}}\right)\). Typical half-lives: \({}^{226}\text{Ra}\) \(1600\) y, \({}^{60}\text{Co}\) \(5.27\) y, \({}^{131}\text{I}\) \(8\) days, \({}^{222}\text{Rn}\) \(3.8\) days, \({}^{235}\text{U}\) \(7\times10^8\) y.

Nuclear reactions, fission, fusion and reactors

  • Nuclear reaction \(a+X\to Y+b\): charge, mass number (nucleon number), energy, linear and angular momentum are conserved. \(Q=\left(\sum m_{\text{reactants}}-\sum m_{\text{products}}\right)c^2\); \(Q\gt0\) exothermic (energy released), \(Q\lt0\) endothermic. A threshold energy is needed for endothermic reactions: \(K_{th}=|Q|\left(1+\dfrac{m_a}{m_X}\right)\) (projectile \(a\) on a stationary target \(X\)). Examples: \({}^{14}\text{N}+\alpha\to{}^{17}\text{O}+p\) (Rutherford, first artificial transmutation); \({}^9\text{Be}+\alpha\to{}^{12}\text{C}+n\) (Chadwick, discovery of the neutron); \({}^7\text{Li}+p\to2\,{}^4\text{He}\) (\(Q=17.3\) MeV; Cockcroft–Walton).
  • Fission: a heavy nucleus splits into two medium nuclei, e.g. \({}^{235}\text{U}+n\to{}^{141}\text{Ba}+{}^{92}\text{Kr}+3n+\approx200\) MeV (about \(0.85\) MeV per nucleon; \(\approx8\times10^{10}\) J per gram of \({}^{235}\text{U}\)). The energy is mostly the kinetic energy of the fragments (\(\approx168\) MeV), plus neutrons, prompt and delayed \(\gamma\), \(\beta\) and neutrinos. The average number of neutrons per fission is \(\approx2.5\), with average energy \(\approx2\) MeV (fast neutrons).
  • Chain reaction: multiplication factor \(k=\dfrac{\text{neutrons in a generation}}{\text{neutrons in the preceding generation}}\); \(k=1\) critical (steady, nuclear reactor), \(k\gt1\) supercritical (explosive, atomic bomb), \(k\lt1\) subcritical. Critical mass: the minimum mass for a sustained chain reaction. \({}^{235}\text{U}\) is fissioned by slow (thermal, \(\approx0.025\) eV) neutrons; \({}^{238}\text{U}\) captures neutrons and gives \({}^{239}\text{Pu}\) via two \(\beta^-\) decays (breeder).
  • Reactor parts: fuel (enriched uranium, \(\approx3\%\) \({}^{235}\text{U}\)); moderator (heavy water, graphite, ordinary water) to slow the fast neutrons by elastic collisions — light nuclei are best (a head-on elastic collision with a nucleus of mass number \(A\) leaves the neutron a fraction \(\left(\dfrac{A-1}{A+1}\right)^2\) of its energy); control rods (cadmium, boron) absorb neutrons to control \(k\); coolant (water, liquid sodium); shielding (concrete). Uncontrolled energy release forms the atomic bomb.
  • Fusion: light nuclei fuse, e.g. \({}^2\text{H}+{}^2\text{H}\to{}^3\text{He}+n+3.27\) MeV, \({}^2\text{H}+{}^3\text{H}\to{}^4\text{He}+n+17.6\) MeV (about \(3.5\) MeV per nucleon, more than fission per nucleon), and the proton–proton chain in the Sun: \(4\,{}^1\text{H}\to{}^4\text{He}+2e^++2\nu+26.7\) MeV (also the carbon–nitrogen cycle in hotter stars). The Coulomb barrier needs a temperature of about \(10^7\)–\(10^8\) K (thermonuclear reactions); hence the hydrogen bomb and tokamak (magnetic confinement) research. Conditions: high temperature, high density and long enough confinement (Lawson’s criterion).
  • The Sun radiates about \(3.8\times10^{26}\) W, converting about \(4\times10^9\) kg of mass per second into energy. Neutron-induced reactions are efficient because the neutron is uncharged and meets no Coulomb barrier; the proton needs about \(1\) MeV to approach another proton to the nuclear range.
  • Nuclear waste: fission products are radioactive and require shielding and long storage; the three-stage nuclear power programme of India uses natural uranium, then plutonium, then thorium–\({}^{233}\text{U}\).

Elementary particles and fundamental forces

  • Antiparticles: each particle has an antiparticle of the same mass and opposite charge (positron \(e^+\) of Dirac; antiproton); a particle and its antiparticle annihilate into photons. Neutrinos \(\nu_e,\nu_\mu,\nu_\tau\) have tiny masses and almost no interaction.
  • Leptons (do not feel the strong force): \(e^-,\mu^-,\tau^-\) and their neutrinos. Hadrons (feel the strong force): baryons (\(p\), \(n\), \(\Lambda\), … with baryon number \(1\)) and mesons (\(\pi^+,\pi^-,\pi^0\), \(K\), baryon number \(0\)). Hadrons are made of quarks: up (\(+\tfrac23e\)), down (\(-\tfrac13e\)), strange (\(-\tfrac13e\)), charm, bottom, top; a proton is \(uud\) (charge \(+1\)), a neutron \(udd\) (charge \(0\)), a \(\pi^+\) is \(u\bar d\).
  • Fundamental forces with their mediating particles: strong (gluons; relative strength \(1\); range \(\sim1\) fm), electromagnetic (photon; \(\sim10^{-2}\); infinite range), weak (\(W^\pm,Z^0\); \(\sim10^{-6}\); range \(\sim10^{-18}\) m, responsible for \(\beta\)-decay), gravitational (graviton; \(\sim10^{-38}\); infinite range).
  • Conservation laws: energy, momentum, angular momentum, electric charge, baryon number, lepton number (separately for each lepton family). Example: in \(\beta^-\)-decay \(n\to p+e^-+\bar\nu\) the lepton number is \(0=0+1-1\). The Higgs boson (mass \(\approx125\) GeV/\(c^2\)) was found at CERN in 2012.
26
Optics & modern physics

Wave Optics

Interference of waves of intensity \(I_1\) and \(I_2\)

\[I=I_1+I_2+2\sqrt{I_1I_2}\cos(\Delta\phi)\qquad\Delta\phi=\text{phase difference}=\frac{2\pi}\lambda\Delta p\]\[\text{Constructive: }I_{max}=\left(\sqrt{I_1}+\sqrt{I_2}\right)^2\qquad\text{Destructive: }I_{min}=\left(\sqrt{I_1}-\sqrt{I_2}\right)^2\]

If the sources are incoherent, \(I=I_1+I_2\) at each point. If \(I_1=I_2\), then \(I=4I_1\cos^2\!\left(\dfrac{\Delta\phi}2\right)\).

Young's double slit experiment (YDSE)

Path difference \(\Delta p=S_2P-S_1P=d\sin\theta\). If \(d\ll D\) and \(y\ll D\), then \(\Delta p=\dfrac{dy}D\).

Maxima: \(\Delta p=n\lambda\ \Rightarrow\ y=n\beta,\quad n=0,\pm1,\pm2,\ldots\)

Minima:

\[\Delta p=\begin{cases}(2n-1)\dfrac\lambda2,&n=1,2,3,\ldots\\[6pt](2n+1)\dfrac\lambda2,&n=-1,-2,-3,\ldots\end{cases}\ \Rightarrow\ y=\begin{cases}(2n-1)\dfrac\beta2,&n=1,2,3,\ldots\\[6pt](2n+1)\dfrac\beta2,&n=-1,-2,-3,\ldots\end{cases}\]

Fringe width \(\beta=\dfrac{\lambda D}d\), where \(\lambda\) is the wavelength in the medium.

Highest order maxima: \(n_{max}=\left[\dfrac d\lambda\right]\), and the total number of maxima \(=2n_{max}+1\).

Highest order minima: \(n_{max}=\left[\dfrac d\lambda+\dfrac12\right]\), and the total number of minima \(=2n_{max}\).

Square brackets mean the integer part.

YDSE with two wavelengths \(\lambda_1\) and \(\lambda_2\)

Nearest point to the central maximum where the bright fringes coincide:

\[y=n_1\beta_1=n_2\beta_2=\text{LCM of }\beta_1\text{ and }\beta_2\]

Nearest point to the central maximum where the dark fringes coincide:

\[y=\left(n_1-\tfrac12\right)\beta_1=\left(n_2-\tfrac12\right)\beta_2\]

Optical path difference

\[\Delta p_{opt}=\mu\,\Delta p\qquad\Delta\phi=\frac{2\pi}\lambda\Delta p=\frac{2\pi}{\lambda_{vacuum}}\Delta p_{opt}\]

Shift of the fringe pattern when a slab of thickness \(t\) is placed over a slit:

\[\Delta=(\mu-1)t\cdot\frac Dd=(\mu-1)t\,\frac\beta\lambda\]

YDSE with oblique incidence

The ray is incident on the slits at an inclination \(\theta_0\) to the axis of symmetry of the set-up. The central maximum is where \(\Delta p=0\), i.e. \(\theta_2=\theta_0\) (the point \(O'\)). The path difference is

\[\Delta p=\begin{cases}d(\sin\theta_0+\sin\theta)&\text{for points above }O\\d(\sin\theta_0-\sin\theta)&\text{for points between }O\text{ and }O'\\d(\sin\theta-\sin\theta_0)&\text{for points below }O'\end{cases}\]

Thin-film interference

In reflected light, \(2\mu d\) equals

\[\begin{cases}n\lambda&\text{for destructive interference}\\\left(n+\tfrac12\right)\lambda&\text{for constructive interference}\end{cases}\]

In transmitted light, \(2\mu d\) equals

\[\begin{cases}n\lambda&\text{for constructive interference}\\\left(n+\tfrac12\right)\lambda&\text{for destructive interference}\end{cases}\]

Polarisation

  • Brewster's angle: \(\mu=\tan\theta_p\). Also \(\theta_p+\theta_r=90^\circ\), i.e. the reflected and refracted rays are mutually perpendicular.
  • Law of Malus: \(I=I_0\cos^2\theta\), or \(I=KA^2\cos^2\theta\)
  • Optical activity:
\[[\alpha]^\lambda_{t^\circ C}=\frac\theta{L\times C}\]

\(\theta\) is the rotation in length \(L\) at concentration \(C\).

Diffraction (single slit of width \(a\))

  • Maxima: \(a\sin\theta=(2m+1)\dfrac\lambda2\), \(m=1,2,3,\ldots\)
  • Minima: \(\sin\theta=\dfrac{m\lambda}a\), \(m=\pm1,\pm2,\pm3,\ldots\)
  • Linear width of the central maximum: \(\dfrac{2D\lambda}a\) (screen at distance \(D\))
  • Angular width of the central maximum: \(\dfrac{2\lambda}a\)
\[I=I_0\left[\frac{\sin(\beta/2)}{\beta/2}\right]^2,\qquad\beta=\frac{2\pi a\sin\theta}\lambda\]

Resolving power

\[R=\frac\lambda{\lambda_2-\lambda_1}=\frac\lambda{\Delta\lambda}\qquad\text{where }\lambda=\frac{\lambda_1+\lambda_2}2,\ \Delta\lambda=\lambda_2-\lambda_1\]

Wavefronts, Huygens’ principle and coherence

  • Wavefront: the locus of points of the same phase. A point source gives spherical wavefronts (intensity \(\propto\dfrac1{r^2}\), amplitude \(\propto\dfrac1r\)); a line source gives cylindrical wavefronts (intensity \(\propto\dfrac1r\), amplitude \(\propto\dfrac1{\sqrt r}\)); a distant source gives plane wavefronts (constant intensity). Rays are perpendicular to the wavefronts.
  • Huygens’ principle: every point of a wavefront is a source of secondary wavelets; the new wavefront is the forward envelope of the wavelets after time \(t\) (radius \(vt\)). Light waves are transverse electromagnetic waves.
  • Reflection: the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection (\(\lambda\), \(f\) and \(v\) unchanged). Refraction: \(\dfrac{\sin i}{\sin r}=\dfrac{v_1}{v_2}=\dfrac{\lambda_1}{\lambda_2}=\dfrac{\mu_2}{\mu_1}\); the frequency does not change, the speed and wavelength decrease in a denser medium (a plane wavefront turns toward the normal). For a medium of index \(\mu\): \(v=\dfrac c\mu\), \(\lambda=\dfrac{\lambda_0}\mu\). Reflection from a denser medium changes the phase by \(\pi\) (path change \(\tfrac\lambda2\)); there is no phase change on reflection at a rarer medium or in transmission.
  • Coherent sources: have a constant phase difference (and the same frequency). Two independent ordinary sources (two bulbs) cannot give interference; coherent sources are obtained by dividing a wavefront (Young’s slits, Lloyd’s mirror, biprism) or the amplitude (thin films, Michelson interferometer) of one source; lasers are highly coherent. Temporal coherence relates to the monochromaticity (coherence time \(\tau_c\sim\dfrac1{\Delta\nu}\); coherence length \(c\tau_c\)); spatial coherence to the size of the source (the source slit must be narrow, less than \(\dfrac{\lambda D'}d\) where \(D'\) is its distance from the slits).
  • Conditions for good interference: coherent sources, nearly equal amplitudes, a small separation (\(d\ll D\)), a narrow source, monochromatic light, and the two waves with the same polarisation.

Interference: intensity, visibility, slit-width ratio and conservation of energy

\[I=I_1+I_2+2\sqrt{I_1I_2}\cos\phi,\qquad \frac{I_{\max}}{I_{\min}}=\left(\frac{a_1+a_2}{a_1-a_2}\right)^2=\left(\frac{\sqrt{I_1}+\sqrt{I_2}}{\sqrt{I_1}-\sqrt{I_2}}\right)^2,\qquad V=\frac{I_{\max}-I_{\min}}{I_{\max}+I_{\min}}=\frac{2\sqrt{I_1I_2}}{I_1+I_2}\]
  • The intensity is proportional to the square of the amplitude (\(I\propto a^2\)); for two slits of widths \(w_1,w_2\) the intensities are in the ratio \(\dfrac{I_1}{I_2}=\dfrac{w_1}{w_2}\) and the amplitudes \(\dfrac{a_1}{a_2}=\sqrt{\dfrac{w_1}{w_2}}\). If the intensity ratio of two coherent sources is \(\dfrac{I_1}{I_2}=n\), then \(\dfrac{I_{\max}}{I_{\min}}=\left(\dfrac{\sqrt n+1}{\sqrt n-1}\right)^2\); for equal sources \(I_{\min}=0\) and \(V=1\) (best contrast).
  • Energy is conserved: the average intensity over a fringe pattern is \(I_1+I_2\) (equal to that of incoherent sources); the energy is only redistributed. For two identical sources \(I_{\max}=4I_0\), \(I_{\text{avg}}=2I_0\), \(I(\Delta)=4I_0\cos^2\dfrac{\pi\Delta}\lambda\): \(I=I_{\max}\) for \(\Delta=n\lambda\); \(\dfrac{I_{\max}}2\) at \(\Delta=\dfrac\lambda4\); \(\dfrac34I_{\max}\) at \(\Delta=\dfrac\lambda6\); \(\dfrac14I_{\max}\) at \(\Delta=\dfrac\lambda3\).
  • The phase difference \(\phi=\dfrac{2\pi}\lambda\Delta\) (\(\Delta\) = path difference) \(=\dfrac{2\pi}{\lambda_0}(\Delta_{\text{optical}})\), where the optical path in a medium \(=\mu\times\) geometric path. A change of path \(\lambda\) corresponds to \(2\pi\), a change by \(\dfrac\lambda2\) to \(\pi\). When the waves are given by \(y_1=a_1\sin\omega t\), \(y_2=a_2\sin(\omega t+\phi)\), the resultant amplitude is \(\sqrt{a_1^2+a_2^2+2a_1a_2\cos\phi}\), and its phase relative to wave 1 is \(\tan\delta=\dfrac{a_2\sin\phi}{a_1+a_2\cos\phi}\).
  • Superposition of intensities from \(N\) coherent equal sources in phase: \(I=N^2I_0\) (not \(NI_0\), as for incoherent sources).

Young’s double-slit experiment: more results

  • The exact path difference at a screen point of height \(y\) (measured from the central point): \(\Delta=\sqrt{D^2+\left(y+\tfrac d2\right)^2}-\sqrt{D^2+\left(y-\tfrac d2\right)^2}\approx\dfrac{yd}D\). Bright fringes at \(y_n=\dfrac{n\lambda D}d\), dark at \(y_n=\left(n+\tfrac12\right)\dfrac{\lambda D}d\); fringe width \(\beta=\dfrac{\lambda D}d\) (equal for all fringes), angular fringe width \(\dfrac\lambda d\). \(\beta\) increases with \(\lambda\) and \(D\) and decreases with \(d\). Red fringes are wider than blue ones. If the entire apparatus is immersed in a liquid of index \(\mu\): \(\beta'=\dfrac\beta\mu\) (the pattern contracts).
  • Total number of fringes: the order of a maximum cannot exceed \(\dfrac d\lambda\) (since \(\Delta=d\sin\theta\le d\)); so the number of maxima is \(2\left[\dfrac d\lambda\right]+1\). If \(d\lt\lambda\) only the central maximum exists.
  • Shifting of the source: if the source slit (at distance \(D'\) from the slit plane) is moved by \(s\) parallel to the slit plane, the whole fringe pattern shifts by \(s\dfrac D{D'}\) in the opposite direction, with the fringe width unchanged. The central maximum is then at the point where the total path difference (source to slits plus slits to screen) is zero: \(\dfrac{sd}{D'}\pm\dfrac{yd}D=0\).
  • Thin sheet over a slit: the pattern shifts toward the slit covered by the sheet by \((\mu-1)\dfrac{tD}d\) (the number of fringes shifted \(=\dfrac{(\mu-1)t}\lambda\)); the fringe width is unchanged. If sheets are placed on both slits, the shift depends on the difference \((\mu_1-1)t_1-(\mu_2-1)t_2\) of the extra optical paths. A sheet that adds a path of exactly \(\lambda\) shifts the pattern by one fringe (the pattern looks unchanged).
  • White light: the central fringe is white (all colours are in phase), the nearby fringes are coloured (violet nearest the centre, red farthest in each order); beyond a few orders the colours overlap and produce uniform white. At a point \(y\) the wavelengths that are present (bright) satisfy \(\lambda=\dfrac{yd}{nD}\) and the missing ones (dark) satisfy \(\lambda=\dfrac{yd}{\left(n-\tfrac12\right)D}\); count only the \(n\) values that give \(400\) nm \(\le\lambda\le700\) nm.
  • Unequal slits: \(I_{\min}\ne0\) and the contrast falls; if one slit is closed, the pattern is a single-slit diffraction pattern with no fringes. If a slit is covered by a polaroid, the other by a polaroid with perpendicular axis, there is no interference (the polarisations are orthogonal).
  • Experiment details: the screen distance \(D\) and the small separation \(d\) (sub-millimetre) give fringes of a few millimetres; the double slit pattern is modulated by the single-slit diffraction envelope of width \(a\) (see diffraction).

Other two-source arrangements and the Michelson interferometer

  • Lloyd’s mirror: a slit source \(S\) at height \(h\) above a plane mirror and its image \(S'\) at \(h\) below act as coherent sources with \(d=2h\); \(\beta=\dfrac{\lambda D}{2h}\). The reflection gives a phase change of \(\pi\), so the fringe at the mirror edge (zero geometrical path difference) is dark: bright and dark fringes are interchanged compared with Young’s experiment.
  • Fresnel biprism (small prism angle \(\alpha\), index \(\mu\), at distance \(a\) from the slit; screen at distance \(b\) from the biprism): the virtual sources are separated by \(d=2a(\mu-1)\alpha\), \(D=a+b\), \(\beta=\dfrac{\lambda(a+b)}{2a(\mu-1)\alpha}\). The separation of the sources is found by the displacement method: \(d=\sqrt{d_1d_2}\) (two positions of a convex lens that give a sharp image of the sources).
  • Fresnel’s double mirror (mirrors inclined at a small angle \(\alpha\); source at distance \(a\) from the line of intersection; screen at \(b\) from it): \(d=2a\alpha\), \(D=a+b\).
  • Michelson interferometer: a beam splitter sends light along two perpendicular arms ending in mirrors \(M_1,M_2\); the beams recombine and interfere. Moving a mirror by \(\Delta x\) shifts the fringes by \(N=\dfrac{2\Delta x}\lambda\) (\(\lambda=\dfrac{2\Delta x}N\)). Inserting a transparent film of thickness \(t\) and index \(\mu\) in one arm shifts the fringes by \(N=\dfrac{2(\mu-1)t}\lambda\) (used to find refractive indices of gases, \(\lambda\), small displacements). The Michelson–Morley null result disproved the ether; LIGO uses this principle to detect gravitational waves.

Thin films, wedges, Newton’s rings and coatings

  • Thin film of thickness \(t\), refractive index \(\mu\), in air (refraction angle \(r\)): optical path difference between the two reflected rays \(\Delta=2\mu t\cos r\) (+ \(\tfrac\lambda2\) because the ray reflected from the upper surface, going air to film, suffers a phase change of \(\pi\)). Reflected light: bright \(2\mu t\cos r=\left(n+\tfrac12\right)\lambda\), dark \(2\mu t\cos r=n\lambda\); transmitted light is complementary (bright when \(2\mu t\cos r=n\lambda\)). A film much thinner than \(\lambda\) looks dark in reflected light (e.g. a soap film just before it bursts). The colours of oil on water and soap bubbles come from this; at normal incidence \(\cos r=1\).
  • Phase changes on reflection: a change of \(\pi\) occurs when light is reflected at a boundary with a medium of higher index. In a coating on glass (\(\mu_{\text{air}}\lt\mu_{\text{film}}\lt\mu_{\text{glass}}\)) both reflections have a phase change, so the conditions reverse: reflected light is bright for \(2\mu t=n\lambda\) and dark for \(2\mu t=\left(n+\tfrac12\right)\lambda\).
  • Anti-reflection coating (e.g. MgF\(_2\), \(\mu=1.38\), on glass of \(\mu_g=1.5\)): the minimum thickness for no reflection of wavelength \(\lambda\) is \(t=\dfrac\lambda{4\mu}\) (\(\approx100\) nm for \(550\) nm); exact cancellation needs \(\mu_{\text{film}}=\sqrt{\mu_{\text{glass}}}\). A film of \(\mu_{\text{film}}\gt\mu_{\text{glass}}\) and thickness \(\dfrac\lambda{4\mu}\) strongly reflects (used in dielectric mirrors and cold mirrors).
  • Wedge-shaped film (angle \(\theta\), index \(\mu\), light nearly normal): straight fringes parallel to the edge with fringe width \(\beta=\dfrac\lambda{2\mu\theta}\); the thickness at the \(n\)-th dark fringe \(t_n=\dfrac{n\lambda}{2\mu}\) (the edge is dark in reflected light). A wire or hair of diameter \(d\) between two glass plates at distance \(L\) from the contact edge: \(\theta=\dfrac dL\), so \(d=\dfrac{N\lambda}2\) for \(N\) dark fringes (air). Filling the wedge with a liquid shrinks the fringes: \(\beta'=\dfrac\beta\mu\).
  • Newton’s rings (plano-convex lens of radius of curvature \(R\) on a flat glass plate): thickness of the air film at distance \(r\) from the contact point \(t=\dfrac{r^2}{2R}\). In reflected light the centre is dark; dark rings: \(r_n^2=n\lambda R\); bright rings: \(r_n^2=\left(n-\tfrac12\right)\lambda R\). In a liquid, \(r_n^2=\dfrac{n\lambda R}\mu\) (the rings contract; \(\mu=\dfrac{D_n^2(\text{air})}{D_n^2(\text{liquid})}\)). The ring radii \(\propto\sqrt n\), so the rings crowd outward. The diameters: \(D_n^2=4n\lambda R\); \(\lambda=\dfrac{D_{n+m}^2-D_n^2}{4mR}\) (the usual experimental method; it removes the error due to imperfect contact at the centre). In transmitted light the pattern is complementary (bright centre). If the lens is raised by \(h\), the rings collapse toward the centre and \(\dfrac{2h}\lambda\) rings pass any point.

Diffraction: single slit, circular aperture, double slit with finite width, grating

  • Single slit (Fraunhofer) of width \(a\): minima at \(a\sin\theta=n\lambda\) (\(n=\pm1,\pm2,\dots\)); the central maximum is bounded by \(\theta=\pm\dfrac\lambda a\) (angular width \(\dfrac{2\lambda}a\), linear width \(\dfrac{2\lambda D}a\) on a screen at distance \(D\)); each secondary maximum has half that width, \(\dfrac{\lambda D}a\). The intensity \(I=I_0\left(\dfrac{\sin\alpha}\alpha\right)^2\) with \(\alpha=\dfrac{\pi a\sin\theta}\lambda\); secondary maxima (at \(a\sin\theta\approx1.43\lambda,2.46\lambda,\dots\)) have intensities \(\approx4.5\%,1.6\%,0.8\%\) of the central maximum. A narrower slit gives a wider pattern; for \(a\gg\lambda\) light travels in straight lines (rays), and for \(a\lt\lambda\) the wave spreads completely. White light gives a white central maximum with coloured edges.
  • Fresnel distance: \(z_F=\dfrac{a^2}\lambda\); for a distance \(z\ll z_F\) the light is described by rectilinear propagation (ray optics), for \(z\gg z_F\) the spreading (diffraction) is important. Fresnel diffraction (source or screen at a finite distance, curved wavefronts) versus Fraunhofer diffraction (parallel beams, i.e. at infinity or using lenses).
  • Circular aperture of diameter \(D\): the central bright disc (Airy disc) is bounded by the first minimum at \(\sin\theta=\dfrac{1.22\lambda}D\). Rayleigh criterion: two point sources are just resolved when the central maximum of one falls on the first minimum of the other; minimum angular separation \(\Delta\theta=\dfrac{1.22\lambda}D\) (the eye of pupil \(2\) mm sees \(\approx1'\) at \(550\) nm). The minimum separation resolvable at a distance \(L\) is \(\dfrac{1.22\lambda L}D\).
  • Double slit with finite slit width (width \(a\), separation \(d\)): the interference fringes (\(d\sin\theta=n\lambda\)) are modulated by the single-slit envelope. The number of bright fringes inside the central diffraction maximum is \(\dfrac{2d}a-1\) (for \(d/a\) an integer); the interference maximum of order \(n=\dfrac{md}a\) is missing when it coincides with the \(m\)-th diffraction minimum (\(d=3a\) removes orders \(3,6,\dots\)).
  • Diffraction grating (slit separation \(d=\dfrac1{N_{\text{per m}}}\), \(N\) rulings in all): principal maxima \(d\sin\theta=n\lambda\), the highest order \(n_{\max}\) is the integer part of \(\dfrac d\lambda\) (since \(\sin\theta\le1\)); there are \(N-1\) minima and \(N-2\) weak secondary maxima between the principal ones; the principal maxima get sharper as \(N\) rises (half-width \(\Delta\theta=\dfrac{\lambda}{Nd\cos\theta}\)). Angular dispersion \(\dfrac{d\theta}{d\lambda}=\dfrac n{d\cos\theta}\); resolving power \(\dfrac\lambda{\Delta\lambda}=nN\) (increases with the order and the number of lines used). In white light the central maximum is white and in each order violet is nearest the centre (opposite to the prism spectrum); the spectra of adjacent orders overlap when \(n\lambda_{\text{red}}\gt(n+1)\lambda_{\text{violet}}\) (the second and third orders overlap for the visible range).
  • Interference versus diffraction: interference is the superposition of waves from a few coherent sources and gives fringes of equal intensity (YDSE); diffraction is the superposition of secondary wavelets from one wavefront and gives fringes of decreasing intensity. Both are explained by Huygens’ principle and the superposition principle. Sound diffracts more easily around corners than light because its wavelength is larger.

Polarisation: Malus’ law, Brewster’s angle, double refraction

  • Polarisation shows that light is a transverse wave (the electric vector vibrates in a plane); longitudinal waves such as sound cannot be polarised. A polaroid transmits only the component of \(\vec E\) along its pass axis. Unpolarised light through a polaroid: \(I=\dfrac{I_0}2\), plane-polarised. Malus’ law: \(I=I_0\cos^2\theta\) (\(\theta\) = angle between the polariser and analyser axes) and the transmitted amplitude is \(E_0\cos\theta\). Unpolarised light through two polaroids at an angle \(\theta\): \(I=\dfrac{I_0}2\cos^2\theta\); three polaroids with the middle one at \(45^\circ\) to crossed outer polaroids transmit \(\dfrac{I_0}8\) (of the unpolarised \(I_0\)). On rotating the analyser through \(360^\circ\) the plane-polarised light shows two maxima and two zeros (partially polarised light shows two maxima and two non-zero minima; unpolarised light shows no change).
  • Brewster’s law: reflected light is completely plane-polarised (with \(\vec E\) perpendicular to the plane of incidence) when the angle of incidence \(i_B\) satisfies \(\tan i_B=\mu\); then the reflected and refracted rays are perpendicular, \(i_B+r=90^\circ\). For glass (\(\mu=1.5\)) \(i_B\approx56^\circ\); for water (\(\mu=1.33\)) \(\approx53^\circ\). The refracted ray is partially polarised. A pile of glass plates used at \(i_B\) makes the transmitted light nearly plane-polarised. If light passes from a medium of index \(\mu_1\) to \(\mu_2\): \(\tan i_B=\dfrac{\mu_2}{\mu_1}\).
  • Methods of polarisation: selective absorption (polaroid, dichroism), reflection (Brewster), scattering (skylight is partly polarised, with maximum polarisation at \(90^\circ\) from the Sun), double refraction (calcite, quartz: an ordinary ray (O) obeying Snell’s law and an extraordinary ray (E) with direction-dependent speed, polarised at right angles; the optic axis is the direction of no double refraction). The Nicol prism uses total internal reflection of the O ray to transmit only the E ray.
  • Wave plates: a quarter-wave plate introduces a path difference \(\dfrac\lambda4\) between the O and E components (thickness \(t=\dfrac{\lambda}{4|\mu_o-\mu_e|}\)); with plane-polarised light at \(45^\circ\) to the optic axis it produces circularly polarised light; a half-wave plate (\(\dfrac\lambda2\)) rotates the plane of polarisation by \(2\theta\) (\(\theta\) is the angle to the optic axis).
  • Optical activity: some substances (sugar solutions, quartz) rotate the plane of polarisation; specific rotation \([\alpha]=\dfrac\theta{L\,C}\) (\(\theta\) = rotation in a length \(L\) at concentration \(C\)); measured with a polarimeter (e.g. to find the sugar concentration).
  • Uses: Polaroid sunglasses (cut the polarised glare from horizontal surfaces), 3-D movies, photography filters, LCD displays, stress analysis in transparent solids (photoelasticity), identification of crystals.

Doppler effect for light and special interference topics

  • Doppler effect of light (source and observer moving with a relative velocity \(v\) along the line joining them, \(v\ll c\)): \(\dfrac{\Delta\nu}\nu=\dfrac{v}{c}\), i.e. \(\nu'=\nu\left(1+\dfrac vc\right)\) for approach (blue shift, wavelength decreases) and \(\nu'=\nu\left(1-\dfrac vc\right)\) for recession (red shift, \(\dfrac{\Delta\lambda}\lambda=\dfrac vc\)). Exact relativistic form: \(\nu'=\nu\sqrt{\dfrac{1\pm\beta}{1\mp\beta}}\), \(\beta=\dfrac vc\) (approach: upper sign). Light has no medium, so only the relative velocity matters. A transverse motion gives a (second order) transverse Doppler effect.
  • Applications: the red shift of galaxies shows the expansion of the universe (Hubble’s law \(v=H_0d\)); speed of a rotating star or the Sun’s rotation from the shifts of its limbs; radar speed guns (\(\Delta f=\dfrac{2vf}c\) for a moving reflector); the broadening of spectral lines by thermal motion (Doppler broadening, \(\dfrac{\Delta\lambda}\lambda\sim\dfrac{v_{\text{rms}}}c\)).
  • Dependence on the medium: when light enters a medium of index \(\mu\), the frequency (and the colour) is unchanged; \(\lambda\) and \(v\) are reduced by \(\mu\). The optical path difference \(\Delta_{\text{opt}}=\mu\Delta\) is what determines interference; the minimum path difference for destructive interference equals half a wavelength.
  • Resolution and diffraction limit of optical instruments (telescope: \(\dfrac{1.22\lambda}D\); microscope: \(\dfrac{0.61\lambda}{\text{NA}}\)) are consequences of diffraction at the aperture.
  • Speed of light in vacuum \(c=\dfrac1{\sqrt{\epsilon_0\mu_0}}=3\times10^8\) m s\(^{-1}\); in a medium \(v=\dfrac1{\sqrt{\epsilon\mu}}\); refractive index \(\mu=\sqrt{\epsilon_r\mu_r}\) (the permittivity changes with frequency, causing dispersion). Fizeau and Foucault measured \(c\) by the toothed-wheel and rotating-mirror methods.
27
Gravitation, fluids & waves

Gravitation

Universal law of gravitation

\[F\propto\frac{m_1m_2}{r^2}\quad\text{or}\quad F=G\frac{m_1m_2}{r^2}\]

\(G=6.67\times10^{-11}\) N m² kg⁻² is the universal gravitational constant.

Newton's law of gravitation in vector form

\[\vec F_{12}=\frac{Gm_1m_2}{r^2}\hat r_{12}\qquad\&\qquad\vec F_{21}=\frac{Gm_1m_2}{r^2}\hat r_{21}\]

Now \(\hat r_{12}=-\hat r_{21}\), thus \(\vec F_{21}=-\dfrac{Gm_1m_2}{r^2}\hat r_{12}\). Comparing, \(\vec F_{12}=-\vec F_{21}\).

Gravitational field

\[E=\frac Fm=\frac{GM}{r^2}\]

Gravitational potential

\[V=-\frac{GM}r\qquad E=-\frac{dV}{dr}\]

1. Ring (radius \(a\), at distance \(r\) along the axis)

\[V=\frac{-GM}{(a^2+r^2)^{1/2}}\qquad E=\frac{-GMr}{(a^2+r^2)^{3/2}}\hat r\quad\text{or}\quad E=-\frac{GM\cos\theta}{x^2}\]

The gravitational field is maximum at \(r=\pm\dfrac a{\sqrt2}\), where its value is \(-\dfrac{2GM}{3\sqrt3\,a^2}\).

2. Thin circular disc (radius \(a\))

\[V=-\frac{2GM}{a^2}\left[(a^2+r^2)^{1/2}-r\right]\qquad E=-\frac{2GM}{a^2}\left[1-\frac r{(r^2+a^2)^{1/2}}\right]=-\frac{2GM}{a^2}[1-\cos\theta]\]

3. Non-conducting solid sphere (radius \(a\))

(a) Point \(P\) inside the sphere, \(r\le a\):

\[V=-\frac{GM}{2a^3}(3a^2-r^2)\qquad E=-\frac{GMr}{a^3}\]

At the centre: \(V=-\dfrac{3GM}{2a}\) and \(E=0\).

(b) Point \(P\) outside the sphere, \(r\ge a\):

\[V=-\frac{GM}r\qquad E=-\frac{GM}{r^2}\]

4. Uniform thin spherical shell / conducting solid sphere (radius \(a\))

(a) Point \(P\) inside the shell, \(r\le a\): \(V=-\dfrac{GM}a\) and \(E=0\)

(b) Point \(P\) outside the shell, \(r\ge a\): \(V=-\dfrac{GM}r\) and \(E=-\dfrac{GM}{r^2}\)

Variation of acceleration due to gravity

1. Effect of altitude

\[g_h=\frac{GM_e}{(R_e+h)^2}=g\left(1+\frac h{R_e}\right)^{-2}\approx g\left(1-\frac{2h}{R_e}\right)\quad(h\ll R)\]

2. Effect of depth

\[g_d=g\left(1-\frac d{R_e}\right)\]

3. Effect of the shape of the Earth. The equatorial radius is about 21 km longer than the polar radius. Since \(g=\dfrac{GM_e}{R_e^2}\), we have \(g_{pole}\gt g_{equator}\).

Satellite velocity (orbital velocity)

\[v_0=\left[\frac{GM_e}{(R_e+h)}\right]^{1/2}=\left[\frac{gR_e^2}{(R_e+h)}\right]^{1/2}\]

When \(h\ll R_e\): \(v_0=\sqrt{gR_e}=\sqrt{9.8\times6.4\times10^6}=7.92\times10^3\ \text{m s}^{-1}=7.92\ \text{km s}^{-1}\)

Time period of a satellite

\[T=\frac{2\pi(R_e+h)}{\left[\frac{gR_e^2}{(R_e+h)}\right]^{1/2}}=\frac{2\pi}{R_e}\left[\frac{(R_e+h)^3}g\right]^{1/2}\]

Energy of a satellite (orbit radius \(r\)):

\[U=-\frac{GM_em}r\qquad K.E.=\frac{GM_em}{2r}\qquad E=-\frac{GM_em}{2r}\]

Kepler's laws

Law of area: the line joining the sun and a planet sweeps out equal areas in equal intervals of time.

\[\text{Areal velocity}=\frac{\text{area swept}}{\text{time}}=\frac{\frac12r(r\,d\theta)}{dt}=\frac12r^2\frac{d\theta}{dt}=\text{constant}\]

Hence \(\tfrac12r^2\omega=\) constant.

Law of periods:

\[\frac{T^2}{R^3}=\text{constant}\]

Gravitational field and potential of standard bodies: more results

\(G=6.67\times10^{-11}\) N m\(^2\) kg\(^{-2}\) (dimension \([M^{-1}L^3T^{-2}]\), measured by Cavendish’s torsion balance). Gravitational field \(\vec E=\dfrac{\vec F}m\); \(E=-\dfrac{dV}{dr}\), \(V=-\displaystyle\int\vec E\cdot d\vec r\); superposition holds for the field and the potential (the potential adds as a scalar).

Body (mass \(M\), radius or length)FieldPotential
Point mass, distance \(r\)\(\dfrac{GM}{r^2}\)\(-\dfrac{GM}r\)
Thin spherical shell, \(r\ge R\) / \(r\lt R\)\(\dfrac{GM}{r^2}\) / \(0\)\(-\dfrac{GM}r\) / \(-\dfrac{GM}R\) (constant inside)
Solid uniform sphere, \(r\ge R\) / \(r\le R\)\(\dfrac{GM}{r^2}\) / \(\dfrac{GMr}{R^3}\)\(-\dfrac{GM}r\) / \(-\dfrac{GM(3R^2-r^2)}{2R^3}\) (centre: \(-\dfrac{3GM}{2R}\))
Ring (radius \(a\)), at distance \(x\) on its axis\(\dfrac{GMx}{(x^2+a^2)^{3/2}}\) (max \(\dfrac{2GM}{3\sqrt3a^2}\) at \(x=\dfrac a{\sqrt2}\); zero at the centre)\(-\dfrac{GM}{\sqrt{x^2+a^2}}\)
Thin disc (radius \(a\), surface density \(\sigma\)) on its axis\(2\pi G\sigma\left(1-\dfrac{x}{\sqrt{x^2+a^2}}\right)\)\(-\dfrac{2GM}{a^2}\left(\sqrt{x^2+a^2}-x\right)\)
Infinite sheet (\(\sigma\)) / infinite thin rod (\(\lambda\) per length, distance \(r\))\(2\pi G\sigma\) (uniform) / \(\dfrac{2G\lambda}r\)(logarithmic; no zero at infinity)
Uniform rod of length \(L\): on its axis at distance \(a\) from the near end / on the perpendicular bisector at \(r\)\(\dfrac{GM}{a(a+L)}\) / \(\dfrac{GM}{r\sqrt{r^2+L^2/4}}\)\(-\dfrac{GM}L\ln\dfrac{a+L}a\) (on the axis)
Semicircular wire (mass \(M\), radius \(R\)), at its centre\(\dfrac{2GM}{\pi R^2}\)\(-\dfrac{GM}R\)
  • Cavity in a uniform sphere (density \(\rho\)): the field inside a spherical cavity is uniform, \(\vec E=-\tfrac43\pi G\rho\,\vec d\), where \(\vec d\) is the vector from the centre of the big sphere to the centre of the cavity (superposition: the full sphere minus a sphere of the same density filling the cavity). Its magnitude is \(\tfrac43\pi G\rho\,d\) and it is directed toward the centre of the large sphere.
  • Gravitational potential energy of a system of point masses \(U=-\sum_{i\lt j}\dfrac{Gm_im_j}{r_{ij}}\); for three masses at the vertices of an equilateral triangle of side \(a\), \(U=-\dfrac{3Gm^2}a\). The work done by an external agent in assembling the system equals \(U\). Self-energy of a thin shell \(-\dfrac{GM^2}{2R}\); of a uniform solid sphere \(-\dfrac{3GM^2}{5R}\) (energy needed to disperse the matter to infinity).
  • Potential difference between two points = work done per unit mass against the field in moving between them; the field is conservative and \(\oint\vec E\cdot d\vec r=0\). A body at rest at the centre of a shell is in equilibrium; no shielding of gravity is possible (the force between two masses is unaffected by a third body).
  • Force on a particle of mass \(m\) at the axis point of a ring is \(F=\dfrac{GMmx}{(x^2+a^2)^{3/2}}\); for small \(x\) it is a restoring force \(-\dfrac{GMm}{a^3}x\) giving SHM with \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{a^3}{GM}}\).

Variation of \(g\): height, depth, rotation of the Earth

\[g=\frac{GM}{R^2}=\frac43\pi G\rho R,\qquad g_h=\frac{gR^2}{(R+h)^2}\approx g\left(1-\frac{2h}R\right),\qquad g_d=g\left(1-\frac dR\right),\qquad g'=g-\omega^2R\cos^2\varphi\]
  • Constants for the Earth: \(M=6\times10^{24}\) kg, \(R=6.4\times10^6\) m, \(g=9.8\) m s\(^{-2}\), mean density \(5.5\times10^3\) kg m\(^{-3}\); for the Moon \(g_m\approx\dfrac g6\) (\(1.62\) m s\(^{-2}\)).
  • Different planets: \(g\propto\dfrac MR^2\propto\rho R\). If the radius shrinks (mass constant) \(g\propto\dfrac1{R^2}\); if the density is constant, \(g\propto R\). For two planets \(\dfrac{g_1}{g_2}=\dfrac{M_1R_2^2}{M_2R_1^2}=\dfrac{\rho_1R_1}{\rho_2R_2}\).
  • Height: \(g_h=g\left(\dfrac R{R+h}\right)^2\); the fractional decrease \(\dfrac{\Delta g}g\approx\dfrac{2h}R\) (for \(h\ll R\)); \(g_h=\dfrac g4\) at \(h=R\); \(g\) is reduced by \(1\%\) at \(h\approx\dfrac R{200}=32\) km.
  • Depth: inside the Earth (uniform density) \(g_d=g\left(1-\dfrac dR\right)=\dfrac{gr}R\); zero at the centre; maximum at the surface (for a uniform Earth). The real Earth (denser core) has \(g\) increasing slightly to about \(10.7\) m s\(^{-2}\) at the core–mantle boundary before decreasing to zero at the centre. The fractional decrease at depth \(d\) is \(\dfrac dR\) (compared to \(\dfrac{2h}R\) at the same height).
  • Rotation and latitude \(\varphi\): the effective acceleration \(g'=g-\omega^2R\cos^2\varphi\) (approximately); at the equator \(g_{eq}=g-\omega^2R\) (\(\omega^2R=0.034\) m s\(^{-2}\)), at the poles \(g'=g\) (unaffected by rotation). Hence the weight is least at the equator and greatest at the poles. The “effective” \(g\) is the one measured by a spring balance or a plumb line; the plumb line points to the centre only at the poles and equator. If the Earth rotated fast enough, \(\omega=\sqrt{\dfrac gR}\) (period \(84.6\) min \(\approx1.4\) h), a body at the equator would be weightless (\(g'=0\)). At latitude \(\varphi\) a body of mass \(m\) at rest in the Earth’s frame has \(mg'\) as its apparent weight, with \(g'^2=g^2-\omega^2R\cos^2\varphi\,(2g-\omega^2R)\) exactly.
  • Non-uniform shape: the Earth is flattened at the poles (polar radius \(21\) km shorter), which makes \(g\) larger at the poles; \(g\) also varies with the local density, altitude and latitude (\(9.78\) m s\(^{-2}\) at the equator and \(9.83\) m s\(^{-2}\) at the poles).
  • Weight in a lift or satellite: apparent weight \(=m(g\pm a)\) in an accelerating lift; in free fall (\(a=g\)) it is zero (weightlessness). An orbiting satellite and everything in it is in free fall, so astronauts feel weightless although \(g\) there is not zero (\(g\approx8.7\) m s\(^{-2}\) at \(400\) km height).
  • Gravitational and inertial mass: they are equal to a high precision (principle of equivalence: a uniformly accelerated frame is equivalent to a gravitational field; the basis of general relativity, light bends in a gravitational field). Because \(m_g=m_i\), all bodies fall with the same acceleration (apart from air resistance).

Gravitational potential energy, escape velocity and trajectories

\[U=-\frac{GMm}r,\qquad v_e=\sqrt{\frac{2GM}R}=\sqrt{2gR}=11.2\ \text{km s}^{-1},\qquad v_o=\sqrt{gR}=7.9\ \text{km s}^{-1},\qquad v_e=\sqrt2\,v_o\]
  • The potential energy is zero at infinity and negative everywhere else. The work needed to raise a mass \(m\) from the Earth’s surface to a height \(h\): \(W=\dfrac{GMm\,h}{R(R+h)}=\dfrac{mgh}{1+h/R}\) (\(\approx mgh\) for \(h\ll R\)); to take it to infinity \(W=\dfrac{GMm}R=mgR\). From a height \(h\) the escape speed is \(\sqrt{\dfrac{2GM}{R+h}}\).
  • Escape velocity is independent of the mass and (neglecting air and other bodies) of the direction of projection; it depends on the planet (Moon \(2.4\) km s\(^{-1}\), Mars \(5.0\), Jupiter \(59.5\), Sun \(618\) km s\(^{-1}\)). Escape velocity \(=\sqrt{\dfrac{8\pi G\rho}3}\,R\) in terms of density. A particle with the speed \(v\gt v_e\) leaves with a speed \(\sqrt{v^2-v_e^2}\) at infinity.
  • Energy conservation for a projectile fired vertically from the surface with a speed \(v\lt v_e\): \(\dfrac12mv^2-\dfrac{GMm}R=-\dfrac{GMm}{R+h}\), so the maximum height \(h=\dfrac{R}{\dfrac{v_e^2}{v^2}-1}=\dfrac{v^2R}{2gR-v^2}\). For \(v=v_o\) (\(=\sqrt{gR}\)), \(h=R\); for \(v=\dfrac{v_e}2\), \(h=\dfrac R3\); for \(v\ll v_e\), \(h\approx\dfrac{v^2}{2g}\). A body falling from rest at a height \(h\) strikes the surface with \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{2gRh}{R+h}}\); from infinity \(v=v_e\).
  • Atmosphere of planets: a planet retains a gas only if the escape velocity is much larger than the gas molecules’ rms speed (\(v_e\gtrsim5\,v_{\text{rms}}\)); the Moon has no atmosphere (\(v_e\) small), hydrogen and helium have escaped from the Earth (large \(v_{\text{rms}}\) at the top of the atmosphere), while the Sun and Jupiter hold them.
  • Types of trajectories for a body projected with speed \(v\) at the Earth’s surface (horizontally): \(v\lt v_o\) falls back (ellipse intersecting the Earth); \(v=v_o\) circular orbit near the surface; \(v_o\lt v\lt v_e\) elliptical orbit (the point of projection is the perigee); \(v=v_e\) parabolic path (escape; total energy \(0\)); \(v\gt v_e\) hyperbolic path (positive total energy). Total energy of a bound orbit \(E\lt0\).
  • Black holes: if the escape velocity at the surface of a body equals \(c\), nothing (not even light) escapes; Schwarzschild radius \(R_s=\dfrac{2GM}{c^2}\) (about \(3\) km for the Sun, \(9\) mm for the Earth).
  • Gravitational binding energy of the Moon in the Earth–Moon system; the work against gravity between two shells etc. is found by \(W=U_f-U_i\). Work done by gravity in taking a mass from \(r_1\) to \(r_2\): \(W=GMm\left(\dfrac1{r_2}-\dfrac1{r_1}\right)\) (positive when moving closer).

Satellites: orbital speed, period, energy, geostationary orbit

\[v_o=\sqrt{\frac{GM}r}=\sqrt{\frac{gR^2}r},\qquad T=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{r^3}{GM}},\qquad K=\frac{GMm}{2r},\ \ U=-\frac{GMm}r,\ \ E=-\frac{GMm}{2r}=-K=\frac U2\]
  • Relations for circular orbits of radius \(r\): \(v\propto\dfrac1{\sqrt r}\); \(\omega=\sqrt{\dfrac{GM}{r^3}}\propto r^{-3/2}\); \(T\propto r^{3/2}\); \(K\propto\dfrac1r\); \(|U|\propto\dfrac1r\); angular momentum \(L=m\sqrt{GMr}\propto\sqrt r\); centripetal acceleration \(a=\dfrac{GM}{r^2}\). A satellite just above the Earth’s surface: \(v=\sqrt{gR}=7.9\) km s\(^{-1}\), \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac Rg}\approx84.6\) min (\(\approx1.4\) h). The speed and period do not depend on the mass of the satellite.
  • Energy: the total energy \(E=-\dfrac{GMm}{2r}\) is negative (bound). The binding energy (energy to be supplied to free the satellite) \(=\dfrac{GMm}{2r}\). To move a satellite from an orbit of radius \(r_1\) to a larger orbit \(r_2\), the energy to be supplied is \(\dfrac{GMm}2\left(\dfrac1{r_1}-\dfrac1{r_2}\right)\), while the kinetic energy decreases (the potential energy increases by twice as much). In a small tangential speed boost the satellite goes to a bigger elliptical orbit.
  • Atmospheric drag: a satellite that loses energy spirals inward (smaller \(r\)) and its speed increases (\(v\propto r^{-1/2}\); the energy \(E\) decreases, so \(\Delta K=-\Delta E\gt0\) and \(\Delta U=2\Delta E\lt0\)); the period decreases.
  • Change of speed in an orbit: if the speed is suddenly increased to \(kv_o\) in the tangential direction at radius \(r\): \(1\lt k\lt\sqrt2\) gives an elliptical orbit with \(r\) as the perigee (apogee distance \(r_a=\dfrac{r}{\dfrac2{k^2}-1}\)), \(k=\sqrt2\) escape, \(k\gt\sqrt2\) hyperbolic. If the speed is decreased (\(k\lt1\)) the point becomes the apogee of an ellipse with the perigee \(\dfrac{k^2r}{2-k^2}\); if the speed is reduced to zero the satellite falls straight to the centre. A radial boost does not change \(L\).
  • Geostationary (synchronous) satellite: \(T=24\) h, in the equatorial plane, moving west to east, at \(r\approx4.2\times10^7\) m from the centre of the Earth (height \(h\approx36\,000\) km \(\approx5.6R\)), orbital speed \(3.07\) km s\(^{-1}\); it appears fixed in the sky (used for communication, broadcast, weather). Only one such orbit exists (fixed \(r\)). The orbit of a geostationary satellite has \(\omega=\omega_{\text{earth}}=7.27\times10^{-5}\) rad s\(^{-1}\). Polar satellites orbit at about \(500\)–\(800\) km, \(T\approx100\) min, passing over the poles; as the Earth rotates they scan the whole surface (remote sensing). The Moon: \(T=27.3\) days, \(r=3.84\times10^8\) m.
  • The relation to the ratio of periods of two satellites: \(\dfrac{T_1}{T_2}=\left(\dfrac{r_1}{r_2}\right)^{3/2}\). Using \(g\) at the Earth’s surface, \(GM=gR^2\), \(v=R\sqrt{\dfrac g{R+h}}\), \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{(R+h)^3}{gR^2}}\) for a satellite at height \(h\).
  • A satellite in a circular orbit: the gravitational force provides the centripetal force; the net force is not zero, but the apparent weight inside is zero (no normal reaction), so a pendulum clock, a liquid, and a balance do not work (a spring or inertial balance works).

Kepler’s laws, ellipses and the vis-viva equation

  • Kepler’s laws: (1) planets move in ellipses with the Sun at one focus; (2) the line joining the Sun to a planet sweeps out equal areas in equal times (the areal velocity \(\dfrac{dA}{dt}=\dfrac L{2m}=\) constant — conservation of angular momentum for a central force); (3) \(T^2\propto a^3\) (\(a\) = semi-major axis), \(T^2=\dfrac{4\pi^2a^3}{GM}\); the same constant \(\dfrac{T^2}{a^3}\) for all planets of the Sun. For a circular orbit \(a=r\). Also \(\dfrac{T_1^2}{T_2^2}=\dfrac{a_1^3}{a_2^3}\).
  • Ellipse: semi-major axis \(a\), semi-minor \(b=a\sqrt{1-e^2}\), eccentricity \(e\); perihelion distance \(r_p=a(1-e)\), aphelion \(r_a=a(1+e)\), \(a=\dfrac{r_a+r_p}2\), \(e=\dfrac{r_a-r_p}{r_a+r_p}\). Area \(=\pi ab\), so \(T=\dfrac{\pi ab}{dA/dt}\).
  • Conserved quantities: angular momentum \(L=mv_pr_p=mv_ar_a\), so \(\dfrac{v_p}{v_a}=\dfrac{r_a}{r_p}=\dfrac{1+e}{1-e}\); mechanical energy \(E=-\dfrac{GMm}{2a}\) (depends only on \(a\)); \(L=m\sqrt{GMa(1-e^2)}\).
  • Vis-viva equation: the speed at a distance \(r\) from the Sun \(v^2=GM\left(\dfrac2r-\dfrac1a\right)\); \(v_p=\sqrt{\dfrac{GM}a\,\dfrac{1+e}{1-e}}\), \(v_a=\sqrt{\dfrac{GM}a\,\dfrac{1-e}{1+e}}\). For a circle it reduces to \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{GM}r}\). A planet moves fastest at perihelion and slowest at aphelion; its kinetic energy is greatest at perihelion, potential energy least (most negative).
  • At the ends of the minor axis \(r=a\), the speed is \(\sqrt{\dfrac{GM}a}\) (that of a circular orbit of radius \(a\)), and the angle between the velocity and the radius vector is not \(90^\circ\) except at the perihelion, aphelion and for a circle. The speed at the ends of the minor axis is \(\sqrt{v_pv_a}\).
  • Two planets with the same \(a\) have the same period and energy per unit mass, whatever the eccentricity. Halley’s comet: \(T\approx76\) years, \(e\approx0.97\). For a comet near the Sun with \(r\ll a\): \(v\approx\sqrt{\dfrac{2GM}r}\) (nearly parabolic).
  • Newton derived the inverse-square law \(F\propto\dfrac1{r^2}\) from Kepler’s third law. Only \(F\propto\dfrac1{r^2}\) and \(F\propto r\) give closed orbits (Bertrand’s theorem). Kepler’s second law holds for any central force.

Two-body problems, tunnels through the Earth and tidal effects

  • Binary stars (masses \(M_1,M_2\), separation \(r\)): both orbit their centre of mass with the same angular velocity \(\omega\) and period \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{r^3}{G(M_1+M_2)}}\); the radii of their orbits \(r_1=\dfrac{M_2r}{M_1+M_2}\), \(r_2=\dfrac{M_1r}{M_1+M_2}\) (\(M_1r_1=M_2r_2\)); speeds \(v_1\propto r_1\), the lighter star moves faster; \(\dfrac{v_1}{v_2}=\dfrac{M_2}{M_1}\). Gravitational force on each \(\dfrac{GM_1M_2}{r^2}=M_1\omega^2r_1\). The total energy \(E=-\dfrac{GM_1M_2}{2r}\); angular momentum from the centre of mass \(L=\mu\sqrt{G(M_1+M_2)r}\), \(\mu=\dfrac{M_1M_2}{M_1+M_2}\). Three equal masses \(M\) at the vertices of an equilateral triangle of side \(a\) revolve about the centroid with \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{GM}a}\), \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{a^3}{3GM}}\); four equal masses at the corners of a square of side \(a\): the net inward force on one mass is \(\dfrac{GM^2}{a^2}\left(\sqrt2+\dfrac12\right)\), the orbit radius is \(\dfrac a{\sqrt2}\), so \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{GM}a\left(1+\dfrac1{2\sqrt2}\right)}\) and \(T=\dfrac{2\pi(a/\sqrt2)}v\).
  • Tunnel through the Earth (uniform density, no friction): the field inside is \(g\dfrac rR\), so a particle dropped into a straight tunnel through the centre executes SHM with \(\omega=\sqrt{\dfrac gR}\), \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac Rg}\approx84.6\) min, the speed at the centre \(\sqrt{gR}=7.9\) km s\(^{-1}\) (the same as the orbital speed near the surface); the time to go from one end to the other is \(42.3\) min. A chord (not through the centre) also gives SHM along the chord with the same period (the component of the force along the chord is \(\dfrac{mg}R\) times the displacement from the chord’s midpoint); so the travel time between any two points of the surface through a frictionless straight tunnel is \(42.3\) min. A particle dropped from rest at a height \(h\) above the surface into a tunnel through the centre reaches the centre with \(\dfrac12v^2=\dfrac{3GM}{2R}-\dfrac{GM}{R+h}\), i.e. \(v^2=gR\left(3-\dfrac{2R}{R+h}\right)\) (\(v=\sqrt{gR}\) for \(h\to0\)).
  • Tides: due to the difference in the gravitational pull of the Moon (and the Sun) on different parts of the Earth (tide-raising force \(\propto\dfrac{M}{d^3}\)); two high tides a day; the Moon’s effect is about twice that of the Sun; spring tides at new and full moon (Sun, Moon, Earth in line), neap tides at the quarters.
  • Reduced-mass method: for an isolated two-body system, the relative motion is the motion of a particle of mass \(\mu=\dfrac{M_1M_2}{M_1+M_2}\) in the field of mass \(M_1+M_2\); the relative acceleration of two bodies approaching under gravity \(=\dfrac{G(M_1+M_2)}{r^2}\); the time of the head-on fall from rest at a separation \(r_0\) to contact is found from the energy equation \(\dfrac12\mu v^2=GM_1M_2\left(\dfrac1r-\dfrac1{r_0}\right)\); time of fall of a body from rest at \(r_0\) to the centre is \(\dfrac{\pi}{2\sqrt2}\sqrt{\dfrac{r_0^3}{GM}}\), i.e. half the period of a degenerate ellipse of semi-major axis \(\dfrac{r_0}2\).
28
Gravitation, fluids & waves

Fluid Mechanics & Properties of Matter

1. Hydraulic press

\[p=\frac fa=\frac FA\quad\text{or}\quad F=\frac Aa\times f\]

Hydrostatic paradox: \(P_A=P_B=P_C\)

Liquid in an accelerating frame

(i) Liquid in an elevator accelerating upward with acceleration \(a_0\): the pressure at depth \(h\) and the buoyant force are

\[p=\rho h\,[g+a_0]\qquad B=m(g+a_0)\]

(ii) Free surface of a liquid in horizontal acceleration:

\[\tan\theta=\frac{a_0}g\qquad p_1-p_2=\rho\ell a_0\ \text{ (pressures at points 1 and 2)}\qquad h_1-h_2=\frac{\ell a_0}g\]

(iii) Free surface of a liquid in a rotating cylinder:

\[h=\frac{v^2}{2g}=\frac{\omega^2r^2}{2g}\]

Equation of continuity

\[a_1v_1=a_2v_2\]

In general \(av=\) constant.

Bernoulli's theorem

\[\frac P\rho+\tfrac12v^2+gh=\text{constant}\]

(vi) Torricelli's theorem (speed of efflux)

\[v=\sqrt{\frac{2gh}{1-\dfrac{A_2^2}{A_1^2}}}\]

\(A_2\) is the area of the hole and \(A_1\) is the area of the vessel.

Elasticity and viscosity

\[\text{Stress}=\frac{\text{restoring force}}{\text{area of the body}}=\frac FA\]\[\text{Strain }\epsilon=\frac{\text{change in configuration}}{\text{original configuration}}\]
  • (i) Longitudinal strain \(=\dfrac{\Delta L}L\)
  • (ii) Volume strain \(\epsilon_v=\dfrac{\Delta V}V\)
  • (iii) Shear strain: \(\tan\phi\) or \(\phi=\dfrac x\ell\)

Young's modulus of elasticity

\[Y=\frac{F/A}{\Delta L/L}=\frac{FL}{A\Delta L}\]

Potential energy per unit volume \(=\tfrac12(\text{stress}\times\text{strain})=\tfrac12(Y\times\text{strain}^2)\)

Inter-atomic force constant: \(k=Yr_0\)

Newton's law of viscosity

\[F\propto A\frac{dv}{dx}\quad\text{or}\quad F=-\eta A\frac{dv}{dx}\]

Stokes' law

\[F=6\pi\eta rv\]

Terminal velocity:

\[\frac29\frac{r^2(\rho-\sigma)g}\eta\]

Surface tension

\[T=\frac{\text{total force on either side of the imaginary line }(F)}{\text{length of the line }(\ell)}\qquad T=S=\frac{\Delta W}A\]

Surface tension is numerically equal to the surface energy, or the work done per unit increase in surface area.

Excess pressure

  • Inside a (soap) bubble: \((p-p_a)=\dfrac{4T}r=p_{excess}\)
  • Inside a drop: \((p-p_a)=\dfrac{2T}r=p_{excess}\)
  • Inside an air bubble in a liquid: \((p-p_a)=\dfrac{2T}r=p_{excess}\)

Capillary rise

\[h=\frac{2T\cos\theta}{r\rho g}\]

Hydrostatics: pressure, manometers, forces on walls, rotating liquid

\[P=P_0+\rho gh,\qquad P_{\text{gauge}}=P-P_0,\qquad P_0=1.013\times10^5\ \text{Pa}=76\ \text{cm Hg}=10.3\ \text{m of water}\]
  • Pressure \(P=\dfrac FA\) (scalar; unit pascal \(=\) N m\(^{-2}\); \(1\) atm \(=1.013\times10^5\) Pa \(=760\) torr; \(1\) bar \(=10^5\) Pa). In a liquid at rest, the pressure is the same at all points at the same level in the same connected liquid, acts normally on any surface, and increases with depth; its dependence is only on the depth (the shape of the vessel does not matter — hydrostatic paradox). Pascal’s law: a pressure change applied to an enclosed fluid is transmitted undiminished to every point of the fluid (hydraulic lift and brakes: \(F_2=F_1\dfrac{A_2}{A_1}\), displacements \(x_1A_1=x_2A_2\), work conserved).
  • Barometer: \(P_0=\rho_{Hg}gh\) with \(h=76\) cm of mercury; if the tube is tilted, the vertical height stays \(h\) (the length of the mercury column increases); an air bubble above the mercury column lowers the reading. A simple manometer: \(P_{\text{gas}}=P_0+\rho gh\) (open-tube) with \(h\) the difference of the liquid levels. In a U-tube with two immiscible liquids in equilibrium: \(\rho_1h_1=\rho_2h_2\) (heights measured above the common interface level).
  • Force on a vertical wall or a submerged plane surface of area \(A\): \(F=\rho g\bar hA\), where \(\bar h\) is the depth of the centroid; the line of action (centre of pressure) is deeper than the centroid. For a rectangular wall of width \(b\) holding water of depth \(H\): \(F=\tfrac12\rho gH^2b\) acting at a height \(\dfrac H3\) above the base; the torque about the base is \(\dfrac{\rho gH^3b}6\). For a dam the gauge force increases as \(H^2\), so dams are thicker at the base. For a vertical wall the force is \(\int P\,dA\); for a surface tilted at \(\theta\) to the horizontal the pressure force is normal to the surface.
  • Force on the base of a vessel \(=\rho ghA\) (depends on the base area and depth, not on the weight of the liquid); the vessel’s weight as read on a balance is the total weight of vessel and liquid. A vessel wider at the top than the base exerts a force on the base smaller than the weight of the liquid (the sides carry the rest).
  • Rotating vessel (angular velocity \(\omega\), seen from the rotating frame): the free surface is a paraboloid, \(y=\dfrac{\omega^2r^2}{2g}\) above its lowest point; the pressure at distance \(r\) from the axis and a given depth is larger by \(\tfrac12\rho\omega^2r^2\) than on the axis. For a cylinder of radius \(R\) initially filled to a height \(h_0\) (no spilling, the paraboloid not touching the bottom) the liquid rises at the wall by \(\dfrac{\omega^2R^2}{4g}\) and falls at the centre by the same amount. In a horizontal tube rotating about a vertical axis through one end, with the liquid surface at \(r_0\), the pressure at \(r\) is \(P_0+\tfrac12\rho\omega^2(r^2-r_0^2)\).
  • Effective gravity: in an accelerated container use \(\vec g_{\text{eff}}=\vec g-\vec a\): the free surface is perpendicular to it and \(P=P_0+\rho g_{\text{eff}}h\). For a liquid in a cart accelerating horizontally at \(a\): \(\tan\theta=\dfrac ag\), and the pressure difference between the back and the front at the same level is \(\rho a\ell\). A small air bubble (or any body lighter than the liquid) moves toward the front, in the direction of \(a\), while a denser body moves toward the back (compare a balloon in an accelerating car).
  • Atmosphere: \(\dfrac{dP}{dh}=-\rho g\); for an isothermal atmosphere \(P=P_0e^{-Mgh/RT}\), with the scale height \(\dfrac{RT}{Mg}\approx8\) km (the pressure falls to \(\dfrac1e\) of \(P_0\) there); the atmospheric pressure is much lower on mountains (boiling point of water falls).

Buoyancy, floating bodies and oscillations

  • Archimedes’ principle: the buoyant force equals the weight of the fluid displaced, \(B=\rho_fV_{\text{sub}}g\), acting at the centre of buoyancy (the centroid of the displaced fluid); it is a result of the pressure difference between the bottom and the top surfaces, so \(B=\rho_fg\cdot\)(volume of the immersed part). It does not depend on the depth for a fully immersed body (incompressible fluid) nor on the material of the body.
  • Floating: a body of density \(\rho_s\) floats in a liquid of density \(\rho_l\) if \(\rho_s\lt\rho_l\) with the fraction \(\dfrac{V_{\text{sub}}}V=\dfrac{\rho_s}{\rho_l}\) submerged; it sinks if \(\rho_s\gt\rho_l\); it stays at any depth if \(\rho_s=\rho_l\). An iceberg (\(\rho=0.9\) g cm\(^{-3}\)) in sea water (\(1.03\)) shows about \(12\%\) above the surface. Apparent weight in a liquid \(=W-B=Vg(\rho_s-\rho_l)\); the relative density (specific gravity) \(=\dfrac{W}{W-W_{\text{app}}}\).
  • Melting of floating ice: ice floating in water — the water level is unchanged on melting (the water formed has the volume of the water displaced); ice with a stone or an iron piece frozen inside — the level falls (when the piece sinks it displaces only its own volume); ice with an air bubble or a cork inside — unchanged; ice floating in a liquid denser than water (brine) — the level rises; in a liquid less dense than water (oil of density between \(0.9\) and \(1\) g cm\(^{-3}\)) — the level falls. In general compare the displaced volume \(\dfrac m{\rho_{\text{liquid}}}\) with the volume of the melted water \(\dfrac m{\rho_{w}}\).
  • Tied bodies: a body lighter than the liquid tied by a string to the bottom of a vessel: \(T=B-W\); if the string is cut it accelerates upward with \(a=\dfrac{B-W}m\). Two bodies connected by a string and floating or sinking together are treated as one system: in equilibrium the total buoyant force equals the total weight, and the tension in the string follows from the equilibrium of either body.
  • In an accelerating container (acceleration \(a\) upward): the effective gravity is \(g+a\) and \(B=\rho_fV(g+a)\); the fraction submerged is unchanged (\(\rho_s/\rho_l\)); in free fall (\(a=-g\)) there is no buoyant force. In a horizontally accelerating container a floating body is displaced so that the net force along the effective gravity is zero.
  • Oscillation of a floating cylinder (cross-section area \(A\), mass \(m\), liquid density \(\rho\)) when pushed down a little by \(x\): restoring force \(-\rho Agx\), \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac m{\rho Ag}}=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{h_0}g}\) where \(h_0\) is the depth submerged at equilibrium. U-tube: liquid of total length \(L\) in a U-tube of uniform cross-section oscillates with \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac L{2g}}\) (restoring force \(2\rho Agy\)). A ball in a liquid of density \(\rho\) (a dense ball dropped): the acceleration \(a=g\left(1-\dfrac\rho{\rho_s}\right)\); a light ball released at depth \(h\) in water rises with \(a=g\left(\dfrac{\rho_l}{\rho_s}-1\right)\) and leaves the surface with the speed \(\sqrt{2ah}\).
  • Stability of a floating ship: stable if the metacentre lies above the centre of gravity; for a fully submerged body, stable if the centre of buoyancy is above the centre of gravity. A hydrometer (floating stem) reads density: \(\dfrac{h_1}{h_2}=\dfrac{\rho_2}{\rho_1}\) (for a uniform stem the submerged length is inversely proportional to the density); a liquid with its density as a function of depth: \(B=\int\rho(y)gA\,dy\).
  • A vessel on a balance: when a body is lowered into the liquid by a string without touching the bottom, the reading increases by \(B\) (the reaction to the buoyant force on the body), while the tension in the string falls by \(B\); when the body floats, the reading increases by its whole weight.

Fluid flow: Bernoulli applications, efflux, venturi, lift

\[P+\tfrac12\rho v^2+\rho gh=\text{constant},\qquad Av=\text{constant}\ (\text{incompressible}),\qquad Q=Av=\frac{dV}{dt}\]
  • Conditions: steady (streamline), incompressible, non-viscous (ideal) flow along a streamline. The three terms are the pressure, the kinetic energy per unit volume and the potential energy per unit volume. Bernoulli’s equation is the work–energy theorem for a fluid. The rate of mass flow \(\rho Av\) is constant. A stream of water falling from a tap narrows because \(v\) increases while \(Av\) is constant: \(v^2=v_0^2+2gy\), \(A=A_0\dfrac{v_0}v\).
  • Speed of efflux (Torricelli) from a small hole at depth \(h\) below the free surface of an open tank: \(v=\sqrt{2gh}\) (if the tank is closed with a gauge pressure \(P\) above the liquid: \(v=\sqrt{2gh+\dfrac{2P}\rho}\)). The jet reaches the ground at the horizontal range \(x=2\sqrt{h(H-h)}\) (hole at depth \(h\), the liquid depth \(H\)): the range is maximum, \(x_{\max}=H\), for \(h=\dfrac H2\); holes at depths \(h\) and \(H-h\) give the same range. Reaction force on the tank \(F=\rho av^2=2\rho agh\) (\(a\) = area of the hole); a tank with a hole on the side tends to move backwards (rocket principle).
  • Time to empty a tank (area \(A\), a small hole of area \(a\), depth falling from \(H_1\) to \(H_2\)): \(t=\dfrac Aa\sqrt{\dfrac2g}\left(\sqrt{H_1}-\sqrt{H_2}\right)\); for complete emptying \(t=\dfrac Aa\sqrt{\dfrac{2H}g}\). The time for the level to fall from \(H\) to \(\dfrac H2\) is \(\left(\sqrt2-1\right)\dfrac Aa\sqrt{\dfrac Hg}\). If liquid is poured in at a constant rate \(Q_0\), the level becomes steady where \(Q_0=a\sqrt{2gh}\); the efflux speed when the vessel area is not very large: \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{2gh}{1-a^2/A^2}}\).
  • Venturimeter (a constriction in a pipe; cross-sections \(A_1\gt A_2\), pressure difference \(\Delta P\) measured by a manometer): \(v_1=A_2\sqrt{\dfrac{2\Delta P}{\rho\left(A_1^2-A_2^2\right)}}\), \(Q=A_1A_2\sqrt{\dfrac{2\Delta P}{\rho\left(A_1^2-A_2^2\right)}}\); the pressure is lower where the speed is greater, and \(\Delta P=\rho_mgh\) (manometer liquid). Pitot tube: measures the speed of a fluid (aircraft speed) from the stagnation pressure: \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{2\Delta P}\rho}=\sqrt{\dfrac{2\rho_mgh}{\rho}}\).
  • Siphon: speed of discharge \(v=\sqrt{2gh}\), where \(h\) is the vertical drop from the free surface to the outlet; the maximum height of the highest point above the free surface for a siphon to work is \(\dfrac{P_0}{\rho g}\) (\(10.3\) m of water; practically less); the pressure at the top is lower than the atmospheric.
  • Dynamic lift (aerofoil): the faster flow over the upper surface gives a lower pressure: lift \(F=\tfrac12\rho\left(v_{\text{upper}}^2-v_{\text{lower}}^2\right)A\). Magnus effect: a spinning ball curves in flight because the speed of the air relative to the ball is larger on one side (the side where the surface moves along the air flow), lowering the pressure there. Other applications: atomiser and the Bunsen burner (low pressure at the constriction draws in the fluid), two ships moving side by side attract each other, a roof is lifted in a storm (the pressure above is lower), a paper held between two parallel sheets blown in the gap moves inward.
  • Force of a jet (area \(A\), speed \(v\), density \(\rho\)) on a wall: striking perpendicularly and stopping, \(F=\rho Av^2\); rebounding elastically \(F=2\rho Av^2\); on a moving wall (velocity \(u\) away) \(F=\rho A(v-u)^2\) (in that frame); hitting a wall at an angle \(\theta\) with the normal and stopped along the wall: \(F=\rho Av^2\cos\theta\). A water jet leaving a pipe: \(\dfrac{dm}{dt}=\rho Av\), thrust \(\dfrac{dm}{dt}v\); power of a jet \(P=\tfrac12\rho Av^3\) (used in a water turbine or in a wind turbine of rotor area \(A\), which can extract at most about \(59\%\) of this — Betz limit).
  • Pressure in a flowing fluid at a bend or at different heights: the pressure at the narrow part of a horizontal pipe is lower: \(P_1-P_2=\tfrac12\rho\left(v_2^2-v_1^2\right)\); at the same speed in a vertical pipe the pressure difference is just \(\rho g\Delta h\). A stream turning in a bend exerts a force given by the change of momentum per unit time. Bernoulli’s principle fails for viscous or turbulent flow.
29
Gravitation, fluids & waves

Elasticity, Viscosity & Surface Tension

Elasticity: stress, strain, moduli, Poisson’s ratio and the stress–strain curve

\[Y=\frac{F/A}{\Delta L/L},\quad B=-\frac{\Delta P}{\Delta V/V},\quad G=\eta=\frac{F/A}{\phi},\quad \nu=-\frac{\text{lateral strain}}{\text{longitudinal strain}},\quad Y=3B(1-2\nu)=2G(1+\nu)\]
  • Stress \(=\dfrac FA\) (restoring force per unit area, unit Pa, dimension \([ML^{-1}T^{-2}]\)): tensile, compressive, shear (tangential) and hydrostatic (volume) stress. Strain is dimensionless: longitudinal \(\dfrac{\Delta L}L\), volumetric \(\dfrac{\Delta V}V\), shear \(\phi=\dfrac x\ell\) (angle of shear). Hooke’s law: stress \(\propto\) strain within the proportional limit. The moduli depend on the material only (not on the shape or size); \(Y\) is for solids, \(B\) for solids, liquids and gases, \(G\) only for solids (fluids have zero rigidity).
  • Typical values: \(Y\) of steel \(2\times10^{11}\) Pa, copper \(1.1\times10^{11}\), aluminium \(7\times10^{10}\), rubber \(\sim10^{7}\) (not Hookean); \(B\) of water \(2.2\times10^9\) Pa; steel is more elastic than rubber (a larger stress is needed for the same strain). Compressibility \(K=\dfrac1B\). For an ideal gas \(B=P\) (isothermal) and \(B=\gamma P\) (adiabatic).
  • Wire: \(\Delta L=\dfrac{FL}{AY}=\dfrac{4FL}{\pi d^2Y}\); the force constant \(k=\dfrac{YA}L\) (so \(\Delta L\propto\dfrac L{r^2}\), a longer or thinner wire stretches more); inter-atomic force constant \(k=Yr_0\). Wires in series: \(\dfrac1k=\sum\dfrac1{k_i}\) (same force); in parallel: \(k=\sum k_i\) (same extension, force shared in the ratio \(\dfrac{A_iY_i}{L_i}\)). A mass \(m\) hanging on a wire oscillates with \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{mL}{AY}}\).
  • Poisson’s ratio \(\nu=\dfrac{\Delta d/d}{\Delta L/L}\) (lateral contraction / longitudinal elongation); \(-1\lt\nu\lt0.5\) (in practice \(0\) to \(0.5\); steel \(\approx0.3\), rubber \(\approx0.5\)). The fractional change in the volume of a stretched wire is \(\dfrac{\Delta V}V=\dfrac{\Delta L}L(1-2\nu)\); an incompressible material (\(\nu=0.5\)) keeps its volume. Relations: \(\dfrac9Y=\dfrac3G+\dfrac1B\); \(Y=\dfrac{9BG}{3B+G}\); \(\nu=\dfrac{3B-2G}{2(3B+G)}\); \(B\gt0\) requires \(\nu\lt0.5\), \(G\gt0\) requires \(\nu\gt-1\).
  • Stress–strain curve (ductile metal): O–A straight line (proportional limit, Hooke’s law, slope \(=Y\)); A–B elastic region up to the elastic limit (the wire returns to its original length on unloading); beyond B the yield point and plastic deformation (permanent set); the ultimate tensile strength (maximum stress); then necking and fracture. Ductile materials (copper, iron) show a long plastic region; brittle materials (glass, cast iron) break soon after the elastic limit. Elastomers (rubber) can be strained a lot, do not obey Hooke’s law and show elastic hysteresis (the loading and unloading curves differ; the area of the loop equals the energy dissipated as heat per unit volume per cycle — rubber is used in shock absorbers and vehicle tyres). The breaking stress of a material divided by the safe stress is the factor of safety. A steeper curve means a larger \(Y\); the same material has the same \(Y\) whatever its thickness (but different breaking force).
  • Elastic potential energy stored in a stretched wire: \(U=\tfrac12F\Delta L=\tfrac12k\Delta L^2=\dfrac{F^2L}{2AY}\); per unit volume \(u=\tfrac12\,\text{stress}\times\text{strain}=\dfrac{\sigma^2}{2Y}=\tfrac12Y\epsilon^2\) (area under the stress–strain curve). Energy stored in a compressed fluid \(\tfrac12B\left(\dfrac{\Delta V}V\right)^2V\). A load \(mg\) gently hung on a wire does work \(mg\Delta L\), but only \(\tfrac12mg\Delta L\) is stored as elastic energy; the other half is dissipated as the wire settles.

Elasticity problem types: hanging wire, rotating rod, thermal stress, torsion, bending

  • Wire hanging under its own weight (length \(L\), density \(\rho\)): the stress increases linearly toward the support; total elongation \(\Delta L=\dfrac{\rho gL^2}{2Y}=\dfrac{MgL}{2AY}\) (half of that under a hanging mass equal to its weight); the stress is maximum at the support, \(\rho gL\). A uniform rod of mass \(M\) pulled by force \(F\) along a smooth horizontal surface: tension at a distance \(x\) from the free end \(T=\dfrac{Fx}L\), elongation \(\dfrac{FL}{2AY}\).
  • Rod rotating about one end (angular speed \(\omega\), length \(L\)): the tension at distance \(r\) from the axis \(T=\tfrac12\rho A\omega^2(L^2-r^2)\); elongation \(\Delta L=\dfrac{\rho\omega^2L^3}{3Y}\). Tapered wire (radii \(r_1,r_2\), length \(L\)): \(\Delta L=\dfrac{FL}{\pi Yr_1r_2}\).
  • Composite wires (same \(F\), in series): \(\Delta L=F\left(\dfrac{L_1}{A_1Y_1}+\dfrac{L_2}{A_2Y_2}\right)\); equal extension in parallel gives \(\dfrac{F_1}{F_2}=\dfrac{A_1Y_1L_2}{A_2Y_2L_1}\). Two wires of the same material and length with radii in the ratio \(1:2\) have extensions in the ratio \(4:1\) for the same load.
  • Thermal stress: a rod of length \(L\) heated by \(\Delta T\) and free to expand: \(\Delta L=L\alpha\Delta T\), no stress; if it is fixed between rigid supports, the stress \(=Y\alpha\Delta T\) and the force \(=YA\alpha\Delta T\) (independent of length); the strain energy per unit volume \(\tfrac12Y\alpha^2\Delta T^2\). A steel tyre made slightly smaller is heated to fit on a wheel and then, on cooling, grips it with the tension \(YA\alpha\Delta T\). For wires of two materials joined in series between rigid walls the total extension is zero, \(\dfrac{F}{A}\left(\dfrac{L_1}{Y_1}+\dfrac{L_2}{Y_2}\right)=\left(\alpha_1L_1+\alpha_2L_2\right)\Delta T\).
  • Bulk compression of liquids: at a depth \(h\) in an ocean, \(\dfrac{\Delta V}V=\dfrac{\rho gh}B\) (about \(0.45\%\) at \(1\) km for water); the density increases to \(\rho'\approx\rho\left(1+\dfrac{\rho gh}B\right)\); the speed of sound in a liquid \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac B\rho}\), in a solid rod \(\sqrt{\dfrac Y\rho}\).
  • Torsion of a cylindrical wire (radius \(r\), length \(\ell\), modulus of rigidity \(\eta\)) twisted through an angle \(\theta\): restoring torque \(\tau=C\theta\) with \(C=\dfrac{\pi\eta r^4}{2\ell}\) (hollow cylinder \(\dfrac{\pi\eta(r_2^4-r_1^4)}{2\ell}\)); a torsional pendulum has \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac IC}\); elastic energy \(\tfrac12C\theta^2\). A hollow shaft is stronger for the same mass; a thicker wire resists twisting as \(r^4\).
  • Bending of beams (length \(\ell\), breadth \(b\), depth \(d\)): a beam supported at its ends with a load \(W\) at the centre sags by \(\delta=\dfrac{W\ell^3}{4Ybd^3}\); a cantilever with the load at its free end \(\delta=\dfrac{4W\ell^3}{Ybd^3}\) (\(\propto\dfrac1{d^3}\): a deep beam, an I-section girder, is stiff). An ideal fluid has zero shear modulus, and an incompressible fluid has an infinite bulk modulus.

Viscosity: Poiseuille’s equation, Stokes’ law, Reynolds number

  • Viscous force between layers \(F=\eta A\left|\dfrac{dv}{dx}\right|\); coefficient of viscosity \(\eta\): SI unit Pa s \(=\) N s m\(^{-2}\) \(=\) kg m\(^{-1}\)s\(^{-1}\) (\(1\) Pa s \(=10\) poise); dimension \([ML^{-1}T^{-1}]\); water \(1\times10^{-3}\) Pa s, glycerine \(1.4\) Pa s, air \(1.8\times10^{-5}\) Pa s at room temperature. The viscosity of a liquid decreases with temperature while that of a gas increases (\(\propto\sqrt T\)). Kinematic viscosity \(\nu=\dfrac\eta\rho\) (m\(^2\) s\(^{-1}\); stokes in CGS). Viscosity is the internal friction of fluids.
  • Poiseuille’s formula: the volume rate of flow through a tube of radius \(r\) and length \(\ell\) under the pressure difference \(P\): \(Q=\dfrac{\pi Pr^4}{8\eta\ell}\). Fluid resistance \(R_f=\dfrac{8\eta\ell}{\pi r^4}\) so \(P=QR_f\) (analogue of Ohm’s law: \(P\leftrightarrow V\), \(Q\leftrightarrow I\)); in series \(R=\sum R_i\) (same \(Q\)), in parallel \(\dfrac1R=\sum\dfrac1{R_i}\) (same \(P\)). \(Q\propto r^4\): doubling the radius increases the flow 16 times; a small decrease of the arterial radius increases the blood pressure needed considerably. The power needed to maintain the flow \(=PQ\).
  • Velocity profile in laminar flow in a tube of radius \(R\): parabolic, \(v(r)=\dfrac{P}{4\eta\ell}\left(R^2-r^2\right)\); maximum at the axis \(v_{\max}=\dfrac{PR^2}{4\eta\ell}\); the average velocity is half the maximum, \(v_{\text{avg}}=\dfrac{PR^2}{8\eta\ell}\); the speed is zero at the wall. The pressure falls uniformly along the length. Streamline flow occurs in a thin tube at low speed.
  • Stokes’ law: the drag on a small sphere of radius \(r\) moving at speed \(v\) in a fluid, \(F=6\pi\eta rv\) (valid for low Reynolds number). Terminal velocity of a sphere of density \(\rho\) in a fluid of density \(\sigma\): \(v_T=\dfrac{2r^2(\rho-\sigma)g}{9\eta}\) (\(\propto r^2\); for a rising bubble or a light sphere \(\rho\lt\sigma\) the velocity is upward).
