The approach to terminal speed: \(v=v_T\left(1-e^{-t/\tau}\right)\), \(\tau=\dfrac{2r^2\rho}{9\eta}\) (\(=\dfrac m{6\pi\eta r}\)). At the terminal velocity: weight \(=\) buoyancy \(+\) viscous drag. Larger drops fall faster; \(n\) equal drops combining into one fall with \(n^{2/3}\) times the speed. A dust particle in air or a rain drop reach a steady speed quickly.
  • Reynolds number \(Re=\dfrac{\rho vD}\eta\) (\(D\) = diameter of the pipe): for \(Re\lt1000\) the flow is streamline (laminar), for \(Re\gt2000\) turbulent, in between unstable; the critical velocity \(v_c=\dfrac{Re_c\,\eta}{\rho D}\) — a thin pipe, a more viscous or less dense liquid has a higher critical speed. In turbulent flow the drag \(\propto v^2\): \(F=\tfrac12C_D\rho Av^2\) and the terminal speed of a falling body \(v_T=\sqrt{\dfrac{2mg}{C_D\rho A}}\) with \(A\) the cross-section (parachute).
  • Methods of measuring \(\eta\): Poiseuille’s capillary flow method, Stokes’ falling-ball method, the Ostwald viscometer, rotating cylinder. Lubricants, engine oils and blood (\(\eta\approx3\) times that of water) are examples; the viscosity of honey drops sharply on heating.

Surface tension: surface energy, drops, bubbles, excess pressure

  • Surface tension \(T=\dfrac F\ell=\dfrac{W}{\Delta A}\) (force per unit length acting in the surface perpendicular to an imaginary line; equals surface energy per unit area): SI unit N m\(^{-1}\) \(=\) J m\(^{-2}\); dimension \([MT^{-2}]\). Water \(0.072\) N m\(^{-1}\) at \(20^\circ\text{C}\), mercury \(0.465\), soap solution \(\approx0.03\). It decreases linearly with temperature and vanishes at the critical temperature; dissolved soap or detergent lowers it, a salt raises it slightly. Origin: the net inward cohesive force on surface molecules, which have extra (surface) energy. The liquid surface behaves like a stretched membrane and tends to the minimum area (a drop is spherical).
  • Film on a frame: a soap film has two surfaces, so a sliding wire of length \(\ell\) experiences \(F=2T\ell\); the work to move it by \(x\) is \(2T\ell x\) (a thin film’s surface energy \(2TA\)). A plate or a ring lifted out of a liquid feels an extra force \(T\times\)(wetted perimeter); for a ring (inner radius \(r_1\), outer \(r_2\)) \(F=W+2\pi T(r_1+r_2)\). A needle floats if \(2T\ell\cos\theta\ge\) its weight (\(\ell\) its length). A drop falling from a tube of radius \(r\): \(mg=2\pi rT\) (Tate’s law, the drop-weight method).
  • Surface energy: drop \(4\pi R^2T\); soap bubble \(2\times4\pi R^2T=8\pi R^2T\). Work done in blowing a soap bubble from radius \(r_1\) to \(r_2\): \(W=8\pi T\left(r_2^2-r_1^2\right)\); for a drop \(4\pi T\left(r_2^2-r_1^2\right)\).
  • Excess pressure inside a curved surface: liquid drop or air bubble in a liquid \(\dfrac{2T}R\); soap bubble (two surfaces) \(\dfrac{4T}R\); liquid cylinder \(\dfrac TR\); in general \(\Delta P=T\left(\dfrac1{R_1}+\dfrac1{R_2}\right)\). An air bubble of radius \(R\) at a depth \(h\) in a liquid has the pressure inside \(P_0+\rho gh+\dfrac{2T}R\). The excess pressure is larger in a smaller bubble; if two bubbles are connected by a tube, air flows from the smaller to the larger one and the smaller bubble shrinks.
  • Splitting and coalescing of drops: a drop of radius \(R\) splitting into \(n\) equal drops of radius \(r=\dfrac R{n^{1/3}}\) absorbs the surface energy \(W=4\pi T\left(nr^2-R^2\right)=4\pi R^2T\left(n^{1/3}-1\right)\); the reverse process releases this energy and warms the liquid by \(\Delta\theta=\dfrac{3T}{\rho s}\left(\dfrac1r-\dfrac1R\right)\) (\(s\) = specific heat in J kg\(^{-1}\)K\(^{-1}\)). The pressure and the surface area change but the volume is conserved: \(nr^3=R^3\).
  • Two soap bubbles (radii \(r_1\lt r_2\)) in contact: the shared film is curved toward the larger bubble with radius \(r=\dfrac{r_1r_2}{r_2-r_1}\), and the pressure difference across it \(4T\left(\dfrac1{r_1}-\dfrac1{r_2}\right)\). If they merge isothermally into a bubble of radius \(r\) in an atmosphere \(P_0\): \(P_0\left(r^3-r_1^3-r_2^3\right)=4T\left(r_1^2+r_2^2-r^2\right)\) (for \(P_0\) large, \(r^3\approx r_1^3+r_2^3\); if the pressure is negligible compared with the surface tension effect, \(r^2=r_1^2+r_2^2\)).
  • Effects: dew drops are spherical; a small hole in a fine mesh keeps the water by surface tension; a rain-proof cloth; insects (water striders) walk on water; camphor pieces dart on water because the surface tension decreases toward them; hot water cleans better because of the lower \(T\); the soap and detergents lower \(T\) and the contact angle. Capillary length \(\sqrt{\dfrac T{\rho g}}\) (\(\approx2.7\) mm for water): drops smaller than this are spherical, larger ones are flattened by gravity.

Contact angle and capillarity

\[h=\frac{2T\cos\theta}{r\rho g},\qquad mg=2\pi rT\cos\theta\ \text{(weight of liquid raised)},\qquad hr=\text{constant}\ (\text{Jurin})\]
  • Angle of contact \(\theta\): the angle between the tangent to the liquid surface at the point of contact and the solid surface, measured inside the liquid. \(\theta\lt90^\circ\) (adhesion greater than cohesion): the liquid wets the solid, the meniscus is concave, the liquid rises (water–clean glass, \(\theta\approx0^\circ\)); \(\theta\gt90^\circ\) (cohesion greater): the meniscus is convex and the liquid is depressed (mercury–glass \(\approx140^\circ\), water–paraffin \(107^\circ\)). \(\theta=0\) is complete wetting.
  • Capillary rise in a tube of radius \(r\): the pressure just below the meniscus is lower than \(P_0\) by \(\dfrac{2T\cos\theta}r\), and this is balanced by \(\rho gh\); \(h=\dfrac{2T\cos\theta}{r\rho g}\) (\(\propto\dfrac1r\); for \(\theta\gt90^\circ\), \(h\) is negative, a depression). The weight of the liquid raised is supported by the vertical component of the surface tension: \(\pi r^2h\rho g=2\pi rT\cos\theta\). Water rises \(\approx3\) cm in a tube of radius \(0.5\) mm (\(T=0.072,\ \theta=0\)).
  • Details: if the tube is tilted at \(\alpha\) to the vertical, the length of the liquid column is \(\dfrac h{\cos\alpha}\) (the vertical height remains \(h\)); if the tube is shorter than \(h\), the liquid just rises to the top (the meniscus becomes flatter, \(\cos\theta'=\dfrac{h'r\rho g}{2T}\)); in a lift accelerating upward \(g\to g+a\) and \(h\) decreases; on the Moon \(h\) is six times larger; in free fall (weightlessness) the liquid fills the whole tube. \(h\) falls on heating (\(T\) decreases, \(\rho\) decreases a little). A U-tube with arms of radii \(r_1,r_2\): the level difference \(\left|\Delta h\right|=\dfrac{2T\cos\theta}{\rho g}\left|\dfrac1{r_1}-\dfrac1{r_2}\right|\) (the liquid stands higher in the narrower arm). For two liquids, \(h\propto\dfrac{T\cos\theta}{\rho}\).
  • Between two parallel plates separated by \(d\): \(h=\dfrac{2T\cos\theta}{\rho gd}\); the plates stick together by the pressure difference \(\dfrac{2T}d\) across the meniscus (the force \(F=\dfrac{2TA}d\) for a film of area \(A\), a thin water film between two glass plates is hard to separate). Plates meeting at a small angle (wedge) give a hyperbolic rise, \(xh=\) constant.
  • The mass of the liquid in a capillary: \(m=\dfrac{2\pi rT\cos\theta}g\) (independent of the density of the liquid). Two capillaries of different radii in a liquid: the heights are in the inverse ratio of the radii.
  • Applications: rise of oil in a wick or a lamp, ink in a blotting paper, sap in trees (partly), water in the soil and in plants, towels, a capillary ink pen; waterproofing raises the angle of contact above \(90^\circ\) so that water cannot pass through the pores; flotation process separates ore from rock using the different wetting; detergents help water wet fabric by reducing \(\theta\) and \(T\).
30
Gravitation, fluids & waves

Sound Wave

(i) Longitudinal displacement

\[\xi=A\sin(\omega t-kx)\]

(ii) Pressure excess

\[P_{ex}=-B\frac{\partial\xi}{\partial x}=(BAk)\cos(\omega t-kx)\]

This holds for travelling as well as standing waves. The amplitude of the pressure excess is \(BAk\).

(iii) Speed of sound

\[C=\sqrt{\frac E\rho}\]

\(E\) is the elastic modulus of the medium and \(\rho\) its density.

\[\text{Solid: }C=\sqrt{\frac Y\rho}\qquad\text{Liquid: }C=\sqrt{\frac B\rho}\qquad\text{Gas: }C=\sqrt{\frac B\rho}=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma P}\rho}=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma RT}{M_0}}\]

\(Y\) is Young's modulus of the solid, \(B\) is the bulk modulus, and \(M_0\) is the molecular weight of the gas in kg/mole.

Intensity of a sound wave

\[\langle I\rangle=2\pi^2f^2A^2\rho v=\frac{P_m^2}{2\rho v}\]\[\langle I\rangle\propto P_m^2\]

(iv) Loudness of sound

\[L=10\log_{10}\!\left(\frac I{I_0}\right)\ \text{dB}\]

\(I_0=10^{-12}\) W/m² is the minimum intensity the human ear can hear.

Intensity at distance \(r\) from a point source:

\[I=\frac P{4\pi r^2}\]

Interference of sound waves

For \(P_1=p_{m1}\sin(\omega t-kx_1+\theta_1)\) and \(P_2=p_{m2}\sin(\omega t-kx_2+\theta_2)\), the resultant excess pressure at point \(O\) is

\[p=P_1+P_2=p_0\sin(\omega t-kx+\theta)\]\[p_0=\sqrt{p_{m1}^2+p_{m2}^2+2p_{m1}p_{m2}\cos\phi}\qquad\phi=[k(x_2-x_1)+(\theta_1-\theta_2)]\]\[I=I_1+I_2+2\sqrt{I_1I_2}\cos\phi\]
  • (i) Constructive interference: \(\phi=2n\pi\) and \(p_0=p_{m1}+p_{m2}\)
  • (ii) Destructive interference: \(\phi=(2n+1)\pi\) and \(p_0=|p_{m1}-p_{m2}|\)

If \(\phi\) is due to path difference only, \(\phi=\dfrac{2\pi}\lambda\Delta x\). Constructive: \(\Delta x=n\lambda\). Destructive: \(\Delta x=(2n+1)\dfrac\lambda2\).

  • (a) If \(p_{m1}=p_{m2}\) and \(\theta=\pi,3\pi,\ldots\), the resultant \(p=0\), i.e. no sound.
  • (b) If \(p_{m1}=p_{m2}\) and \(\phi=0,2\pi,4\pi,\ldots\), then \(p_0=2p_m\) and \(I_0=4I_1\).

Organ pipes

Closed organ pipe (\(n\) = overtone):

\[f=\frac v{4\ell},\ \frac{3v}{4\ell},\ \frac{5v}{4\ell},\ \ldots,\ \frac{(2n+1)v}{4\ell}\]

Open organ pipe:

\[f=\frac v{2\ell},\ \frac{2v}{2\ell},\ \frac{3v}{2\ell},\ \ldots,\ \frac{nv}{2\ell}\]

Beats

\[\text{Beat frequency}=|f_1-f_2|\]

Doppler's effect

Observed frequency, where \(v_0\) is the observer's speed and \(v_s\) the source's speed:

\[f'=f\left(\frac{v-v_0}{v-v_s}\right)\]

Apparent wavelength:

\[\lambda'=\lambda\left(\frac{v-v_s}v\right)\]

Speed of sound in different media and its dependence on conditions

\[v_{\text{solid}}=\sqrt{\frac Y\rho},\qquad v_{\text{liquid}}=\sqrt{\frac B\rho},\qquad v_{\text{gas}}=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma P}\rho}=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma RT}M}\]
  • Sound is a longitudinal mechanical wave (compressions and rarefactions) that needs a medium. Newton assumed isothermal changes, \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac P\rho}\approx280\) m s\(^{-1}\) (in air at STP, wrong); Laplace corrected this by taking the compressions to be adiabatic (they are too rapid for heat exchange), giving \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{\gamma P}\rho}\approx332\) m s\(^{-1}\) at \(0^\circ\text{C}\) (\(\gamma=1.4\) for air). Bulk modulus: \(B=P\) (isothermal) or \(\gamma P\) (adiabatic).
  • Temperature: \(v\propto\sqrt T\) (kelvin): \(\dfrac{v_{t}}{v_0}=\sqrt{\dfrac{273+t}{273}}\approx1+\dfrac t{546}\); in air \(v\approx331+0.61\,t\) m s\(^{-1}\) (\(t\) in \(^\circ\text{C}\)), \(343\) m s\(^{-1}\) at \(20^\circ\text{C}\). \(v\) increases by about \(0.61\) m s\(^{-1}\) per kelvin.
  • Pressure: at a constant temperature \(v\) is independent of pressure (because \(\dfrac P\rho\) is constant for a gas, Boyle’s law). Humidity: moist air is lighter, so \(v\) increases with humidity. Nature of the gas: \(v\propto\sqrt{\dfrac\gamma M}\) at the same temperature: hydrogen \(1270\) m s\(^{-1}\), helium \(970\), oxygen \(316\), carbon dioxide \(260\) (at \(0^\circ\text{C}\)). The relation to the rms speed: \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac\gamma3}\,v_{\text{rms}}\). Wind: the speed relative to the ground changes by the wind speed in the direction of the wave. Frequency, amplitude and wavelength do not affect \(v\) (no dispersion in air); on passing into another medium the frequency stays the same, \(\lambda\) changes with \(v\).
  • Typical speeds: air \(\approx340\) m s\(^{-1}\), water \(\approx1480\), iron/steel \(\approx5000\)–\(5900\), rubber \(\approx50\)–\(100\). Sound travels faster in solids than liquids than gases (the larger \(Y\) or \(B\) more than compensates the larger density). Longitudinal waves exist in all media; transverse waves only in solids (and on strings, and at surfaces). A longitudinal wave in a long thin rod \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac Y\rho}\).
  • Audible range: \(20\) Hz – \(20\) kHz; infrasonic \(\lt20\) Hz (earthquakes, whales, elephants); ultrasonic \(\gt20\) kHz (bats, dolphins, SONAR, medical ultrasound). The audible \(\lambda\) in air: \(17\) m to \(1.7\) cm.
  • Echo: the persistence of hearing is about \(0.1\) s, so for a distinct echo the reflecting surface must be at least \(\dfrac{v\times0.1}2\approx17\) m away; depth of the sea \(d=\dfrac{vt}2\) (SONAR); reverberation is the prolonged sound from repeated reflections; reverberation time (Sabine) \(T=0.161\dfrac VA\) (\(V\) = volume of the hall in m\(^3\), \(A\) = total absorption in m\(^2\)).
  • With altitude, the temperature of the air falls and the speed of sound decreases, so sound waves are refracted upward; at night the cold air near the ground makes sound bend downward, so it can be heard farther. The speed of a sound wave in a gas mixture: \(v=\sqrt{\dfrac{\gamma_{\text{mix}}P}{\rho_{\text{mix}}}}\).

Displacement, pressure and intensity of a sound wave; the decibel scale

\[s=s_0\sin(\omega t-kx),\qquad \Delta P=-B\frac{\partial s}{\partial x}=\Delta P_0\cos(\omega t-kx),\qquad \Delta P_0=Bks_0=\rho v\omega s_0,\qquad I=\frac{\Delta P_0^2}{2\rho v}=\tfrac12\rho v\omega^2s_0^2\]
  • The pressure wave is \(\dfrac\pi2\) out of phase with the displacement wave: the pressure variation is maximum where the displacement is zero (a displacement node is a pressure antinode, and vice versa). The displacement amplitude \(s_0\) is of the order of \(10^{-11}\) m at the threshold of hearing; the pressure amplitude at the threshold \(\approx3\times10^{-5}\) Pa; at the threshold of pain \(\approx30\) Pa.
  • Intensity \(I=\dfrac{\text{power}}{\text{area}}=\dfrac P{A}\); energy density \(u=\tfrac12\rho\omega^2s_0^2=\dfrac I v\); for a point source in all directions \(I=\dfrac P{4\pi r^2}\) (\(I\propto\dfrac1{r^2}\), \(s_0,\Delta P_0\propto\dfrac1r\)); for a line source \(I\propto\dfrac1r\). Intensity \(\propto\) (frequency)\(^2\)(amplitude)\(^2\). The power of a source through a surface of area \(A\) \(=IA\).
  • Sound level (loudness) \(\beta=10\log_{10}\dfrac I{I_0}\) decibel (dB), \(I_0=10^{-12}\) W m\(^{-2}\) (threshold of hearing at \(1\) kHz); \(\beta=0\) dB at the threshold, \(\approx120\) dB at the threshold of pain (\(1\) W m\(^{-2}\)). Differences: \(\beta_2-\beta_1=10\log_{10}\dfrac{I_2}{I_1}=20\log_{10}\dfrac{\Delta P_2}{\Delta P_1}=20\log_{10}\dfrac{r_1}{r_2}\) (point source). A factor of \(10\) in intensity is \(10\) dB; doubling the intensity adds \(3\) dB; \(n\) identical incoherent sources: \(\beta=\beta_1+10\log_{10}n\); two sources of \(60\) dB each give \(63\) dB; a \(100\)-fold intensity change is \(20\) dB.
  • Examples: whisper \(\approx20\) dB, conversation \(\approx60\), traffic \(\approx80\), a rock concert \(\approx110\)–\(120\). Loudness depends on the intensity and the frequency (the ear is most sensitive around \(2\)–\(4\) kHz; the unit phon). Pitch depends on the frequency, quality (timbre) on the waveform (the number and relative intensities of the overtones).
  • Characteristics: loudness \(\leftrightarrow\) intensity, pitch \(\leftrightarrow\) frequency, quality \(\leftrightarrow\) waveform. A note and its harmonics: the fundamental plus overtones; the musical interval of an octave means the frequency ratio \(2:1\) (a fifth is \(3:2\), a fourth \(4:3\)).

Organ pipes, resonance tube and Kundt’s tube: more results

  • Open pipe (both ends open; displacement antinodes at the ends): \(L=\dfrac{n\lambda}2\), \(f_n=\dfrac{nv}{2L}\), \(n=1,2,3,\dots\) (all harmonics). Closed pipe (one end closed, which is a displacement node): \(L=\dfrac{(2n-1)\lambda}4\), \(f_n=\dfrac{(2n-1)v}{4L}\) (odd harmonics only: \(1:3:5\)). The fundamental of an open pipe is twice that of a closed pipe of the same length; the third harmonic is the first overtone of the closed pipe. The closed pipe has a node at the closed end where the pressure change is maximum; the open end has a pressure node (\(\Delta P=0\)).
  • End correction: the antinode forms slightly outside the open end at a distance \(e\approx0.6r\) (\(r\) = radius). Effective length: closed pipe \(L+e\), open pipe \(L+2e\) (both ends). So \(f_1=\dfrac v{4(L+0.6r)}\) (closed) and \(\dfrac v{2(L+1.2r)}\) (open).
  • Resonance tube experiment (a tuning fork of frequency \(f\) over a tube whose water level can be changed): the first resonance at length \(L_1\) with \(L_1+e=\dfrac\lambda4\), the second at \(L_2+e=\dfrac{3\lambda}4\), so \(\lambda=2(L_2-L_1)\), \(v=2f(L_2-L_1)\), the end correction \(e=\dfrac{L_2-3L_1}2\) (so use two resonances to cancel the end correction). The loudness is maximum at resonance.
  • Pipe partly filled or changed: an open pipe dipped partly in water becomes a closed pipe of the length above the water; an open pipe of length \(L\) has the same fundamental as a closed pipe of length \(\dfrac L2\) (\(f=\dfrac v{2L}\)). If an open pipe is closed at one end its fundamental frequency is halved. Pipes of the same length with different gases: \(f\propto v\). When the temperature rises, \(f\propto\sqrt T\) (the length changes only slightly). A pipe closed at one end at the fundamental has \(\dfrac L\lambda=\dfrac14\).
  • Two pipes sounding together: two open pipes of slightly different lengths \(L_1\gt L_2\) give a beat frequency \(\dfrac v2\left(\dfrac1{L_2}-\dfrac1{L_1}\right)\); an open and a closed pipe of the same length have fundamentals \(\dfrac v{2L}\) and \(\dfrac v{4L}\) (an octave apart).
  • Kundt’s tube: a rod clamped at its middle and stroked vibrates longitudinally with a node at its middle (\(f=\dfrac{v_r}{2l}\)); the dust in the tube collects at the nodes with the spacing \(\dfrac{\lambda_g}2\); \(\dfrac{v_g}{v_r}=\dfrac{\lambda_g}{\lambda_r}=\dfrac{\text{node spacing}_{\text{gas}}}{l_{\text{rod}}}\) (\(\lambda_r=2l\)). For a rod clamped at one end: \(f=\dfrac{v_r}{4l}\). Quincke’s tube: the sound splits into two paths and recombines; sliding one tube by \(x\) changes the path by \(2x\); successive minima occur at displacements \(\dfrac\lambda2\) (\(\lambda=2\Delta x\)), \(\lambda\) is found from the distance between consecutive minima.
  • A string (sonometer) and a pipe in resonance: the string of a given tension and length vibrates at \(f=\dfrac p{2l}\sqrt{\dfrac T\mu}\), and can be tuned to a tuning fork of frequency \(f\); a stretched string can excite air columns at the same frequency (forced vibrations: resonance when the natural frequency of the air column matches the driving frequency).

Interference and beats: more results

  • Two coherent sources (e.g. loudspeakers) in phase: the path difference \(\Delta=S_1P-S_2P\); maximum intensity at \(\Delta=n\lambda\), minimum at \(\left(n+\tfrac12\right)\lambda\); with a phase difference \(\phi_0\) between the sources the phase difference at \(P\) is \(\phi=\dfrac{2\pi}\lambda\Delta+\phi_0\). \(I=I_1+I_2+2\sqrt{I_1I_2}\cos\phi\); equal sources \(I=4I_0\cos^2\dfrac\phi2\). On the line joining two in-phase sources a distance \(d\) apart, a point at distance \(x\) from the midpoint (between the sources) has \(\Delta=2x\): maxima at \(x=\dfrac{n\lambda}2\) and minima at \(x=\dfrac{(2n+1)\lambda}4\), with \(|x|\lt\dfrac d2\) (a standing-wave pattern); outside the segment \(\Delta=d\) and the intensity is constant (maximum if \(d=n\lambda\)).
  • Beats: two waves of slightly different frequencies \(f_1\) and \(f_2\) give a resultant of frequency \(\dfrac{f_1+f_2}2\) with a slowly varying amplitude \(2A\cos\left(\pi(f_1-f_2)t\right)\); the intensity maxima occur \(|f_1-f_2|\) times a second: beat frequency \(f_b=|f_1-f_2|\). The maximum and minimum intensities \(\propto(a_1+a_2)^2\) and \((a_1-a_2)^2\) (for equal amplitudes \(4I_0\) and \(0\)); the beats are heard if \(f_b\lt\) about \(10\) Hz. Tuning fork problems: loading a fork with wax lowers its frequency; filing it raises the frequency; if the beat frequency changes after loading (it increases, the fork’s frequency was lower than the standard) one can decide which of the two is higher. If a fork is in resonance with an air column of length \(L\) and another one gives \(n\) beats per second with it, the change of \(L\) can find the unknown.
  • Beats with strings and wires: for wires of the same material, tension and diameter \(f\propto\dfrac1L\); a small change of tension \(\Delta T\) changes the frequency by \(\Delta f=\dfrac f2\dfrac{\Delta T}T\). Two open pipes of slightly different lengths \(L_1\gt L_2\) give beats at \(\dfrac v2\left(\dfrac1{L_2}-\dfrac1{L_1}\right)\). In Doppler problems the direct and reflected sounds from a moving body beat at \(\Delta f\approx\dfrac{2uf}v\) (\(u\ll v\)).
  • Reflection of sound: at a rigid wall (or a closed end) the displacement wave is reflected with a phase change of \(\pi\) (a displacement node, pressure antinode) and the pressure wave without a change; at an open end the displacement wave is reflected without a phase change (a displacement antinode) while the pressure wave is inverted (a pressure node).

Doppler effect: general formula, wind, reflection, Mach cone

\[f'=f\,\frac{v+v_O}{v-v_S}\ \ (\text{velocities positive for approach}),\qquad f'=f\,\frac{v+w-v_O}{v+w-v_S}\ \ (\text{all positive in the direction }S\to O)\]
  • Rules: \(v\) = speed of sound in the medium; \(v_O\) and \(v_S\) the speeds of the observer and the source relative to the medium; observer moving toward the source \(\Rightarrow+v_O\) (numerator \(v+v_O\)); source moving toward the observer \(\Rightarrow-v_S\) (denominator \(v-v_S\)); away: reverse the signs. The source’s motion changes the wavelength (\(\lambda'=\dfrac{v-v_S}f\)), the observer’s motion changes the relative speed of the wave (the wavelength is unchanged). Only the component of the velocity along the line joining the source and the observer matters: \(f'=f\dfrac{v+v_O\cos\theta_O}{v-v_S\cos\theta_S}\) (the angles with the line of sight at that instant). For a source moving in a circle, the observed frequency varies between a maximum and a minimum when the velocity is along the line of sight.
  • Wind of speed \(w\) in the direction of the line from the source to the observer: \(f'=f\dfrac{v+w-v_O}{v+w-v_S}\) (with both \(v_O\) and \(v_S\) taken positive in the same direction \(S\to O\)); if the source and the observer move with the same velocity along the line joining them, \(v_O=v_S\) and \(f'=f\), with or without a wind.
  • Reflection from a moving surface (a source at rest, a reflector approaching at \(u\)): the reflector receives \(f_1=f\dfrac{v+u}v\) and re-emits as a moving source: \(f_2=f_1\dfrac v{v-u}=f\dfrac{v+u}{v-u}\) (receding reflector: \(f\dfrac{v-u}{v+u}\)). The beat frequency between the direct and reflected sound: \(\dfrac{2uf}{v-u}\approx\dfrac{2uf}v\). For a source and an observer both moving and a stationary reflector, use the Doppler formula twice (once for the wave arriving at the reflector, once for the reflected wave).
  • Observer or source accelerating: use the instantaneous speeds. A source oscillating in SHM with the speed amplitude \(v_{S0}\) is heard with a frequency varying between \(f\dfrac v{v+v_{S0}}\) and \(f\dfrac v{v-v_{S0}}\) (the extremes at the equilibrium position, where the speed is maximum). The frequency heard from a train passing at a constant speed drops abruptly from \(f\dfrac v{v-v_S}\) to \(f\dfrac v{v+v_S}\) as it passes.
  • Shock waves: if a source moves faster than sound (\(v_S\gt v\)), the wavefronts form a cone (Mach cone) with half-angle \(\sin\alpha=\dfrac v{v_S}=\dfrac1M\), where \(M=\dfrac{v_S}v\) is the Mach number (supersonic if \(M\gt1\)). The shock wave (sonic boom) is heard as a sharp bang along the cone. Bow waves on water and the Cherenkov radiation (a charged particle moving faster than light in a medium) are analogues.
  • Applications: radar speed guns, the Doppler blood-flow meters, Doppler weather radar, measuring the speed of stars (see Doppler effect for light). For light, only the relative velocity matters; for sound, the velocities relative to the medium matter separately.

Ultrasound, acoustics and applications

  • Ultrasound (above \(20\) kHz, produced by the piezoelectric effect in quartz crystals or by magnetostriction in nickel rods): short wavelength so little diffraction, directional beams; uses: SONAR (range finding under water, \(d=\dfrac{vt}2\)), flaw detection, medical imaging and therapy (ultrasonography), cleaning (cavitation), welding, drilling hard materials, bats and dolphins use echo-location, and the smallest detail seen is about \(\lambda\).
  • Reflection and refraction of sound: the laws of reflection hold for sound (echo, whispering gallery, the sound board, the parabolic reflectors); refraction at the boundary between two media of different speeds follows \(\dfrac{\sin i}{\sin r}=\dfrac{v_1}{v_2}\); sound from a source above the water is mostly reflected, since the impedance of water is much greater than that of air. A sound wave is not polarised. Diffraction: sound bends around obstacles of the size of its wavelength, so it is heard around a corner but light is not.
  • Musical instruments: a stretched string (sitar, violin, guitar) \(f=\dfrac p{2L}\sqrt{\dfrac T\mu}\); wind instruments (flute — open pipe, clarinet — a closed pipe); the same note on two instruments differs in the quality (the overtone structure); a stretched membrane (a drum) gives inharmonic overtones.
  • Resonance and forced vibration: a tuning fork on a table (sound board) gives a louder note by forcing the table’s air column to vibrate; a vibrating system has a maximum amplitude when the driving frequency equals its natural frequency; the vibrations of an air column are resonantly excited by a tuning fork. A soldier’s march on a bridge may excite resonance.
31
Gravitation, fluids & waves

Electro Magnetic Waves

Maxwell's equations

\[\oint\vec E\cdot d\vec A=\frac Q{\epsilon_0}\qquad\text{(Gauss's law for electricity)}\]\[\oint\vec B\cdot d\vec A=0\qquad\text{(Gauss's law for magnetism)}\]\[\oint\vec E\cdot d\vec\ell=-\frac{d\Phi_B}{dt}\qquad\text{(Faraday's law)}\]\[\oint\vec B\cdot d\vec\ell=\mu_0i_c+\mu_0\epsilon_0\frac{d\Phi_E}{dt}\qquad\text{(Ampere-Maxwell law)}\]

Oscillating electric and magnetic fields

\[E=E_x(t)=E_0\sin(kz-\omega t)=E_0\sin\!\left[2\pi\left(\frac z\lambda-\nu t\right)\right]=E_0\sin\!\left[2\pi\left(\frac z\lambda-\frac tT\right)\right]\]\[\frac{E_0}{B_0}=c\qquad c=\frac1{\sqrt{\mu_0\epsilon_0}}\ \ (\text{speed of light in vacuum})\qquad v=\frac1{\sqrt{\mu\epsilon}}\ \ (\text{speed of light in a medium})\]

Momentum delivered by radiation

\[p=\frac Uc\]

If the energy transferred to a surface in time \(t\) is \(U\), the magnitude of the total momentum delivered to the surface (for complete absorption) is \(p\).

Electromagnetic spectrum

TypeWavelength rangeProductionDetection
Radio\(\gt0.1\) mRapid acceleration and deceleration of electrons in aerialsReceiver's aerials
Microwave0.1 m to 1 mmKlystron valve or magnetron valvePoint contact diodes
Infra-red1 mm to 700 nmVibration of atoms and moleculesThermopiles, bolometer, infrared photographic film
Light700 nm to 400 nmElectrons in atoms emit light when they move from one energy level to a lower energy levelThe eye, photocells, photographic film
Ultraviolet400 nm to 1 nmInner shell electrons in atoms moving from one energy level to a lower levelPhotocells, photographic film
X-rays1 nm to \(10^{-3}\) nmX-ray tubes or inner shell electronsPhotographic film, Geiger tubes, ionisation chamber
Gamma rays\(\lt10^{-3}\) nmRadioactive decay of the nucleusAs for X-rays

Properties, energy, intensity and the Poynting vector of an electromagnetic wave

\[c=\frac1{\sqrt{\mu_0\epsilon_0}}=3\times10^8\ \text{m s}^{-1},\qquad \frac{E_0}{B_0}=c,\qquad u=\epsilon_0E^2=\frac{B^2}{\mu_0},\qquad I=\tfrac12\epsilon_0cE_0^2=\frac{cB_0^2}{2\mu_0}=\frac{E_0B_0}{2\mu_0},\qquad \vec S=\frac{\vec E\times\vec B}{\mu_0}\]
  • An EM wave is transverse: \(\vec E\perp\vec B\perp\) the direction of propagation \(\hat k\), and \(\vec E\times\vec B\) points along \(\hat k\). The \(\vec E\) and \(\vec B\) oscillate in phase (both are maximum or zero at the same instant), with \(\dfrac EB=c\) at every instant. For a wave along \(+x\): \(E_y=E_0\sin(kx-\omega t)\), \(B_z=B_0\sin(kx-\omega t)\), \(\omega=ck\), \(c=f\lambda\). Wave equation: \(\dfrac{\partial^2E}{\partial x^2}=\mu_0\epsilon_0\dfrac{\partial^2E}{\partial t^2}\). The wave is produced by an accelerated charge (a charge at rest gives only \(\vec E\), a charge in uniform motion gives \(\vec E\) and a static \(\vec B\), an oscillating charge or an oscillating dipole radiates); the radiated power \(\propto a^2\) (Larmor: \(P=\dfrac{q^2a^2}{6\pi\epsilon_0c^3}\)). EM waves need no medium, and carry energy and momentum; they are not deflected by electric or magnetic fields (the wave itself is neutral).
  • In a medium (permittivity \(\epsilon=\epsilon_r\epsilon_0\), permeability \(\mu=\mu_r\mu_0\)): \(v=\dfrac1{\sqrt{\mu\epsilon}}=\dfrac c{\sqrt{\mu_r\epsilon_r}}\), refractive index \(n=\sqrt{\mu_r\epsilon_r}\) (\(\approx\sqrt{\epsilon_r}\) for non-magnetic materials), \(\dfrac EB=v\). The frequency is unchanged when the wave enters a medium; the wavelength becomes \(\dfrac\lambda n\).
  • Energy density: half is electric, half magnetic: \(u_E=\tfrac12\epsilon_0E^2=u_B=\dfrac{B^2}{2\mu_0}\); the average \(u_{\text{avg}}=\tfrac12\epsilon_0E_0^2=\dfrac{B_0^2}{2\mu_0}=\epsilon_0E_{rms}^2\). Intensity (average energy crossing unit area per second) \(I=u_{\text{avg}}\,c\); \(E_{rms}=\dfrac{E_0}{\sqrt2}\). The Poynting vector \(\vec S\) is the instantaneous energy flux; \(\langle S\rangle=I\).
  • Field of a point source of power \(P\) at a distance \(r\): \(I=\dfrac P{4\pi r^2}\), \(E_0=\sqrt{\dfrac{2I}{\epsilon_0c}}=\sqrt{\dfrac{\mu_0cP}{2\pi r^2}}=\dfrac{\sqrt{60P}}r\), \(B_0=\dfrac{E_0}c\). For sunlight at the Earth (solar constant \(1.36\) kW m\(^{-2}\)): \(E_0\approx10^3\) V m\(^{-1}\), \(B_0\approx3.4\ \mu\)T. If \(E_0=3\) V m\(^{-1}\) then \(B_0=10^{-8}\) T. The amplitude falls as \(\dfrac1r\), the intensity as \(\dfrac1{r^2}\).
  • Momentum and radiation pressure: energy \(U\) carries momentum \(\dfrac Uc\); on a perfectly absorbing surface the pressure is \(\dfrac Ic\) (force \(\dfrac{IA}c\)); on a perfectly reflecting surface \(\dfrac{2I}c\); for oblique incidence at angle \(\theta\) with the normal the pressure is \(\dfrac{I}{c}\cos^2\theta\) (absorbing) or \(\dfrac{2I\cos^2\theta}c\) (reflecting). A perfectly absorbing sphere of radius \(R\) feels \(F=\dfrac{I\pi R^2}c\) (the cross-section \(\pi R^2\)); a perfectly reflecting sphere (specular reflection) feels the same force \(\dfrac{I\pi R^2}c\). The radiation pressure of sunlight at the Earth is about \(4.5\times10^{-6}\) Pa; it gives the tail of comets and the solar sail. For a source of power \(P\) emitting in all directions on a plate at distance \(r\): \(F=\dfrac{P}{4\pi r^2}\dfrac{A}{c}\).
  • Displacement current (Maxwell): \(I_d=\epsilon_0\dfrac{d\Phi_E}{dt}\) — a changing electric field produces a magnetic field, a changing magnetic field produces an electric field; it extends Ampère’s law and predicts EM waves. Hertz (1887) produced and detected the waves by spark discharge; Bose produced \(\sim\)mm waves; Marconi used radio waves for communication.
  • Polarisation: the plane of polarisation of an EM wave is the plane containing \(\vec E\) and \(\hat k\) (the direction of \(\vec E\) defines the polarisation); light from ordinary sources is unpolarised. In a perfect conductor the fields inside vanish; EM waves are reflected by a conductor (shielding), and by the ionosphere (radio waves).

The electromagnetic spectrum: frequency, production, detection and uses

RegionWavelengthFrequencySourceUses
Radio waves\(\gt0.1\) mup to \(3\times10^9\) HzOscillating circuits, antennaRadio and TV broadcast (AM \(530\)–\(1710\) kHz, FM \(88\)–\(108\) MHz), mobile phones
Microwaves\(0.1\) m – \(1\) mm\(3\times10^9\)–\(3\times10^{11}\) HzKlystron, magnetron, Gunn diodeRadar, microwave ovens (\(2.45\) GHz, absorbed by water molecules), satellite and mobile links
Infrared\(1\) mm – \(700\) nm\(3\times10^{11}\)–\(4\times10^{14}\) HzHot bodies, molecular vibration and rotationHeat waves; physiotherapy, remote controls, night vision, greenhouse effect, IR photography
Visible\(700\)–\(400\) nm\(4\times10^{14}\)–\(8\times10^{14}\) HzElectronic transitions in atomsVision; the eye is most sensitive at \(555\) nm (yellow-green)
Ultraviolet\(400\)–\(1\) nm\(8\times10^{14}\)–\(3\times10^{17}\) HzVery hot bodies, the Sun, arcs, inner-shell transitionsSterilisation, vitamin D, fluorescence, LASIK; absorbed by the ozone layer
X-rays\(1\) nm – \(10^{-3}\) nm\(3\times10^{16}\)–\(3\times10^{20}\) HzX-ray tubes, deceleration of fast electronsMedical imaging and therapy, crystallography, flaw detection
Gamma rays\(\lt10^{-3}\) nm\(\gt3\times10^{19}\) HzRadioactive nuclei, nuclear reactionsCancer therapy, sterilisation, tracers
  • All move at \(c\) in vacuum; they differ in wavelength (frequency), so in production, detection and properties. A shorter wavelength means a more penetrating and more ionising wave; the regions overlap (e.g. long X-rays and short UV). The photon energy \(E=h\nu=\dfrac{1240}{\lambda(\text{nm})}\) eV: visible photons \(1.8\)–\(3.1\) eV.
  • Infrared and the greenhouse effect: the Earth’s surface radiates infrared (at \(\sim10\ \mu\)m) which is absorbed by \(\text{CO}_2\), \(\text{CH}_4\) and water vapour; the atmosphere re-radiates part of it back, warming the planet (the greenhouse effect; global warming). Infrared is detected by thermopiles, bolometers and special photographic films; water and CO\(_2\) absorb IR strongly. Ultraviolet is absorbed by ordinary glass but not by quartz; it makes the skin tan and can damage eyes and skin; the ozone layer absorbs the harmful UV-B and UV-C. Microwaves: in an oven, the water molecules (electric dipoles) rotate and absorb energy; radar uses the reflection of short pulses (the range \(d=\dfrac{ct}2\)).
  • Antennas: an efficient antenna has a length of the order of \(\dfrac\lambda4\) to \(\dfrac\lambda2\) (a very low-frequency signal would need a very long antenna, hence modulation). Detection of radio waves: tuned LC circuit \(f=\dfrac1{2\pi\sqrt{LC}}\) resonating with the station’s carrier frequency. Radio waves are produced by oscillating electric circuits; a dipole antenna radiates mainly perpendicular to its axis.
  • Ordering: in order of increasing wavelength: \(\gamma\)-rays \(\lt\) X-rays \(\lt\) UV \(\lt\) visible (violet to red) \(\lt\) infrared \(\lt\) microwaves \(\lt\) radio waves. In order of frequency, the reverse. The speed is the same in vacuum for all.
32
Measurement & electronics

Error and Measurement

1. Least count

InstrumentLeast count
mm scale1 mm
Vernier0.1 mm
Screw gauge0.1 mm
Stop watch0.1 s
Temperature thermometer0.1 °C

These are the example values given in the booklet; the actual least count depends on the instrument.

2. Significant figures

  • Non-zero digits are significant.
  • Zeros between two non-zero digits are significant.
  • A change of units cannot change the number of significant figures.
  • In a number less than one, all zeros after the decimal point and to the left of the first non-zero digit are insignificant.
  • Terminal or trailing zeros in a number without a decimal point are not significant.

3. Permissible error

The maximum permissible error in a measured quantity equals the least count of the measuring instrument. If the least count is not given, the maximum permissible error is the place value of the last digit.

\[f(x,y)=x+y\ \Rightarrow\ (\Delta f)_{max}=\max\text{ of }(\pm\Delta X\pm\Delta Y)\]\[f(x,y,z)=\text{constant}\cdot x^ay^bz^c\ \Rightarrow\ \left(\frac{\Delta f}f\right)_{max}=\max\text{ of }\left(\pm a\frac{\Delta x}x\pm b\frac{\Delta y}y\pm c\frac{\Delta z}z\right)\]

4. Errors in averaging

\[\text{Absolute error: }\Delta a_n=|a_{mean}-a_n|\]\[\text{Mean absolute error: }\Delta a_{mean}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^n|\Delta a_i|}n\]\[\text{Relative error}=\frac{\Delta a_{mean}}{a_{mean}}\qquad\text{Percentage error}=\frac{\Delta a_{mean}}{a_{mean}}\times100\]

5. Experiments

Reading of a screw gauge

\[\text{Thickness}=\text{main scale reading}+(\text{circular scale reading})\times\text{least count}\]\[\text{Least count of screw gauge}=\frac{\text{pitch}}{\text{number of circular scale divisions}}\]

Vernier callipers

\[\text{Thickness}=\text{main scale reading}+(\text{vernier scale reading})\times\text{least count}\]\[\text{Least count of vernier calliper}=1\ \text{MSD}-1\ \text{VSD}\]

Types of errors and the propagation rules

  • Systematic errors (always in the same direction): instrumental (zero error, faulty calibration, worn scale), imperfect method or approximation (neglecting air resistance, heat loss), personal bias (parallax in reading); they can be removed or corrected. Random errors: unpredictable variations (fluctuating conditions, the observer’s judgement) which can be reduced by taking many readings and averaging. Gross errors: carelessness, misreading, wrong recording. Accuracy (closeness to the true value) versus precision (closeness of repeated readings to each other, limited by the least count).
  • Absolute error \(\Delta a_i=|a_i-a_{\text{mean}}|\) (units of \(a\)); mean absolute error \(\Delta a_{\text{mean}}=\dfrac{\sum|\Delta a_i|}n\); relative (fractional) error \(\dfrac{\Delta a_{\text{mean}}}{a_{\text{mean}}}\); percentage error \(\dfrac{\Delta a_{\text{mean}}}{a_{\text{mean}}}\times100\). The final result is written as \(a=a_{\text{mean}}\pm\Delta a_{\text{mean}}\). The mean \(\bar a=\dfrac{a_1+\dots+a_n}n\) of \(n\) readings is more reliable: the random error of the mean falls as \(\dfrac1{\sqrt n}\). Standard deviation \(\sigma=\sqrt{\dfrac{\sum(a_i-\bar a)^2}{n-1}}\); the standard error of the mean \(\dfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt n}\).
  • Sum or difference \(z=a\pm b\): \(\Delta z=\Delta a+\Delta b\) (the absolute errors add, even for a difference). Product or quotient \(z=ab\) or \(\dfrac ab\): \(\dfrac{\Delta z}z=\dfrac{\Delta a}a+\dfrac{\Delta b}b\). Power \(z=a^pb^q c^r\): \(\dfrac{\Delta z}z=|p|\dfrac{\Delta a}a+|q|\dfrac{\Delta b}b+|r|\dfrac{\Delta c}c\) (the maximum possible error; the exponent multiplies the relative error, e.g. a square doubles it, a square root halves it). If the errors are independent and random the probable error combines in quadrature: \(\dfrac{\Delta z}z=\sqrt{p^2\left(\dfrac{\Delta a}a\right)^2+q^2\left(\dfrac{\Delta b}b\right)^2+\dots}\).
  • Functions \(z=f(x)\): \(\Delta z=\left|\dfrac{df}{dx}\right|\Delta x\) (e.g. \(z=\sin\theta\Rightarrow\Delta z=\cos\theta\,\Delta\theta\) with \(\Delta\theta\) in radian; \(z=\ln x\Rightarrow\Delta z=\dfrac{\Delta x}x\); \(z=e^x\Rightarrow\dfrac{\Delta z}z=\Delta x\)). For \(f(x,y)\): \(\Delta f=\left|\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x}\right|\Delta x+\left|\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial y}\right|\Delta y\). A constant factor (such as \(4\pi^2\) or a number) does not contribute any error; \(\pi\) and exact counts carry no error.
  • Standard examples: pendulum \(g=\dfrac{4\pi^2L}{T^2}\): \(\dfrac{\Delta g}g=\dfrac{\Delta L}L+2\dfrac{\Delta T}T\); with \(n\) oscillations timed, \(T=\dfrac tn\), \(\Delta T=\dfrac{\Delta t}n\) (the error in the time period is reduced by timing many oscillations). Density of a cylinder \(\rho=\dfrac{m}{\pi r^2\ell}\): \(\dfrac{\Delta\rho}\rho=\dfrac{\Delta m}m+2\dfrac{\Delta r}r+\dfrac{\Delta\ell}\ell\). Resistance \(R=\dfrac VI\): \(\dfrac{\Delta R}R=\dfrac{\Delta V}V+\dfrac{\Delta I}I\). Young’s modulus \(Y=\dfrac{4FL}{\pi d^2l}\): \(\dfrac{\Delta Y}Y=\dfrac{\Delta F}F+\dfrac{\Delta L}L+2\dfrac{\Delta d}d+\dfrac{\Delta l}l\). Lens \(f=\dfrac{uv}{u+v}\): \(\dfrac{\Delta f}f=\dfrac{\Delta u}u+\dfrac{\Delta v}v+\dfrac{\Delta u+\Delta v}{u+v}\) (using absolute values). The diameter measured by a screw gauge has an error of one least count, so the relative error in a thin quantity is large. Quantity measured as a small difference of two nearly equal numbers has a large relative error.
  • The least count of an instrument is the maximum permissible error of a single reading; the percentage error is smaller if the measured quantity is larger (use the instrument whose least count is small compared with the quantity). The error in a reading can also be estimated as half of the least count for an analogue pointer. A result with three significant figures implies a relative uncertainty of about \(1\) part in \(10^2\)–\(10^3\).

Significant figures, rounding and orders of magnitude

  • Rules: all non-zero digits are significant; zeros between non-zero digits are significant (\(2008\) has 4); leading zeros are not (\(0.0042\) has 2); trailing zeros after the decimal point are significant (\(4.700\) has 4, \(0.0500\) has 3); trailing zeros in an integer with no decimal point are ambiguous (written \(1.20\times10^4\) has 3, \(1.2\times10^4\) has 2). A change of units does not change the number of significant figures (\(2.50\) m \(=250\) cm \(=2.50\times10^2\) cm). Exact numbers (counts, defined conversions like \(100\) cm \(=1\) m) have infinitely many.
  • Arithmetic: in addition and subtraction the result has as many decimal places as the term with the fewest decimal places (\(12.11+18.0+1.013=31.1\)); in multiplication and division the result has as many significant figures as the factor with the fewest (\(4.7\times2.00=9.4\)). Do not round intermediate results; round at the end.
  • Rounding: if the digit to be dropped is less than \(5\), the preceding digit is unchanged; if more than \(5\), increase it by \(1\); if exactly \(5\) (nothing after), round to make the preceding digit even (\(2.45\to2.4\), \(2.35\to2.4\)); many school texts simply round a final \(5\) up.
  • Orders of magnitude: write the number as \(a\times10^b\) with \(1\le a\lt10\) (\(\lt3.16\) rounds down, \(\ge3.16\) rounds up to the next power for the order of magnitude); Earth’s radius \(6.4\times10^6\) m, the atomic size \(10^{-10}\) m, nuclear size \(10^{-15}\) m, the age of the universe \(\sim4\times10^{17}\) s.
  • Reporting results: the error is quoted to one (at most two) significant figures and the value is rounded to the same decimal place as the error: \(g=9.81\pm0.05\) m s\(^{-2}\), not \(9.8137\pm0.05\).

Zero errors and least counts: Vernier callipers, screw gauge, spherometer

  • Vernier callipers: \(N\) vernier divisions (VSD) coincide with \(N-1\) main-scale divisions (MSD); \(1\) VSD \(=\dfrac{N-1}N\) MSD; least count \(=1\) MSD \(-1\) VSD \(=\dfrac{\text{value of 1 MSD}}N\) (e.g. \(1\) mm\(/10=0.1\) mm \(=0.01\) cm). Reading \(=\) MSR \(+\) (coinciding VSD) \(\times\) LC. Zero error: when the jaws are closed, the zero of the vernier scale does not coincide with the zero of the main scale. Positive zero error: the vernier zero is to the right of the main zero; if the \(x\)-th vernier division coincides, the error is \(+x\times\text{LC}\) and the correct reading \(=\) observed \(-\) zero error. Negative zero error: the vernier zero is to the left; if the \(x\)-th division coincides, the error is \(-(N-x)\times\text{LC}\) and the correct reading \(=\) observed \(+(N-x)\text{LC}\). In general \(\text{correct}=\text{observed}-\text{zero error}\) with the sign included. A vernier measures an external diameter (outer jaws), an internal diameter (upper jaws), and a depth (the strip).
  • Screw gauge (micrometer): pitch \(=\) distance moved per full rotation of the circular scale; least count \(=\dfrac{\text{pitch}}{\text{number of circular divisions}}\) (e.g. \(\dfrac{0.5\ \text{mm}}{50}=0.01\) mm; \(\dfrac{1\ \text{mm}}{100}=0.01\) mm). Reading \(=\) pitch-scale reading \(+\) (circular-scale reading) \(\times\) LC. Zero error: with the studs just in contact read the circular scale against the reference line. If the reading is a small positive number \(x\) divisions (the zero has not yet reached the line), the zero error is \(+x\times\text{LC}\) (positive) and it is subtracted from every reading. If the reading is \(y\) divisions near the top of the scale (the zero has passed the line), the zero error is \(-(N-y)\times\text{LC}\) (negative) and its magnitude is added to every reading. Backlash error arises from the play in the screw threads (always turn the screw in the same direction). The ratchet is used to apply a uniform pressure.
  • Spherometer: three legs at the vertices of an equilateral triangle of side \(\ell\) and a central screw; pitch and LC as for a screw gauge; radius of curvature of a spherical surface \(R=\dfrac{\ell^2}{6h}+\dfrac h2\) where \(h\) is the height of the central screw above (or below) the plane of the legs; the side \(\ell\) is the mean distance between the legs (the spherometer’s pitch is typically \(1\) mm with \(100\) divisions, LC \(=0.01\) mm). The focal length of a thin lens made from this glass follows by the lens-maker’s formula.
  • Other instruments: metre scale (LC \(1\) mm; use a mid-scale to avoid the damaged end and read without parallax), stopwatch (LC \(0.1\) s or \(0.01\) s; the human reaction time \(\sim0.2\) s is the dominant error, so time many oscillations), thermometer (LC \(1^\circ\text{C}\) or \(0.1^\circ\text{C}\)), ammeter and voltmeter (LC = one small division; zero error corrected by adjusting the pointer), physical balance.

Graphs and the analysis of data

  • Plot the independent variable on the \(x\)-axis, choose a suitable scale (the line should cover most of the sheet), label the axes with units, mark the points clearly with small crosses or circles and draw the best-fit straight line so that the points are scattered equally on both sides. Slope \(m=\dfrac{y_2-y_1}{x_2-x_1}\) using two well-separated points on the line (not data points). The intercept gives the constant term; for a line passing through the origin, a non-zero intercept shows a systematic error.
  • Linearising: \(T^2=\dfrac{4\pi^2}gL\) (plot \(T^2\) against \(L\), slope \(\dfrac{4\pi^2}g\), \(g=\dfrac{4\pi^2}{\text{slope}}\)); \(V=IR\) (slope of \(V\)–\(I\) is \(R\)); \(\dfrac1v-\dfrac1u=\dfrac1f\) (plot \(\dfrac1v\) against \(\dfrac1u\), intercept \(\dfrac1f\) on the \(\dfrac1v\)-axis); \(\ln N=\ln N_0-\lambda t\) (a semi-log plot for decay, the slope \(-\lambda\)); \(\ln\) of both sides for a power law \(y=kx^n\) (slope \(n\)); stopping potential against frequency (slope \(\dfrac he\)); \(y=mx+c\) forms in general.
  • Least-squares fit: \(m=\dfrac{n\sum xy-\sum x\sum y}{n\sum x^2-(\sum x)^2}\), \(c=\dfrac{\sum y-m\sum x}n\). The uncertainty in the slope is estimated from the maximum and minimum slope lines through the error bars. The error of a result obtained from a graph is smaller than from a single reading because many readings contribute.
  • Dimensional consistency (units of the slope and intercept) can be used to check a relation; a plot in which the quantity expected to be constant does not vary with the changing variable confirms the law (a straight horizontal line).
33
Measurement & electronics

Experimental Skills

Mechanics experiments: pendulum, metre scale, Young’s modulus, surface tension, viscosity

  • Simple pendulum: \(T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac Lg}\), where \(L=\) (length of the string from the point of suspension) \(+\) (radius of the bob) is the distance to the centre of the bob. Use a heavy, small bob, an inextensible thread, a rigid clamp (split cork), an amplitude of less than about \(10^\circ\), and time \(20\)–\(50\) oscillations (the error in \(T\) is \(\dfrac{\Delta t}n\)) starting from the mean position as the pendulum passes it. The \(T^2\)–\(L\) graph is a straight line through the origin of slope \(\dfrac{4\pi^2}g\), so \(g=\dfrac{4\pi^2}{\text{slope}}\); an intercept on the \(L\)-axis indicates an error in the effective length (e.g. the bob’s radius omitted). A second’s pendulum (\(T=2\) s) has \(L\approx99.4\) cm. The \(T\)–\(L\) graph is a parabola; the \(L\)–\(T^2\) graph is straight. Damping: the amplitude decays as \(A=A_0e^{-bt/2m}\), so \(\ln A\) against \(t\) is a straight line.
  • Metre scale and the principle of moments: balance the scale on a knife edge; its centre of gravity is at the \(50\) cm mark. With a known mass \(m\) hung at a distance \(d_1\) from the knife edge and the unknown mass \(M\) at \(d_2\) on the other side for balance: \(Md_2=md_1\) (the weight of the scale does not appear when the knife edge is at its centre of gravity). If the knife edge is not at the centre of gravity of the scale of mass \(m_s\), include \(m_s\,g\) acting at \(50\) cm. Use the scale for repeated trials with different positions and average the result.
  • Young’s modulus by Searle’s apparatus: \(Y=\dfrac{Mg\,L}{\pi r^2\,l}\), where \(l\) is the elongation for a load \(M\) on a wire of length \(L\) and radius \(r\) (the radius is measured with the screw gauge at several places and in two perpendicular directions). Plot the load against the extension: the slope \(\dfrac{\Delta M}{\Delta l}\) gives \(Y=\dfrac{gL}{\pi r^2}\left(\dfrac{\Delta M}{\Delta l}\right)\). Apply the load in equal steps, repeat during unloading (to check the elastic behaviour; stay within the elastic limit); a dead-weight on the wire first removes kinks; a reference wire cancels the effect of temperature and the sagging of the frame. The main source of error is the radius (\(\dfrac{\Delta Y}Y\) contains \(2\dfrac{\Delta r}r\)).
  • Surface tension of water by capillary rise: \(T=\dfrac{r\rho g h}{2\cos\theta}\approx\dfrac{r\rho gh}2\) (\(\theta\approx0\) for clean glass and water); the bottom of the meniscus is taken as the liquid level, and for greater accuracy \(h\) is replaced by \(h+\dfrac r3\) (the liquid in the meniscus above the lowest point). The radius \(r\) is measured with a travelling microscope, and the height \(h\) as the difference between the readings at the meniscus and the free surface (pointer). The glass tube must be clean and vertical. With a detergent \(T\) decreases (\(h\) decreases); \(T\) decreases with temperature; \(rh\) is constant (Jurin’s law).
  • Viscosity by terminal velocity (Stokes’ method): a steel ball of radius \(r\) and density \(\rho\) falls through a tall jar of liquid (density \(\sigma\)); measure the time \(t\) to cross a fixed distance \(d\) well below the surface (after the terminal velocity is reached): \(v=\dfrac dt\), \(\eta=\dfrac{2r^2(\rho-\sigma)g}{9v}\). Use a small ball, a wide jar (the wall correction \(v_T\approx v(1+2.4\dfrac rR)\) with \(R\) the radius of the jar), a clean ball and a transparent, very viscous liquid (glycerine, castor oil); the ball is released along the axis, and the temperature is noted because \(\eta\) depends on it strongly. The dominant error is in \(r\) (\(\dfrac{\Delta\eta}\eta\) contains \(2\dfrac{\Delta r}r\)).

Heat and sound experiments: speed of sound, specific heat, cooling curve

  • Speed of sound by a resonance tube: a vibrating tuning fork (frequency \(f\)) held over the mouth of a tube whose water level is adjusted. The first resonance (loudest) at the air-column length \(L_1\) with \(L_1+e=\dfrac\lambda4\), the second at \(L_2\) with \(L_2+e=\dfrac{3\lambda}4\); \(\lambda=2(L_2-L_1)\), \(v=f\lambda=2f(L_2-L_1)\), end correction \(e=\dfrac{L_2-3L_1}2\) (\(\approx0.6\) times the radius of the tube). Strike the fork on a rubber pad (not on a hard surface), lower the water slowly and find each resonance by the maximum loudness; repeat with several forks and with rising and falling water levels. To refer \(v\) to \(0^\circ\text{C}\) use \(v_0=v_t\sqrt{\dfrac{273}{273+t}}\) (or \(v_t=v_0+0.61t\)). The temperature of the air in the tube should be noted.
  • Specific heat of a solid by the method of mixtures: a solid of mass \(m_s\) heated in a steam heater to \(T_2\) (about \(100^\circ\text{C}\)) is quickly dropped into water (mass \(m_w\), initial temperature \(T_1\)) in a calorimeter with stirrer of water equivalent \(w\) (\(=m_cc_c/c_w\)); the final temperature \(T\): \(m_sc_s(T_2-T)=(m_w+w)c_w(T-T_1)\), so \(c_s=\dfrac{(m_w+w)c_w(T-T_1)}{m_s(T_2-T)}\). Precautions: transfer the solid quickly, lag the calorimeter (to reduce losses), stir gently, use a polished calorimeter (low radiation) and choose \(T_1\) as much below the room temperature as the final \(T\) is above it (the cooling correction cancels), keep the thermometer bulb in the middle of the water.
  • Specific heat of a liquid: either by the method of mixtures with a standard solid (copper, aluminium) of known \(c_s\) dropped into the liquid (\(m_sc_s(T_2-T)=(m_l c_l+w\,c_w)(T-T_1)\)), or by electrical heating (a heating coil in the liquid): \(VIt=(m_lc_l+m_cc_c)\Delta T\), with \(V\), \(I\) from a voltmeter and ammeter.
  • Newton’s cooling curve: a calorimeter of hot water (about \(80^\circ\text{C}\)) cools in the surroundings at \(T_0\); record \(T\) every minute. Newton’s law: \(\dfrac{dT}{dt}=-k(T-T_0)\), \(T-T_0=(T_i-T_0)e^{-kt}\) for \(T-T_0\) small (about \(30^\circ\) at most). The \(T\)–\(t\) curve is an exponential decay; \(\ln(T-T_0)\) against \(t\) is a straight line of slope \(-k\); the rate of cooling (slope of the \(T\)–\(t\) curve) is proportional to \(T-T_0\) (a straight line through the origin when the rate is plotted against \(T-T_0\)). Precautions: cover the calorimeter, keep the same surface and surroundings, stir gently; the cooling curve of different liquids of the same mass compares their specific heats (the liquid with the smaller specific heat cools faster).

Electrical experiments: metre bridge, Ohm’s law, potentiometer, galvanometer

  • Metre bridge (Wheatstone bridge with a \(100\) cm wire): at balance (null point at \(l\) cm from the end with the unknown resistance \(R\) in the left gap and the standard resistance \(S\) in the right gap) \(\dfrac RS=\dfrac l{100-l}\), so \(R=\dfrac{Sl}{100-l}\). Take readings with \(S\) chosen so that \(l\) is near \(50\) cm (the greatest sensitivity and least error), and then interchange \(R\) and \(S\) to remove the end error. The jockey must not be dragged or pressed hard (it changes the wire’s resistance by heating and the contact), and the current should be passed for a short time. Specific resistance of the wire: \(\rho=\dfrac{RA}L=\dfrac{R\pi d^2}{4L}\) with \(d\) from the screw gauge and \(L\) the length of the test wire. The relative error \(\dfrac{\Delta R}R=\dfrac{\Delta l}{l(100-l)}\times100\) is least at \(l=50\) cm.
  • Ohm’s law: for a metallic wire at constant temperature, \(V=IR\): plot \(V\) against \(I\) (a straight line through the origin whose slope is \(R\)). The ammeter is connected in series and the voltmeter in parallel with the resistor. Instrument corrections: with the voltmeter across the resistor only, the measured \(\dfrac VI=\dfrac{RR_V}{R+R_V}\) (smaller than \(R\)); with the voltmeter across the resistor and the ammeter, \(\dfrac VI=R+R_A\) (greater than \(R\)). The first arrangement is good for a small \(R\) (compared with \(R_V\)), the second for a large \(R\) (compared with \(R_A\)). The current should be passed briefly, otherwise the heating changes \(R\).
  • Potentiometer (a long uniform wire carrying a steady current from a driver cell, potential gradient \(k=\dfrac{V}{L}\) (V per cm)): the emf of a cell \(E=kl\) at the null point of length \(l\); comparison of two emfs: \(\dfrac{E_1}{E_2}=\dfrac{l_1}{l_2}\); internal resistance of a cell: \(l_1\) is the null length on open circuit and \(l_2\) with a known resistance \(R\) across the cell: \(r=R\,\dfrac{l_1-l_2}{l_2}\). The driver cell’s emf must be greater than that of the cell under test; the positive terminals of both cells must be connected to the same end of the wire; a longer wire (or a smaller gradient, with a rheostat) improves the sensitivity; at balance no current is drawn from the cell, so the potentiometer acts as an ideal voltmeter.
  • Galvanometer: resistance by half deflection: a large resistance \(R\) is put in series to give a deflection \(\theta\); a shunt \(S\) across the galvanometer is adjusted until the deflection is \(\dfrac\theta2\); then \(G=\dfrac{RS}{R-S}\) (\(\approx S\) if \(R\gg S\)). Figure of merit (current per division) \(k=\dfrac{E}{(R+G)\,\theta}\) (A per division), where \(\theta\) is the deflection in divisions with the resistance \(R\) in series with a cell of emf \(E\). The current sensitivity is \(\dfrac1k\); the full-scale current \(I_g=k\,n\) for \(n\) divisions.
  • Conversion of the galvanometer: to an ammeter of range \(I\) by a shunt \(S=\dfrac{I_gG}{I-I_g}\) in parallel (the ammeter resistance \(\dfrac{GS}{G+S}\), small); to a voltmeter of range \(V\) by a high resistance \(R_s=\dfrac V{I_g}-G\) in series (the voltmeter resistance \(G+R_s\), large). The converted meter is checked against a standard ammeter or voltmeter in the same circuit.

Optics experiments: mirrors, lenses, prism, travelling microscope

  • Parallax method: the image is located by moving the eye side to side and adjusting the pin until it shows no parallax with the image (the tip of the pin appears to coincide with the tip of the image whatever the eye position). The distances are measured from the pole or the optical centre; correct for the end error of the optical bench (the index correction).
  • Concave mirror: (a) rough \(f\) from a distant object (a window) by getting a sharp image on a screen; (b) \(u\)–\(v\) method: \(\dfrac1f=\dfrac1u+\dfrac1v\) (magnitudes of the distances) and the plot of \(\dfrac1u\) against \(\dfrac1v\) has intercepts \(\dfrac1f\) on both axes; the \(u\)–\(v\) curve cuts \(u=v\) at \(2f\); (c) with the needle at the centre of curvature (the image and the object coincide, \(u=v=R\)) \(f=\dfrac R2\).
  • Convex mirror (virtual image, found with an auxiliary convex lens): put a convex lens between the object needle and the mirror and adjust the positions until a real image of the needle formed by the lens-mirror system coincides with the needle (the rays retrace their path, i.e. fall normally on the mirror). Measure the image distance \(v\) of the lens alone (mirror removed) and the lens–mirror separation \(x\): \(R=v-x\), \(f=\dfrac{v-x}2\).
  • Convex lens: (a) from a distant object, \(f\) is roughly the lens–screen distance; (b) \(u\)–\(v\) method: \(\dfrac1f=\dfrac1v-\dfrac1u\) with \(u\) negative (or \(\dfrac1f=\dfrac1u+\dfrac1v\) with magnitudes); take \(u\gt f\), preferably between \(1.5f\) and \(2.5f\); plot \(\dfrac1v\) against \(\dfrac1u\) (a straight line with intercepts \(\dfrac1f\)) or \(v\) against \(u\) (the point on \(v=u\) gives \(2f\)); (c) displacement method: \(f=\dfrac{D^2-d^2}{4D}\) with the object–screen distance \(D\gt4f\) and the separation \(d\) between the two lens positions that give sharp images.
  • Concave lens (virtual image): combine with a convex lens of smaller focal length \(f_1\) so that the combination is convergent with focal length \(F\); then \(\dfrac1{f_2}=\dfrac1F-\dfrac1{f_1}\), i.e. \(f_2=\dfrac{Ff_1}{f_1-F}\) (negative).
  • Prism (angle of deviation against angle of incidence): the object pins \(P_1P_2\) and the emergent pins \(P_3P_4\) give \(i\) and \(\delta=i+e-A\); the \(\delta\)–\(i\) curve is a U-shape with a minimum \(\delta_{\min}\) at \(i=e\); \(\mu=\dfrac{\sin\frac{A+\delta_{\min}}2}{\sin\frac A2}\). The angle of the prism \(A\) is found by reflecting a parallel beam from its two faces (\(A=\dfrac12\) of the angle between the two reflected beams).
  • Refractive index of a glass slab by a travelling microscope (least count \(0.01\) mm): focus on a cross on paper (reading \(R_1\)); place the slab over it and refocus (the cross appears raised, \(R_2\)); sprinkle lycopodium powder on the top surface of the slab and focus on it (\(R_3\)): \(\mu=\dfrac{\text{real thickness}}{\text{apparent thickness}}=\dfrac{R_3-R_1}{R_3-R_2}\). Move the microscope in the same direction to remove the backlash error; the microscope’s parallax must be removed by focusing.

Electronics experiments: diode, Zener, transistor, multimeter and component identification

  • p–n junction diode: forward bias (p to the positive terminal): negligible current until the threshold (knee) voltage (\(\approx0.7\) V for Si, \(0.3\) V for Ge), then the current rises rapidly (the exponential \(I=I_0\left(e^{eV/kT}-1\right)\)); use a milliammeter and a protective series resistance; the dynamic resistance \(r_d=\dfrac{\Delta V}{\Delta I}\) is small. Reverse bias: a very small saturation current (a microammeter; \(\mu\)A for Ge, nA for Si) almost independent of the voltage until breakdown. The \(I\)–\(V\) curve is non-linear and asymmetric (unidirectional).
  • Zener diode (reverse biased with a series resistor): the current is almost zero until the reverse voltage reaches the breakdown (Zener) voltage \(V_z\); then the current rises steeply while the voltage across the diode stays nearly constant at \(V_z\) (the basis of voltage regulation). Plot \(I\) against \(V_{\text{reverse}}\) and read \(V_z\) at the knee. The current must be limited below the power rating \(P=V_zI_{z,\max}\).
  • Transistor (CE configuration) characteristics: input characteristic \(I_B\) against \(V_{BE}\) at a constant \(V_{CE}\) (like a forward diode curve): input resistance \(r_i=\left(\dfrac{\Delta V_{BE}}{\Delta I_B}\right)_{V_{CE}}\); output characteristic \(I_C\) against \(V_{CE}\) at constant \(I_B\) (flat in the active region): \(r_o=\left(\dfrac{\Delta V_{CE}}{\Delta I_C}\right)_{I_B}\); current gain \(\beta_{ac}=\left(\dfrac{\Delta I_C}{\Delta I_B}\right)_{V_{CE}}\); voltage gain \(A_V=\beta_{ac}\dfrac{R_L}{r_i}\) (the voltage gain can also be found from the output voltage change \(\Delta I_CR_L\) divided by \(\Delta V_{BE}\)); \(A_V\) is large and negative (phase reversal \(180^\circ\)). Regions: cut-off (\(I_B=0\)), active (the amplifier) and saturation (\(V_{CE}\) small, a closed switch).
  • Multimeter: as an ohmmeter on the diode: a low resistance when forward biased and a very high one when reversed (an open or shorted diode shows high or low in both directions); in the diode-test mode of a digital meter the forward drop (\(0.5\)–\(0.7\) V for Si) is shown with the red lead on the anode. An LED conducts and lights up only in forward bias (the longer lead is the anode; the flat side is the cathode). Transistor: the base is the terminal that shows conduction to both others with one polarity of the probe; if the base is connected to the (+) probe (red in a digital meter in diode mode) and both junctions conduct, the transistor is npn; if the base is connected to the (\(-\)) probe it is pnp; the emitter–base junction usually has a slightly higher forward reading than the collector–base junction (it identifies the emitter); no conduction between the emitter and the collector with the base open. A transistor is in working order if the base–emitter and base–collector junctions each behave as a diode (low resistance one way, very high the other) and there is no conduction between the emitter and the collector in either direction.
  • Identification of components: a resistor (colour code: black \(0\), brown \(1\), red \(2\), orange \(3\), yellow \(4\), green \(5\), blue \(6\), violet \(7\), grey \(8\), white \(9\); resistance \(=\left(10a+b\right)\times10^c\) \(\Omega\) with tolerance gold \(\pm5\%\), silver \(\pm10\%\), none \(\pm20\%\); the multimeter shows the stable resistance); a capacitor (the ohmmeter pointer jumps to a low resistance and then slowly returns to a high value as it charges; the electrolytic type has polarity marks); a diode (a band marks the cathode; low in one direction, high in the other); an LED (glows); a transistor (three leads, a metal or plastic case, the pin identification as above); an IC (a multi-pin chip with a notch or dot marking pin \(1\), part number printed on it).
34
Measurement & electronics

Principle of Communication

Transmission from a tower of height \(h\)

For transmitting tower height \(h_T\), receiving tower height \(h_R\) and Earth's radius \(R\):

\[\text{Distance to the horizon }d_T=\sqrt{2Rh_T}\qquad d_M=\sqrt{2Rh_T}+\sqrt{2Rh_R}\]

Amplitude modulation

The modulated signal \(c_m(t)\) can be written as

\[c_m(t)=A_c\sin\omega_ct+\frac{\mu A_c}2\cos(\omega_c-\omega_m)t-\frac{\mu A_c}2\cos(\omega_c+\omega_m)t\]

Modulation index:

\[m_a=\frac{\text{change in amplitude of carrier wave}}{\text{amplitude of original carrier wave}}=\frac{kA_m}{A_c}\]

\(k\) is a factor that determines the maximum change in amplitude for a given amplitude \(E_m\) of the modulating signal. If \(k=1\):

\[m_a=\frac{A_m}{A_c}=\frac{A_{max}-A_{min}}{A_{max}+A_{min}}\]
  • If a carrier is modulated by several sine waves, the total modulation index is \(m_t=\sqrt{m_1^2+m_2^2+m_3^2+\cdots}\)
  • Side band frequencies: \((f_c+f_m)\) is the upper side band (USB) frequency; \((f_c-f_m)\) is the lower side band (LSB) frequency.
  • Bandwidth \(=(f_c+f_m)-(f_c-f_m)=2f_m\)

Power in AM waves

\[P=\frac{V_{rms}^2}R\]\[\text{(i) Carrier power: }P_c=\frac{\left(\frac{A_c}{\sqrt2}\right)^2}R=\frac{A_c^2}{2R}\]\[\text{(ii) Total power of side bands: }P_{sb}=\frac{\left(\frac{m_aA_c}{2\sqrt2}\right)^2}R+\frac{\left(\frac{m_aA_c}{2\sqrt2}\right)^2}R=\frac{m_a^2A_c^2}{4R}\]\[\text{(iii) Total power of the AM wave: }P_{Total}=P_c+P_{sb}=\frac{A_c^2}{2R}\left(1+\frac{m_a^2}2\right)\]\[\text{(iv) }\frac{P_t}{P_c}=1+\frac{m_a^2}2\qquad\frac{P_{sb}}{P_t}=\frac{m_a^2/2}{1+\frac{m_a^2}2}\]

(v) Maximum power in AM (without distortion) occurs when \(m_a=1\), i.e. \(P_t=1.5P_c=3P_{sb}\).

(vi) If \(I_c\) is the unmodulated current and \(I_t\) the total (modulated) current:

\[\frac{P_t}{P_c}=\frac{I_t^2}{I_c^2}\ \Rightarrow\ \frac{I_t}{I_c}=\sqrt{1+\frac{m_a^2}2}\]

Frequency modulation

  • Frequency deviation \(\delta=(f_{max}-f_c)=f_c-f_{min}=k_f\dfrac{E_m}{2\pi}\)
  • Carrier swing \(CS=2\times\Delta f\)
  • Frequency modulation index
\[m_f=\frac\delta{f_m}=\frac{f_{max}-f_c}{f_m}=\frac{f_c-f_{min}}{f_m}=\frac{k_fE_m}{2\pi f_m}\]
  • Frequency spectrum: an FM signal consists of an infinite number of side bands, at frequencies \((f_c\pm f_m),(f_c\pm2f_m),(f_c\pm3f_m),\ldots\)
  • Deviation ratio \(=\dfrac{(\Delta f)_{max}}{(f_m)_{max}}\)
  • Percent modulation \(m=\dfrac{(\Delta f)_{actual}}{(\Delta f)_{max}}\times100\%\)

Communication system, bandwidth and modes of propagation

  • Elements: transmitter (the message signal from a transducer, modulator, power amplifier, transmitting antenna) \(\to\) channel (wire, coaxial cable, optical fibre or free space; noise and attenuation) \(\to\) receiver (receiving antenna, amplifier, demodulator or detector, final amplifier, transducer). Signal: analogue (continuous in time and amplitude, e.g. speech) or digital (discrete, \(0\) and \(1\)). Noise is unwanted random disturbance; attenuation is the loss of strength with distance; repeaters (amplifier plus transmitter) restore the signal; range is the largest distance giving a usable signal; baseband is the original band of frequencies of the message.
  • Bandwidth of signals: speech \(300\)–\(3100\) Hz (\(\approx2.8\) kHz); music up to \(20\) kHz; video (TV) \(\approx4.2\) MHz; a TV signal with the sound \(6\) MHz; digital data needs a larger band. Bandwidth of channels: twisted pair \(\sim1\) MHz; coaxial cable \(\sim750\) MHz; free space (radio) \(1\) kHz–\(100\) GHz; optical fibre \(10^{12}\)–\(10^{15}\) Hz (the highest).
  • Need for modulation: (i) antenna size: an efficient antenna has a length of the order of \(\dfrac\lambda4\); for \(f=10\) kHz, \(\lambda=30\) km (an impossible antenna) but for \(f=1\) MHz \(\lambda=300\) m and a \(75\) m antenna is possible; (ii) the effective power radiated \(\propto\left(\dfrac\ell\lambda\right)^2\) — a high frequency radiates more; (iii) mixing of the signals of different transmitters, since all audio signals are in the same low band; modulation shifts each message to a different high-frequency carrier so that many stations can share the medium (multiplexing).
  • Ground (surface) wave: travels along the Earth’s surface, for frequencies up to about \(2\) MHz (AM broadcast); its attenuation increases rapidly with frequency (absorption by the ground), range tens of km, limited by the power and the frequency. Sky wave: \(3\)–\(30\) MHz (short wave), reflected back by the ionosphere (layers D at \(60\)–\(90\) km, E at \(\approx100\) km, \(F_1,F_2\) at \(150\)–\(400\) km; D absorbs, mostly the F layer reflects). The critical frequency (the highest frequency reflected for vertical incidence) \(f_c=9\sqrt{N_{\max}}\) (\(N_{\max}\) electrons per m\(^3\) in the layer, \(f_c\) in Hz); for oblique incidence at an angle \(i\) the maximum usable frequency \(\text{MUF}=\dfrac{f_c}{\cos i}=f_c\sec i\); the skip distance is the shortest ground distance from the transmitter at which the sky wave returns to the Earth (the zone between the ground-wave range and the skip distance is a silent zone). Frequencies above the \(f_c\) pass through the layer.
  • Space wave (line of sight, LOS): frequencies above about \(40\) MHz (VHF, UHF, microwave: TV, FM, radar, satellites) travel in a straight line; the range is limited by the curvature of the Earth: the distance to the horizon of an antenna of height \(h\) is \(d=\sqrt{2Rh}\) (\(R=6.4\times10^6\) m); \(d_{\max}=\sqrt{2Rh_T}+\sqrt{2Rh_R}\) between two towers (e.g. \(h=100\) m gives \(d\approx36\) km). The area covered by a transmitter is \(A=\pi d^2=2\pi Rh\); population covered \(=A\times\) population density. A tall antenna increases the range (the broadcast tower on a hill).
  • Satellite communication: a geostationary satellite (height \(36\,000\) km) receives the signal from an earth station (uplink, \(\approx6\) GHz), amplifies it, shifts the frequency and retransmits (downlink, \(\approx4\) GHz) — a transponder. Signals above \(30\) MHz pass through the ionosphere and so are used with satellites. Uses: television broadcast, telephony, weather, the global positioning system (GPS), remote sensing.
  • Radar: a short pulse of microwaves reflected from a target: the distance \(d=\dfrac{ct}2\); the maximum range is set by the pulse repetition time (\(d_{\max}=\dfrac{c\,T_{PRT}}2\)); a Doppler radar measures the speed from \(\Delta f=\dfrac{2v}{c}f\).

Modulation: AM, FM, pulse modulation, demodulation

  • Amplitude modulation: the carrier \(c(t)=A_c\sin\omega_ct\) and the message \(m(t)=A_m\sin\omega_mt\) give \(c_m(t)=\left(A_c+A_m\sin\omega_mt\right)\sin\omega_ct\). Modulation index \(m_a=\dfrac{A_m}{A_c}=\dfrac{A_{\max}-A_{\min}}{A_{\max}+A_{\min}}\) (\(m_a\le1\); for \(m_a\gt1\) there is over-modulation and distortion). Frequencies present: \(f_c\), \(f_c+f_m\) (USB), \(f_c-f_m\) (LSB); bandwidth \(2f_m\); the useful message is carried by the side bands, the carrier carries no information. \(A_{\max}=A_c(1+m_a)\), \(A_{\min}=A_c(1-m_a)\).
  • Power and efficiency: \(P_t=P_c\left(1+\dfrac{m_a^2}2\right)\), the side-band power \(P_{sb}=\dfrac{m_a^2}2P_c\); efficiency \(\eta=\dfrac{P_{sb}}{P_t}=\dfrac{m_a^2}{2+m_a^2}\) (\(33\%\) at most, for \(m_a=1\)); the current \(I_t=I_c\sqrt{1+\dfrac{m_a^2}2}\). In single-sideband (SSB) transmission only one side band is sent, reducing the bandwidth and power.
  • Demodulation (detection): a rectifier (diode) removes one half of the modulated wave and an RC low-pass filter (the envelope detector) removes the carrier and recovers the message envelope; the time constant must satisfy \(\dfrac1{f_c}\ll RC\ll\dfrac1{f_m}\). Superheterodyne receiver: the carrier is mixed with a local oscillator to a fixed intermediate frequency (IF \(=455\) kHz for AM), then amplified and detected: \(f_{IF}=|f_{LO}-f_c|\).
  • Frequency modulation: the carrier’s frequency varies with the message amplitude: \(f=f_c+k_fA_m\cos\omega_mt\); the frequency deviation \(\delta\) (maximum change); \(m_f=\dfrac\delta{f_m}\); the carrier swing \(2\delta\); the bandwidth \(\approx2(\delta+f_m)\) (Carson’s rule; for a small \(m_f\), \(\approx2f_m\)). FM broadcast: \(88\)–\(108\) MHz, \(\delta=75\) kHz, audio up to \(15\) kHz, a \(200\) kHz channel. Advantages of FM over AM: high noise immunity (the information is in the frequency, and the amplitude can be limited), a constant amplitude (efficient class-C amplifiers), a good fidelity; disadvantages: a much larger bandwidth, a more complex circuit, line-of-sight range. TV: the video is amplitude modulated (vestigial sideband) and the sound is frequency modulated.
  • Pulse modulation: PAM (amplitude), PWM (width), PPM (position) and PCM (pulse code, digital). Sampling theorem: a band-limited signal of the highest frequency \(f_{\max}\) is fully recovered if sampled at \(f_s\ge2f_{\max}\) (the Nyquist rate; e.g. \(44.1\) kHz for audio); in PCM the samples are quantised to \(2^n\) levels and coded in \(n\) bits, the bit rate \(=f_sn\). Digital signals are more immune to noise and can be regenerated, stored and encrypted.
  • Noise: thermal (resistors), shot (transistors), atmospheric, man-made, cosmic; the signal-to-noise ratio \(\dfrac SN=10\log_{10}\dfrac{P_S}{P_N}\) dB. Optical fibre communication: light pulses guided by total internal reflection in a glass fibre with a core and a lower-index cladding; very high bandwidth, low loss (a fraction of a dB per km, \(\alpha=\dfrac{10}{L}\log_{10}\dfrac{P_{in}}{P_{out}}\) dB km\(^{-1}\)), immune to electromagnetic interference, secure, light and cheap.
  • Other systems: telephony (the telephone transmits speech in the \(300\)–\(3400\) Hz band); mobile phones use the cellular concept (hexagonal cells with base stations, the frequency reuse and hand-off between cells, \(800\)–\(2600\) MHz, now \(5\)G bands); the Internet (a network of networks with packet switching); fax; e-mail.
35
Measurement & electronics

Semiconductor

Conductivity and resistivity

\(\rho\) (\(\Omega\) m)\(\sigma\) (\(\Omega^{-1}\)m\(^{-1}\))
Metals\(10^{-2}\)–\(10^{-6}\)\(10^{2}\)–\(10^{8}\)
Semiconductors\(10^{-5}\)–\(10^{6}\)\(10^{5}\)–\(10^{-6}\)
Insulators\(10^{11}\)–\(10^{19}\)\(10^{-11}\)–\(10^{-19}\)

Charge concentration and current

  • Intrinsic semiconductors: \(n_h=n_e\)
  • p-type: \(n_h\gg n_e\), with \(n_h\approx N_a\). n-type: \(n_e\gg n_h\), with \(n_e\approx N_d\)
  • \(i=i_e+i_h\)
  • \(n_en_h=n_i^2\)
  • Number of electrons reaching the conduction band from the valence band: \(n=AT^{3/2}e^{-E_g/2kT}\), where \(A\) is a positive constant
  • \(\sigma=e\,(n_e\mu_e+n_h\mu_h)\)
  • Dynamic resistance of a p-n junction in forward bias \(=\dfrac{\Delta V}{\Delta I}\)

Transistor: common base (CB) amplifier

  • (i) ac current gain: \(\alpha_{ac}=\dfrac{\text{small change in collector current }(\Delta i_c)}{\text{small change in emitter current }(\Delta i_e)}\)
  • (ii) dc current gain: \(\alpha_{dc}=\dfrac{\text{collector current }(i_c)}{\text{emitter current }(i_e)}\), with a value between 0.95 and 0.99
  • (iii) Voltage gain: \(A_V=\dfrac{\Delta V_0}{\Delta V_i}=\alpha_{ac}\times\text{resistance gain}\)
  • (iv) Power gain: \(\dfrac{\Delta P_0}{\Delta P_i}=\alpha_{ac}^2\times\text{resistance gain}\)
  • (v) Phase difference (between output and input): same phase
  • (vi) Application: high frequency

Transistor: common emitter (CE) amplifier

  • (i) ac current gain: \(\beta_{ac}=\left(\dfrac{\Delta i_c}{\Delta i_b}\right)_{V_{CE}=\text{constant}}\)
  • (ii) dc current gain: \(\beta_{dc}=\dfrac{i_c}{i_b}\)
  • (iii) Voltage gain: \(A_V=\dfrac{\Delta V_0}{\Delta V_i}=\beta_{ac}\times\text{resistance gain}\)
  • (iv) Power gain: \(\dfrac{\Delta P_0}{\Delta P_i}=\beta_{ac}^2\times\text{resistance gain}\)
  • (v) Transconductance \((g_m)\): the ratio of the change in collector current to the change in emitter-base voltage: \(g_m=\dfrac{\Delta i_c}{\Delta V_{EB}}\). Also \(g_m=\dfrac{A_V}{R_L}\), where \(R_L\) is the load resistance.

Relation between \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\):

\[\beta=\frac\alpha{1-\alpha}\quad\text{or}\quad\alpha=\frac\beta{1+\beta}\]

Energy bands, intrinsic and extrinsic semiconductors

  • Band theory: in a solid the atomic levels spread into a valence band (filled with the bonding electrons) and a conduction band (empty or partly filled), separated by the forbidden gap \(E_g\). Conductors: the bands overlap or the conduction band is partly filled (\(E_g=0\)); insulators: \(E_g\gt3\) eV (diamond \(5.4\) eV; the valence band is full and the conduction band empty); semiconductors: \(0\lt E_g\lt3\) eV (Ge \(0.67\) eV, Si \(1.1\) eV, GaAs \(1.43\) eV). At \(0\) K a semiconductor is an insulator; as \(T\) increases electrons are excited across the gap, leaving holes in the valence band.
  • Temperature dependence: the resistance of a semiconductor decreases with temperature (a negative temperature coefficient, the number of carriers rises: \(n_i\propto T^{3/2}e^{-E_g/2kT}\)), while that of a metal increases (more collisions). A thermistor is made of a semiconducting oxide. The conductivity \(\sigma=e\left(n_e\mu_e+n_h\mu_h\right)\); the mobility \(\mu=\dfrac{v_d}E\) (\(\mu_e\gt\mu_h\)); current \(I=I_e+I_h\); the drift current density \(\vec J=\sigma\vec E\). Resistivities: metals \(10^{-8}\)–\(10^{-6}\) \(\Omega\) m, semiconductors \(10^{-5}\)–\(10^{6}\), insulators \(10^{11}\)–\(10^{19}\) \(\Omega\) m.
  • Intrinsic semiconductor (pure Si, Ge; four valence electrons in covalent bonds): the electron concentration equals the hole concentration, \(n_e=n_h=n_i\); a hole is a vacancy behaving as a positive carrier of the same charge magnitude. Extrinsic semiconductors are made by doping (about one impurity atom in \(10^6\)–\(10^8\) host atoms) which greatly increases the conductivity.
  • n-type: doped with a pentavalent impurity (P, As, Sb); each donor atom gives one extra electron; the donor level lies just below the conduction band (\(\approx0.01\) eV for Ge); \(n_e\gg n_h\), \(n_e\approx N_D\), electrons are the majority carriers and holes the minority carriers; the crystal is electrically neutral. p-type: doped with a trivalent impurity (B, Al, Ga, In); each acceptor atom creates a hole; the acceptor level is just above the valence band; \(n_h\gg n_e\), \(n_h\approx N_A\). Law of mass action: \(n_en_h=n_i^2\) (a constant at a given temperature), so adding donors reduces the hole concentration: \(n_h=\dfrac{n_i^2}{N_D}\). In the energy band picture the Fermi level is at the middle of the gap in an intrinsic semiconductor, shifts toward the conduction band for n-type and toward the valence band for p-type.
  • The compensation: doping a p-type semiconductor with equal donors makes it intrinsic again. Compound semiconductors: GaAs, GaP, InP, CdS (used in LEDs, lasers, photocells); Si is used for integrated circuits and solar cells. An integrated circuit (IC) packs thousands to billions of transistors on a silicon chip.

p–n junction: depletion layer, barrier, biasing and breakdown

  • Formation: electrons diffuse from the n-side to the p-side and holes the other way, leaving immobile positive donor ions in the n-side and negative acceptor ions in the p-side, which form the depletion region (no free carriers, width \(\approx10^{-7}\)–\(10^{-6}\) m) with a built-in field (\(\sim10^{6}\) V m\(^{-1}\)) and the barrier potential \(V_0\) (\(\approx0.7\) V for Si, \(0.3\) V for Ge) that opposes further diffusion. At equilibrium, the diffusion current equals the drift current (due to the minority carriers) and the net current is zero.
