  • Forward bias (p to the positive terminal): the applied voltage opposes the barrier, which becomes \(V_0-V\); the depletion region narrows; the diffusion current rises exponentially: \(I=I_s\left(e^{eV/kT}-1\right)\); the current is appreciable only after the threshold (knee) voltage. Reverse bias: the barrier increases to \(V_0+V_R\), the depletion region widens, only a tiny reverse (saturation) current of the minority carriers flows (\(\mu\)A for Ge, nA for Si), nearly independent of \(V_R\) but rising with temperature. A diode thus conducts in one direction (rectification). The forward dynamic resistance \(r_d=\dfrac{\Delta V}{\Delta I}\approx\dfrac{kT}{eI}\) (about \(26\ \text{mV}/I\) at room temperature); the reverse dynamic resistance is very large (\(\sim10^5\ \Omega\) or more).
  • Breakdown (a large reverse voltage): Zener breakdown — in heavily doped junctions with a thin depletion layer (\(V_z\lt5\) V), the strong field (\(\sim10^7\) V m\(^{-1}\)) pulls electrons out of the bonds directly (field emission); avalanche breakdown — in lightly doped junctions at higher voltages (\(\gt6\) V), minority carriers accelerated by the field ionise atoms by collision, giving a multiplication. The Zener diode is designed to operate in the breakdown region without damage.
  • Junction capacitance: the depletion layer acts as the dielectric of a capacitor (reverse-biased diodes used as varactors in tuned circuits; \(C\) decreases when the reverse voltage rises). An ideal diode is a switch with zero resistance in forward bias and infinite resistance in reverse; a practical Si diode drops \(\approx0.7\) V.
  • Circuit analysis tip: the diode is conducting (forward biased) if its p-end is at a higher potential than its n-end; replace it by a short (ideal) or a short plus a battery \(V_0\), and a reverse-biased one by an open circuit; check the assumption afterwards. A diode in series with a resistor: the current \(I=\dfrac{V-V_0}{R}\) when conducting.

Rectifiers, filters and the Zener voltage regulator

QuantityHalf-wave rectifierFull-wave rectifier
Output (ripple) frequency\(f\)\(2f\)
Average (dc) output \(V_{dc}\), \(I_{dc}\)\(\dfrac{V_m}\pi\), \(\dfrac{I_m}\pi\)\(\dfrac{2V_m}\pi\), \(\dfrac{2I_m}\pi\)
rms value \(I_{rms}\)\(\dfrac{I_m}2\)\(\dfrac{I_m}{\sqrt2}\)
Rectification efficiency (maximum)\(40.6\%\)\(81.2\%\)
Ripple factor \(r=\dfrac{I_{ac,rms}}{I_{dc}}\)\(1.21\)\(0.48\)
Peak inverse voltage (PIV)\(V_m\)\(2V_m\) (centre-tap), \(V_m\) (bridge)
  • Here \(V_m\) is the peak voltage across the load (the transformer secondary peak less the diode drop), \(I_m=\dfrac{V_m}{R_L+r_d}\). The efficiency \(\eta=\dfrac{P_{dc}}{P_{ac}}\) with \(P_{dc}=I_{dc}^2R_L\). Full-wave: a centre-tapped transformer with two diodes (each conducts in alternate half-cycles) or a bridge of four diodes (two conduct in each half-cycle; in each half-cycle the load current has the same direction; two diode drops in the path).
  • Filters: a capacitor filter (in parallel with the load) charges to the peak and discharges slowly through \(R_L\): the ripple \(\Delta V\approx\dfrac{I_L}{fC}\) (half-wave) or \(\dfrac{I_L}{2fC}\) (full-wave), small if \(R_LC\gg\) the period; an \(LC\) or \(\pi\) filter further reduces the ripple. The ac component is passed by the capacitor (it offers a low reactance \(\dfrac1{\omega C}\)) while the choke blocks it.
  • Zener voltage regulator: a Zener diode (reverse biased, breakdown voltage \(V_z\)) in parallel with the load \(R_L\), with a series resistor \(R_s\) from the unregulated supply \(V_{in}\): \(V_{out}=V_z\), \(I_s=\dfrac{V_{in}-V_z}{R_s}=I_z+I_L\). If \(V_{in}\) rises, \(I_s\) rises and the excess flows through the Zener (\(V_{out}\) unchanged); if the load current rises, \(I_z\) falls. The regulation holds as long as \(I_z\) lies between \(I_{z,\min}\) and \(I_{z,\max}\) (the rated power \(P_z=V_zI_{z,\max}\)). Example: \(V_{in}=15\) V, \(V_z=9\) V, \(R_s=300\ \Omega\): \(I_s=20\) mA; for \(I_L=5\) mA, \(I_z=15\) mA.
  • A clipper (a diode with a series resistor) cuts off a part of a waveform above or below a reference level; a clamper shifts the dc level; a voltage doubler uses two diodes and capacitors. The diode can also act as a logic gate (OR, AND).

Special-purpose diodes: LED, photodiode, solar cell

  • Light-emitting diode (LED): a heavily doped p–n junction that is forward biased; on the recombination of electrons and holes in the junction the energy is emitted as photons, \(h\nu\approx E_g\) (\(\lambda=\dfrac{1240}{E_g(\text{eV})}\) nm). Made of direct-band-gap compound semiconductors (GaAs: infrared; GaAsP: red; GaP: green/red); Si and Ge are indirect-gap and give heat, not light. The forward voltage is \(1.5\)–\(3.5\) V (more than a Si diode). Advantages: low operating voltage and power, no warm-up time, fast, long life, rugged, monochromatic and small. Used in displays, indicators, remote controls, optical communication and lighting.
  • Photodiode: a junction operated in reverse bias with a window; photons of energy \(h\nu\ge E_g\) create electron–hole pairs in the depletion region and the reverse (photo)current increases in proportion to the intensity of the light (the relative change is large compared with the very small dark current); used as a light detector, in optical communication, in the remote receivers and as a light meter; its response time is very short.
  • Solar cell (photovoltaic cell): a p–n junction without any bias with a large area and a thin top layer (p- or n-); the light creates electron–hole pairs in the depletion region, the built-in field separates them (electrons to the n-side, holes to the p-side) and the cell delivers an emf (\(\approx0.5\)–\(0.6\) V for Si, with a current proportional to the light intensity and the area); the \(I\)–\(V\) curve is in the fourth quadrant (the power delivered). Efficiency about \(15\)–\(20\%\); panels in series and parallel; used in satellites, calculators, pumps and power plants. Its energy gap should match the solar spectrum (\(\sim1.5\) eV is the best). The three photo-devices differ in their bias: LED forward, photodiode reverse, solar cell no bias.
  • Others: varactor (a reverse-biased variable capacitor), tunnel diode (negative resistance, a heavily doped junction), Schottky diode (a fast metal–semiconductor junction), laser diode (a coherent LED), photoconductive cells (LDR: the resistance falls with light).

Transistor: structure, relations, amplifier, switch and oscillator

  • Structure: a three-terminal device with two junctions: emitter (heavily doped, medium size, supplies the majority carriers), base (very thin and lightly doped), collector (moderately doped, the largest, to collect and dissipate heat). npn (n-p-n) and pnp types; the arrow on the emitter points in the direction of the conventional current (npn: out of the emitter; pnp: into the emitter). Normal (active) operation: emitter–base junction forward biased, collector–base junction reverse biased. In an npn transistor the emitter electrons cross the thin base (few recombine) and are swept into the collector.
  • Current relations: \(I_E=I_B+I_C\); \(I_B\) is only \(\approx2\)–\(5\%\) of \(I_E\). \(\alpha=\dfrac{I_C}{I_E}\) (\(0.95\)–\(0.99\), always \(\lt1\)); \(\beta=\dfrac{I_C}{I_B}\) (\(20\)–\(200\)); \(\beta=\dfrac\alpha{1-\alpha}\), \(\alpha=\dfrac\beta{1+\beta}\), \(I_E=(1+\beta)I_B\). The ac (small-signal) values \(\alpha_{ac}=\left(\dfrac{\Delta I_C}{\Delta I_E}\right)_{V_{CB}}\), \(\beta_{ac}=\left(\dfrac{\Delta I_C}{\Delta I_B}\right)_{V_{CE}}\). Transconductance \(g_m=\dfrac{\Delta I_C}{\Delta V_{BE}}=\dfrac{\beta_{ac}}{r_i}\).
  • Biasing (CE, base-resistor): \(I_B=\dfrac{V_{CC}-V_{BE}}{R_B}\), \(I_C=\beta I_B\), \(V_{CE}=V_{CC}-I_CR_C\) (the dc load line); the Q-point should be in the middle of the active region for undistorted amplification. Kirchhoff’s laws applied to the input and output loops.
  • CE amplifier: input resistance \(r_i=\dfrac{\Delta V_{BE}}{\Delta I_B}\), output resistance \(r_o=\dfrac{\Delta V_{CE}}{\Delta I_C}\); current gain \(A_I=\beta_{ac}\); voltage gain \(A_V=-\beta_{ac}\dfrac{R_L}{r_i}\) (the negative sign: a phase reversal of \(180^\circ\) between the output and the input); power gain \(A_P=A_IA_V=\beta_{ac}^2\dfrac{R_L}{r_i}\) (magnitude). CB amplifier: \(A_I=\alpha\lt1\), \(A_V=\alpha\dfrac{R_L}{r_i}\) (large, since \(r_i\) is small, no phase reversal), \(A_P=\alpha^2\dfrac{R_L}{r_i}\); the CE has the highest power gain and is the most widely used amplifier. In the CE amplifier a small change of the base current \(\Delta I_B\) causes a large change \(\Delta I_C=\beta\Delta I_B\).
  • Transistor as a switch (CE): for a small input \(V_i\lt0.6\) V (Si) the transistor is in cut-off (\(I_C\approx0\)) and \(V_o=V_{CC}\) (the switch is OFF); in the active region \(V_o\) falls linearly with \(V_i\) (an amplifier); for a large \(V_i\) it is in saturation, \(V_{CE}\approx0.2\) V, \(V_o\approx0\) (the switch ON). The transfer characteristic \(V_o\)–\(V_i\) shows the three regions; with a high and a low level, the CE circuit works as a NOT gate (an inverter).
  • Oscillator: an amplifier with positive feedback: a part of the output is fed back in phase with the input; sustained oscillations require the loop gain \(A\beta=1\) and a total phase shift of \(0^\circ\) (or \(360^\circ\)) (Barkhausen’s criterion); the frequency is set by an \(LC\) tank, \(f=\dfrac1{2\pi\sqrt{LC}}\) (Hartley, Colpitts) or an \(RC\) network. The amplifier alone gives \(180^\circ\) (CE), and the feedback network adds another \(180^\circ\).
  • Compare: the vacuum tube (triode) is a voltage-controlled device, large and hot; the transistor is a current-controlled device, small, cool, low power and reliable, with no warm-up time; the field-effect transistor (FET, MOSFET) is voltage-controlled with a very high input resistance.
36
Measurement & electronics

Digital Electronics & Logic Gates

Digital signals and number systems

  • Digital signal: a signal that takes only two discrete levels, high (\(1\), e.g. \(5\) V in TTL logic; \(2\)–\(5\) V accepted) and low (\(0\), e.g. \(0\) V; up to \(0.8\) V accepted). Positive logic: high \(=1\); negative logic: high \(=0\). A digital circuit works with the binary digits (bits) only, which makes it noise-immune and easy to build with a transistor switch.
  • Binary number system (base \(2\)): the place values are \(2^0,2^1,2^2,\dots\): \(1011_2=1\cdot8+0\cdot4+1\cdot2+1=11_{10}\); a fractional part \(0.101_2=\tfrac12+\tfrac18=0.625\). Decimal to binary: repeatedly divide by \(2\) and read the remainders upward (\(25\to11001_2\)); for a fraction, multiply by \(2\) repeatedly. An \(n\)-bit number represents \(0\) to \(2^n-1\) (\(2^n\) combinations); a byte \(=8\) bits (\(256\) values).
  • Octal (base \(8\), digits \(0\)–\(7\); three bits per digit) and hexadecimal (base \(16\), digits \(0\)–\(9,A\)–\(F\), \(A=10\ldots F=15\); four bits per digit): \(1011\,1101_2=BD_{16}=189_{10}\). BCD (binary-coded decimal) codes each decimal digit in four bits (\(59\to0101\,1001\)); the Gray code changes only one bit between successive numbers.
  • Binary arithmetic: \(0+0=0\), \(0+1=1\), \(1+1=10\) (sum \(0\), carry \(1\)), \(1+1+1=11\); multiplication is as in the decimal system (\(1\cdot1=1\), otherwise \(0\)); the subtraction by the 2’s complement: invert all the bits (the 1’s complement) and add \(1\); the subtraction \(A-B=A+\text{(2’s complement of }B)\), ignoring the final carry. Example: \(1101_2+0111_2=10100_2\) (\(13+7=20\)).
  • The ASCII code represents characters in \(7\) (or \(8\)) bits; the number of bits \(n\) needed to represent \(N\) states is \(\left\lceil\log_2N\right\rceil\).

Logic gates and truth tables

A logic gate is a digital circuit with one or more inputs and one output whose value follows a Boolean rule. In the symbols a small circle (bubble) at the output means inversion. A gate with \(n\) inputs has a truth table with \(2^n\) rows.

GateBoolean expression\(A,B=0,0\)\(0,1\)\(1,0\)\(1,1\)Output is high when
OR\(Y=A+B\)0111at least one input is high
AND\(Y=A\cdot B\)0001all inputs are high
NAND\(Y=\overline{A\cdot B}\)1110at least one input is low
NOR\(Y=\overline{A+B}\)1000all inputs are low
XOR\(Y=A\oplus B=\overline AB+A\overline B\)0110the inputs are different
XNOR\(Y=\overline{A\oplus B}=AB+\overline A\,\overline B\)1001the inputs are the same
  • NOT gate (inverter, one input): \(Y=\overline A\); \(A=0\to Y=1\), \(A=1\to Y=0\). The NOT gate is realised by a transistor in the CE switch mode (the input high drives the transistor into saturation and the output goes low).
  • An AND gate is a series switch circuit (both switches closed), an OR gate is a parallel switch circuit (either switch closed). The buffer gives \(Y=A\) (used for drive). Multi-input gates: AND of \(n\) inputs is high only for the one row with all \(1\)s; OR is low only for the all-\(0\) row; NAND and NOR are the complements.
  • Diode gates: an OR gate: two diodes with their anodes at the inputs and their cathodes joined to the output, which is connected to ground through a resistor (the output is high when any input is high); an AND gate: two diodes with their cathodes at the inputs and the anodes joined to the output and to \(+V\) through a resistor (the output is low if any input is low).
  • ICs: 7400 (quad NAND), 7402 (NOR), 7404 (hex NOT), 7408 (AND), 7432 (OR), 7486 (XOR). The gates are made from transistors (TTL, CMOS); the NAND and NOR are cheaper to build and are universal.

Boolean algebra, De Morgan’s theorems and universal gates

  • Identities: \(A+0=A\), \(A+1=1\), \(A\cdot0=0\), \(A\cdot1=A\); \(A+A=A\), \(A\cdot A=A\); \(A+\overline A=1\), \(A\cdot\overline A=0\); \(\overline{\overline A}=A\); commutative \(A+B=B+A\), \(AB=BA\); associative; distributive \(A(B+C)=AB+AC\) and \(A+BC=(A+B)(A+C)\); absorption \(A+AB=A\), \(A(A+B)=A\), \(A+\overline AB=A+B\).
  • De Morgan’s theorems: \(\overline{A+B}=\overline A\cdot\overline B\) and \(\overline{A\cdot B}=\overline A+\overline B\) (an OR with inverted output equals an AND of the inverted inputs, i.e. a NOR = a bubbled-input AND; a NAND = a bubbled-input OR). They extend to any number of variables; “break the line and change the sign”.
  • Universal gates: any logic function can be built from NAND gates alone (or NOR alone). With NAND: NOT \(\overline A=\overline{A\cdot A}\) (tie the inputs together); AND \(=\overline{\overline{AB}}\) (a NAND followed by a NOT); OR \(A+B=\overline{\overline A\cdot\overline B}\) (invert each input, then NAND). With NOR: NOT \(\overline A=\overline{A+A}\); OR \(=\overline{\overline{A+B}}\) (a NOR followed by a NOT); AND \(AB=\overline{\overline A+\overline B}\) (invert both inputs, then NOR). XOR using four NAND gates: let \(N=\overline{AB}\); then \(Y=\overline{\overline{A\,N}\cdot\overline{B\,N}}=A\oplus B\).
  • Simplification and analysis: write the Boolean expression for the output step by step through the circuit and make a truth table; e.g. an OR gate followed by a NOT gives a NOR; the output \(Y=\overline{A}B+A\overline B\) is XOR; a circuit \(Y=\overline{(A+B)}+\overline{(A\cdot B)}=\overline{A+B}+\overline{AB}\): for \(A=B=0\), \(Y=1\); for \(A=1,B=0\), \(Y=0+1=1\); for \(A=B=1\), \(Y=0+0=0\) — it equals NAND. Sum of products from a truth table: for every row with output \(1\) write the AND of the inputs (complemented if the input is \(0\)) and OR the terms together (the minimal form is found with a Karnaugh map).
  • A key trick: the NAND/NOR with both inputs tied together is a NOT gate; two inputs of an AND or an OR tied together give a buffer; an XOR gate with one input tied high is a NOT gate (a controlled inverter); an XOR with the input tied low is a buffer.

Combinational circuits: adders; a first look at flip-flops

  • Half adder (adds two bits \(A,B\)): sum \(S=A\oplus B\), carry \(C=A\cdot B\) (an XOR and an AND gate). \(1+1\): \(S=0\), \(C=1\). Full adder (adds \(A\), \(B\) and a carry-in \(C_{in}\)): \(S=A\oplus B\oplus C_{in}\), \(C_{out}=AB+C_{in}(A\oplus B)\) (two half adders and an OR gate). A multi-bit adder is a cascade of full adders (ripple carry).
  • The half subtractor: difference \(D=A\oplus B\), borrow \(=\overline A\,B\). A multiplexer selects one of several inputs, a decoder converts a binary code to one active line, a comparator gives \(Y=\overline{A\oplus B}\) for equality of two bits.
  • Sequential circuits (the output depends on the past inputs, i.e. memory): a flip-flop stores one bit. A latch of two cross-coupled NOR (or NAND) gates is the SR flip-flop: \(S=1,R=0\) sets \(Q=1\); \(S=0,R=1\) resets \(Q=0\); \(S=R=0\) holds the previous state; \(S=R=1\) is forbidden (for NOR gates). The D flip-flop stores the input at the clock edge; the JK flip-flop toggles for \(J=K=1\). Flip-flops are used in registers and counters (a counter of \(n\) flip-flops counts up to \(2^n-1\)); frequency division by \(2\) per flip-flop.
  • Logic families: TTL (transistor–transistor logic, \(5\) V, fast), CMOS (low power, wide supply range), ECL (very fast). Applications: alarms (AND of conditions), lighting control, security, calculators (adders), computers (millions of gates on a chip), counters, digital clocks.
  • Worked patterns: an alarm that rings if the door is open (\(A=1\)) and the switch is on (\(B=1\)): AND; one that rings if either window sensor trips: OR; a gate that is high only when the inputs differ (a parity check of two bits): XOR; a safety circuit that is on only when no input is active: NOR.
End of booklet
